Hey everyone and welcome to Data Science, Natural Language Processing with Transformers. This is the best and most in-depth online resource for learning about how to use transformers to analyze, understand and even generate language. So what is this course about? This course we'll teach you about Transformers. Which are these state of the art method of applying machine learning to language? Transformers are the latest deep learning architecture to outperform all existing methods.

You might know about Transformers for NLP, but they've also made significant contributions to computer vision and even computational biology. In the field of NLP, transformers produced the best models for translation, question, answering and even generating text, which is indistinguishable from text generated by humans. I couldn't have said that just a few years ago. Transformers are so good they can even write computer code. In fact, transformers are so good that models such as GPT-3 have everyone talking about how this could be the key to AGI or artificial general intelligence. In computer vision,

Transformers have impressed everyone by combining image and text representations in order to generate amazing art based only on a simple text description. In molecular biology, transformers have helped scientists to solve a longstanding problem called the protein structure prediction. That is, how can we map your genetic code to actual physical proteins and what do they look like? This is the perfect task for NLP since the genetic code really is just a programming language.

It is the programming language of life. So I hope you're convinced that Transformers are pretty awesome. So who should take this course? Anyone who wants to understand and apply the latest state of the art deep learning methods to NLP should take this course. This course is something for everyone, whether you are beginner, intermediate or advanced. For developers who are more practically oriented, the first part of this course will have no theory and minimal code.

You will learn how to use the most powerful transformers with just a few minutes of efforts. For developers with a little more experience, the second part of this course will look at how to fine tune Transformers so that you can optimize them on your own data sets. This will help you build state of the art NLP products at your company and for your clients. For expert developers who want to understand it. Transformers In-Depth. The third part of this course is for you.

In this section, we will take a deep dive into how the attention mechanism works and eventually how to build a transformer from scratch. This will be especially useful for those of you who don't want to just fine tune NLP models, but to apply transformers to new areas such as genomics, time, series and finance. Now you know that no Lazy Programmer course is complete without in-depth material, and that's really what the advance section is all about.

Whereas other courses might just show you a few lines of API code. My goal in this course is to really help you understand that transformers. In fact, by the end of this course, you'll understand Transformers so well you'll be able to build one yourself from scratch. It's important to remember that transformers aren't just one thing. There are multiple kinds of transformers, such as BERT and GPT. In this course, you'll learn about how to categorize between these different types of transformers.

What is BERT and what is GPT? And how are they different? Importantly, how do you know which one to choose for a specific task? This is the real value behind this course. And by learning about transformers in depth, these skills will be yours.

So in this lecture, I'm going to give you a brief overview of everything you will learn in this course. This course is quite a large course and was several years in the making. In fact, years ago, when I began creating this course, a lot of the tools we're going to use didn't even exist or existed in primitive form, thanks to the hard work of machine learning engineers around the world. This course is very accessible, even for complete beginners. The best way to understand this course is that at a high level it's made up of three parts the beginners section, the intermediate section and the advanced section.

So the beginners corner is all about how to use Transformers as a beginner. This means that you might not be too great with coding and you definitely do not like math and theory in this course. That's okay, at least for this part. In this section, you'll be taught how to use state of the art models with just one or two lines of code. We'll cover various real world, very practical tasks, such as sentiment analysis. We'll even look at how to use GPT-2 to generate text.

This was one of the major breakthroughs with Transformers. Since before this machine learning models were not able to generate believable, coherent text that might be confused for what a human wrote. We'll look at how to perform classic NLP tasks like named entity recognition, text summarization and neural machine translation. Transformers have pushed the state of the art for all of these. Another interesting task is the masked language model. This might sound complicated, but in fact it's quite simple.

I introduced this idea in my first NLP course, released a few years before people even trained at Transformers to do this. I called it the article Spinner. This is because such models can be used for SEO purposes to spin existing content so that search engines can't figure out that you borrowed it from somewhere else. Now, to be clear, this is a blackhat technique, and I don't condone doing it, but nonetheless, it is something that exists. It is something that is very practical, and it is something that many people are willing to pay good money for.

Finally, we'll cover a few modern tasks, such as question answering, as well as zero shot classification. Question answering is a very impressive feat because it gives us the sense that the model really understands the text that it has read. You can give it a passage and then ask it a question, just like you would to schoolchildren after giving them a reading assignment. Does this mean Transformers really have achieved reading comprehension? I'll let you be the judge of that.

Zero shot classification is also extremely impressive. Normally, when we want to train a classifier like, say, a spam detector, we need to give it a label dataset. That means someone like you and me has to gather large amounts of data and manually assign labels to each documents one by one. As you can imagine, this is either time consuming or costly, or both. With zero-shot classification, we don't have to train at all. Instead, we simply give our model some text and a set of possible labels, and it will magically understand the text well enough to predicts the correct label very often.

Now, although this all seems pretty impressive, do keep in mind that this is just the first section of the course. In the next section, we'll move on to intermediate content. This section will focus on how you can fine tune a transformer on your own data sets. By doing this, you're no longer limited to the pre-trained models from the first section, but instead you can train a state of the art transformer for any task. Transformers have borrowed a technique from computer vision known as transfer learning, which allows us to use the weights from one task and apply them to a different but similar task for state of the art performance.

In other words, the model you get from transfer learning will be superior to a model you train from scratch. We achieve this by leveraging massive amounts of unlabeled data on the internet and millions of dollars worth of training time. All for free. What this means is that you can take advantage of these neural networks that have essentially learned everything in the world and apply them to your own datasets with little effort. The researchers who built these models spent millions of dollars doing so, but you get to use them at no cost.

This is amazing in this part of the course. We'll build things up from the simplest task to more complicated tasks. However, don't be fooled into thinking that simple is not practical. The simplest task is probably the most practical. Specifically, we'll begin with how to fine tune a model to classify text example applications, including spam detection and sentiment analysis. In the next section, we'll look at how to fine tune a model to classify each word within a text.

Some examples are named entity recognition and parts of speech tagging. We'll then move on to more complex applications like machine translation and question answering. In other words, you'll learn how to take the powerful transformers from the first section and make them even better. In the third part of this course, things get really interesting. And in my opinion, this is the best part. This is where you get to learn how transformers actually work.

The previous sections are nice because you get to use Transformers, but for professional machine learning engineers, you often need to understand things on a deeper level. Now, don't be fooled as there's a practical component to this, too. Who wants to learn this stuff? Well, those who want the big bucks, the best and highest paying machine learning jobs require this knowledge. Everyone can copy and paste three lines of code, and because of this, employers aren't willing to pay that much, if that's all you can do.

But those who put in the hard work to really understand machine learning at a theoretical level are able to command higher salaries. In this section, we will learn precisely how the self-attention mechanism works, which is the key component behind Transformers. We'll then extend this to build multi-head attention and then add a few more ingredients to finally build up a full transformer block. Once we have our transformer block, we'll then study specific architectures such as the encoder, the decoder and the encoder-decoder combined.

We'll also look at specific models which have been prominent in the field, such as BERT and GPT. Importantly, you'll learn about how to categorize the different types of transformers so that you'll know which kind to pick for any particular task. For instance, what BERT excels at is different from what GPT excels at. And there are other things which neither BERT nor GPT can do. Understanding which type of transformer is used for which type of task is necessary if you want to use transformers in practice.

After looking at the theory behind Transformers, you'll be armed with all the knowledge you need to actually implement one from scratch. Now, when I say from scratch, you should also keep in mind the correct level of abstraction. In the past, although we've implemented a simple neural networks using numpy, that would not be an appropriate tool at this stage. At this stage, when we say from scratch, we mean using a library like TensorFlow or PyTorch and possibly others.

And this is actually practical for two reasons. Reason number one is that transformers can really be applied to anything. They've even been applied to computer vision and computational biology with great success. Perhaps they might also be applied to computational chemistry, physics, and other important fields. But the library we're using is focused around NLP, and the PRE-TRAINED models we have are trained on text. So if you ever want to use transformers in other fields to advance the state of the arts, there won't be any pre-trained models for you to use.

Or maybe there will be pre-trained models, but they won't be popular enough to be part of a simple to use library like what you see in this course. You'll simply have to know how to build a transformer from scratch. Reason number two is that you don't really, truly understand Transformers until you've built one yourself. I've been saying this motto for over five years now, and it's still true. If you can't implement it, then you don't understand it. The great physicist Richard Feynman said something similar.

He said, what I cannot create, I do not understand. So I hope you're convinced that understanding the theory behind Transformers so that you can build one from scratch is something worth doing, not only for higher pay and a more prestigious job, but also to advance science and to make new discoveries. Thanks for listening and I'll see you in the next lecture.

So in this lecture, I want to talk about how to practice what you've learned in this course and how to make sure you are able to carry out the tasks you've been taught to do. Now, firstly, as mentioned in the introduction to this course, this is a hands on course. So what does this mean? This means that in this course you are strongly encouraged to get your hands dirty. Yes, you your actual hands typing on your keyboard, the things you learned in these lectures.

So a few of you might be wondering, how does this work? So let's talk about how this works. Basically, the lectures in this course will proceed in a two step pattern. The first step will always be to discuss the concepts. What's going on? What are we trying to accomplish, how does it work? And so on and so forth. That's the first step. The second step will be to implement those ideas in actual working code. This is where getting your hands dirty comes into play.

You see, what I want you to do is when you see these lectures, I want you to open your own a notebook or your coding environment and start typing out the stuff you see so that you learn how to implement these concepts yourself. Too often what I see is students just downloading notebooks and hitting play, and what ends up happening is that they think they know what they're doing, but in practice they are completely incapable of writing any code themselves.

And if you ask them, Do you know how to write this code, they will say yes. And then if you ask them, well, how much of this code did you actually write? The answer is none of it. And, you know, if you ever get a job or work in a project, that's what you have to do. You need to write actual code, not just believe in your mind that it can be done. The real world requires you to code, not to just watch other people code or download code that's already been written.

The real world doesn't reward you for simply believing in yourself. The real world rewards you when you produce useful outputs. So that's what you're learning to do in this course. Produce useful outputs, not just thinking you can. Now, this course is mostly an API focused course. This is opposed to a more theoretical course where you might be taught an algorithm and then be expected to implement that algorithm from scratch. For this course being mostly an API focused course, you're not going to know the syntax until I actually show it to you.

So in that sense, you will need to see the code as you code. So in this short lecture, I'm going to explain how you, as the student, should navigate this course. This is something which didn't exist in my older courses and doesn't exist at all in other courses. So this may be new for you. For this course, there is a wide range of difficulty from topic to topic. For this reason, I've designated some lectures and some sections by their level, which can be either beginner, intermediate or advanced. Obviously beginner content is the easiest, whereas intermediate is a bit more difficult.

Well, advanced is the most difficult. So please watch out for these designations which will be in the titles of either the lectures or full sections, typically in parentheses. Now, the reason I've done this is sometimes I encounter beginner students who bite off more than they can chew, and as a result, they become frustrated when the content is too difficult for their current abilities. Of course, there is no need for that. Simply be more aware of your abilities and accept yourself as you are.

As a teacher, I always encourage you to improve your skills such that you'll be able to handle more advanced content. But one thing you should not try to do is to tackle an advanced topic when you are not at that stage in your journey. I don't want you to hope or expect that the advanced content will somehow be explained in a so-called intuitive way such that you can understand it even if you don't have the prerequisite skills. Now, unfortunately, these designations are somewhat arbitrary because nobody can really agree on precisely what skills are considered beginner, intermediate or advanced.

Therefore, it may take some trial and error on your part and you'll have to adjust as needed. So for example, if you try some intermediate content but it's too difficult for you, then clearly the advanced content will be even more difficult. At the same time, advanced students will get the most out of this course. That's simply because they'll have the abilities to understand all of the content from beginner to intermediate to advanced. So not only the advanced content is for the advanced students, but all the content is for the advanced students.

In this sense, advanced doesn't mean you already know the content, since at that point taking this course would be pointless. Advanced really means you are mentally prepared to handle the more challenging tasks. Conversely, beginner doesn't mean you're new and unfamiliar with these topics. It simply means you are not mentally prepared. So you should use that as motivation to get yourself to an advanced level. If you are not there yet, a lot of the time this is simply due to your experience level.

This is not something I can give to you. It's simply something you have to cultivate yourself. Nothing I can give you will replace the simple act of practice and gaining experience. I would if I could. But maturity and experience are things you must earn but that you cannot take. For example, some projects in this course will simply require a good amount of coding. Some people are good at this, and some people not so good. But luckily, it's a skill that anyone can cultivate as long as they work hard and have patience.

So you may not understand complex code today, but with more practice and experience you will. At that point, the more advanced content will feel more accessible. Note that some lectures and sections have no designation, so you can feel free to watch any of that content regardless of your level. At the same time, remember to pace yourself and remember that there might simply be some things which you do not know in those cases. You should use the Q&A to ask for help.

Now, while there are prerequisites to this course, realize that they are not the be all and end all. Sometimes you might meet the prerequisites, but simply lack practice and experience applying those prerequisites. So as an example, Python coding is a prerequisite to this course, but clearly there are varying levels of expertise. At the same time, we can't say something like must have five years of experience in Python because that wouldn't make sense.

Some people have been coding for ten years who are not very good and some people have been coding for six months and are already very good. So it's not time that matters, but rather mental preparedness and maturity. At the same time, you shouldn't be alarmed because there is still content in the course for you to complete no matter your level. So if you find that there is any content in the course that seems beyond your reach, simply cultivate your skills and come back at a later time.

Remember that you have this course forever, so there is no rush to complete all the lectures at once.

So in this lecture, I want to give you a few quick tips in order to help you succeed in this course. Now, generally speaking, I find that when students follow these tips, they always succeed. And most often when I see students that are not succeeding, it is because they are not following these tips. Now, I want to first mention that I truly believe that anyone can succeed in this course. This is the case whether you are a teenager still in high school or a senior who's retired.

And it's been decades since you've studied college math. In all cases, it really comes down to following instructions. Being diligent is key. As long as you can make a promise to yourself to be diligent and follow these guidelines, then I can promise you that you will succeed. Now, that being said, what are the tips? In brief, tip number one is to use the Q&A. Too often I see comments from students saying, oh, X, Y, Z is missing from this course. My response is typically, well, actually X, y, z is covered in lecture five.

You simply missed it. But you see, I can't tell you that if you don't ask. So what's the better outcome? You live your whole life never knowing the answer to this question, or you simply ask. Maybe bruise your ego a little bit, but at the very least improve your knowledge and skill. I prefer the outcome that results in you being better and more knowledgeable. Now, sometimes students reject this tip and say, but I didn't know what question to ask. This is good.

You are exactly who should be asking questions. The reason you don't know how to formulate your question is because you haven't thought through the subject thoroughly enough. Most of the time, what I've seen is that in the process of trying to come up with a question, the question often answers itself. Okay, so tip number two is to meet the prerequisites to this course. Now, a lot of students think of prerequisites as a barrier to entry. They think, Oh, if the instructor mentions the prerequisites, he's punishing me by making me do more work.

And he's saying I'm dumb for not knowing this stuff. Guys, this is not true. The prerequisites are simply information which exists to help you. It's information. It's not a barrier. It's not anything to attack you. It's simply information. So how does this information help you? Well, after you have this information, if you ever see anything in this course and you think that looks weird, I've never seen this before at that point, it's now a good time to revisit the prerequisites, to make sure that there wasn't something you missed.

If you didn't have the prerequisite list, then you would have no idea where to look. But with the prerequisite list now you have a guide that tells you what you might be missing. Okay, So now on to tip number three. This is something I've mentioned already, but it's so important that it's worth it to repeat. Be actively involved and get your hands dirty in this course. Like if your hands are not physically moving while you are taking this course, you are not doing this correctly.

There are two types of lectures in this course. Conceptual lectures and coding lectures. With conceptual lectures, you need to be taking handwritten notes. What I've seen myself in the past, what I've seen many other people say and what a lot of psychological research demonstrates, is that taking handwritten notes is a superior method of learning and absorbing information. This is better than staring at slides, better than watching videos passively and better than even typing notes.

Something about physically writing builds a superior mind muscle connection. Now, when it comes to coding lectures, you already know this, but open up your favorite coding environment and write that code. If it's a course where you're learning an algorithm, then remember that implementing the algorithm is your exercise for the course. If you're just watching the lectures all the way through and twiddling your thumbs, then you are cheating by looking at the solution before completing the exercise yourself.

But whether you're implementing an algorithm or just learning about a library, you must actually write code. I don't know any data scientists or machine learning engineers who don't code. Now, I just want to mention that this list is a condensed version of the full How to Succeed Lecture, which can be found in the FAQ of this course. So if you have any objections to these tips or you think that they don't quite make sense, please check out that lecture for more detail.

Okay, so what is the common thread between all these tips? The common thread is that the reason students don't follow these tips is because they let their egos get in the way. In fact, ego is the number one thing holding you back from succeeding in this course. Your pride is holding you back from succeeding in this course. Here's a fun fact. I've noticed a disproportionately large number of students claiming to have PhDs, but who do very poorly. Yet I see undergrads, masters and even high school students who do better.

How can this be? Well, you see, the problem with PhDs is that they think they are so smart that they have nothing left to learn. This is an example of ego and pride, holding them back from learning new things. For tip number one, ego holds people back from using the Q&A. Often using the Q&A involves you asking a question which reveals your mistaken assumptions, which will then be corrected by whomever answers the question. This might hurt you a little bit knowing that you got something wrong.

But as mentioned, it's better to learn that you are wrong instead of continuing to be wrong and blissfully ignorant. For tip number two, Ego holds people back from meeting the prerequisites. They think I took calculus, therefore I know calculus and anything that involves calculus that I see in the future, I must understand. Otherwise it wasn't explained correctly. Guys, this is not a good way to go through life. I learned new things about machine learning constantly, despite the fact that I took my first machine learning course over a decade ago.

And let's be clear. If you got a B or a C in your course, which is a so called average grade, that means quantitatively your understanding was only at 60 or 70%. Now, another common excuse I see is. Oh, yeah, well, I took calculus 20 years ago, so I forgot everything. It's now your responsibility to teach me everything again. Guys, no, this does not count as meeting the prerequisites. Remember that the prerequisites are there to ensure you have a skill that you can make use of.

It is not a list of credentials. Credentials might matter when you apply for a job, but they're irrelevant in a self-paced course when all you have to depend on is yourself. Think of it this way. Would you fly an airplane if you had a piece of paper saying that you could? But in actuality you forgot everything about how to actually fly airplanes? I think that would be very dangerous. And I don't think that any normal person would answer yes to this question.

Finally, for tip number three, ego holds people back from getting their hands dirty and taking notes and writing code. I can tell that this is the case because when I mention how writing code is a great exercise and it is literally the job you will do as a data scientist, people would rather argue with me than accept this advice. They care more about calling me names and making threats than they do about actually becoming better data scientists. They simply cannot handle the reality that muscle memory exists.

Coding is a useful exercise, and coding is literally the job description. They would rather believe in their minds that they can write perfect code without any practice. The reason they're afraid to code is that once they start, they might make mistakes which would expose their imperfect coding abilities. And that's the most difficult thing for someone with too much pride and too much ego. They simply cannot face the idea of being wrong or making mistakes.

It's too burdensome to bear. Okay, so I hope you guys are getting the idea. Do not let your ego get in the way of your success. Don't be afraid to be wrong and follow these instructions as diligently as possible. Thanks for listening and I'll see you in the next lecture.

### 3. Beginner's Corner

1. Beginner's Corner Section Introduction
=========================================

So in this lecture we are going to introduce the next section of this course, which is a beginners corner on Transformers. Now, you must be wondering if you are new to my courses or even if you've taken my courses before. What is a beginners corner? So to answer this question, the beginners corner section is a section that is designed with the typical beginner in mind. So what is the typical beginner? Well, the typical beginner sentiment is I just want to learn to drive the car.

I don't care about how the car works. You might also think of this in terms of becoming a doctor. Perhaps you don't actually want to be a doctor or to put in the years of work required to become a doctor. You just want to understand what a doctor does in their day to day job. Maybe your goal is to become a doctor's assistant. In terms of machine learning. Perhaps it's the case that you don't want to be a machine learning engineer or to understand how machine learning works.

Perhaps you're an Android developer or a web developer. And the intuition is enough. In this case, the beginners corner is for you. Now, the reason I've done this is my courses are normally pretty in-depth, and they go far beyond what a typical random person off the street wants to see. Of course, ignoring the fact that a typical random person off the street does not become a machine learning practitioner in a few months. It's still nice to give them something they can take with them from this course.

So maybe not all of the course suits your needs, but at least you might find some use in 20 to 30% of the course. This will be useful for those of you who once you know about transformers and want to use Transformers but perhaps might be too inexperienced to get into the details. Okay. So the basic thing to understand about this section is that this section is about how to use transformers, but it is not a section about how Transformers work, since that will come at a later time.

In fact, for this section, there is no need to understand how Transformers work, since everything can be done in just one or two lines of Python code. One thing this does is it really highlights the power that you have at your fingertips, even without much of an understanding. This is unlike earlier forms of deep learning where you really needed to be pretty knowledgeable to do anything useful. And this is thanks to the Hugging Face library, which brings many powerful models into a simple universal interface.

Now for non beginners, you might be wondering, is this section useful for me if I do want to understand how transformers work? And the answer is yes. And so how can that be? Well, the answer is that by this point, if you are a non beginner, you have a pretty good idea of the steps involved in building a neural network. For NLP in particular, you understand the process of tokenization converting tokens into integers and converting those integers into embedding vectors.

You also understand how embeddings act as the initial layers in a neural network. Furthermore, you also understand how different outputs of a neural network are suited to different tasks. For example, the output of a binary spam detector would be different from the output of a text generator. So as you go through this section, think about how the inputs and outputs would be structured in order to complete each task. Some of these will be pretty easy. For instance, sentiment analysis, which just uses a sigmoid when the output is binary.

On the other hand, tasks like question answering and zero-shot classification are very different from what you may be used to. And because these new architectures are so different from what you've seen before, it really is useful to look only at the interface first before looking inside to see how things actually work. Okay. So let's go through an outline of what you will learn in this section. Now to begin, I don't want to leave you without any idea of what Transformers even are.

So we'll discuss the kind of derivation for how we got to Transformers, starting with RNNs after this brief introduction. We'll then begin to look at some applications for each of these applications will proceed in two steps. The first step will be to discuss what the task is and why we would want to do it, along with a brief look at the corresponding Hugging Face syntax. The second step will be to look in a notebook to demo the code, actually working on a real data set.

You'll be surprised to see that for all of these tasks there is no training required at all. Of course this makes sense since language is language no matter what data set it comes from. For example, that's why you can learn English in grade school, but after you graduate, you can still communicate with new people, despite the fact that you've never met them in the past. As another example, you can read and understand the sentiment of a movie review, even if you've never read a movie review before.

There's just a universality to language in that sense. So what are the tasks we will look at? Well, you'll recognize many of these tasks as ones you've seen before. If you've taken my courses or perhaps other courses within this field, of course this doesn't make these applications redundant, since transformers tend to provide a state of the art performance. In other words, hopefully better than what you've been able to achieve in the past. So the first task we'll look at is called sentiment analysis.

We'll see that even though the transformer we'll use has never seen our data set before, it will still do decently well. And thus this might be a suitable solution. If you want to do sentiment analysis on generic data you find on the internet or at your workplace. The next task we will look at is how to transform text into vectors or embeddings using a transformer network. Using these embeddings, we can do things like similarity search, also known as nearest neighbor search.

The next task we'll look at is called the named entity recognition. As you recall, this is an example of a many-to-many task. This is because for each token in the input, we have a target output. Either that will tell us what kind of named entity the token is or if it is not a named entity. Thus there will be many outputs one for each of the input tokens. This is opposed to a task like sentiment analysis where we only have one output for the whole input document.

The next task we'll look at is text generation, where we use an auto regressive transformer to generate text one token after another. This is very similar to how we use Markov models, except that deep neural networks can account for the entire past, whereas Markov models are limited to just one or a few tokens. We'll also look at a similar task known as masked language modeling. In my courses we've referred to this as article spinning, where a common approach is to change random words in a document using the surrounding context to choose the most appropriate substitutions.

As you recall, this is also a form of language model, except that instead of our distribution being conditioned on past words only, it can be conditioned on both past and future words. The next task we'll look at is called text summarization. Again, this is a different task from the others since the input is a document in the output is also a document with a different length than the input, hopefully shorter. This is another example of a sequence to sequence task.

The next task we'll look at is language translation. This is yet another example of a sequence-to-sequence task. Since both the input and target will be strings of text. One interesting fact about translation is that because of how grammar works in different languages, translating is not simply a matter of mapping one word to another for every word in the source text. Instead, you might have one word mapped to multiple words. Multiple words map to one word, multiple words mapped to multiple words and re-ordering of words to match the grammar of the target language.

This task is very commonly used for building up the intuition for the attention mechanism in transformers since at each point in the output. We need to decide which input to pay attention to. The next task we'll look at is question answering. So this is one task you may have not seen before. Technically, it is a sequence to sequence task, but there are many interesting complexities. Firstly, the input to the neural network is made up of two pieces of text, the question and the context, which contains the answer to the question.

This is interesting because for most other tasks in NLP, there is only one piece of text that goes into the input. In fact, although we do have two pieces of text, what we end up doing is concatenating them into a single document. What's really impressive is that modern neural networks are still able to parse what this means and answer the question with good performance. The final task we'll look at is also a new task, which you probably haven't seen before.

This task is called zero-shot classification, which is another very interesting task in terms of what neural networks are capable of. So how this task works is you ask your model to classify a piece of text. But unlike the other classifiers we've seen, the set of possible labels have never been seen by the model before. So I can ask my model is this document about biology or physics? And you'll see that it will be able to tell me the answer, despite never being trained to classify biology and physics documents.

In my view, this shows that the neural network has attained a higher level of understanding. It's able to choose from a set of classes based only on its understanding of language. So you could do spam detection without actually training on a spam detection dataset. Okay, so I hope you're excited to learn about these topics. I'll see you in the next lecture.

So in this lecture, we'll be going over a brief discussion about where Transformers came from and how this idea was derived from earlier neural network architectures, specifically recurrent neural networks. Please note that this lecture is optional since it's not necessary to be able to use the code that you will see later in this section. However, if you plan to go further in your study of Transformers, you may find this useful. So this lecture is for those of you who have a pretty good understanding of deep learning fundamentals, and you also want to learn the basic intuition for what transformers and attention do.

Now, for those of you who do not want to go through this lecture, I want to provide you with at least two main points to keep in mind. Point number one, the attention mechanism is what allows the neural network to learn very long term dependencies over sequences. In fact, it does a much better job than the LSTM, which was previously the most popular architecture for sequential data sets. Recall that the LSTM is a type of recurrent neural network.

Attention originated from RNNs. The transformer is essentially the attention mechanism, but without using any recurrent units to process the input. Point number two transformers are very large neural networks and as a consequence go very slow. In other words, they may take more time in computation then what you're used to. However, the type of computation it does can be done in parallel. Whereas this is not the case for RNNs. RNN computations must be done sequentially, and there is no way around this.

Now, again, since this lecture is optional, there is no need to go through the rest. So if you just want to remember these main points, please feel free to skip the remaining parts. Okay. So to begin our story, we'll start with RNN. As you recall, RNNs are used for sequences for each input x of t, an RNN unit will produce a hidden vector at the corresponding time step h of t. This hidden vector can be considered the output of the RNN unit, but also as a kind of state.

This is because this state is actually fed into the RNN unit at the next time step. So in other words, h of t is computed not only from X of T but also h of t minus one. h of t plus one is computed from x of t plus one and h of t. So you can imagine this like a train of RNN units, although in reality they are the same unit being applied to multiple pieces of data in the sequence. And as you recall, there are multiple kinds of RNN units such as the LSTM or G, r u in this lecture, knowing which kind of unit we're dealing with is not required.

So as you recall, RNN units are used for multiple kinds of tasks which all use slightly different architectures. For instance, we might be doing a many-to-one task, such as spam detection. In this case, we only have a single output from our RNN, which is typically computed from the final hidden state in the simplest case. This final hidden state, h of big T, is passed through a simple, dense layer which then produces an output probability. However, there is another kind of task known as a many-to-many task where we have multiple outputs.

One example of this is parts of speech tagging. In this case, we have one output for every word in the input sequence. In other words, every word gets a parts of speech tag. So for this kind of task, we keep all the hidden states, h of one, h of two and so forth, up to h of big T. And we pass all of these through a final dense layer to get predictions for y one, y two, all the way up to y big T. Now, here's a scenario where neither of these previous two methods will work.

Suppose we're doing language translation where the input is a sequence of words in the target is also a sequence of words, but in a different language. As you may know, if you speak different languages, this is not as straightforward as simply translating one word at a time due to every language having different rules and different numbers of tokens or words to represent the same concept. Put more simply, there are two problems. Problem number one is that the input sequence length is not necessarily equal to the target sequence length.

For instance, one word from the input might map to multiple words in the target or vice versa. Problem number two is that if we produce the output in a recurrent manner, this may not give us all the information we need to compute an accurate prediction. As you recall, each output, let's call it y hat of t, depends only on the hidden state h of t. But what does h of t depend on? Well, it depends only on X of T and the previous hidden state h of t minus one.

In turn, h of t minus one depends on x of t minus one and h of t minus two. And of course, we keep repeating this pattern, and we can see that y hat of t depends only on x of one up to x of t. In other words, the prediction at time t depends only on the inputs up to this time. However, this doesn't make any sense for language translation because there can be scenarios where the translated word depends on an input word that comes later in the input sequence. As an example of this,

we can note that in French adjectives come after nouns, which is not the case in English. For instance, take the English sentence "She is a smart girl". In French, The equivalent ordering would be she is a girl smart. Another example is that in Spanish questions not only ends with a question mark, but they also begin with an inverted question mark and thus there are two tokens. If we're translating a question into Spanish that would be used to denote that our input was a question, but that corresponds to only one token in English, which appears at the end of the sentence.

So what is the solution to this problem? Well, the initial solution to this problem was called seq2seq, short for sequence to sequence. This introduces the concept of having two RNNs, one called the encoder and one called the decoder. This is helpful in understanding transformers as well, since some transformers also use an encoder decoder system. So the way this works is the encoder is used to ingest the input. Note that this does not have any final dense layer.

We simply compute all the h of t's at every time step of the input. This will give us one final hidden state vector, which we'll call h of big T, assuming that the input sequence has length big T. h of big T will be considered the output of the encoder while also being the input to the decoder. You can think of h of big T as a vector, which is like a compressed representation of the input sequence, hence why we call it an encoder. So the decoder is also an RNN, but a different RNN from the encoder.

Now, as you recall, every RNN unit has two sources of input data, the first being the actual input and the second being the previous hidden state. You can see that in this diagram. The inputs go along the bottom of the RNN unit while the previous hidden state comes through the left. Now, for a normal RNN the initial hidden state is usually just a vector of zeros, or maybe some trainable but fixed vector. In this case, for the decoder, you can see that the initial hidden state actually comes from the encoders output.

Thus, the job of the decoder is to decompress the compressed representation of the input. In the past, some people have called this hidden state vector a thought vector, since every sequence of words represents a thought and we're essentially converting that thought into a sequence of numerical components. So that's one way to think of the intermediate representation between the encoder and decoder. It's essentially a thought but represented in numerically.

Now the next question we have to answer is what goes into the bottom of the decoder RNN unit? Initially, this is just a special token called a start token, which obviously signifies the start of a sentence or a sequence. From here, the decoder RNN is essentially a text generation network. As you recall, this is done in an auto regressive manner similar to how we forecast a time series. So from the first start token and hidden state, we can then compute the first word in the translated sequence.

Once we have this first word, we then feed that back into the input of the decoder, and then for the next time step this will be used to generate the second word. From this point, we simply repeat this process until we've generated the translated sequence. We'll know when we're done, when our model produces the special end token. So that's how we know when our neural network should stop producing more outputs. So seq2seq turned out to work pretty well. But there was one small issue.

As you recall, every input sequence is converted using an encoder RNN into a single vector. Here's the problem. What if your input sentence is very long? No matter the length of the input, the final thought vector still has the same size. This is not really very intuitive. We know that longer sequences naturally contain more information. In addition, we know that RNNs are not good at remembering things in very long sequences. Furthermore, this is not really how we as humans translate sequences.

We don't memorize a whole passage and then try to generate the translation one word at a time from that memorized concept. Instead, we're more likely to read a few words at a time and produce the translation while focusing on localized areas of the input. Sometimes we might have to go a few words forward or a few words back, but the key is there. We might want to scan the input multiple times to figure out which parts to pay attention to and translate next.

So this is the main concept behind attention, which was originally developed for Encoder Decoder RNNs. Now the details get pretty complex and the diagrams get pretty messy. So for this lecture we'll be focusing mainly on intuition. In addition, note that if your goal is to learn about Transformers, the way attention works there is a bit different from how it works for seq2seq. So there isn't really a good reason at this point to go into detail. In any case, the main idea is that for each output token, we want to know which input tokens to pay attention to.

So why don't we discuss attention? It's helpful to draw a seq2seq diagram a bit differently in particular. Instead of drawing the encoder and decoder side by side, we draw the decoder on the top row while the encoder goes in the bottom row. One important detail is that for the encoder, instead of just using a regular RNN we use a bidirectional one. Basically, you can think of this like two stacked RNNs where one reads the input in the forward direction while the other reads the input in the backward direction.

In this way, every hidden state will now take into account both the context of the previous words and the context of the following words. For our diagram, this ends up looking pretty much the same. For the decoder. We still generate the output one word at a time. Using the password will be generated as an input to the next time step. So this is not a bi directional RNN. However, the important part is we now also take in a context vector as input as well to help us differentiate between the input and output.

We'll say that the input has T x time steps, while the output has T y time steps. We'll index the input time step using the letter t prime and we'll index the output time step using the letter t. Now, before we get into the details behind attention, let's discuss the intuition for what we would like to do, what we would like to do for each of the t y output time steps is to pick which one of the t x input time steps to pay attention to. So imagine that we had importance weights for each of the t inputs.

Our goal is to pick the H of T prime that has the corresponding maximum importance. In this simple scheme, we simply set the context vector to be this h of t prime and it goes into the decoder as a regular input. Now you might be wondering how can we have two inputs into the decoder RNN, since it must also accepts the previously generated word. The answer, to put it simply, is to just concatenate it with the context so that we get a single vector.

Now in deep learning, we know that instead of making hard decisions like picking the hidden state with maximum importance, we usually make soft decisions instead. In other words, instead of picking just a single hidden state, h of t prime, we'll take a weighted sum of all hidden states from h of one up to h of T x. The key is every hidden state from the encoder will influence every input to the decoder. Through weights we call attention weights, we'll call the attention weights

alpha of t and t prime, which is a number that represents how much the output at time step t should pay attention to the input at time step t prime. In this case, the context vector is simply the weighted sum of ages where the weights are the alphas. So this is the basic idea behind attention. We have weights from every input to every output, which tells us how much each output should pay attention to each input. So it's worth thinking about how these attention weights can be interpreted.

Firstly, let's consider how many such weights we should have. As you can see, alpha is indexed by t and t prime. t goes from one up to T y, while t prime goes from one up to T x. Therefore we have T y times T x attention weights, one for every input and output pair. Note that it's common to plot the attention weights to help us interpret why our model might produce a certain output. In the case of translation, this allows us to directly see how each translated word was found, or in other words, which input it was paying attention to when it produced each output.

Now the story of attention doesn't end here. Some years after the introduction of attention for seq2seq, there was a paper titled Attention is All You Need, which introduced the Transformers. Essentially, this says forget about the RNN and only use attention. Hence, attention is all you need. You can see that by doing this, we still get the most important feature of our translation model, which is that every output knows which input to pay attention to.

So we can still do complex tasks like machine translation, but without any of the disadvantages of RNNs. Specifically, there are two main drawbacks to using RNNs. Firstly, RNNs are slow because every output must be computed sequentially from the previous output. There is simply no way around this and you cannot parallelize this operation since the previous value must be known before you compute the next value. The second drawback to RNNs is the concept of the vanishing gradient.

Modern RNN units like the LSTM and the GRU do an okay job of preventing this problem, but only to a certain point. There is still a limit at which even LSTMs stopped performing well On the other hand, with attention, even with very long sequences, There is a direct connection between every input and every output. Therefore, we don't have to worry about vanishing gradients. Furthermore, since the computations don't have to be performed sequentially, there is an opportunity to speed things up by doing computations in parallel.

The only downside is that the attention mechanism is inherently quadratic. We say that it's O of n squared because if we have n inputs and outputs, we need to compute n squared attention weights. So what is a transformer? At a very high level. A transformer is just a stack of attention layers that repeatedly perform this n squared computation to figure out which parts of the input to pay attention to. And this is the intuition behind Transformers.

So in this lecture, we are going to look at our first task of this section, which is sentiment analysis. Now, at this point, I'm going to assume that you know what sentiment analysis is and why it's useful. So we're only going to review the main points. The main focus of this lecture, or the thing which will likely be new for you, is the code. In this section, we'll be using a very high level interface for Transformers, which is the Hugging Face pipeline.

Using this interface, you'll see that we can perform any of the tasks in the section with just one or two lines of code. So this should be useful for those of you who get intimidated when you see code or math or both. So what is sentiment analysis? Consider the following sentences. Wow, that was a really great film. And now consider this sentence. I can't believe I wasted 2 hours of my life on that film. So what is the difference between these two sentences?

Well, it's probably very clear that one of these sentences is a very positive reaction to a film, while the other is very negative. This is the concept of sentiment. Typically, we categorize sentiment as being either positive or negative or somewhere in between. Note that sentiment analysis is typically thought of as a classification task. Sometimes we simply call it sentiment classification, which is, in my opinion, a bit more clear now because sentiment can be thought of as being either positive or negative.

We often build binary classifiers to discriminate between these two classes. However, you will find instances where there might be three categories or even five categories. For example, in some datasets the categories are positive, negative and neutral in the other datasets. Perhaps we'd like to take a more fine-grained view. So we have positive, very positive, negative, very negative and neutral. So there are some different possibilities when we want to categorize sentiment.

Now, this is really dependent on the dataset you are using, or if you created the dataset yourself, then it's dependent on how you decided to label it. But because we will be using a prebuilt classifier, this means that we will only be able to use the classes that this pre-trained model has been built to use. In this case, it will be binary. Thus, the only possible classes for us will be either positive or negative. So the next topic of this lecture will be to try and understand why sentiment analysis is useful in the real world.

As always, I like to think of this in terms of money because most students understand money, and most students understand that either as a business or as an individual. Your goal is to make more money. So how can you use sentiment analysis to make more money for your business or yourself? In order to understand this. It's helpful to look at some examples. One example is reputation management, which involves monitoring of social media. Basically, the idea is you want to automatically look at tweets and posts on Reddit, Facebook and so forth that users are making about your company.

You can then pass these through your sentiment analyzer and create reports showing user sentiment statistics. Note that this can all be done programmatically so no one has to go in and read tweets themselves, which saves your company time and money. You can then take action based on what your customers have said about your company. Furthermore, note that this is not limited to your own company. You can do the same with your competitors as well. So if you're Apple, you might be interested in the sentiment behind the Google Pixel or the Samsung Galaxy.

Another example is to predict the sentiment of customers who are using your customer support. Perhaps the action taken by your customer support team is different based on the sentiment of the customer. Another popular example is to use sentiment analysis on news articles, tweets and other media concerning companies and industries whose stock prices might be affected by the news. So if your model predicts that the sentiment of an article is negative, you might want to consider selling your stock and vice versa if the sentiment of an article is positive.

By making more intelligent trades, you can profit by buying and selling stocks at the right times. Now you've probably seen that simple bag of words. Models can perform pretty well on certain data sets when it comes to the task of sentiment analysis. However, there are some tricky corner cases that require more advanced models. As you recall, a bag of words does not take order into account, and thus it is unlikely to be able to understand the relationships between words.

For example, consider the word interesting. We know that this has positive sentiment. For example, this movie was very interesting. But what if we say something like this movie was not very interesting? Now all of a sudden it has negative sentiment. The simple bag of words approach does not have an easy time figuring out that the word not negates the polarity of the word. Interesting. Or consider the sentence. This was a good movie. Clearly this has positive sentiment.

But now consider the sentence. I can't say that this was a good movie. And so in this case, it's not just a single word that negates another word, but a whole phrase that negates another phrase. This would be very difficult for a bag of words model, which would effectively just add up the sentiment of each individual word. So if we want to learn how the sentiment changes with the relationships between words, we need a more advanced approach. Luckily, sequential models like RNNs and CNNs can help.

In the past, I've discussed recursive neural networks, which are neural networks with a tree structure that also do very well with negation and even negating whole phrases since they just become nodes of the tree. In the next lecture, we'll see that transformers, which are sequence models with attention, also have this capability. Okay. So now that you know what sentiment analysis is and why it's useful, let's look at some code that can do sentiment analysis using Hugging Face.

The key function we need is a high level abstraction called a pipeline. Essentially, the pipeline is designed to do all the work for you, including tokenizing the text, converting the text into integers, and passing those integers through the model to get the output. So all the work you may have learned to do in the past, whether that was in TensorFlow, PyTorch or any other library, all of that is done inside this one interface. So to begin, we import pipeline from the Transformers library.

And as usual, if you do not have this library installed already, it's simply a matter of installing it using your favorite method. For example, using pip install transformers. Once you've imported the pipeline function, you can then call the pipeline function specifying the task you want to perform as an argument. In this case, we pass in the string sentiment dash analysis. Now you might be wondering where can I get a list of all possible tasks that can be done with the pipeline?

And the answer to that is through the Hugging Face documentation. Either copy the link you see here or go to my website, which is lazyprogrammer.me. Although do note that we will be going through most of these tasks in this section anyway. So when you call the pipeline function, this basically returns a model which you can then use to make predictions. However, as mentioned, this is a special kind of model because it does all the work for you.

This is unlike a PyTorch model, for example, where you'd have to convert your data into PyTorch tensors before actually using it. Or in the case of TensorFlow, TensorFlow tensors and so forth. In this case we can simply pass in raw text. So for example, I can pass in the string. This is such a great movie. This will return a dictionary with the corresponding prediction. In particular, this dictionary contains two items a label and a score. Note that the score corresponds with the label.

For example, if the label is positive and the score is 99%. You can interpret that as the posterior probability for the text. Being positive is 99%. But note that this will not return 1% if the label is negative. In the case where the prediction is negative, then under the key label it will be negative and the score might be something like 99%. Again, meaning that there is a 99% posterior probability that the input text was negative. So for example, if I pass in the string, I can't understand any of this.

The instructor kept telling me to meet the prerequisites. I might expect to get a dictionary containing a negative label with a score of 99%, not a positive label with 1%. So one additional detail I want to mention is that this pipeline is pretty flexible. So if you want to pass in more than one sample, you don't need to do it in a loop. Simply pass in a list containing all the text documents you want to classify. In return, you'll get a list containing dictionaries of the same form as above.

Okay. So that's pretty much it for sentiment analysis. As promised, it can all be done in just one line of code. To recap, the steps are number one to import the pipeline function. Number two, to load up a pre-trained model by calling the pipeline function and specifying the desired task. And number three, use the pre-trained model by passing in a string or list of strings.

However, note that the F1 score is not symmetric since it depends on what we consider the positive class. As you've learnt, the F1 score is the harmonic mean of the precision and recall, which are easiest to understand in terms of document retrieval. So it really depends on what we are trying to detect. Or in other words, retrieve. To demonstrate this, we can simply invert both the targets and predictions by subtracting them from one.

If the value was one, we get one minus one, which becomes zero. And if the value was zero, we get one -0. And it becomes one. So now we can see that the new F1 score is about 93% and thus it really depends on which label we assign to zero and which label we assign as one. The next step is to check the AUC, which is not sensitive to the meaning of the labels. Note that this uses probabilities since the ROC and hence the AUC is generated by setting different thresholds for detection.

Okay. So for the AUC, we get about 95%, which is decent. And just to demonstrate that the AUC doesn't depend on the label assignment, we're going to compute the AUC on the inverted labels as well. As you can see, we get the same answer as promised.

So one of the main goals of building a time series model is to predict the future. The reason why we would want to do this should be pretty obvious. If it's not obvious to you and none of this makes sense. Please let me know on the Q&A so that we can get that sorted.

Okay. So why would we want to predict the future? Here are some simple but powerful examples. Suppose that we could predict how much the price of a stock will go up or down. As a side note, just in case this is an obvious, the price of a stock over time forms a time series. Okay, so what is the advantage to us? If we could predict how much a stock's price will go up or down? Well, the answer is we could potentially make lots of money. If we predict that the price of a stock will go up, then we should buy that stock and sell it later for more money, hence making a profit.

If we predict that the price of a stock will go down, then we might want to sell it now to avoid incurring losses in the future. So that's one obvious example. Another example is with sales. If we can predict the demand for a product, we can ensure that we have sufficient inventory to meet this demand. This type of prediction is especially useful in the field of operations research. So if you work in business, this should be another obvious example. Here's another obvious example that I think everyone should understand, and that is predicting counts related to COVID.

For example, the count of new cases per day or the count of new hospital admissions per day. And again, this is obviously useful so that our health care providers can ensure that we have sufficient resources to handle your health care needs. Okay. So how do we build models to predict the future? The most Common Time series model is auto regressive. Now, in this context, regressive really is a double entendre. We're doing regression because we're predicting a number, but it's also regressive in the sense that we're looking into the past.

Auto as usual, means self. So in other words, our auto regressive model predicts the next value in a time series using previous values in the same time series. For example, I can predict X of t plus one using x of t, x of t minus one, x of t minus two and so forth. The classic form of this model is a linear model known as ARIMA. In this case, the next value in a time series is a linear function of the previous values and also the previous errors. Now, please don't worry if you find math intimidating, as it's not necessary to understand for this lecture, this is just for intuition only.

The point is that this kind of structure can be applied to other models as well, including the random forest. recurrent neural networks like LSTMs and Transformers. So really any kind of model can use past values to predict future values. And this is one of the main tasks of interest when studying time series analysis. Okay. So that makes sense for a time series. But what about for language? Well, language is simply a time series of categorical objects.

So it's not typically what we would think of as a time series, but it is a sequence and time series are sequences as well. Thus, we might expect that the same kinds of approaches might work well. In fact, this is the case. In particular, when we do this for language, we call it a language model. Our goal is to find a conditional distribution for the next word. Given the past words in a sequence, mathematically, we could write this says P of X of t plus one given x of t, x of t minus one, x of t minus two and so forth.

As you recall, such language modeling has a history going back over 100 years. Initially, Andrei Markov used Markov chains to describe language. In this framework, we use the Markov assumption, which is that each word or token depends only on the immediate past. Mathematically, this means that X of T plus one depends only on x of t. In turn, x of t depends only on x of t minus one. This is mathematically convenient, but it's also such a strong assumption that the resulting models are not very proficient at more advanced tasks like generating coherent text.

For example, take the word great, as in that this was a great movie. So the word movie is a plausible continuation of a sentence containing the word great, but now take the sentence. Today in school, we learned about Alexander the Great. Suppose that we had just generated the sentence and we were to use our Markov model to generate the next word. But our Markov model ignores all the past words and only considers the final word great. Because of this, the word movie is still a plausible continuation of this sentence.

So our sentence would end up looking like. Today in school, we learned about Alexander the great movie, which obviously makes no sense. However, if we were to account for more of the past, then we might not make such a mistake. As you recall, recurrent neural networks in convolutional neural networks are two ways to include more context in a scalable manner. The reason why Markov models are not scalable is due to the curse of dimensionality. If we want to build a model for n-grams and we have a vocabulary size V, then the size of our distribution would be V, times v, times v and so forth and times.

In other words, v to the power n. Clearly, this is exponential, which, as you recall, is intractable. But by using neural networks, we can find more efficient ways to model such distributions. So in the past we've used these auto regressive language models to generate poetry. Usually this works okay, since poetry tends to be more accepting of strange or unexpected phrases. On the other hand, it's much more difficult to generate coherent text. In fact, even today, we cannot generate fully coherent text.

We're getting much closer than we used to be. But ultimately, our language models still make silly mistakes. That being said, that's not to say we shouldn't try. In fact, this still remains to this day one of the most important tasks in NLP. For example, the largest and most popular model today called GPT-3 is pre-trained using this method. So although it doesn't fully quote unquote work, you shouldn't take this as a sign that it's not something worth pursuing.

In fact, even simpler language models have been quite successful in a variety of applications, including text completion. So imagine you were writing an email and your email service predicts the next word as you type. I'm sure you've all seen this before. This is an example of a language model predicting the next word. Another example is with creative writing. If you're a writer, perhaps you sometimes have some trouble thinking of what to say. A language model can assist without any effort.

Thus, this is a way to augment your human capabilities. Impressively, GPT-3 goes even further. Some examples include a GitHub copilot which can automatically generate working code. So if you're a software engineer, this is again an opportunity to augment your capabilities with the machine. It's been shown that we can even build full website designs, compose music and even answer medical queries. Many of these ideas have turned into real money making businesses.

So if you think a language model is just a language model, now is the time to expand your horizons and to really see the full array of possibilities. Transformers have been the key technology to make these language models work thanks to their ability to capture long range dependencies. As you recall, this is due to the attention mechanism where each output token does a full scan of all the input tokens to figure out which to attend to. Now there is one downside to this, which I think is quite amusing.

When the more advanced GPT models were invented, OpenAI was hesitant to share their pre-trained models. There were probably several reasons for that, but there is one interesting consequence. So for at least some portion of the training data, the machines are just copying older versions of themselves. Whether or not that's actually an issue remains to be seen. Okay.
