7. Masked Language Modeling (Article Spinner)
=============================================

In this lecture, we'll be looking at another task very closely related to the previous one on generating text. To recap, the previous lecture looked at an auto regressive language model, also known as a causal language model. This is because our model learns a distribution of tokens conditioned on the past. So we want to predict X of T plus one given X of T, X of T minus one and so forth. Importantly, this only goes in one direction, which is forward.

In this lecture, we'll consider a slightly different model, one that goes in both directions. In particular, suppose I build a distribution to predict X of T given the past and also given the future. So, for example, I conditioned on X of T minus one, x of t minus two and so forth, but also x of t plus one x of T plus two and so forth. This is called a masked language model for reasons which will become obvious shortly. So at this point it should be mentioned how these methods of pre training language models are related to actual specific models.

We use in particular at the auto regressive language model is the method used by GPT. That's why, for example, the default chosen in the Hugging Face pipeline was GPT-2. On the other hand, the masked language model is the method used by BERT. BERT stands for bidirectional encoder representations from Transformers. The key word here is bidirectional, meaning that the model learns how to predict words from both the past and the future. Now, this should be very interesting to you.

If you've studied my courses in the past, in particular, we've been doing this kind of training and building these kinds of language models since 2016, which was two years before GPT and three years before BERT. So although these tasks seem a bit simplistic and perhaps if you were just beginning machine learning total nonsense, they are in fact very powerful. As I always mentioned, it seems that those who are just beginning their machine learning journey typically have a pretty tough time understanding why unsupervised learning is so useful and powerful.

Mostly it's because they lack patience and are looking for a quick fix. Supervised learning gives you that quick fix because you immediately know what the task is and hence what the application is. But to move beyond the beginner level, you have to be more patient and more creative and more open minded about the possibilities, even if what you're learning just seems like a bunch of useless math. So if you were one of those students stuck at this beginner level, take this as a lesson about how to approach machine learning, even if it requires some patience on your part.

Perhaps if you had taken these ideas to heart in 2016, it could have been you that invented BERT or GPT. In other words, by opening your mind to these possibilities, it empowers you to create new and useful things that no one else has done before. Okay. So let's talk about a very useful application of the masked language model, which is article spinning. This is what I've called it in the past. Since it's closely related to a very widely used tool by blackhat marketers and SEOs in particular.

There are actual real products like the one shown here that do exactly what we've been describing. Essentially, you take random words in a document and replace them with a machine generated word such that the sentence still makes sense and hopefully retains its meaning. Now, just to give you a bit of background, why is this useful? So this part is not about machine learning, but it helps to understand the big picture. Now, the best way to understand this is if you've had personal experience building a website or a blog or an online business.

As you know, it's pretty difficult to get visitors to your site. That's why online advertising is such a big industry. However, there is a free method to get visitors, which is through search engines such as Google and DuckDuckGo. The techniques which people apply to improve their search engine rankings are collectively known as SEO. Okay, so SEO is a set of techniques that people use to improve their search engine rankings. But what does this have to do with article spinning?

Well, one of these techniques is to simply create lots of content with keywords that might match a user's query. For example, if you're a target audience is interested in running shoes, then your goal might be to write lots of articles about running shoes and other aspects of running. Now, as anyone who's had experience writing should know, writing is not easy and takes quite a bit of time and effort. Thus, if you're the type of person who perhaps lacks ethics, you might decide to simply copy or steal articles written by others.

But doing that by itself is not enough. We know that Google is pretty smart, so if they notice that you've simply copied someone else, your rankings will end up being penalized, not improved. So what black hat marketers end up doing is they still want to steal articles from others, but they try to change random words such that the result still makes sense but is no longer a good match to the articles they copied from. And that's precisely how article spinning works.

So to be a bit more detailed, suppose we take the sentence. The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. What do we want to do in article spinning is to take one of these words such as Fox and replace it with something else. Now, obviously the goal is to reduce human effort. So although you could come up with a fine substitution yourself, you'd rather be helped by a computer. And that's where our language model enters the picture. Clearly, what I want to do is ask my model to generate a word, given that the previous words were the quick brown and the future words are jumps over the lazy dog.

And this is exactly what models like BERT do. Now I realize that some people may be skeptical that this is a real world application. However, it's quite easy to simply do a search and find screenshots of real computer programs that do exactly what I've described. So the screenshots I've been showing you in this lecture are real programs. People actually pay money to buy and use these programs. Now, there's one pretty recent example where a popular YouTuber tried to use this method but failed.

I won't name any names, but if you watch machine learning videos on YouTube, you may have come across this story. The summary is this YouTuber, although he was very popular in the area of machine learning, all of his videos used code that was stolen from GitHub without any attribution. That wouldn't have been a problem if he gave credit to the people he stole from. But he always named his videos like Watch Me Build Such and such, implying that he was the one who wrote the code, which was obviously false.

After a few more controversies which you can read up on if you like, he ended up pretending to write a paper on neural qubits, as was his style. He simply copied everything from the text to the images to the formulas from other papers. So in order to disguise the text, he used the tool for article spinning. Except that it wasn't very good. In particular, it changed the complex Hilbert space into complicated Hilbert space. Obviously this doesn't make sense, although complicated is a synonym for complex in one context.

Complex in this context refers to complex numbers. So it's not a synonym for complicated in this context. Another example is changing quantum gates to quantum doors again in the right context. Gate can be a synonym to door. But clearly this is not that context. So it may be said that if this YouTuber had used a better language model that can take context into account, such as the models in this course, he wouldn't have made such embarrassing mistakes. At the same time, it is still likely he would have been caught since people still read the other parts of the text, recognize the images and so forth.

But the point of this is this concept of article spinning really is used in the real world, typically for nefarious purposes. Okay. So now that you understand the masked language model and you're convinced of its real world application, let's discuss how we do this with Transformers. Again, it's very simple with just one or two lines of code. We begin by calling the pipeline function, passing in the string fill-mask as before. Once we've done this, we can immediately use the pipeline simply by passing in some text.

The format of this text should be such that it includes a special mask token denoting the token you want to fill. Note that the mask token is surrounded by angled brackets. As output, you will get multiple suggestions stored in a list. Each item is a dictionary with the completed sequence, the replaced token corresponding score and even the token ID of the replacement. So as before, it's pretty easy to use. The next thing I want to do in this lecture is give you some insight on why we sometimes call this an auto encoding language model.

The best way to understand this is if you've had experience with unsupervised, deep learning. Although if you don't, then intuition should be enough. Okay, so the intuition comes from auto encoders. Auto encoders are a type of neural network that taken some input and simply tried to produce the same thing as output. That is, it tries to simply replicate the input. Now at first that might seem pretty useless, but in fact there have been quite a few applications which I've discussed in the past, such as recommender systems and pre-training, like what we do in this course.

So if you would like to know more, simply ask me on the Q&A. One variation of the basic auto encoder is the denoising autoencoder. As an example, suppose we working with images. This is where we pass in a corrupted version of the image and our neural network tries to produce the uncorrupted image. That is, it tries to restore the image and remove the noise. This noise can be of any kind, such as Gaussian noise or simply setting a pixel value to zero.

But this should remind you of what this lecture is about. Effectively, what we're doing when we mask a word in a sentence is we are adding noise. Our goal is to build a neural network that will find the missing word and hence restore the sentence to its original state. So this is another way to think of the masked language model. It's simply a noisy auto encoder. But for text, it's also another way to think of how this relates to the causal language model we previously studied.

As you recall, that was auto regressive. So that was auto regressive and this is auto encoding.

9. Named Entity Recognition (NER)
=================================

So in this lecture we are going to look at how to do named entity recognition, also known as NER with transformers. So what is NER? Well, basically the idea is you have a document and you want to identify all the people, places and companies in that document. This data might be used as a preprocessing step in a larger piece of code. So, for example, if I see Apple, I would mark that as a company. If I see Steve Jobs. I would mark that as a person. And if I see California, I would mark that as a place.

In any case, suppose you have some use for it. How does this actually work? Well, it turns out that NER is essentially exactly the same as parts of speech tagging, which you already know how to do. As you recall, that was a many-to-many task where we assign each word to a tag and we do the same thing with NER. So every place has the tag LOC. Every person has the tag PER. And every company has the tag ORG. Now, one challenging aspect of NER is that some entities span multiple tokens.

So, for example, consider Steve Jobs. This has two tokens, but both of them are part of a name. If you consider jobs by itself, that would be a regular word, meaning occupations. Another challenging aspect of NER is that this data is highly imbalanced. You can see that most words just get the tag O, which means that it's not a place, person or company. Consider this sentence from our dataset. Sheep have been long known to contract scrapie, a brain wasting disease similar to BSE, which is believed to have been transferred to cattle through feed containing animal waste.

This is a very long sentence and it has no named entities. All the labels are just. Oh. Let's briefly discuss the format of NER tags, since you're probably wondering what all these A's and B's signify. This is called IOB format, since obviously we use the letters IO and B in our tags. The idea is that each entity potentially appears as a chunk. A chunk is a sequence of multiple tokens. An example of that is Steve Jobs. In this case, we would use B-PER to represent the fact that Steve is a person tag and it's the beginning of a chunk.

For jobs, we would use I-PER to represent the fact that Jobs is also a person tag and it's inside a chunk. So B stands for beginning. I stands for inside, and O stands for outside meaning outside of any chunk. Luckily, these details don't really concern us, since ultimately our dataset looks exactly like POS tagging from the previous lecture. We have a sequence of words and each word is map to a tag. Okay. So now that we know what NER is, let's discuss how we can accomplish this with Transformers.

As usual, we create a pipeline object and we specify the task as NER. Note that we also pass in an argument for aggregation strategy, which in my opinion, is best set to simple. As you recall, some entities can span multiple tokens. Using this method ensures that instead of returning the same entity for each token separately, it will put them all together. Unfortunately, the Hugging Face documentation is not that great. So if you want to look this up, it's probably better to look at the source code on GitHub.

In any case, when you do this, the output will look as follows. As you can see, this returns a list and the list contains a dictionary for each entity it found for each entity. Notice that the word key specifies the actual string corresponding to the entity. Sometimes this will contain multiple actual words. Note that the key entity group specifies the type of entity. Also note that each tag comes with a score so you can see how confident the model is in its prediction.

And finally, note that the result includes the start and end position of the entity with respect to the input. This will be useful in the coming notebook and as usual, note that if you pass in multiple strings in a list, the above result would be a nested list inside another list.

11. Text Summarization
======================

So in this lecture we will do a brief introduction to text summarization and how to accomplish this task with Transformers. Now, as always, we're going to answer the question Why is this practical? And I hope that this is another one of those tasks where the answer is very obvious. So why should we summarize text? Well, notice how we already do this all the time. For example, when you read a scientific paper, it always comes with an abstract, and abstract is a summary of that paper.

So you don't need to read the whole paper if you just want to understand the basic idea. You can get away with just reading the abstract and then read the paper in full. If you want more details. Professional documents also have a similar concept, although instead of calling it an abstract, it's sometimes called an executive summary. So clearly this is a useful concept in the real world. Since we do this already, even without the help of computers and obviously we can extend this concept to our own lives.

I know that for myself, my reading list is longer than the time I have to spend reading. So it would be very useful if I could reach the same outcome or reach the same conclusions by reading only the most important parts of each document that I have to read. Another reason we might want to build text summarization systems is for pushing forward the field of artificial intelligence. Now, you might think this seems kind of trivial. build AI algorithms to develop the field of AI.

But that's not what I mean. What I mean is that text summarization is kind of a critical aspect of learning. For instance, think of everyday conversations that you have with other people. Sometimes when people explain things to you, you will paraphrase or summarize what they've told you in response. Then the other person might say, Yes, you're correct or No, you're not correct. So summarization is a way for learning systems, whether that's yourself or a machine, to demonstrate their understanding of a concept.

If you think about it further, you'll realize that whenever you summarize an article or write an abstract, it really requires you to have a good understanding of what you are trying to summarize. In other words, it seems to require a true intelligence. Here's another application for building summaries out of text. In fact, you see this so often, you probably don't even realize that it happens. As you recall, our sort of window to the Internet is search engines such as Google, Bing, DuckDuckGo and so forth.

Now, without looking. Think about what you see after you type in a query. And while the answer is you see a bunch of search results and how are these search results presented? Well, typically, you have a Web page title, a link to the Web page and a corresponding summary, which is these days usually customized to your query. So this is another very important application. So now that you know why text summarization is useful, let's discuss one detail before we outline this section.

This detail is that we can actually categorize different methods of summarizing text. These categories are extractive and abstractive. So what do these mean? Well, extractive summaries are summaries that are built out of the document itself. So the summary is a subset of the document that was given. In other words, the summary was extracted from the document. abstractive summaries, on the other hand, are novel sequences of text that are not just partial copies of the given document.

As you can imagine, extractive summaries are much easier to generate compared with abstractive summaries. With extractive summaries, we only need to identify the most relevant parts of the input. But with abstractive summaries, we need to develop a deeper understanding of the input, and we also need to convert that understanding into coherent text. Now using more basic techniques, extractive summaries are usually the only choice in order to build abstractive summaries.

We need to have models that not only understand text but can also generate new text. Luckily, you seq2seq models and transformers do exactly this. Okay. So now that you understand what text summarization is all about, let's look at how we do it in Python. As usual, we begin by creating a pipeline object, passing in the string summarization as the one and only argument. Once we have our pipeline objects, we can then pass in any text we wish to summarize.

The output will be a list containing one or more dictionaries, each of which contains a key called summary text. So very simple. As per usual.

13. Neural Machine Translation
==============================

So in this lecture we will be discussing the next task in this section, which is on machine translation. As with many of our other tasks, I'm sure this needs no introduction, and most, if not all students will already be aware of the applications. But just in case, let's briefly go over why this is a useful task. Firstly, to be clear, machine translation is a task where we convert phrases from one language into another language, for example, from English to French or Spanish to Portuguese.

Now, why is this useful? Well, if you only know one language, then you can only communicate with other people who know that language. On the Internet, you can only understand Internet content in your language. If you're reading a book, you can only understand books in your language. But with machine translation, a lot more content suddenly becomes available. Now you can watch TV shows from a different country by translating the subtitles. You can meet new people in foreign partnerships with businesses from overseas.

So clearly, machine translation opens up many opportunities that you otherwise wouldn't have had. One important fact to note is that like text summarization, machine translation is also an example of a sequence to sequence task, and thus it uses sequence to sequence architectures such as those that can be implemented with RNNs and transformers. In fact, if you apply my rule, all data is the same. You will understand that these two tasks are not really so different.

Consider what a dataset for these tasks might look like. In both cases, you essentially have two columns of text. The first column of texts contains the input sequence, and the second column of text contains the target sequence. As usual, the type of data makes no difference to a neural network because the neural network just sees numbers. All of these words are tokens will simply be converted into integer IDs. Let's now discuss how to perform machine translation in Python.

Again, this turns out to be very simple. Thanks to the pipeline API, we begin by creating a pipeline object passing in the string translation. Now, unlike the other task we've learned about, this is not all we need to do in particular. This is because this is not enough information for us to know which language we want to translate from, in which language we want to translate to. So for this task, you'll also need to specify a model which has been built specifically for the language pair you want.

As usual, you can use the pipeline by passing in a string or list of strings, and this will translate your input into the target language. The output will be a list of one or more dictionaries, and each of these dictionaries will contain one key called the translation text. Now you might be wondering, where do you even find these models? The answer is to use the Hugging Face model hub. Basically, you go to Hugging Face, dot, CEO slash models and you'll see what is basically a search engine for different model types.

On the left side, you can filter models by task and language. So for the task you would want to select translation. And for language you would want to select your source in target languages. Once you find a model that works for you, you simply click on it, copy its name, and pass this in as the model argument into the pipeline builder. So as one final topic in this lecture, I want to briefly discuss how we evaluate translation models. This is actually quite complicated since many valid translations can exist for a given input.

So you can't simply compare each output token to each target token. Furthermore, the output and target may not even be the same length. As an example, consider hi and hello. Both of these would be valid translations. But if you consider hi to be correct, then for hello. You would mark that as incorrect even though it's still valid. Consider also that there can be many ways to say the same thing. For example, it's Yoda. It is Yoda. And Yoda. It is. So clearly we should have some way of accounting for these differences and to still return a high score, even when the output doesn't exactly match the target.

Now, I don't want to get into the details of this point, since this is just the beginners corner. But the most popular metric for this task is the BLEU score. Out of many possible metrics. The BLEU score is the metric that has been said to be the most correlated with human judgment. And that's what matters, since the purpose of text is for humans to read it. But note that it's not perfect and still has a few disadvantages. Nonetheless, it is what is commonly used.

Basically, what you need to know is three facts. Number one, the BLEU score compares your prediction with multiple reference texts. So it allows for multiple possible answers. Number two, the BLEU score returns a number between zero and one, where zero is the worst and one is the best. So it's intuitively on the same scale as other metrics we use. And number three, the BLEU score looks at the precision of n-grams for multiple values of n. So for example, unigrams, bigrams, trigrams and so forth.

So for each of the n-grams we find in the prediction, we're asking, can we also find this n-gram in one of the targets? And if the answer is yes, that's good.

In a general sense, our ultimate goal is to really have a system that can answer any question. For example, if this were possible, you can imagine that in a I could replace a doctor or a teacher in some instances. In this ideal scenario, the system would have some database of knowledge, or perhaps that knowledge could be stored in neural network weights.

When the user asks the question, the neural network would simply understand the question related to its stored knowledge and provide the appropriate answer, whatever that may be. Now we're going to end up making a number of simplifications to this ideal scenario in practice. Note that some systems such as Apple's Siri already do this to some extent. For example, you can ask Siri for the weather and it will tell you the weather. You can even ask Siri for nutritional facts like how much caffeine is in coffee.

But this is not general enough since it eventually ends up as a database lookup. In practice, what we would like to do is more akin to natural language understanding. So one of the standard question answering benchmark data sets in this modern NLP era is SQuAD. SQuAD stands for Stanford question answering data set. As you recall, Stanford is a pretty famous NLP department, which hosts famous NLP researchers such as Christopher Manning and Dan Jurafsky They've also developed some widely used tools, such as GloVe and recursive neural networks, which I've discussed in the past.

SQuAD is an example of an extractive question answering dataset, and it aims to test a reading comprehension. So what do we mean by extractive? As mentioned in practice, we place some restrictions on the ideal scenario of having a robot answer any question we ask. Extractive means that your input actually contains both the question and the answer, and all the model has to do is choose the right part of the input. In other words, extract the answer. So, for example, the input might be a whole Wikipedia page about Albert Einstein.

You might then ask, When was Albert Einstein born? And the model would have to choose the right part of the Wikipedia page, which contains the answer to when Albert Einstein was born. What this means is that there are some scenarios where it's not possible to have an answer. For example, if my input contains information about Albert Einstein and I ask What goes on a peanut butter sandwich, this would not be an answerable question. So in this case, the model isn't learning how to draw from a database of knowledge, but rather it's learning how to read and comprehend and then to answer questions about what it has just read.

In other words, there's a lack of memory. Now, don't let this fool you, since, as you'll see, the results are quite impressive. So one thing that's interesting about this task is that it's very different from the previous task we've discussed. In particular, the previous tasks have all been simple cases, like many-to-one of many-to-many, sequence to sequence, language modeling and variations thereof. Now this technically could be structured as a kind of sequence to sequence task, but instead the solution is simpler.

As mentioned, the answer is always contained in the input. Furthermore, the answer is always a contiguous sequence and thus a simpler way to represent. The answer is by providing just the start and end indices from the input. For example, if my input string was Albert Einstein was born on March 14, 1879. When was Albert Einstein born? The answer to that would simply be start equals 28 and end equals 42. This is because if I were to index this string starting at 28 and ending at 42, I would get back March 14, 1879.

And if you have experience with neural networks, it would be helpful to consider how you might build a neural network to output such information. So that's one thing that might be different from what you're used to. Something else that might be different is the input. Typically, the input to our neural network is usually just one piece of information. For example, one document we want to translate or one document we want to classify. As you recall, large language models are pre-trained using very simple inputs and outputs, like trying to predict the next word in a sentence.

So during this pre-training stage, the neural network never sees anything terribly complex, such as a context in question pairing. And yet somehow during the fine tuning process, the model learns how to interpret what it means when you concatenate a context and question together, in particular as input to our model, we create a string with the following format. We start with a special class token followed by the question, followed by a special separator, then the context, and then another separator.

The context is the part which contains the answer. And so not only does the model need to understand the question and how to find the answer in the context, it also has to learn what it means to have an input formatted this way, it learns that when you pass into sequences like this, its job is to answer the question based on the information in the context. So the interesting part about this is even though we have two things to pass into the input, we are still managing to represent it as a single piece of text.

It's interesting because of the fact that the neural network was not originally designed to understand what this means. And the reason this is powerful is because it will let us do even crazier things, such as few shot learning. Okay. So now that you understand what question answering is and why it's useful, let's look at some code. As usual, it's extremely simple. Using the pipeline from Hugging Face to create a question. Answering pipeline we call the pipeline function passing in the string question answering.

From there, we simply use the pipeline as a function which takes in two arguments context in question. And both of these are strings. As mentioned, the context should be text containing the answer to the question. For example, the context might be Today I made a peanut butter sandwich, and the question might be, what did I put in my sandwich? And the answer to this question is provided as a dictionary as usual. In the key answer we have the actual answer represented as text.

We also have start and end keys, which are indices into the context where the answer can be found. Finally, we have a score representing how confident our model is in its answer. So perhaps you might want to throw away or double check any answers with the low score.

17. Zero-Shot Classification
============================

So in this lecture, we will be discussing another new and interesting application called zero-shot Classification. This is one of those fancy new terms of the deep learning era. So let's consider what this means. As you know, regular classification is where you train a model based on a labeled dataset. The labels are discrete categories, and each input sample is assigned at one of these labels. For example, we might train an image classifier to differentiate between people with masks and people without masks.

After training such a model, we can then use it to make a new predictions on new images. The caveat is that this model can do nothing else. It can only be used to predict whether or not a person is wearing a mask. But suppose that you built a model so powerful that it could recognize anything. For instance, suppose I had some arbitrary model and I asked it. Is this a picture of a dog or a cat? And suppose this model could tell me this is a picture of a cat.

Now suppose I could use that same model and ask it. Is this a picture of a plane or a boat? You might think there's no way a model could be that flexible, when in fact this is precisely what zero shot classification is. Now since this is a course about NLP, let's consider some text based examples. Although do note that this type of paradigm works with images too. Suppose my input document is a wikipedia page on Albert Einstein and I want to know is Albert Einstein a scientist or a painter?

So I can ask my NLP model to classify this document given the classes scientist and painter. Obviously the correct answer is scientist and in fact the NLP model would likely give us this answer. Now, with this same model, I can input a completely different document and I can specify a completely different set of classes. For example, I can pass in a document about the mitochondria and I can ask, Which subject does this belong to? Biology, math, or psychology?

And impressively, the model will likely output biology. And again, much like the question answering task, if you have some experience with building neural networks, it would be useful to think about how would you build a model to do this? Keep in mind that this does not work like a typical softmax neural network. As you recall, for a typical classification task, we would have one final dense layer with a number of outputs equal to the number of possible classes.

Each output corresponds to a different class, but of course, zero-shot classification does not work this way. And this is clear because it can handle any set of classes you give it. So think about how you might accomplish this. So I hope it's pretty clear why zero shot classification is useful and relevant. We don't have different brains for different tasks. We have one brain which can do all tasks. So if I give you a sentence and I ask you to categorize that sentence, you would be able to do so without needing to see many examples of each category.

Now, that might not be the case if it's something completely novel to you. But for everyday sentences, you should do pretty well. So that's why zero-shot classification is so intriguing. So now that you understand what zero-shot classification is and why it's relevant, let's discuss how to do it in code. As usual, we begin by calling the pipeline function and this time we use the string zero-shot classification. Note that this is quite slow, so you'll want to use the GPU.

The next step is to use the model by passing in the text you want to classify and the labels you want to classify the text into. The labels must be passed in using the argument candidate labels while the text is simply the first argument. As usual, you may also pass on a list of strings, in which case each of them will be classified according to the provided labels. The output of the model will be a dictionary which requires a bit of explanation. So there will be three keys in this dictionary.

The first key is sequence, which is simply the text you passed in. The second key is labels, which is an ordered list of the labels you passed in. Note that they are ordered in descending order according to their score. As usual, you can interpret the score as a posterior probability. The third key is scores, which contains the corresponding scores, and as per the labels, they will be in descending order. Thus, you have to be careful that you treat the scores correctly.

In particular, you can't assume that the scores in each position will correspond to the same labels for each sample, which is what we normally do in deep learning. For example, in the first output, the first score corresponds to positive, while the second score corresponds to negative. This is because the model prediction is positive and this score is the highest. However, in the second output, the first score corresponds to negative, while the second score corresponds to positive.

This is because the model prediction is negative and this score is the highest.

19. Beginner's Corner Section Summary
=====================================

So now that you've seen plenty of examples of how transformers can be used, it's now time to close off this section. This lecture will recap each of the applications we looked at, and we'll also review the sort of big picture of why they are relevant for this course. So we began by looking at sentiment analysis, which is a simple to understand task, but also non-trivial in some ways. As you recall, simple models that use bag of words can obtain pretty good performance, but they fail to account for relationships between words due to the fact that order is not taken into account.

For example, seeing this show was interesting versus this show was not interesting has opposite meanings. We know that not interesting has negative sentiment, but a bag of words model might only know that interesting is positive and the word not does not negate its polarity. With transformers, this negation can be taken into account. The next task we looked at was how to convert documents into vectors or embeddings. This is similar to what we do with images where we pre-trained a neural network and then we take the embedding vector from the second last layer.

These turn out to map the inputs into a useful embedding space where we can do things like face recognition for documents. This is useful for things like similarity search. We then looked at a many-to-many task, namely named entity recognition. This is a classic task in NLP which can be handled by sequential models like RNNs as well. The next task we looked at was how to generate text by training a language model. This is a completely natural thing for a transformer to do because this task is actually what we use to train our transformers.

We learned that the GPT family is trained as an auto regressive or causal language model, meaning that each word is predicted from the past. We observed firsthand the impressive capability for these models to generate very long and coherent documents. We then looked at a different kind of language model, which was the auto encoding or masked language model. This is essentially the article spinner, which I've described in the past, and I showed you how many people have been making use of such technologies.

Importantly, these are people who are willing to pay money for such tools. We then turned our attention back to supervised training, where we looked at some sequence to sequence tasks. Now, although parts of speech tagging and named entity recognition are also sequence to sequence tasks, there is a big difference between those and these other examples, namely text summarization and language translation, in particular with summarization and translation.

The output does not have the same length as the input. This is unlike tasks like named entity recognition where we have a 1 to 1 mapping from each input token to each prediction. The next task we looked at was a completely new task known as question answering. This is an interesting task because unlike the others, it requires more understanding of the relationships between the words and the input. Firstly, we noted that the input actually consists of two items.

The question in the context, which is a document that contains the answer to the question. So this task not only requires the model to understand the relationships between the words and the document, it must also know that the question is something to be answered and that the answer lies somewhere in the context. Personally, I find these results very impressive. The next task we looked at was another new task known as zero-shot classification. This is another very interesting task because it is unlike typical machine learning where we must train a model on a labeled dataset if we want it to be able to classify inputs from a certain set of classes.

In other words, it used to be the case that we had to train a model specifically for a task if we wanted to do that task. But this is different using the zero shot method. We can use the model to classify inputs from among any set of classes, which means that we are asking the model to do something it has never done before. To me, this is very impressive as well. And thus these two final tasks are a good way to end the session by demonstrating the true potential of transformers.

### 

1. Fine-Tuning Section Introduction
===================================

So in this lecture we will be introducing the next section of the course, which is all about how to fine tune a Transformer model. So let's think about why this is different from the previous section of the course. The previous section of the course was easy because it only involved using models that someone else has trained. We were just using those models in inference mode, also known as predictor mode. The code was very simple because the library we're using provides a nice universal interface for different types of models.

So we have the same interface for different kinds of tasks. In this section, we won't just be making predictions, but training our own models as well. This will make things much more complex than the previous section in terms of the code we have to write. We'll need to dig deeper into what goes on inside the pipeline interface. So let's do a quick outline of this section so you understand what's coming next. The first thing we'll do is review how text processing works.

As you know, machine learning models work with numbers, not text. Therefore, we'll need a strong grasp of how we convert text into numbers so that our machine learning models can work. This involves a process called tokenization, which splits a single text document into a list of tokens. It also involves mapping tokens into integers as well as padding so that our model inputs all have the same length. This brings us to the next point, which is what exactly goes on when you use a pipeline.

We know that we can just pass in text and magically get an answer. But I just told you how machine learning models don't work with text directly. Well, the key is that the pipelines are actually composed of two parts the tokenizer and the model itself. In the next few lectures, we'll look at how we can use tokenized and models directly with Hugging Face instead of using a pipeline. This will be necessary in order to proceed to the next step, which is how to actually train a transformer.

This is where one of the key innovations in NLP has been made in the past few years. You may have heard that transformer models have become so large that they take millions of dollars and weeks of time to train. This is true. Luckily, we'll never have to train a transformer from scratch in this course, which most of us would not have the resources to do. Instead, NLP has borrowed a technique from computer vision, which is to use the pre-trained weights of a large and powerful model as the backbone of a new neural network built for a new task.

NLP uses a technique known as fine tuning, which we'll review in this section. After reviewing how fine tuning works. Well, then look at how to accomplish this in Hugging Face. In fact, Hugging Face makes this very easy as well. You don't even really have to know how to write your own gradient descent loop since the library will do all that work for us. Although do note that you might want to have some idea of how it works since the hyper parameters are still free for you to choose.

If you don't want to use the defaults. Now this first example will be a very simple example where we do sentiment analysis on an important benchmark dataset included with the Hugging Face suite of packages. But it's so simple that you might be left wondering, How do I apply this on my own dataset? That's the next thing we'll cover. This will probably be one of the most practical things we do in this course. Since text classification is a very common NLP task in in the real world, you'll most likely have your own dataset, which is not part of the Hugging Face Datasets package.

To end this section. We'll do one more example, which will teach you a bit more about how Transformers work. In this example, we'll look at a classifier that can process not one, but two input sentences at the same time. You'll see how transformers are very flexible in this regard. This will be the first step in learning how Transformers can handle all kinds of different input formats. Thanks for listening and I'll see you in the next lecture.

2. Text Preprocessing and Tokenization Review
=============================================

So in this lecture we are going to review how to preprocess text for use in a neural network. We'll begin by enumerating all the steps that need to be done, and then we'll dig a little deeper into each step and the reasons why they are needed. So the steps are as follows Given a document which is represented as a string of text, we always begin by tokenizing the text. Note that there isn't just one way to do this. This can be as simple as calling string that split, splitting the documents into words, splitting the documents into characters, or as we'll see in this course, splitting the documents into sub words.

The key point is our first task is to split up our text into a sequence of tokens. Once we have our text corpus represented as sequences of tokens, we then need to come up with an integer representation that is, instead of a sentence saying, I like cats. Well, turn this into a sequence of token IDs like four, 26, 50 and one or three. One obvious thing we need to figure out is which tokens map to which integers and how to keep track of that information.

Once our data is in the form of sequences of integers, it is theoretically possible to process them using a neural network. However, we often want to process more than one sequence at once. As you recall, in deep learning, we normally process batches of data, for example, when we are doing mini-batch gradient descent. Unfortunately, neural networks are not capable of directly processing sequences of varying length. Instead, what we need to do is pad the sequences so that they all have the same length.

So we'll have a look at padding and related concepts like truncation. Okay. So now let's take a more in-depth look at each of the steps we just discussed. The first and probably most important step is to tokenize the text. If you've studied other machine learning and deep learning techniques for NLP, you're probably already familiar with tokenizing text into words. In this case, we treat each word as a token. Sometimes this can even be as simple as calling string that split.

In fact, before machine learning became popular, programmers did think of string split and tokenization as the same thing. As an example, check out the strtok function in C and PHP. This function takes in a string and a delimiter as input and will return an array containing the tokens which result from splitting the string by the delimiter. In Java there is a StringTokenizer class that does the same thing. So this is all to point out the fact that the vernacular has changed over time.

In the era of machine learning, tokenization now normally means something more than just a string split. Consider, for example, punctuation. Let's say you have an input document which says, I like cats. Do you like cats? If we just did a string split, you can see that the first occurrence of the word cats would be joined with a period. The second would be joined with a question mark. In effect, these would be treated like two totally distinct tokens, which is probably not what you want.

Instead, what you probably want to do is to split the punctuation as well, so that the period and question mark are themselves separate tokens. Another option for tokenization you may have seen in the past is character level tokenization using this type of tokenization. You can build a character level language model. One example of that might be building a name generator. This type of tokenization has also been used for machine translation. But the type of tokenization we tend to use for transformers is even more complex.

In the era of Transformers, it has been customary to use sub word tokenization. That means we end up sometimes splitting words into multiple tokens. This makes sense. For example, consider the words run running runs and so forth. If we only did word level tokenization, these would all be treated as separate tokens and we would have to learn their meaning separately from the rest. That's not ideal because we know that all of these words have the same root word, and so there is some relation between them.

But suppose that we did subword tokenization. In this case, we would recognize that they all contain the token run, but each with a different suffix that slightly changes their meaning. Now it's important to note that although we often use sub word tokenization with transformers, they are still separate concepts. So for example, suppose you want to build an RNN model for some NLP task. You can still choose to use subword tokenization for that task, even though you are not using transformers.

In other words, although it may seem like subword tokenization is tied to transformers, this is not the case. We can have Transformers with any kind of tokenization and we can use any kind of tokenization with any kind of machine learning model. Another important question to consider when it comes to subword tokenization is how do we choose the subword boundaries? It's possible that we could just have a predefined set of rules like we do in NLTK, for instance.

If we see a word like wouldn't we know that this is actually a contraction of would not. This could be determined just by having a preset list of rules. But when it comes to subword tokenization, we can be smarter. In fact, we can train a model to learn the best subword tokenization based on information-theoretic concepts. As you recall, information theory is the field of study that shows us how to come up with algorithms for compressing data like images, video and text.

These same algorithms can also be applied to find the best way to tokenize text. We'll have more to say about this in the in-depth sections. Finally, one more important point to consider is that different models use different tokenization schemes because tokenization can essentially be trained on a data set. We can expect that the tokenizer we end up with will not be the same for every model. Since this is the case, we always have to make sure that when we use a specific model, we also use that model's corresponding tokenizer.

Okay. So now we are finally ready to move on to the next step. Now that we have tokens, our next task is to map each token into an integer. As you recall, in Python, we represent a mapping with a dictionary. We call this a token2index mapping because the key to the dictionary is a token and the value is its corresponding unique integer. On this slide, I've shared with you some pseudocode that demonstrates how one might generate such a mapping, given a data set that has been tokenized.

This is not because you'll need to code this yourself in this course, but because it will give you a better idea of what a token to index mapping is and where it comes from. The algorithm is very simple. All we do is loop through our entire data set of tokens every time we encounter a new token. We assign that a new ID and then we increment the next ID so that every ID is unique. Note that there are actually two steps to this algorithm. The first step is to simply build the token to index mapping, which gives us our dictionary.

The second step is to actually convert our text dataset into sequences of integers. This will be necessary if we want to pass our data into a neural network, because neural networks don't know how to deal with strings, they only process numbers. So the second loop here simply goes through each input document, which is a sequence of tokens, and then uses that mapping to get the corresponding sequence of token IDs. Additionally, we also often make use of the reverse mapping, which is a mapping from index to token.

This is useful when we need to convert from neural network predictions back into text. So for example, if our task is machine translation, the neural network will predict a series of token IDs. But in order to represent the sequence of token IDs as an actual sentence we can read. We need to convert them back into tokens and then join these tokens back into full words. The final step we want to discuss in this lecture is the concept of padding. We just converted our input documents into sequences of integers, but of course every document will have a different length.

In deep learning, we often want to process data in batches, which means multiple input documents at the same time. However, we can't process multiple inputs of different lengths simultaneously. This is because neural networks work with matrices and vectors and other high dimensional objects of constant size in each dimension. So what we need to do is called padding. Essentially, this means that we'll add a special pad token to all of our sequences so that they all have the same length.

Since the pad token is technically a token, it will also have a corresponding integer ID. One thing to note is that different tokenizers will use different IDs for the pad token. So you can't, for instance, assume that it's always zero. So in practice it's important to note that we don't pad all of our input sequences relative to the whole dataset, meaning we don't want to pad everything to be as long as the longest input document. This is because the longest input document may be an outlier in terms of size.

For instance, perhaps is the case that most of your documents are 1000 tokens long, but you have one document that's 100,000 tokens long. You don't want to end up padding all the 1000 token documents to be 100 times their size, since that would be a waste of space in computational effort. Instead, we should pad our sequences dynamically so that we only pad it relative to the current batch. As you recall, this is also the case with RNNs. Because of how RNNs work, they can handle inputs of any sequence length.

As you recall, the equation for an RNN is just W_x times x of t plus W_h times h of t minus one, plus b and this is the case for any T, so t can be arbitrarily large. The only constraint is that if you pass in multiple sequences at once, they must have the same length. Since X of T can represent a whole batch of data. But here's a question to consider. We know that transformers and attention are not recurrent. They do not work like RNNs as such.

How is it even possible for transformers to handle sequences of different lengths? In order to understand this, we'll really need to understand the theory of how self attention and transformers really work in terms of the math. So stay tuned for that. Another concept to keep in mind is the concept of truncation. Truncation is kind of the opposite of padding with padding. We want our documents to be longer with truncation. We want our documents to be shorter.

Unlike with RNNs, transformers do have a limit on the sequence length of the input. For example, BERT has a limit of 512 tokens. GPT-2 has a limit of 1024 tokens. As you recall, attention has quadratic complexity. So the longer your input sentence, the longer it takes to process in a quadratic sense. Therefore, you will quickly reach computational limits if your input sequences are too long. With CNNs, RNNs and transformers. A common technique is to truncate the input sequence.

If it is too long, that means cut off the beginning or cut off the ends so that the result does not exceed the limit. For tests like sentiment analysis and spam detection. This doesn't matter since the prediction is only a single value. The answer can likely be determined by the part that you didn't remove. This is a problem. However, if you're doing a many-to-many task like a named entity recognition or machine translation, if you're doing named entity recognition, you have a prediction for every word.

So if you cut off any words, you won't have predictions for those words. Similarly, for machine translation, if your document consists of multiple sentences and you cut off a few sentences, then those sentences won't be translated. One solution is to simply break up those inputs into multiple inputs and then translate them in parts.

3. Models and Tokenizers
========================

So in this lecture we will begin looking at concepts specific to the Hugging Face library, specifically what components make up the pipeline and how that's related to the text preprocessing steps we just discussed. Essentially, we can picture the pipeline as containing three distinct steps. The first step is the text preprocessing. This takes in raw text and converts it into a numerical format that can be handled by a neural network. The second step is the model.

This is the actual neural network that crunches the numbers. The third step is post-processing. This takes the numbers output by the neural network and converts them back into a human readable format. In this section, the will mainly be focused on the first two steps. The third step is not really handled by a specific thing, since it will be dependent on the kind of task. For instance, if it's just classification, then a prediction will be an integer class ID and we just need to map that back into a class label.

If it's machine translation, then our prediction will be a sequence of token IDs which will need to convert back into tokens and then back into a full document. Luckily, that can be done by the tokenizer. One hard example is named entity recognition, since entities correspond to full words well for transformers, tokens can be sub words. In this scenario you'll have to write the code yourself. So that will be outside the scope of this lecture and we'll just deal with any issues as they arise.

The first step, which is text preprocessing, is done by what we call a tokenizer in Hugging Face. You may have seen similar examples of tokenizers in other libraries, such as Keras. The reason why this might be a bit confusing is because while this is called a tokenizer, it does not only do tokenization. It actually does all the text preprocessing steps we previously discussed. But in Hugging Face, we call them tokenizers. As you recall, there are different tokenizers for every kind of model, so there's a BERT tokenizer, a DistilBERT tokenizer, a GPT-2 tokenizer and so forth.

However, we'll see that there is a nice universal interface to all of these tokenizers that will let us make our code more generic. The second step is the model. The model is essentially the neural network, which is just like the model object you've worked with in TensorFlow and PyTorch in the past. The Hugging Face model is basically a wrapper around either a PyTorch or TensorFlow model, which adds a few bells and whistles on top of what they already do.

Like the tokenizer, there is a universal interface to all types of models, so you never have to explicitly specify the BERT model that should be easy to model and so forth unless you really want to. So now that you understand the main steps, let's see what the code actually looks like. We'll begin by looking at the tokenizer. Now, as mentioned, there are multiple tokenizers that are model specific. But there is a universal interface so that you don't have to worry about picking the right class.

Specifically, there was a class called AutoTokenizer. This class has a method called from_pretrained, where you can pass in a model checkpoints just like we did with pipelines. This will automatically give you back the correct tokenized objects. So for example, if your model checkpoint is based on BERT, you will get back a tokenized object that has all the right stuff in it. Okay. So once you have your tokenizer, how do you use it? Well, it's actually just as simple as using a pipeline.

You can pass in your text or list of multiple texts, and out comes the model inputs formatted specially for the corresponding model. Note that this is a dictionary with several keys. The key input IDs corresponds to the integer IDs of the tokens that you would have gotten back from tokenizing your input. Note that this key will always be present because you will always want to convert your text into token IDs. The other keys are attention, mask and token type IDs.

We'll discuss what these mean later on in the course, but note that these keys can be specific to the type of model. For instance, token type IDs will show up for BERT, but not to DistilBERT. In any case, if all you want to do is plug in some code, you don't really need to worry about what these things mean, since you can just pass these model inputs into the model without really thinking about what's inside. But the next few slides will look at what's happening more in-depth.

Currently, there are still some alternative ways to produce the same transformations using the tokenizer object. Note that this is optional because we already saw code that does all the steps. This code is meant to just give you some more insight about what goes on. The first method we will look at is the tokenize method. This method, it takes in some text and it only does the tokenization step. That is, the output of this will be a list of tokens. The next step is to convert these tokens into token IDs.

This can be accomplished by using the function convert tokens to IDs. As you can see, this gives us back a list of integer IDs. Furthermore, note that the previous two steps can be done all in one step, which is accomplished by calling the encode method. However, note that this method also does an additional step, which is to add a special BERT specific tokens called CLS and SEP. We'll understand more about what these are for as we go through the course.

In addition, and note that there exist some methods to do the reverse operations. For instance, if we have IDs, we can convert these back into tokens by calling the function convert_ids_to_tokens. As you can see, this will give us back a list of tokens. Furthermore, we can pass in the IDs into a function called Decode and this will give us back a single string with the tokens that join back together. So this does the opposite of tokenize as well. Note that if the CLS and SEP tokens were part of the IDs you passed in, they will also be produced in the decode output.

Now you might be wondering why do all these methods exist when you can just call the tokenize and get everything all at once, including the extra inputs like the attention mask? Well, it's important to remember that this is a new library. Things are always changing. In fact, I found that a few related methods which were not discussed in this lecture still work but are now deprecated. So just keep in mind that things are constantly evolving. And don't be surprised if the syntax changes overnight.

And as a corollary to that, don't worry when it does. Remember that the goal of these courses isn't to get you to memorize one set of syntax. It's about turning you into a kind of programmer that can handle any syntax. So the next thing to note is that what we've done so far won't just work with our model. As you recall, the previous output was a dictionary containing values, which were all lists. But PyTorch doesn't take this as input. Instead, PyTorch models process torch tensors in order to get the values back as torch tensors.

We set the argument return_tensors to the string. Note that there are other possible options. For instance, if you're using TensorFlow, you can use the string 'tf' or if you just want NumPy arrays, you can pass an NP. In this course we'll be using pytorch models and Hugging Face since they are the most flexible. Okay. So the next step is to consider what happens when we have multiple strings or documents and we want to tokenize all of them at the same time.

You'll notice that if you try this naively, you're likely going to get an error message if you also specify that you want PyTorch tensors. So why does this happen? Well, remember that in deep learning we don't like things to have variable length. If we're going to process them in batches. There's no such thing as a jagged pytorch tensor. However, this will work if you just want lists. At the same time, those lists cannot be used as inputs to the model. So you don't want to do that anyway.

What we need to do is to add two additional arguments, which is padding and truncation. In particular, we should set these to true. It is unclear why these are the defaults. But again, remember that this is a constantly evolving and new library. So that may be the case in the future, as you can see. Whereas previously each value in our dictionary was a list. Now each value is a list of lists where each inner list corresponds to each input string. Importantly, notice how each of these inner lists now has the same length.

The shorter sequences have been made artificially longer by adding a pad token at the end. This is also a good time to mention the purpose of the attention mask. Essentially, this tells the model where it should bother to pay attention to for any tokens where the attention mask is zero. Our model will ignore those tokens and it will not be possible to use them to compute the model output, which is of course what we want. So in total to ensure that we get a batch of data that can be used as input into our PyTorch model, which is the default for Hugging Face and currently the most flexible.

We need to specify the padding argument, the truncation argument and the return_tensors argument. Okay. So at this point, we are ready to pass in our absolutely names of model inputs into the model. So first, we'll need to look at how to create a model. Now, although we could take a completely pre-trained model like the ones we looked at for pipelines in this section, we will focus on a fine tuning for text classification. So suppose that we want to fine tune a BERT model for text classification like the tokenize.

Or we could create a BERT-specific object, but will instead use an auto model for more flexibility. In particular, we'll use the class auto model for sequence classification. Note that there are similarly named classes for other tasks which we will encounter in a later section. In order to load a pre-trained BERT model, we simply call the function from_pretrained. Just like we did with the tokenizer. Note that the checkpoint we pass in must match the checkpoint we passed in for the tokenizer so that we get the right tokenize for the model.

Now you'll notice that when you run this, you will get a warning stating that some of the layers have not been trained. This is exactly what we want. What this means is that when we call this method, it does all the work of taking the pre-trained BERT body, cutting off the head, which was meant for another task, and adding a new head for our new task. Because this is classification, our final layer is just a dense layer with K outputs. By default will create a binary classifier.

But you can change this by specifying the argument num labels. Okay. So the next step is to actually pass in some data into your model. At this point, we're not going to discuss how to train a model, since that will come shortly. This is just how to make predictions with the model, which of course is a prerequisite of being able to train it. To do this, we take the model inputs dictionary that we got back from the tokenisation and we pass them into the model with a double asterisk in front.

Now you might be wondering what's with the double asterisk? You recall that in Python at the double asterisk converts a dictionary into named arguments. As an example, suppose I have a function, a create user that takes in a name, email and password. I could call this function like a normal function call where I just pass in the arguments in order. Another option is to specify the argument names explicitly, and yet another option is to have a dictionary with the method parameters and to use the double asterisk.

So how does this relate to our model? Well, you'll recall that in PyTorch when we create a model or a module, we create a forward method where we specify what operations the model should actually do to produce an output. We can then call that module as if it were a function or a method. Of course, this method has arguments which have names, and that's what we're passing in when we pass in our dictionary of model inputs. Finally, let's talk about what kind of outputs you should expect to get back from a model.

Since we're going to be working with PyTorch and since this is a classifier with K classes, we're going to get back a PyTorch tensor with K predictions for each input sample. If we passed in and documents, output will be of size n by k. If we pass in only a single document without a sample dimension that is a 1D tensor of input ids, then our output will just be of size k. Our interpretation of the outputs is that they are logits, which means these are values we have before we apply the softmax function.

If you want probabilities, you can always apply the softmax yourself. But usually what we want to do is take the argmax to get a prediction. Furthermore, note that the type of output is specific to the type of model. Since we built a sequence classifier. The output will be an object of type sequence classifier output. You'll see that this acts like a named tuple in Python, which is kind of like a dictionary and kind of like an object and kind of like a tuple all at the same time.

There is one key called logits, and to access the logits you can use the dot notation like an object. You can use dictionary notation, or you can specify the position of the item you want, just like you would for a tuple.
