So as you can see, the attention mask corresponds to where we have real tokens and where we have padding tokens. So we have one where there are real tokens in zero, where we have padding tokens by passing this into the model. It will avoid it using the padding tokens to do any computation of the output.

Of course, we don't want them to influence our prediction. The next step is to demonstrate that we can pass in these model inputs just the same as we did before. So as you can see, we get back a two by three tensor, which makes sense because we passed in two documents and the number of classes is three.

5. Transfer Learning & Fine-Tuning (pt 1)
=========================================

So in this lecture, we will be discussing another important concept related to transformers known as transfer learning. Now it's important to recognize that transfer learning is not related to the architecture of transformers, nor is it a concept that is specific to Transformers. In fact, transfer learning is a general machine learning technique that's been applied to Transformers. One way to think of this is transfer. Learning is what makes Transformers useful and practical for practitioners in this field.

Without transfer learning, the cost to train a transformer network would simply be too great. So transfer learning is what allows us to make building intelligent networks faster and cheaper so as to be practical for everyday use. Okay. So what is transfer learning all about? The best way to understand this is through its history. In particular, transfer learning comes from the field of computer vision. So as you know, one of the remarkable results we have observes from training CNNs is that each layer seems to learn progressively more complex feature transformations of the input.

For example, in a face recognition network, the first layer might learn basic lines and strokes, while the second layer might learn individual facial features such as noses, eyes and lips. The third layer might learn about different whole faces, so each layer builds up a more complex representation based on the simpler features from the previous layers. In effect, what we can say is that every layer seems to output something useful which can then be made use of by adding further layers.

So initially, one of the main tasks of interest in computer vision was classifying images. To this day, the standard benchmark is the ImageNet dataset, which contains millions of images and 1000 classes. Now, because of the large size and variety of this dataset, these CNNs learn to recognize many things. So many things, in fact, that you could pass in a completely random image, not in the ImageNet dataset. And chances are you would be able to recognize whatever is in that image.

The key point for transfer learning is that because the features learned by these networks are so useful in a generic sense, there is no longer any need to train them. So how does transfer learning work? Firstly, we should recognise that transfer learning is dependent on large organisations and research groups being generous. Thanks to the openness and generosity of researchers in this field, the weights of very powerful neural networks have been uploaded to the internet.

Free for you to access. That is, you can download a neural network that another research group has worked maybe weeks or months to train and use it instantly with much less effort. We call these pre-trained networks. So what do you do with a pre-trained network? Well, you simply chop off the head, meaning the final dense layer, and you add your own a final dense layer with an output size that activation appropriate for your task. Note that on some occasions you might chop off multiple final layers and you might also add multiple final layers.

Once you've added your new layers, you can then train the network on your own and data sets. Crucially, in computer vision, it is typical to only train part of the network, specifically only the layers you added. The earlier layers in the network which come from the pre-trained network, usually do not need to be trained further. In other words, what you are effectively doing is training a very tiny neural network, or even just a tiny linear model using the pre-trained network as a feature extractor.

And the reason why we can do this is because, as you recall, this pre-trained network already outputs very useful features. Having been trained on a very large dataset, importantly, the kind of data you want to train on should be similar to the kind of data the pre-trained network was trained on. For example, if the network was pre trained on RGB images, then your dataset should also consist of RGB images. But you would not want to do something like take a network pre-trained on English text and then use transfer learning on Spanish text.

So one way to think about transfer learning is that the pre-trained network is not part of the model per se. As you recall, in machine learning, we train our models on input features. Those input features can be of any kind, such as raw tensor data, or maybe features we engineered specifically for our domain. One example of this is TF-IDF features which we use in NLP. So in general, the process of training a machine learning model can be visualized by starting with a simple CSV file containing our input features and their corresponding targets.

We then go into Python, instantiate a model like a random forest and we call the fit function on our dataset. Now, where does this CSV file come from? Well, a typical scenario is that we download it from Kaggle or if this is in your workplace, perhaps it was produced by the data engineering team at your company. But one way to think about transfer learning is that this CSV is simply produced by the pre-trained network without its final layer. As you recall, this pre-trained network takes in an image and produces a feature vector.

Thus what we could do is simply pass in our whole data set, compute the corresponding feature vectors and save them as a CSV for later use with other models such as those in scikit-learn. So this is another way to think of transfer learning. In fact, this exact process is demonstrated in both my TensorFlow two and PyTorch courses. So if you've taken those, then you've seen the actual code to do this. Note that it's also much faster since you don't have to keep passing your data through the pre-trained network.

On each step of gradient descent. That can be slow, especially if the pre-trained network is large, which is the case for many modern neural networks. One important thing to note about transfer learning is that you can really do any kind of task. The only requirement is that your final layers have the right number of outputs, the right activations, and that you use the right loss function so the same requirements with any other neural network. The reason I mention this is to make it clear that we are not limited to just classifying images, which is what the pre-trained network was trained to do.

Some examples of what we can do with image CNNs include object detection, where not only do we classify the objects in an image, we also identify their locations using bounding boxes. Another task is style transfer, where we take in one image called the content image and another image called the style image. We then apply the style from the style image to the content image to produce a new image that has the same global structure as the content image, but with the textures and colors of the style image.

So these are two tasks which go far beyond just classifying images. But using a network that has been pre-trained to classify images turns out to be very useful. In all cases, the common element between all these tasks is that the input to the neural network is an image or images. Okay. So Transfer learning was first used in the field of computer vision. But an obvious question to ask is can this method be applied to language as well? Now it's worth thinking about why these two particular types of data are so important.

Consider how we represent information on the Internet. Whether you're on Facebook, Twitter, Reddit, Hacker News, or any other kind of news blogs and so forth, all of this information comes in the form of either images or text. Thus, these are two very important modes of communication. Images and text are how we as humans represent information and how we transfer information from one human to another. These are a kind of natural data because they just exist in the world and they are abundant.

This is unlike tabular data, which is typically curated by a human. We might unnaturally organize it into columns of feature vectors that we've measured or recorded, but images and text are natural. They exist in abundance. And it just so happens that computer vision is the field that deals with images and natural language processing is the field that deals with text. So the point of this is with such an abundant source of data and knowing that this method already works with images, trying to do transfer learning on text is simply an obvious thing to do.

6. Transfer Learning & Fine-Tuning (pt 2)
=========================================

So in this lecture we are going to continue our discussion on transfer, learning and fine tuning. The previous lecture we discussed transfer learning and how it's been applied to the field of computer vision and how it might be applied to NLP. But there's another aspect to transfer learning known as Fine Tuning. Now there's a bit of confusion around the difference between these terms, and I hope that this lecture will clear up any potential confusion you might have.

Transfer learning is a more broad general term, meaning that we take parameters, learns for one task, and we use those same parameters in a some capacity on another task. Fine tuning, on the other hand, is a more specific term that describes a particular mechanism one can apply during the process of transfer learning. Specifically, we know that the process of learning is called a gradient descent. We can picture the loss surface in our parameter space and the pre-trained parameters already being in a pretty good spot.

The fine tuning step would be to slowly and carefully tune these parameters a tiny bit further to improve performance on our task. Now, don't worry if that sounds a bit too abstract, since we'll use actual examples in this lecture. Just remember that transfer learning is what we call it. Any time we use the weights from one task on another task, whether those weights will be fine tuned or not. Okay. So let's describe the process of fine tuning as it applies in the field of computer vision.

In computer vision, we start with a big CNN, which is trained on an image classification dataset such as ImageNet. Using this big CNN. We then chop off the final dense layers and we add new, untrained, final dense layers for our new task. The next step is to train these new layers on our own dataset. This is the process we call transfer learning, but no fine tuning has happened yet. Note that during this process only the new layers were trained, the pre-trained weights remained fixed.

But after this there is an optional next step, which is fine tuning. During this fine tuning step, we unfreeze the whole network and we train all the layers, typically with a very small learning rate. By small, I mean small in comparison to the learning rate we were using before. Remember that the weights are already in a pretty good place, so we only want to take very tiny steps to increase performance by a little bit. If our steps are too large, we might end up in the totally wrong place, which is not good.

Another potential concern is that too much fine tuning could lead your model to over fit. Now please note that there are many options for this fine tuning step. It's really up to you to experiment and determine what is right for you. For some data sets, you may only need to fine tune the last few layers maybe five layers, seven layers or ten layers. Usually the first few layers don't need to change them much, if at all, since they represent very generic features like edges and blobs.

Now, the process I just described for fine tuning is, in my opinion, a bit too specific. The best way to really understand deep learning and neural networks is to understand one of the first uses of fine tuning, and that was with a method called greedy layer wise pre-training. In the old days, we use activation functions like the sigmoid and the hyperbolic tangent which led to vanishing gradients, and as such it was difficult to train neural networks in an end to end fashion like we do today.

One technique invented by Geoffrey Hinton and his students at the University of Toronto was to train each layer separately, one after another. This is a really great way to understand how neural networks work, because it demonstrates how the hidden features learned by a neural network always tend to be useful by training a neural network to do some task, whatever that task may be. Somehow, it always magically turns out that the intermediate representations learned by the neural network have some useful property.

This method is also nice because it applies to regular feedforward NNs. So we don't have to assume we're working with images. In any case, the process works like this. Suppose that we have some data set and we train an auto encoder on it. As you recall, an auto encoder is a neural network that simply learns to output its own input. But as mentioned, neural networks tend to learn the useful intermediate representations. So suppose that we take the intermediate representation of this first auto encoder, call it Z one, and throw away the second half of the network.

What we then do is train another auto encoder, but this time on Z1 instead of the original inputs x. Let's call the intermediate representation of the second auto encoder Z2. Again, we throw away the second half of the auto encoder and repeat the process training a new auto encoder on Z2. What we're effectively doing is we're transforming the input again and again into new higher level representations each time, since each transformation is just a neural network layer.

The whole chain just gives us a big multi-layer neural network. So this multi-layer neural network is made up of the first halves of each of the auto encoders. Eventually we can stop adding new auto encoders and add one final dense layer specific to our task. We then train this final dense layer and all the previous auto encoders, plus the final dense layer, make up our full neural network. The key result was that this neural network performed better than a neural network that was trained end to end.

This process is called greedy, layer wise pre-training, which hopefully makes sense now that you understand the process. So where does fine tuning come into play? Well, it works just like how I described earlier in this lecture. After the greedy, layer wise pre-training process, which only trained one layer at a time, we then take the whole neural network and train the whole thing all at once. But we do this with a very small learning rate, since our parameters are already in a good spot.

We just want to make them slightly better. Now, to be clear about why we are discussing this example, this is an example of where we can have fine tuning but without any use of transfer learning. So while what we are doing in this course might make it seem like these two concepts are conflated, they are in fact distinct. So one purpose of the previous examples was to demonstrate that there are multiple scenarios where one might apply fine tuning. Note that one scenario involves transfer learning while the other did not.

Fine tuning is merely the process of taking pretty good weights and tuning them further to make them even better. Understanding this, we can now discuss what fine tuning will look like for Transformers. So for transformers in the field of NLP, the first part of the process looks essentially the same. We begin by training a transformer on some NLP task, which is to be discussed. We then chop off the last layers and add new final layers for our new task.

At this point, the body has trained weights, but the head does not. But instead of how we do things in computer vision where we train only the head first, the library will be using simply trains, all the weights at the same time. And we also refer to this process as fine tuning. Note that this somewhat contradicts our earlier definition of fine tuning. Technically, not all the weights are in a pretty good place when we start this process, because the new layers have totally random weights.

However, this method seems to work well enough. Interestingly, training only the head doesn't seem to work as well. You're encouraged to try it yourself, to confirm. Finally, it's good to be aware that none of this is set in stone. There are no rules which state that you have to do it this way or you have to do it that way. Perhaps it's the case that freezing the first few layers works well. Perhaps it's the case that freezing the whole pre-trained network works well.

Perhaps it's the case that first training the new layers and then fine tuning as we do in computer vision works well. So it's really up to you to experiment and to decide what works best for your data set. For this course, we won't do this experimentation because the number of possibilities is combinatorial and none of these alternative options are built in. But if you have something you want to try, you are strongly encouraged to do so. Now you don't often get updates to a course before the course is even released, but this is a rare example.

After I made this lecture, I came across a recent journal article that actually came out while I was making this course. As I always mentioned in these courses, get used to the fact that things move fast. The paper is titled BitFit: Simple Parameter-efficient Fine-tuning for Transformer-based Masked Language-models. You can find it in extra_reading.txt as usual. So this paper was last revised only a week prior to me making this update. The paper shows that training only the bias terms or even just a subset of the bias terms can match the performance of full fine tuning.

And in some cases, performance can even be improved. It should go without saying why this is an awesome development. The bias terms make up only a small portion of the total number of parameters. This lets us avoid having to make expensive matrix computations, which will make training much more efficient. So this is a great example for those who just want to memorize the rules of thumb and never explore an experiment by themselves. If you want to make great discoveries like this, you must explore and you must experiment.

And you must go against the rules of thumb.

7. Transfer Learning & Fine-Tuning (pt 3)
=========================================

So with computer vision, the way we pre-trained our neural networks is by classifying images. Recall that this is supervised learning because it requires us to provide label data for each image. We must also specify which class that image belongs to. The question is what kind of task should we use for text? Well, it turns out that supervised learning is not an ideal choice. Firstly, we'd have to consider what we would use as labels. We'd have to make sure that these labels made sense and actually helps the neural network learn the structure of language.

But this is actually not necessary for classifying documents. For example, it's easy to tell whether a document is about biology or physics simply by looking at the kinds of words in the documents. There is no need to learn the structure of language. In other words, merely classifying documents as a pre-training task might not help the neural network learn the structure of language as well as we would like. Secondly, with so much text available on the Internet, it would be difficult and costly to consistently and correctly label every piece of text as an example of why this would be undesirable.

Consider that. Imagine it took three years to label. So the answer is that we don't use supervised learning, but rather what is now known as self-supervised learning. This is a newer term, despite the fact that these techniques have existed for many years. Another way to think of it is unsupervised learning, which is what we would have probably called it many years ago. Note that in the both the original GPT and BERT papers, they referred to the Pre-Training process as unsupervised learning rather than self-supervised learning.

In my opinion, the idea is fine, but I like to use unsupervised learning since there is really no need to make up a new name for something that already had a name. So how does it work? Well, there are multiple ways to pre train, but one method used by large models such as GPT builds what is called a language model, which you may have heard of. So what is a language model to review? This is where we model the probability of the next token in a sentence given the previous tokens.

Mathematically, we would write this says P of x of T, given x of t minus one, x of t minus two and so forth. As you recall, when we use Markov models, these probabilities are reduced to one step transitions. So we just have P of x of T given x of t minus one. With neural networks, we don't have to make the Markov assumption, and thus we can account for more context. So how do we build such a neural network? One example is with RNNs what we take as input the previous words in the sentence, and then as output we try to predict the next word.

And note that because of the structure, this kind of model is what allows us to generate text as well. So if you train a language model in this fashion, you can use it to generate poems, stories, social media comments and even computer code. Now you recall that I said that this method has actually existed for many years. In fact, many decades ago we began doing the same thing with Time series. It turns out that this is how we train at time series models such as A.

We essentially build a mini neural network that takes as input the previous data points in the Time series and tries to predict the next value in the Time series. So this method is not new by any means. It just so happens that this same method turns out to work pretty well these days on new models such as transformers. Now, the previous Pre-Training objective we just discussed is not the only Pre-Training objective that is possible. The previous example described an auto regressive language model because we use past tokens to predict the next token.

But there is another kind of language model called the masked language model. In previous courses, I've called this the article spinner because it allows you to replace words in a sentence with plausible replacements, which is what blackhat marketers do when they want to spin articles to generate content for SEO. In contrast to the previous Pre-Training objective, we will no longer be using the past tokens to predict the next token. Instead, the masked language model uses all surrounding tokens to predict the middle token.

That is, the tokens we conditioned on. It can come from past tokens or future tokens. For example, consider the sentence the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. Imagine that we remove the word fox, replace it with what we call a masked token, and then we pass this through our neural network. The job of our neural network is to predict what the masked word should be. As such, it will learn a probability distribution to predict the missing word. Note that the token we remove can come from anywhere in the sentence.

So it could be Fox jumps over Lazy Dog and so forth. To recap, we just looked at two different Pre-Training objectives for training transformers. The first method is used for training GPT and GPT like models, and it's called a causal language model or an auto regressive language model. This is because they are just like auto regressive time series models where we use past data points in the Time series to predict the next data point. The second method is used for training BERT and BERT like models, and it's called masked language modeling.

Sometimes these models are called auto encoding language models. This is because they are similar in principle to auto encoders, which you've learned about if you study deep learning with me in the past. One kind of auto encoder is the noisy auto encoder where we pass in a feature vector, which could be an image, but more generally could be any kind of feature vector. We then add noise to the input feature vector, which for images might simply mean setting those pixel values to zero.

The job of the auto encoder is to recreate the original image without this noise. As you can see, this is really what we are doing when we remove a word from a sentence and ask the neural network to predict that word. We are asking the model to recreate the original sentence, which has been corrupted with noise. Now, just as a side note, there is actually one additional test that BERT uses for unsupervised pre-training, which is next sentence prediction.

This is not a language modeling task, but it does help with downstream tasks which have to deal with two input sentences at once. One example of such a downstream task is called textual entailment, where we want to know given two sentences whether or not they mean the same thing. An example of that would be Google Files for its IPO and Google goes public, which both mean the same thing in order to pre-trained BERT so that it can understand the relation between the multiple input sentences.

We use next sentence prediction for this task. We simply input two sentences and the model tries to predict whether or not the second sentence followed the first in the training data. Note that while this is a binary classification task, it is also an example of unsupervised or self-supervised learning. Since we don't need to manually label the data, this data can be easily created or generated on the fly from a corpus of text. Now there's one small issue with Pre-Training language models, which is that they take lots of time and lots of compute.

Luckily, there's some parallelization that can be done, which does reduce the time but does not reduce the compute. Compute translates to cost. And thus one of the major barriers to entry is the amount of money you have to spend. To give you an example of this. Let's consider one of the more recent and well known language models called GPT-3. This language model contained 175 billion parameters for comparison of LeNet, which was one of the first popular CNN architectures, had just 60,000 parameters, as you recall, in deep learning.

We found that one of the most powerful ways to improve model performance is to simply make the model bigger and to add more data. So GPT-3 is a bigger version of GPT-2, which is in turn a bigger version of the original GPT. As a side note, GPT stands for generative pre-trained transformer, which should make complete sense to you at this point. Well, it turns out that based on one estimate, GPT-3 would have taken over $4.6 million to train using the cheapest GPU cloud on the market using a single Tesla v 100 GPU.

This would take 355 years to train. Note that this calculation is theoretical since the model is actually too large to fit on a single GPU. In any case, it's definitely not something you're going to do at home. Now, what's the point of mentioning all of this? Well, the point is to highlight the absolute necessity of transfer learning. It's not simply a matter of being a cool new trick. There is just no other way we would be able to ever make use of such large models.

We depend on companies like Google, Meta and OpenAI to open source these weights so that we can make use of them.

Now here's something a bit weird about the Hugging Face API, at least as it is currently implemented.

The map method is analogous to the usual map method in Python, which you can call when you want to apply the same function to every element in a sequence.

What makes this map method nice is that behind the scenes it does the mapping in batches to improve performance by calling the map method and passing in a tokenized function. The data sets library automatically knows it should apply the same function to every data set that is train, validation and test. Now, if you find the map method a bit confusing, that's perfectly understandable. If you're having trouble wrapping your head around it, keep in mind that there's a simple Python analogy. That's what the method is doing, but it's doing it more efficiently. The next item to consider is the training arguments class. Basically, you can think of this like a training configuration. The arguments to the constructor are quite simple. We pass in the model, the trainer arguments, objects we just discussed. The training data set, the validation data set and the tokenizer. So why do we need to pass in the tokenizer again? Unfortunately, it's still needed for further computation. Personally, I don't think this is a great design, but that's the way it currently is. In any case, once you've created your trainer object training the model is very simple. Just call trainer dot train. Now, if you call the train method, as we just described it, you won't get much information other than the loss. But suppose you care about other metrics like accuracy, F1 score and so forth.

Well, discuss later on how to compute custom metrics, but for now we'll look at the Hugging Face way of doing things, which is to use a set of metrics that have been predefined for the data we are using. Specifically, there is a function called the load metric, also part of the data sets library. The arguments to this happens to be the same as the arguments into load_dataset for this particular example. More generally, however, you can pass in an argument to specify a specific metric like the BLEU score or a python file where you've defined your own computations.

So how does this work? Unfortunately, we cannot pass this metric into the trainer directly, which is another oddity about this library. Essentially, we need to define another function to do some extra work. Firstly, the metric we get back from load metric is an object which has a method called compute, which will return a dictionary containing the computed metrics for a given task. This compute method it takes in two arrays, the predictions and the targets, called references.

For some reason, this will give us back a dictionary with the value of the metric or metrics with their names as keys. However, as stated, we cannot just use this function directly. Although you can call this whatever you like. This takes in a tuple of logits and labels, which I've simply named logits and labels inside the compute metrics function we call load metric.

Although please note that it would be more efficient to put this outside. The next step is to unpack the logits and the labels to be separate variables. After doing this and calling Trainer Dot Train as shown earlier, you will see the additional metrics being printed out at the frequency you specified with the trainer arguments. Okay. 11. Hugging Face AutoConfig
===========================

So in this lecture, we'll be investigating something that came up earlier, which is why the fine tune model outputs only generic label names. As you recall in the previous lecture, we solved this in kind of a hacky way, which was to modify the config file after calling the save method. If you're a programmer, this might make you recoil in horror. Luckily, there is a slightly better way. Unfortunately, what you would hope existed doesn't actually exist.

Specifically, it would be nice if you could just pass in specific label names into the from_pretrained method, just as you can specify the number of labels. This would be ideal and in my opinion makes the most sense. But unfortunately, this currently isn't possible. However, there is a way to achieve a similar effect, which is what we'll look at in this lecture in particular. Hugging face also has config objects. We'll pass in this config object into the from_pretrained.

So it pretty much works like our ideal scenario. Note that these config objects are model specific like tokenisation. So you can have a BERT config, a GPT-2 config and so forth. As you might expect, there is also an auto config which automatically chooses the right config object based on the checkpoint you give it. Just as we can have AutoTokenizer and auto models. We also have auto configs. Now please note that most of this notebook is the same as the previous one.

So we'll skip to the relevant parts. So we'll begin by importing auto config along with the auto model and the trainer class. So recall that earlier in this notebook we've loaded in the data sets, converted them into the correct format and so forth. So the next step is to load up a config by calling from pre-trained passing in our checkpoint. The next step is to print out our config just to see what it looks like. So as you can see, it's sort of like a dictionary with keys and values.

Importantly, notice how there's nothing here about label names. Now, if you check the attributes of the config object, you'll see that there are two relevant attributes corresponding to labels, id2label and label2id. So let's look at id2label. As you can see, this is a dictionary mapping an integer label ID to the corresponding label name. As you may have expected. The next step is to look at the label2id. So as you can see, we get the reverse mapping, which again, you may have expected.

So it should be evident that what we need to do is overwrite these id2label in label2id attributes. Now the API for this isn't too great. In my opinion, there should be a function for doing this so that you don't have to manually overwrite attributes yourself. For example, you can just pass in gibberish and it would break your config. But since no such method exists, we'll just stick with what we can get. So you can see here that we're basically assigning these the targeted map from earlier in this notebook.

As you recall, the targeted map had our desired label names mapped to corresponding integer IDs. The next step is to call from_pretrained with their auto model to get back a model object. The difference between what we did before and what we are doing now is that we are now passing in the config object we just looked at. Okay. So essentially all of the remaining steps are the same as the previous notebook, so I won't bother to explain them again. Okay.

So at this point we fine tune our model, saved it and loaded it back in as a pipeline object. At this point, we can just pass in some strings and get back predictions. Okay. So our first input is JetBlue. Thank you. Predictably, the prediction is positive and the label shows up as the string positive instead of something like label one. So passing in the config object was a success. We no longer had to manually modify the config file.

12. Fine-Tuning with Multiple Inputs (Textual Entailment)
=========================================================

So in this lecture, we are going to look at another example of text classification, which is slightly more complicated. In this example, we are going to build a text classifier where the input consists of not just one but two sentences. This is very practical and we can think of examples where we might want to have a model that knows how to process multiple input sentences at the same time. One example is answering multiple choice questions as input. We might want to pass in the question along with all the possible answers, and we want our model to select the right answer.

Another example is with chatbots. With earlier chatbots, a simple way to train them was with single prompt and response pairs. So you would give the chatbot a prompt and the chatbot would memorise appropriate responses. But this doesn't take into account the history of the conversation, which might make the conversation awkward. Another example is question answering where we pass in a question along with a passage of text which contains the answer.

We would then like the model to select the portion of the passage where the answer resides. Now you might be wondering how are we going to build a transformer that can handle multiple sentences as inputs? If you have experience with machine learning or neural networks, you might have some sense that this would be a difficult task. Normally, with transfer learning, we only have to change the head of the pre-trained network while keeping the inputs in the middle layers of the same.

But how can we change the number of inputs? The answer is we don't have to. It turns out that with the single input already in place, we can train the transformer to understand having multiple sentences concatenated into the same inputs. In fact, conceptually, this could work with RNNs as well. I'll leave it as an exercise for you to think about how that might work. You'll recall that with BERT, which is the main model we are using in this section, is pre-trained on unsupervised NLP tasks.

Well, it turns out that one of these tasks involves having multiple sentences in the same input. So it makes sense that BERT should be able to handle multiple sentences. In particular, the task is called next sentence prediction. To build the data for this task, we take two sentences from our training corpus and the label is whether or not the second sentence follows the first. In other words, we build a binary classifier. For BERT, we use special formatting to help the model understand where the first and second sentences are located.

In particular, we always start with the class token. We then follow it with the first sentence. We then add the SEP token. We then follow that with the second sentence. And finally, we add another token at the end. So hopefully you now understand that the utility of these special tokens, when we only had one sentence as input, they may have seemed superfluous, but now they actually have practical utility. The task we'll be looking at next is known as textual entailment.

This might sound complicated, but in fact it is quite simple. Thanks to my rule, all data is the same. It is no different from the next sentence prediction task I just described. In particular, we're still going to have to input sentences and the target will still be binary. The only difference is the meaning of the task. In this case, we want to know whether one sentence entails another. For example, consider the input sentence pair. Bob buys a car and Bob owns a car.

This is an example of where the first sentence entails the second. Now consider Bob purchased cheese and Bob doesn't have cheese. This is an example of where there is no entailment. Okay. So now let's discuss what this will look like in code. If you think about it, we know that the model has to be the same. What changes are the inputs? The input is still text and the output is still a binary prediction. What changes are the inputs? In other words, we should pay attention to the data set and tokenizer.

So the data set will look something like this. Recall that you can think of it like a tabular data set with different columns for different things, just like a csv previously. We only had one input sentence and a label. This time we'll have two sentences and a label in our dataset. These are simply called sentence1 and sentence2. Luckily, the tokenizer from Hugging Face is well equipped to handle two sentences at once. You simply need to pass them in a separate arguments.

This will conceptually convert the inputs into a string formatted as we described earlier, where we have the class token in the first sentence. The SEP token. The second sentence. And another SEP token. In reality, these of course, will be converted into tokens and then token IDs. Now there's one important note to make at this point. You'll recall that for the BERT tokenizer, we generate this input called token type IDs. Having two input sentences will help us understand that the role of this input when we use BERT and we have two sentences as input, the BERT tokenizer will use zeros for the positions of the first sentence and ones for the positions of the second sentence.

So now you know that this input actually has a purpose and isn't totally superfluous. However, this is an interesting point, and the next example will be using a slightly different model called DistilBERT. For this model, there is no input called token type IDs. It is simply not necessary as an exercise. After seeing the following notebook, you should double check that this is the case. when we use BERT, there will be an input called token type IDs, but when we use DistilBERT, there won't be.

Also compare the results to see which model performs better along with their speed of training. As you recall, we would like to apply that tokenisation to every sample in our data set object. So we write a tokenized function that we can pass into the map method. This will also take care of truncation. The next step is to call the map method passing in the tokenize function, as we did before. This gives us back our tokenized data. Of course, at this point, the format of our data is exactly the same as it was in the previous lectures, which means there is no more work to do.

As an exercise. Since this is quite simple. You might want to complete the following notebook on your own. Before looking at the next lecture. We'll be using the RTE dataset, which is also part of the GLUE benchmark. So good luck and I'll see you in the next lecture.

13. Fine-Tuning Transformers with Multiple Inputs in Python
===========================================================

So in this lecture, we'll be looking at how to do fine tuning for text classification when we have more than one input sentence. We'll begin by installing transformers and data sets, as we normally do. The next step is to import load_dataset as well as numpy. The next step is to load in our dataset, which is the RTE dataset. This is part of the GLUE benchmark. Like the earlier example on sentiment analysis. Note that I've pasted a description of the dataset here which explains that the data has been processed to be for binary classification.

Other options are possible, for example, having three classes where the third class is neutral, meaning that the two sentences neither entail nor contradict. 14. Fine-Tuning Section Summary
===============================

So in this lecture, we will be summarizing everything we learned in this section. This section was all about fine tuning a pre-trained transformer so that you can get better performance on your own data sets or even create models for different tasks. This section focused on the task of text classification, which is a simple but very common use case of NLP. It's also a good introduction to what goes on behind the scenes when you use a pipeline. In particular, we looked at the model and the tokenizer.

Although this does require more coding, this is a necessary step if we want to train transformers and not just use them. We started this section with an optional review of two important concepts text, preprocessing and fine tuning. Additionally, we also reviewed Transfer Learning, which is sometimes associated with fine tuning but doesn't have to be. Text preprocessing involves many steps, although we encapsulate them in a single class called a tokenizer.

We noted that the naming is a bit weird because tokenization technically involves only the splitting step while the tokenizer object does much more. This includes converting the tokens into integers, padding and truncation. We then turned our attention to transfer learning and fine tuning. Transfer learning is an important concept, first applied in the field of computer vision, but now also popular in NLP. This is when we train a model on one task and then we use some of those weights for a related task.

For NLP, we also make use of the concept of fine tuning, which involves not just training the new weights but all of the weights simultaneously. We saw that at least for the tasks in this section. It doesn't take many epochs for the model to start overfitting. In this section, we looked at the following tasks. First we looked at the SST-2 task, which is part of the GLUE benchmark. This is a sentiment analysis task which is simple, easy to apply in the real world and easy for everyone to understand.

This also introduced us to the GLUE benchmark, which is itself an important concept in NLP. Since this is what transformers are now tested against. The next task we looked at was also very practical how to train a text classifier on your own custom dataset. We learned that as long as you can convert your data into a simple CSV format, which shouldn't be difficult for anyone taking this course, you can continue to use the Hugging Face Datasets package.

The third task we looked at was also of practical importance and introduced a new concept. In particular, it showed us how to train a model where we have not just one input sentence but two. For this example, we looked at the textual entailment task, which is another task in the GLUE benchmark.

###

1. Token Classification Section Introduction
============================================

So in this lecture we will be introducing the next section of this course, which will be a slightly more advanced version of what you just learned. In the previous section, we looked at how to classify whole documents. This meant that each document belonged to a class, and we wanted to predict what that class was. This section is different, but it extends what we previously did. In particular, this section covers how to create models that make predictions on a per token basis.

This is a type of many-to-many task, since the input is a sequence of many tokens and the output is a sequence of many labels. So instead of predicting just one label, we will be predicting a label for every token in the input. Luckily, we've already seen one example of how this works and how it can be applied. Specifically, we looked at the named entity recognition task where a goal was to find all the named entities in a document. They could be either people, places, companies or miscellaneous entities.

Otherwise, they were assigned to a class of no entity. Another example of this kind of task is parts of speech tagging. For this task, we would like to return whether each word in our document is a noun, verb, adjective and so forth. Thanks to my rule, all data is the same. These two tasks are effectively equivalent. Mechanically speaking. Again, we'll continue using the Hugging Face library, which makes fine tuning convenient now because we're using a specific library, we should get familiar with their terminology.

Specifically, they call this type of task a token classification task. This makes sense because we're assigning each token to its own class, and so we need to make a prediction for every token in the input. Hence it is a token classification task. Okay. So now that you know what this section is all about, let's look at an outline for the remaining lectures. We'll begin by looking at how to load in and process the data, because this is now a many-to-many task.

Our data will be structured a bit differently. We will no longer have a single class as the target, but a whole sequence of classes. And there's another challenging aspect to this. We know that transformers tokenize documents into sub words, but for tasks like NER and parts of speech tagging. The targets are provided on a per word basis. That is to say, after tokenizing the document into sub words, the input tokens and per word targets will no longer be aligned.

The number of targets will always be less than or equal to the number of tokens, whereas we would like to have them be exactly equal. We'll need to fix this somehow. In addition, the data set will be using has already split the document into words. Previously we saw how to use the tokenizer to split a single string into tokens, but we'll need to figure out how to tokenize a document which has already been partially tokenize. So to summarize this, well, first need to figure out what the data is even going to look like.

Since now we have a sequence of targets instead of just a single target, we need to figure out how to properly tokenize the data since the data is already split into words. And finally, we'll need to figure out what to do with the targets. Since after tokenizing the data into sub words, the targets will no longer be aligned with the input tokens. Now there is one more data processing step that we need to learn about, which is the data collator. Although we could have looked at this in the previous section, we didn't because it didn't make a difference in the results.

In fact, it was already being applied internally. In this section, however, it will be required, as you recall, when we pad our data. We want to pad it on a per batch basis. We don't want to pad everything to be as long as the longest document, which would be a waste of space and time. The data collator is what allows us to do that. Once we've discussed that, we'll talk about metrics since will now have a whole sequence of targets and predictions. The way we compute metrics will be different.

Finally, we'll look at the model in the trainer, which, unlike the previous steps, will be relatively simple and will pretty much follow what you learned in the last section. So most of the complicated stuff in the section has to do with the fact that the targets are now sequences and are on a per word basis instead of a per token basis. Now it's worth thinking about where we are in this course from a bird's eye point of view. One question worth considering is Why do we even need this section?

Why couldn't we just apply what we learned in the previous section to a new kind of task? For instance, when we learn about how to build RNNs with TensorFlow or PyTorch, we can just as easily classify a whole document with a single label or classify each token separately. The amount of new work you need to do is not that significant. But for what we are doing now, we have to keep in mind that we don't really know how to build a transformer in TensorFlow or PyTorch just yet.

We are still relying on a library to do the work for us. This library defines specific APIs and classes for each kind of task so we can no longer use auto model for sequence classification because we are no longer trying to classify a whole sequence. But this is what happens when you use a library to do the work. Every time you want to change something or do something new, you can't really figure it out on a level of first principles. If I'm writing a neural network in TensorFlow, I can do anything I want because I know how to build things on a fundamental level.

But if you're using a library like Hugging Face, you cannot do anything you want because you do not have access to the building blocks. It's like going to a restaurant. You can look at the menu and order different items, but you can't give the chef your own recipe. And what's worse is that this is a very complicated menu, so complicated that it's pretty much required for someone else to teach you how to use it. Also suboptimal is that this isn't fundamental, deep learning knowledge per se.

It's more of an exercise in reading documentation and gluing stuff together. At the same time, this is actually very useful because it makes Transformers more easily accessible to everyone. As always, there are pros and cons to both sides.

2. Data & Tokenizer (Code Preparation)
======================================

So in this lecture, our main goal will be to discuss what our data looks like and how we will process it. We'll begin by discussing what our data will look like on a conceptual level. As you know, we'll be looking at the named entity recognition task. So our data will be in the form of input sentences and target tags. Here's an example. Our input sentence is Bill Gates was the CEO of Microsoft in Seattle, Washington. Our target tags are beeper hyper 000.

Be org oh block and b look. Note that in this instance, we are treating Seattle in Washington as two distinct place entities. Although whether or not you really want to do that would be up to you and you would label your data set how you like. So our dataset will be a whole bunch of these sentence and tag sequence pairs. Now, if you need a review of how NER labels work, like what do the B, the I and the O mean, please check out the beginner's corner section of this course.

So now that you understand how the data will look on a conceptual level, let's look at how we'll be working with our data set in the coming notebook. For this example, we'll start again by using a built in data set, which happens to be the same one as we saw before CoNLL-2003. Later in this section, we'll see how you can use your own custom data set as well. As usual, we can load up this data set by importing the data sets package and calling the load_dataset function and passing in the string conll2003.

This will give us back a data set dict object which will contain at train validation and test data sets as before. You can think of this like a tabular dataset with a number of rows are the number of samples and the number of columns are various attributes. For this data set, there are actually three possible tasks you can do corresponding to the different columns. The column for the input sentence is called tokens, which is a bit confusing since these are actually full words and not the separate tokens we'll be seeing later.

But for some reason, the person who uploaded this dataset to the Hugging Face library decided to call them tokens. The Target column that will be relevant for our next example is the one called ner_tags. Since this contains the tags for the NER task, you can also use this dataset for parts of speech tagging for which you would use the column called pos_tags. There's also a column called chunk_tags where you try to predict which words should be chunked together as part of the same entity.

For example, Bill Gates would be a single chunk because the two words together corresponded to a person. But note that when we do NER, we are also effectively doing chunking because the tags will tell us whether we are beginning a chunk, continuing a previous chunk, or we are outside a chunk. Now, one thing to notice at this point is how the data set object is quite flexible. Although I said you can think of it like a table. There's a wide variety of different types of values that can be stored.

In the previous section, when we wanted to classify documents, the input column was a string, while the target column was an integer. But in this section it's different. As you'll see, the inputs are already tokenized into words, and as such they will not be strings, but each element will be a list of strings. Similarly, for the targets, we won't just have an integer, but a list of integers. So as you've seen, once we've loaded in our data, we would like to tokenize it.

The problem is that we've only seen our tokenization do its tokenization steps on documents represented as one string. With this new dataset, our document is already split into words, some of which could possibly be tokenized into sub words. Luckily, the tokenized classes are built to handle this situation. We simply need to pass in. the argument is_split_into_words equals True. When we do this, we can pass in a list of words as input and it will be tokenized as if it were a single string.

For the coming example, we'll continue using the DistilBERT checkpoints, which you can also replace with the BERT checkpoints, if you like. But at this point, you might be wondering why don't we use a different model such as GPT-2? And furthermore, how can the same BERT model be appropriate for both this task and the previous task where we classified a document into a single label? Hopefully you've been thinking about these questions. They will be answered when we finally discuss the theory behind Transformers.
