4. Target Alignment (Code Preparation)
======================================

So in this lecture, we are going to tackle one of the tricky issues with token classification, which is that after we tokenize our data into sub words, the tokens will no longer align with the targets. In fact, we've already seen how this can happen. In the previous lecture, the word lamb got split into two sub words, but in our dataset we would only be provided with one target for the whole word. This lecture is all about how we are going to fix this issue.

So essentially our strategy will be to realign and expand the targets such that they correspond with the tokenized inputs. Now, what precisely do I mean by that? Well, for any input word that is split up into multiple tokens, we are going to assign the same target to all of those tokens. That's what I mean by expand. So if the word lamb has the target five and it's split into two sub words, this simply means that the Target five will repeat twice in the positions corresponding to those sub words.

Effectively by the end of this process, the length of the sequence of targets will be equal to the length of the sequence of the input tokens. Now there is another issue we have to consider, which is that not only do some words get split into sub words, we also have two additional special tokens created, especially for BERT. Some examples are the CLS and SEP tokens which you have seen before, since these are legit tokens that will go into the model.

They will need to be accounted for in the target sequence so that the target sequence has the same length as the input sequence. If you need some intuition for why this is the case, consider how we would train a many-to-many RNN for the same task. We would have a hidden state for every input token and every hidden state would get mapped to a prediction. So we'll have as many predictions as we do input tokens. Therefore, we must have the same number of targets as well.

Again, the theory behind Transformers will come in due time, but for now just relating it back to RNNs should be sufficient. In any case, this is one of those times where the knowledge you need isn't fundamental deep learning knowledge, but rather just documentation familiarity. Specifically, the Hugging Face library uses a special integer value of -100 for outputs and targets that should be ignored. So for CLS and SEP, the corresponding targets should be -100.

Now again, if you want to relate this back to the RNNs you've implemented in the past, that would be a good idea if you were to implement this from scratch. What you would want to do is effectively build a custom loss function where the loss for the special tokens is a constant such as zero. By doing this, the gradient will also be zero and hence those outputs would not contribute to any weight updates. Okay. So now the big question is how do we actually accomplish what I just described?

Unfortunately for some of you, this is where you are going to have to employ your coding abilities. Specifically, we're going to have to write our own algorithm to do this conversion to realign the targets. Now, the typical beginner might ask, But isn't there a library for this? The answer is that we are already using a library. It's called the Hugging Face. But as you know, this is still a new library. So for some tasks, you still do have to write code yourself.

This will be the case for the coming sections as well. At the same time, this is an excellent exercise. So after watching this lecture, I would strongly encourage you to implement what you've seen by yourself. Try to do this before watching the next lecture and before going to the official notebook. Although I am going to show you the code in the next few slides. Okay. So the key piece of data we need in order to do this conversion is the word IDs list, which comes from the output of the tokenizer object.

As you recall, the tokenizer output is a BatchEncoding object which has instance methods. Basically, the format of this list is as follows. This list has the same length as the input tokens, so that includes the CLS and SEP tokens along with all the sub words. if any words were split. For CLS and SEP, the word ID is None. Otherwise, the word ID starts from zero and counts up incrementally by one. So the first word in the original input has ID zero, the second word has ID one and so forth.

The important part is that if one of the original words got split up, that word ID will repeat. So consider this made up example. This lamb is little. Since we have four words, the word IDs will be zero, one, two and three. Now again, this is a made up example, but suppose that lamb is split up into two tokens and little is split up into three tokens. Just for the sake of this lecture. In this case, the word IDs from our tokenizer will be None for CLS, zero for this, one, one for lamb,

two for is and three, three, three for little and one final None for SEP. So hopefully this is sufficient to understand how this works. The next step is to think about a sort of outline of our algorithm. The basic idea is this We're going to loop through our word IDs. From here, we have three scenarios. If we see that the word ID is none, then we should add -100 to our target list. If we see an integer word ID, then there are two scenarios. The first scenario is that this is a new word, in which case we should grab its corresponding label.

The second scenario is that this is not a new word, and this token belongs to a sub word that we've already seen part of. In that case, we should repeat the previous label. So that's kind of a high level overview of what we want to do. However, note that because of how NER tags work, there is a bit of a complication. As you recall, the tags use IOB format where B is for the first token of an entity. While I means we are inside an entity. In other words, not the first token.

So contrary to what I said previously, we cannot simply repeat the same label twice. For instance, again, suppose for the sake of this lecture that the name Steve gets split into two tokens [unclear]. The Steve word would have the label B-PER because it's the beginning of a person tag. But when we split this into two tokens, we do not want to copy B-PER twice. Instead, the second instance should become I-PER. So at this point, you have all the information you need to understand our token alignment method.

In this lecture, I'm going to show you a full code. But as mentioned, after you've seen this, you are strongly encouraged to go and implement this yourself without looking. So we begin by defining a function called align_targets, which takes as input two lists, the labels and the word IDs. Keep in mind the length of these two lists, the word IDs have the same length as the tokenized input, but the labels are just the original labels, so they have the same length as the original input.

In other words, if there are any subwords present, it will be shorter. Our goal is to create a new list called Aligned Labels, which will be the same length as the tokenized inputs. We'll initialize that to be an empty list. We'll also initialize a variable called last_word, which we will set to none. The next step is to loop through each of the word IDs. Inside this loop, we just have to check the three scenarios we outlined before. First, we check whether or not the word ID is none.

If it is, then we know it's a special BERT token, so the label should be -100. The next thing we check is whether or not the current word ID is equal to the previous word. If that is not the case, then this is a new word, at which point we can just index our labels by the word ID. So why does that make sense? Well, remember that the word IDs are just zero, one, two and so forth. Therefore, they also serve as indices into the labels list. So if I index the labels at the index word ID, that will be the correct label for this word.

The else block covers the third scenario, which is the tricky one. We'll begin by assuming that the label should be the same as the labels list, indexed by the word ID, which is the same as the previous label. However, we should have some way of checking this label. If it's a label that starts with a B. We'll need to replace it with the corresponding label that starts with an I. For instance, B-PER becomes I-PER and B-ORG becomes I-ORG. At the same time, This is more complicated than simply checking whether the label starts with a B, because as you recall, the labels are stored as integers and not strings.

The details of this will be left until the actual notebook, but please try to figure it out yourself before we move on. Finally, once we are done checking all three scenarios and finding the correct label, we can append it to our list of aligned labels. We should also update our last word variable to be the current word. Once we exit the loop, we can return our aligned labels as they will have been populated.

5. Create Tokenized Dataset (Code Preparation)
==============================================

Okay. So after we've written our method for aligning targets, there is still more work to be done. In particular, recall that in the previous section, after creating the tokenizer, we wrapped it in another function to add the truncation step. From there we called the map function on our data sets to apply the tokenizer to every sample in batches. However, we weren't able to do that step previously because our targets were not yet aligned with the inputs.

Now we have a function that can do this, so we are ready to move on to this step. As you recall, our data set objects for BERT should have several columns. Specifically, they are input IDs, attention mask, token type IDs and labels. Token type IDs will be present for BERT, but not the DistilBERT. But you don't have to worry about that since the tokenizer will populate those fields for us. We've seen that the data set objects we loaded in currently have the columns, tokens, POS tags, NER tags and chunk tags.

So basically we need to write a tokenize function that takes these input columns and gives us back the columns we need. So again, I'm going to show you some code, but ideally you should try to recreate this yourself before moving on to the notebook. We'll begin by defining a new tokenize function that takes in a batch of data as input. Inside the function, we can immediately call our tokenizer on the tokens column of the batch. These are the inputs. We add truncation equals true as usual, and we also add is_split_into_words equals true.

Since our data is split into words, this will give us back tokenized inputs. In the previous section, this was all that we needed. In this section, we need to do more because we still need to populate the labels. We'll begin by grabbing the tags, which are the original targets. We know that we can't use this because they are not aligned with the tokenized inputs. We'll create a new empty list where we will store the aligned targets. The next step is to loop through each label sequence in the batch.

We use enumerate because this will give us the index which we will use inside the loop. Speaking of which, the next step is to grab the word IDs for the sample in the batch. Luckily, the word IDs method accepts an index as inputs. Once we have a word IDs, we can call our align_targets function, which we looked at in the previous lecture. This will give us back our aligned labels, which we can add to our list. Once that loop is over, we'll have all of our aligned label sequences and we can add that to a column called labels in the tokenized inputs. When we call the map method later,

This will add all of these new columns to the dataset object. So as you know, the next step is to pass our tokenize function into the map function of the data set dict, and this will apply that function to each sample. One extra detail which is useful to know is how to remove irrelevant columns. As you recall, the tokenize function will add new columns that we need for the model such as input IDs, attention mask labels and so forth. But we don't need the original columns like tokens and NER tags and POS tags.

In this case, there is an extra argument called remove columns that we can use. We can pass in the column names of the existing dataset objects. Since we don't make use of any of the original columns.

6. Target Alignment (Code)
==========================

So in this lecture we will be looking at the code to do label alignment, which is a continuation of the same notebook we previously saw. We'll begin by reviewing our sample sentence. It states EU rejects German call to boycott British lamb. Importantly, the word lamb has been split up into two sub words. Our goal is to expand the targets so that the same target will apply to both sub words. The next step is to check out the word IDs for a sentence. So as you can see, the second last word ID, which is seven repeats twice as expected.

Note that eight corresponds to the period. So the next step is to implement our align_targets method. One detail we didn't look at in the theory lecture is how we will map begin tags to inside tags for words that get split up. To do this, we need to know the corresponding IDs. In the comment, I've pasted the label names from above which will help us understand how to map the IDs. As you can see, the B version always precedes the AI version. So we have B-PER then I-PER.

We have B-ORG and then we have I-ORG. And as you know, the indices of these label names correspond to the label IDs. In other words, label one should map to label two, label three should map to label four and so forth. This takes care of all four types of entities. So the next step is to look at our align_targets method. As input, we'll take in a sequence of labels and a sequence of word IDs. Inside the function, we'll begin by initializing the aligned labels to be an empty list.

We'll also set last_word to None, although note that this technically is the last word ID, not the actual word. The next step is to loop through all the word IDs inside the loop. We first check if the word IDis none. Recall that this corresponds to special tokens like CLS. If this is the case, then our label should be -100. The next case we check is if the current word is not equal to the last word. That means we are looking at a new word. Since we are looking at a new word.

We can be sure that B tags don't have to be converted into I tags. So we can just take the current label as is. Now you might wonder why is the label equal to the labels list indexed at the word ID? Well, recall that the word IDs are sequential, starting from zero, then one, then two, and so forth. So clearly these are indices into the labels as well. For instance, the label for the zeroth word is just the labels list at position zero. The label at position one corresponds to the word with word ID one.

So hopefully that makes sense. The else block handles the third case where our word ID is the same as the previous word ID. This means that we are going through the subword IDs corresponding to the same word and this is the second or later sub word. In this case, we'll start by assuming that the label is just the label for the given word ID. The next step is to check whether or not this is the actual correct label. We'll know if it's not if it corresponds to any of the labels that start with B.

Since the current sub word is not the beginning of a word, this corresponds to any of the label IDs which are keys in the dictionary we called begin2inside, which we just defined. So first we check whether or not this label ID is in this dictionary and if it is, we grab the corresponding value, which is the I tag. So after we've checked all of the three cases, we have our label, which can be appended to our list of aligned labels. The final step in the loop is to update the last word ID to be the current word ID.

Lastly, we return the list of aligned labels. The next step is to test our new function on the sentence from above. We begin by getting the labels, which are the NER tags. We then grab the word IDs and then call our function to get the aligned targets. So as you can see, this returns a list which starts and ends with -100. But it's kind of difficult to tell whether or not this is correct, just based on integers. So in the next step will map our target list back into their corresponding label names and print them alongside the original tokens.

So as you can see, the labels appear to be correct. Our aligned labels list is equal in length to the sequence of tokens. And the same target applies to both sub words of the word lamb. Now. Unfortunately, LAMB is not the best word to test on our function because it's just an O tag, which is not that interesting. Instead, it would be better if we could test a tag that's an actual entity because we want to see whether or not B tags get correctly transformed into I tags.

So to that end, I'm going to create a fake sentence with fake word IDs. We know that German should not be split into subwords, but for the sake of this example, we are pretending that it is. I've also put in the word Microsoft so that we have an org label as well. Note the word IDs, the word id zero repeats twice because that corresponds to German, while the word ID four repeats twice because that corresponds to Microsoft. The labels are 7, 0, 0, 0, 3, where seven represents B-MISC and three represents B-ORG.

After that, we run the same code as above. So as you can see, our function works as expected. The second token for German gets converted into I-MISC and the second token from Microsoft gets converted into I-ORG. So the next step in our notebook is to write our tokenize function wrapper, which does a bit more than it did in the previous section of the course. In particular, it not only tokenizes each input sentence in the batch, it also aligns the labels as before.

The input into this function is a batch of samples from a data set. We begin by calling the tokenizer with truncation set to true as before. We also set is_split_into_words equals true since our data is already split into words. This gives us the input IDs, attention mask and so forth. The next step is to align the targets for every sample in the batch. So we begin by grabbing the labels for the whole batch which are stored in the column ner_tags. We then create an empty list where we will store our aligned labels.

The next step is to loop through each label in our batch of labels. Note that we also use enumerate since we will be using the index of the sample. The next step is to call the word_ids method passing in the index i to get the corresponding word IDs for the current sample. The next step is to call the align_targets function, which we wrote above passing in the current labels and the current word IDs. We append the result to our list of aligned labels.

When the loop is complete. We assign the list of aligned labels to a column called labels. As you recall, for this library, the target must be stored with this name. The final step is to return our tokenized input object, which contains both the input IDs, attention mask and so forth, but also the aligned labels. So the next step is to remember that our dataset has a whole bunch of columns which we don't need when we call the map method. Later, the new columns will be added to the existing ones.

In this block of code. We're just going to print out the existing column names. So as you can see, we don't need any of these columns to train or use our model. Our model will be using the input, IDs, attention, mask and labels instead. The next step is to call the map method passing in our tokenize function from above. As before we set batched equal to true so that the operation is more efficient. In addition, we are going to remove the existing columns since they are no longer needed.

So the next step is to just print out the result. So as you can see, we now have a new dataset dict object, but the columns in the data sets are what we need for the model, specifically the input IDs, attention mask and the labels.

7. Data Collator (Code Preparation)
===================================

So in this lecture we will be discussing the concept of the data collator, which we avoided discussing earlier in the course. To understand the role of the data collator Consider our lesson on text preprocessing. As you recall, there were several required steps before we could pass our tokenized input into the model. Specifically, we had to pad the tokens, truncate the tokens, and finally convert them into PyTorch tensors. But you've seen that when we use our tokenize function, we don't do most of these steps.

This begs the question when do these other steps happen? Furthermore, I mentioned earlier that when we pad our tokens, we don't want to pad everything to be the same size relative to the whole data set, since the longest sequence may be much longer than everything else. As such, we would rather do padding on a per batch basis. The answer is that all of this is done by the data collator. So instead of the tokenizer doing the padding and conversion into torch tensors,

These are now done by the data collator. Furthermore, these are done only on the current batch rather than on the whole data set at once. In reality, the data collator has been working behind the scenes this entire time in the previous section. For some reason, the data collator is built into the trainer and works for sequence classification tasks. You can check this yourself to confirm that this is true. If you go back to the notebook from the previous section, you will see that the trainer object has an attribute called data collator.

If you print out a trainer data collator, you will see that this is an object of type DataCollatorWithPadding. This is precisely the data collator we would need in the previous section if we wanted to define one ourselves, but that wouldn't have been useful since it would be the same as what we already got automatically. If you want to try this yourself, here's the code that you would use. First you would import the class DataCollatorWithPadding from transformers.

Then you would create a data collator object passing in the tokenizer we created. Finally, when you create your trainer object, you would pass in the data collator explicitly. You will see that this has no effect and the result will be the same as what we already got. And it will also take the same time to train. Since as I stated, this data collator is already instantiated behind the scenes automatically. On the other hand, for this section, the default data collator does not work for our task and we need to define one manually.

In my opinion, this is just a weakness of the API, since the trainer object should be able to infer what type of task we are doing based on the model and create the correct type of data collator based on the task. Unfortunately, at this time this is not the case and so we still need to define our own data collator objects. Specifically in this section, we'll create an object of type DataCollatorForTokenClassification. But although the object type is different, the steps are exactly the same.

We create an object by passing in our tokenizer. Then later, when we create our trainer, we will pass in this data collator object along with the tokenizer, which is kind of redundant.

8. Data Collator (Code)
=======================

So in this very short lecture, we are going to continue our notebook and look at the steps to create and use the data collator. We'll begin by importing the class DataCollatorForTokenClassification. We'll also create an object passing in the tokenizer that we created above. Now, although we will not normally have to use the data collator manually, it might be nice to see how it works. As you recall, our data set object is quite flexible in the way it can be indexed.

One method, if we want to grab multiple samples at once, is to use a range index. So for instance, if I want to grab at the first two samples, I can index it using zero colon two. As you can see, this returns a dictionary of lists of lists. This is opposed to a list of dictionaries where each dictionary is a separate sample. That should remind you of the JSON we created earlier. Instead, we just get one dictionary which holds all the samples. Unfortunately for some reason, this is not a format that the data collator can work with.

Instead, what we need to do is grab a dictionary for each sample separately and store them in a list. So you can see here that I'm looping through the integers from zero up to two, and for each integer I'm grabbing that sample from our tokenized data sets. As you can see, this returns a different format, which is a list containing dictionaries, and each dictionary is a separate individual sample. The next step is to pass in what we had above into our data collator.

We'll print out the labels to sample what we got back. Although you're encouraged to check out the other attributes as well. So as you can see, we get back a PyTorch tensor. That's one thing to notice. Another thing to notice is that these labels have been expanded. As we described earlier in the section. We have -100 for all the tokens where we don't care about the output. For instance, the CLS and SEP tokens. We can also see that -100 applies to the pad tokens as well.

Since our second sentence is much longer than the first. This makes sense because we don't want any pad tokens to contribute to the loss. If you've taken courses with me before where I taught you how to build RNNs for many-to-many tasks, you've seen that if you do include pad tokens in the loss, this will lead to poor performance. Your model will just end up predicting pad token all the time, which makes the accuracy look good, but it will perform poorly on the labels you actually care about.

9. Metrics (Code Preparation)
=============================

So in this lecture we will look at how to compute metrics for token classification tasks. The main issue, which is the reason why we need to discuss this, is because computing metrics isn't as straightforward as it is when you only have a single target per sample. For instance, consider accuracy. When we compute accuracy, we count up the number of samples whose target we guessed correctly and divide that by the total number of samples. But what if there are multiple targets per sample as there are now?

One easy solution is to simply flatten all the predictions and targets into a single list and compute metrics like you normally would. Now, although you could do such a computation yourself, the standard way in the Hugging Face library is to use another library called seqeval. This is a library whose sole purpose is to compute metrics for NLP tasks where your targets are sequences. The reason we want to use this is because there are a few more details we haven't considered but that are built into this package.

So from a practical standpoint, we don't have to worry about these details and we can just use a standard library for computing metrics. Using this library is very easy. First, we need to install the seqeval package by using pip install as usual. Once we've done that, we can pass in the string seqeval when we call load metric. Once we have a metric object, we can call metric dot compute as before. But one thing that's important to know is that the inputs to compute metric are very specific.

For instance, if I want to test this metric by passing in some dummy data like 000 for the predictions and 001 for the targets, this will not work. There are two requirements that need to be met. Firstly, the targets and predictions should be sequences of sequences like lists of lists. This would make sense because normally we want to compute the metrics for multiple samples at once. So we would be passing in a sequence of targets. And each of those targets is itself a sequence.

For instance, a sequence of NER tags. That brings us to our second point, which is that the actual targets and predictions themselves must consist of valid tags represented as strings, like what you would see in the CoNLL-2003 data set. This is because this library was built with the CoNLL data set in mind. And how it works is based on the rules of the IOB format. In other words, if you try to pass in random symbols like ABCD, this would not work because it doesn't follow the IOB format.

Finally, note that this metric will return typical NLP metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall and F1 score. Note that per label metrics are also reported, so if your input contain the tag PER, you'll get the F1 precision and recall just for this tag alone. If your input contains the tag ORG, you'll get another item for this tag as well. You will not get any individual per label metrics for tags which did not appear in the input. Now you recall that once we have a metric object, we can't just pass it into the trainer directly.

Instead, we need to write a function that accepts as input, a tuple of logits and labels, and returns a dictionary containing the metrics of interest. To that end, we're now going to discuss what such a function will look like. In this section, it's important to realize that the shapes of the logits and labels will not be the same as the previous section. In the previous section, the labels were a one dimensional sequence of integers, while the logits were a two dimensional array of logits.

That is the value before applying the softmax. So for instance, the labels would be an N length vector and the logits would be an N by K matrix. After taking the argmax of the logits, we would get a 1D sequence of predicted labels. In this section, we add a dimension to both the logits and the labels in particular. Suppose all sequences in the batch have length t, then the labels would be a 2D array of size n by t, and each elements would contain an integer from zero up to k minus one.

Except if it corresponds to items we want to ignore, in which case it would be -100. The logits will now be a 3D array of size N by T by K. After taking the argmax in the last dimension, we'll get back an N by T array of predicted labels, which will again be integers from zero up to k minus one. Note that from the logits alone we do not know which predictions to ignore. We need to see where the corresponding true label is. -100. To that end, let's look at the code which will convert our labels and predictions into the required format.

Remember that our goal is to get a list of lists of true labels and a list of lists of predicted labels. Note that at this point we want to remove padding and other special tokens because we don't want to include them when we compute metrics. So our list of lists will become a jagged data structure, which is okay. So the first line of code here essentially does two things. Firstly, it uses a double list comprehension to loop through each sample and each label within the sequence of labels for each sample.

During this process, it filters out any labels which are set to -100. The second thing it does is it maps the integer label back to the corresponding string label. As you recall, we save this in a variable earlier in the notebook. So this line of code will give us back a jagged list of string labels. The second line of code does something similar, but for the predictions instead of the targets in this loop, we are again using a double list comprehension.

But this time we need to loop through the predictions and the true labels in corresponding order. As you recall, we need to do this because we need to know when the target is -100 because those predictions need to be removed. Again, we map these back to string label names. The final step in our function is to call metric compute passing in the string labels and string predictions we just found. From here, we format the results into a new dictionary which gives the metrics better names and also removes the per label metrics which we don't need.

10. Metrics (Code)
==================

So in this lecture, we will be looking at how to evaluate our model, which is different from the previous notebooks, since our targets are now sequences of class labels. As mentioned, we'll need to first install the library seqeval. The next step is to import the load metric function and then call it passing in the string seqeval. The next step is to test the metric by calling metric.compute. We'll begin by simply trying to pass in a single sequence of label IDs.

Okay. So as expected, it fails. As you recall, this metric assumes that we are working with batches of sequences and therefore we cannot just pass in a single list. We should instead pass in a list of lists. The next step is to try metric compute again, but this time passing in a list of lists. Note that the values are the same as what we tried above. Okay. So this time it seems we get a bunch of warnings. Basically, they're telling us that the labels we passed in are not proper tags.

As you recall, this is because this seqeval library was built specifically for CoNLL tasks. As such, they won't work with just any set of labels. Moreover, the labels must be represented as strings and not integers. However, do note that there is a result that gets printed out. The next step is to test metric.compute again, but this time we're going to pass in a sequence of string labels instead of integer labels. Okay. So as you can see, this again gives us the same warning, because even with string labels, the library knows that they are not the proper strings corresponding to tags in the CoNLL

data sets. The next step is to test metric.compute one last time, but this time we're going to pass in real labels from the set of any tags. Okay. So as expected, it works. But note that in the results we not only get overall metrics for accuracy, F1, precision and recall, but we also get metrics on a per entity basis. So we see precision recall in F1 for both ORG and MISC, but we do not have any metric for the O tag since those are not entities.

Now one interesting thing to consider is the precision and recall for the org tag. The precision, recall and F1 are zero, even though out of the two org tags we do get one of them correct. This may seem odd, but remember that this library is doing special computations specifically based on the IOB format. The next step is to write our compute metrics function. We'll begin by importing numpy since will be using it below. The next step is to define the compute metrics function as we normally do, which takes in a tuple of logits and labels as input inside the function.

We first split up the logits and labels. We then apply the argmax on the logits to get the predicted labels. Note that at this point, both the labels and the predictions are arrays of shape n by t where n is the batch size and t is the sequence length. The next step is to remove the -100 values from the labels, since we don't want them to factor into our metrics. Furthermore, they don't have any corresponding label names, which is another thing we do in this step.

So all the labels will be converted into their string representation. The next step is to do the same thing for the predictions. Note that for the predictions we need to use the zip function so that we can loop through the predictions and targets in corresponding order. This is because the targets will be -100. For any predictions we want to ignore. Finally we call metric compute, which will return a dictionary of metrics. We then return the overall metrics in the new dictionary while also giving more concise names.

So at this point we've done all the groundwork to load in our data, set, tokenize the inputs, realign the targets and compute metrics. In the next few lectures, we'll create our model and then pass everything into the trainer where we can then fine tune the model on our data set.

13. POS Tagging & Custom Datasets (Exercise Prompt)
===================================================

So in this lecture, we are going to discuss a practical question, which will also become your next exercise for this course. In particular, the question is how can we do token classification with a custom data set? For the purpose of this lecture, we'll consider the task of parts of speech tagging, which is similar to NER, but simpler since it does not require the IOB format. The main issue is this in the previous section where we looked at predicting a single label for a whole document we used CSVs when we wanted to provide our own custom dataset.

But let's consider how our data was formatted for NER. In this case, our input document wasn't just a single string, and our target wasn't just a single label. In theory, we could find some way to encode both of these strings and then put them into a CSV, but that doesn't seem practical. In fact, for this type of task, the Hugging Face documentation, as far as I know, does not address this issue. However, there is a solution which is to use a different format other than CSV.

Specifically, JSON Lines. In particular, we'll represent our data in a text file. Each line is a separate JSON. Each JSON object represents a single sample. This is pretty flexible and it works for our use case. Since as you may know, JSON keys must be strings, but the values can be many things such as integers, strings, booleans, lists of any of the above and even another JSON objects. In our case, the format will be pretty intuitive. Each JSON will have two keys, inputs and targets.

The inputs will be a list of words, and the targets will be the corresponding list of tags. Once you have a JSON file in the format I just described, it's very easy to load into Hugging Face as we saw before. We'll continue to use the load_dataset function, except the first argument will now be JSON instead of CSV. We continue to pass in the data files using the same format as we saw before. So the next exercise, at least conceptually, is very simple.

You're going to load in the Brown Corpus, which I'll show you how to do shortly. Then you're going to convert this dataset into a JSON file like I just described. Then you're going to take our previous NER notebook and modify it such that it will work for our new data sets. So hopefully you agree that your job is pretty clear cut. Now let's discuss some useful details. Firstly, how do you get the data set? The data set will be using comes from NLTK and it's called the Brown Corpus.

You want to import NLTK as well as the brown module from nltk.corpus. You'll need to download a few files using NLTK. Specifically the Brown Corpus itself, as well as the tag set for this task. Finally you can obtain the data set by calling brown.tagged_sents passing in the argument tagset equals universal. This will give you a list of data in pretty much the same format as CoNLL. Specifically, each element of this big list is another list. Each of these inner lists represents a sentence.

Each of these sentences is represented as a list of tuples in each tuple. We have two items the word and the corresponding POS tag. Of course, there will be some processing you need to do in order to convert it into a JSON like we just discussed. That is part of your exercise. Here are some other tips to consider. Firstly, since the data set isn't split into train and test, you should do that yourself. There's an example of that in the previous section of the course.

Secondly, you'll recall that in the NER data set, the targets were already represented as integers when we loaded in the data. But since this dataset isn't built into Hugging Face, that won't be the case. And this example, the targets will be the actual POS tags represented as strings. So you'll have to modify the code as appropriate. The third tip to consider is that because there is no IOB format. You can delete any code related to this. This also makes the task much more general as it can represent any token classification task.

Whereas IOB is quite specific. Fourth tip because the targets are not in IOB format, we cannot use the seqeval metric which requires it. Instead, a simple solution is to take the jagged arrays of targets and predictions and flatten them into single lists. Once you have those lists, compute the accuracy and F1 as you normally would. Make sure to test your compute metrics function to ensure it behaves as you expect.

14. POS Tagging & Custom Datasets (Solution)
============================================

So in this lecture we will be looking at the solution to the previous exercise, which was to implement parts of speech tagging using a custom dataset. We'll begin by installing transformers and data sets, as we normally do. The next step is to import NLTK as well as the Brown module. The next step is to download the necessary data we need for this data set. The next step is to load in our corpus using brown.tagged_sents, specifying that we want to use the universal tagset.

Okay. So note that our dataset is currently in the form of a list of lists of tuples. The outside list contains a bunch of sentences. Each sentence is represented as a list of tuples, and each tuple contains a word with a corresponding tag. Importantly, we noticed that the data set is not in the form. We need to represent it as a Hugging Face dataset. To take a step towards that, we are going to begin by separating the inputs and targets. So the inputs will become a list of lists of words and the target will become a list of lists of tags.

We'll begin by initializing an empty list to store the inputs and targets. Next, we enter a loop through the corpus. Each element of this list is called sentence tag pairs for obvious reasons. Inside the loop we initialize an empty list called tokens and an empty list called targets. These are where we will store the words and tags respectively. The next step is to loop through the sentence itself. Since each element is a tuple containing the word in the tag, we can say for token comma tag in a sentence tag pairs.

Inside the inner loop, we append the token in the tag to their respective lists. Note that there is a more efficient way to do this, but this is, in my opinion, the more intuitive. Once we've completed the inner loop, we then append the list of words and list of targets to our list of inputs and targets. So the next step is to save our data into JSON formats so that we can load it in as a Hugging Face dataset in the next step. In the real world, you would write your own code to build this JSON.

As you can see, we simply loop through the inputs and targets in corresponding order. And for each of these pairs we create a dictionary called J. We then convert J into a JSON string and then we write it out to a file called Data dot JSON. is none. The label should be -100, and when the word ID is an integer, we should grab the label for that word.
