15. Token Classification Section Summary
========================================

So in this lecture, we will be summarizing everything we learned in this section. In this section, we extended what we learned in the previous section by learning how to not just predict a single label for a whole document, but to predict a label for every token in the document. As you saw, this has several important applications in NLP, including NER, parts of speech tagging and chunking. This section builds off what we learned in the previous section.

So many of the steps remain the same. We learned how to load up a Hugging Face dataset, how to apply tokenized to the dataset, how to download and fine tune a pre-trained model, and how to save it and reload it as a convenient pipeline object. This section added a few new steps, including the data collator and target realignment. We noted that, theoretically speaking, some of these things could have been done automatically by the library, but currently that is not the case.

This section required us to write some of our own algorithms, which is okay since we got to learn a bit more about how things work. This will be the case in the subsequent sections of the course as well. For this section we looked at the named entity recognition task in parts of speech tagging, which was an exercise for you to complete by yourself. Now, after completing this section, you might be wondering. This section only considers tasks where each token is mapped to a tag.

It's a kind of many-to-many task, but there are other kinds of many-to-many tasks where this is not the case. For example, machine translation where the input and the target can have different numbers of words as you'll soon find out. This requires a whole new type of model, and so this will be discussed later in the course.

###

1. Translation Section Introduction
===================================

So in this lecture we will be introducing the next section of this course, which is on how to fine tune a transformer for neural machine translation. This is a continuation of the part of this course that focuses on how to fine tune transformers to improve performance. So let's talk about this section from a bird's eye point of view and why it's similar, but also different from the previous section of the course. In one sense, translation is very similar to what we just did with NER in parts of speech.

Both of these are many-to-many. The input is a sequence of many tokens, and the output is a sequence of many labels. The difference is that in the previous section, the inputs and labels were 1 to 1. That is, for every input. There was a corresponding target for translation. This is not the case. A short sentence in English might turn into a long sentence in Chinese, or vice versa. In addition, this also makes evaluating the model more difficult with NER or parts of speech.

We can just check at each position whether or not our prediction matches the target. But with translation, we cannot do this since there are many possible ways to express the same thought, all of which can be valid. As such, this section will require new tools to handle these challenges. Speaking of new tools, again, this section really amounts to learning about which are the right objects and classes to use. That is to say it's more about learning about the API as opposed to learning fundamental, deep learning knowledge, as you learned in the previous section.

Different tasks require different objects. One example of that is the model instead of using auto model for a sequence or token classification. Now we'll use AutoModelForSeq2SeqLM. Another example is the data collator. Instead of using DataCollatorWithPadding, we'll use DataCollatorForSeq2Seq. Importantly, this section even requires us to use a new trainer. So instead of the regular training arguments class, we use the class Seq2SeqTrainingArguments and instead of the Trainer class, we'll use Seq2SeqTrainer.

Of course, you can expect these to be similar to their non-seq2seq counterparts. Now, at this point, you must be wondering what is seq2seq? For those of you who have studied seq2seq RNNs with me in the past. You're in luck. If you understand seq2seq for RNNs, you pretty much already understand seq2seq for transformers. The concept is exactly the same, except that you replace the RNN unit with a self attention unit. Details of that are to be discussed, but at a high level it should make perfect sense.

So seq2seq would be a useful but not explicitly required background for this course. At this point, it's sufficient to understand that at a high level, we're going to be using a new model architecture. And what that means is we're finally moving away from pure BERTs. So let's now outline the topics in this section. We'll begin by looking at the format of our dataset as well as how to use the tokenizer on it. You should already be thinking about how this might be different since we're now working with two languages instead of just one in this section, both the inputs and the targets need to be tokenized.

The next step will be to look at how to prepare the data so that it can be passed into the model for fine tuning. Well, then look at the model itself, which will be the same as what we saw in the beginners corner. Well, simultaneously look at the data collator, which in this section has a dependency on the model. At this point, we'll also talk a little bit about how seq2seq works, since it'll help us understand the API a bit better. Our goal in this portion of the course is to discuss theory as little as possible.

So this will be just enough to understand why your model inputs look the way they do. Well, then look at metrics. Although we already discussed the BLEU score earlier in this course, we're also going to look at another interesting metric, which is the BERTScore. Finally, we'll look at how to train and evaluate our fine tune model as well as load it back in as a pipeline object.

2. Data & Tokenizer (Code Preparation)
======================================

So in this lecture we will be looking at the data set will be using in the section along with how to apply the tokenizer to it. We'll be using a dataset called KDE4, which, like the other data sets we've seen that can be loaded in automatically by using the load_dataset function. This dataset has a ton of language pairs, so if you're interested in trying this code on a different language pair, I've left a link both in the notebook and extra_reading.txt, which lists the pairs in the following notebook.

We'll be translating English to French. In this case, we want to pass in the arguments lang1 and lang2, which we set to en and fr. When you do this you'll see that we get a data set which has not yet been split into train of validation and test. The data set has over 200,000 examples. Now it turns out that 200,000 examples is way too many for a notebook to finish in a feasible amount of time. You'll notice that our model is so large that it even reaches the limits of what the free version of CoLab can handle.

So there will be a few things we need to do in order to address this issue. In terms of the data set, we're going to draw a subsample of only 1000 pairs. This is less than 0.5% of the full dataset. Doing this will result in the training process taking just under 5 minutes. So you can extrapolate the time it will take if you want to use more data. As you've seen, it's also pretty simple to create our own train test split. So as you saw, our dataset contains two columns which are translation and ID. ID is not really part of the data.

So translation is really what we want to look at. If you print out a sample, you'll see that the value stored in translation is a dictionary which contains two keys en and fr, both of which point to their respective sentences in those languages. Obviously the keys will be different depending on which language codes you choose. As a side note, please note that these are standard two letter language codes. So if you're curious about what the code is for some particular language, it should be easy to look up.

So the data is pretty simple. What we really need to know is how to process this data. One thing that should immediately come to mind is how do we tokenize the target? Clearly we cannot use an English tokenizer because that wouldn't work with languages like Chinese for example. So do we need two separate tokenizers? The answer is yes and no. Yes, because there are two separate tokenizer models internally, but no, because they are both stored in the same tokenizer object.

So the answer really is to choose an appropriate checkpoint for your data set. This is because the checkpoint is what defines what tokenizer you will get when you call AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained. Luckily, as with sequence and token classifiers, there are already checkpoints for many language pairs. In fact, it turns out that we can use the same checkpoint we used in the beginners corner. Obviously with the correct language pairs selected. Once you have your tokenizer, there are two ways to use it.

If you just use the tokenizer like we did before by calling it like a function. This will tokenize the model inputs and this will assume the language specified for the input of the check point. If you want to tokenize the targets, you need to use what is called a context manager. This should look familiar if you've ever worked with files in Python. The syntax is with tokenizer.as_target_tokenizer() followed by the usual tokenizer syntax. This ensures that the correct tokenizer will be used for the target language.

Now, just as a side note, it might be worth knowing that it's possible that the input tokenizer might work, or at least won't crash if you use the input tokenizer by mistake. This is especially true if the input and target languages are similar, like English and French or Spanish. However, the tokenized output will be suboptimal. One thing to be aware of, since we are now no longer using BERT, is that there are different special tokens, as you recall.

BERT uses special tokens like CLS and SEP, but it's more important to keep in mind that these are BERT specific. They don't exist for other models, and other models may have their own set of special tokens and conventions for the model will be using. There will be no CLS and SEP. Instead, every input sentence well ends with an end tag which looks like an HTML or XML tag. In addition, you'll recall that when BERT tokenizes words into sub words the second and later sub words start with two hash symbols.

With our new model, this won't be the case. Instead, the convention is that each new word will start with a big underscore and subsequent sub words won't have any prefix. This is not important for you in terms of a code you will write, but it's good to know so you won't be surprised by what you see.

3. Things Move Fast
===================

So this is just a short lecture to remind all of you that this field moves fast. Things will change and break. This is to be expected. Case in point, in the translation lectures, you'll see that we use a context manager called as_target_tokenizer when we tokenize the translation text. This was the case while I was making this course. Today, as I am still in the midst of making this course, this is no longer the case. Literally, one minute this is working fine.

And when I ran the same code a few minutes later, I was getting a warning telling me that this will change. So the new way of doing this is instead of using as_target_tokenizer you simply pass in your text using a special argument called text_target. In my opinion, this looks nicer since it's kind of weird that a variable you define inside the scope of a context can still be used outside of it. In any case, the following lecture is will use as_target_tokenizer, but you may need to update this when writing your own code.

And just remember, guys, when we are hiring for jobs, we don't care that you've memorized one library. We care that you have the skill to learn any version of any library. And that includes being able to handle changes like this with relatively little efforts, even by yourself. So if I see any of you freak out over this, just remember that this is not what employers want to see. This is one of the many examples I have that things in this field move super fast, and if you're not prepared to handle it well, then you simply won't do as well as those who can.

4. Data & Tokenizer (Code)
==========================

So in this lecture we'll begin looking at the notebook where we will fine tune a translation model. We'll begin by installing transformers, datasets and SentencePiece. Note that SentencePiece is required for the tokenizer to work. The next step is to load up our data set, which is a data set with English and French language pairs. So as you can see, we have over 200,000 samples. Unfortunately, this many samples is way too much for this notebook to complete in any reasonable amount of time.

As such, we'll begin by randomly selecting just 1000 samples from this dataset. We'll call the results small. Note that this is less than 0.5% of the total. Using that, you can make an educated guess about how long this would take with the full dataset. The next step is to split the data set into train and test. The next step is to print out one of the samples from our dataset. As you can see, the samples are kind of weird. The next step is to load up our tokenizer object.

As you can see, we are using the same checkpoint from the beginners corner. This might be surprising since that was not the case in the last two sections. You might be wondering what should you do if you want to train a translation model completely from scratch? Well, given the amount of time and memory that this notebook takes to train, you can imagine that doing it from scratch would require even more resources. The next step is to print out an example sentence pair which we will subsequently tokenize.

The next step is to tokenize the input sentence, which can be done with the usual syntax. So as you can see, the result looks as we expect. The next step is to tokenize the target sentence, which requires us to use the as_target_tokenizer context manager. Again, we note that the result has the expected format. Note that this also comes with the attention mask, which isn't actually required for the target. The next step is to look at the tokens for the target.

So we're going to convert the IDs back into tokens. The goal of this will be to demonstrate what will happen if you do this correctly compared to when you do this incorrectly. So as you can see, for this particular example, we happened to get one token per word. Note that the beginning of each word is denoted by the large underscore. Another thing to note is that the convention here is clearly different from BERT, which we've been using in the course up to this point.

What this teaches us is that each model has their own conventions for how to process the input. Just like computer vision. In this case, there is no special start token, but each sentence ends with a special end of sentence tag. The next step is to check out what will happen if we use the tokenizer on the target sentence incorrectly. We'll convert the IDs back into tokens to see what we would get. So as you can see, this looks a lot different from what we just saw.

There are a few important things to note. Firstly, note that this does not completely fail. If you've studied tokenizers used for deep learning before for example, in TensorFlow, this might surprise you. Usually tokenizers use some form of splitting on whitespace and punctuation and perhaps follow a set of built in rules to further split certain words. Each token is stored in a dictionary, which we call the vocabulary. The vocabulary is the set of all known tokens.

Now, usually what happens if we try to tokenize words that did not appear in the train set is they would simply be replaced by a special unknown token, or they would be ignored altogether. Clearly, that's not what happened in this case. The English tokenizer should not be able to recognise French words, but it is incomplete and tries to tokenize the French words anyway. This is another hint that tokenizers in the era of Transformers work a bit differently than what you have seen in the past.

However, although we didn't get any errors, the result is suboptimal. The English tokenizer doesn't know about French words, and so it splits them up at illogical points. As you can see, tokens which are not the start of a word, do not have any underscore prefix. This is different from the BERT tokenizer, where new words have no prefix and subsequent tokens have the double hash.

5. Aside Seq2Seq Basics (Optional)
==================================

So in this optional lecture, we are going to go off on a slight tangent and talk about how sequence to sequence models work. This will help give you a stronger footing for understanding why the API is the way it is in the notebook. Note that this section does not require any knowledge of transformers, but knowing about RNNs would be very useful. Effectively, anywhere you see an RNN block in this lecture, you can simply replace it with a transformer block.

The inputs and outputs won't change. To help you understand the structure of this lecture, let's start with an outline. We'll begin by discussing language models and how they are trained and used. This might seem kind of off topic at this point, but it's exactly what we need. The language model actually forms the decoder component of the seq2seq architecture. Once we understand the decoder, we can then discuss the encoder and how we can combine both the encoder and decoder to make a full seq2seq model.

The encoder is much simpler to understand and it's basically what we've been using already. The RNN analogy would just be a basic RNN for text classification or parts of speech. Note that these were the two types of tasks we just looked at earlier in the course. Okay. So as per our outline, let's begin by discussing language models at a high level. A language model is a model that predicts a distribution for the next word in a sentence given the previous words in a sentence.

Training an RNN to do this works just like parts of speech tagging, except with different targets. Specifically, we have a target for every input token, but our targets are really just the sentence we want to learn how to generate, and the inputs are a shifted version of that same sentence. As such, this is a many-to-many token classification task, just like NER. It makes more sense with a picture. Consider the sentence. I like cats. As you can see, the first input is just a generic start tag.

The first target is the first word in the sentence. In this case, it's I. On the second step, the input is the first word in the sentence, which is I. Now our model has to try and predict the word like. In other words, it must learn that like comes after I and the start tag. On the third step, The input is the second word in the sentence, which is like. The third target is the final word in the sentence, which is cats. As such, a model is learning how to predict the word cats given the previous token's start tag, I and like.

Finally, the fourth input might be cats and the corresponding target might be an end of sentence token. So it should be pretty intuitive that by training a model in this way, we are training it to predict the next word in a sentence. Given all the previous words, this is a language model. Now, as you recall, language models can also be used to generate text completely from scratch. That is, we don't even need to be given a whole set of previous words.

They can simply generate whole sequences of text by themselves. So how does this work? Well, you'll recall that for the inputs, they always start the same way with the special start tag. From here, the model can really generate any word that would be appropriate for the start of a sentence. It's kind of like building a GAN to generate images. You might train it on MNIST, but with a normal vanilla GAN, you can't control whether you're going to get a 0, 1, 2, 3 and so forth.

It just depends on the random samples you draw. In any case, let's get back to our RNN. Once we generate the first word, you might wonder what can we do to generate the second word? Usually the input for the second time step would be the first word in the target sentence. However, in this case, recall that we are now generating text. That means the second word should be an appropriate follow up to the first word we just generated. Suppose that word was the.

As such, our model is now giving us the distribution of p of x2 given x1 equals the. To obtain this distribution, we feed our first output back into the input of the RNN at the next time step. Now suppose that the next word we generate is cat. Again, we pass this word into the input for the third time step. Now our model is giving us the distribution of x3, given x2 equals cat and x1 equals the. Now suppose that we continue this process until we generate the sequence.

The cat sat on the mat. You might be wondering how does our model know when to stop? If we keep feeding words back into the input, we might just keep generating words forever. Of course, we also taught our model how to generate end of sentence tokens since these were part of the targets. So if our model ever generates this special token, then we know we should stop the process and not feed it back into the input. Okay. So we just discussed how language modeling works.

As mentioned, the language model forms the decoder portion of our seq2seq network. The other part is the encoder. The encoder. If we're talking about RNNs, it's really just a text classification RNN, where we simply do not compute any outputs from the hidden states. Normally, as you recall, we would take the hidden states and pass them through a final dense layer in order to get a prediction. If we're classifying a whole document into a single label, then we would typically take the final hidden state.

But if we were doing a token classification task, then we would do this for every hidden state. As an encoder, we do not use any final dense layer, but instead we simply save the final hidden state. We can think of this like an encoding of the input sentence. Hence why we call this an encoder. It encodes the input sentence into a single vector, which is like an efficient, compressed version of the sentence. Now that we have both our encoder and decoder, we can combine them to form a seq2seq network.

As you recall, every RNN unit has a prior hidden state and outputs a new hidden state. That's why we call it recurrent. It's a network that loops back into itself. In a decoder only language model, the initial hidden state would be some fixed vector, like a vector of zeros. In that scenario, the initial hidden state is kind of irrelevant. And when we generate the first word in such a language model. Recall that what we generate is just random, like an image we would generate from a GAN.

What we generate is not conditioned on anything. We simply generate something from the training sets distribution. But in a seq2seq model, we can condition on the input sentence by having the initial hidden state to the decoder come from the output. Hidden state of the encoder. As you recall, the encoder converts the input sentence into a vector. We can think of this like a concept vector or a thought vector, which lives in a vector space which you can imagine, like the space of all possible concepts or thoughts.

So now our decoder will not just generate a random sentence, but it will be conditioned on this information it gets from the encoder. Specifically, it will try to decode this concept or this thought back into a sentence in the target language. That's why this vector space of thoughts is really a vector space of thoughts. Given the fact that the same thought can be expressed in at least two different languages. The thought really is in fact, language agnostic.

It's like a numerical representation of a thought. Our decoder simply learns how to express this thought using words. However, the key point is that in a decoder only model, the sentence we generate is not conditioned on anything. So what we generate can be totally random with the encoder decoder combination. The decoder is conditioned on an input sentence, and the loss function is such that it learns to translate the input sentence into a new language.

As a final topic for this lecture, let's consider the inputs and targets for training a seq2seq network like we just described. At a high level, it's pretty simple. We just need pairs of input sentences in one language and corresponding target sentences in the target language. After we tokenize these sentences and convert the tokens into integer IDs, they can be used as inputs and targets for the network. But consider how the decoder, which is a language model, has to be trained.

The decoder for the purpose of this lecture is an RNN. It has both inputs and outputs. We know that the outputs should be mapped to the targets and the loss function should be some comparison between the two. But what should the inputs be? Remember that these inputs here are different from the inputs into the model as a whole. The actual model inputs will be the tokenized version of the input sentence that goes into the encoder. But now we have to consider what the inputs to the decoder should be.

In fact, it's very similar to training a language model with no encoder. The decoder input is simply a shifted version of the target. So at every time step the decoder receives the true past tokens. In effect, the decoder language model is learning to predict the next word in a sentence conditioned not only on the original input sentence, but also the previous words in its own sentence. But just like the decoder only language model, when we use the seq2seq model in inference mode to actually generate text, we do not have any true targets.

This is when we want to use our model to translate arbitrary text. In this scenario, the decoder will generate one token at a time, and for every token it generates we feed it back into the input of the decoder for the next time step. This is just like how we use the decoder only language model to generate text. The main difference now is the same as what we saw during training is that the decoder output is conditioned on the input sentence.

6. Model Inputs (Code Preparation)
==================================

So now that we've discussed how to tokenize the inputs and targets, we are ready to think about how to prepare the data to be input into the model for training to understand the next step. Let's relate it back to what we did earlier in the course when we wanted to classify documents, we created a tokenize function that simply called the tokenizer with truncation. As you recall, this fills in the fields such as input, IDs, attention, mask and so forth.

When we wanted to classify each token, we created a new function that not only called the tokenizer but also populated the labels field. As you recall, the labels field is where Hugging Face looks for the targets, and we had to realign the targets manually so that we could set this field properly. In this section, we have a similar situation. We need to populate fields for both the inputs and the labels. In fact, this section is a bit easier because we already know that the inputs and targets can have different sequence lengths.

As such, there is no need to take any special steps to align the targets. So here's what our tokenization function will look like. Essentially, we just need to tokenize the inputs, tokenize the targets, and assign the targets to a field called labels. So we begin our function by first splitting up the inputs and targets into separate lists. We can do this by looping through the translation field in our batch of data and grabbing the sentence corresponding to each language.

The next step is to call the tokenizer for both the inputs and targets, which you've already seen how to do. However, you can see that we've added a few new arguments. Specifically, we added truncation, which you should expect by now, and we also add a parameter to specify the maximum sequence length In the notebook, we'll choose some number that covers most of the sentences in the dataset. The final step is to take the input IDs from the tokenized targets and assign these to a field called labels in the tokenized inputs.

This is because the input IDs store the token IDs for the targets, but we want the targets to be available in a field called labels, as they were for the rest of the models we studied. So everything is stored in the objects that we're calling tokenized inputs. We then return the tokenized inputs object. As usual. The next step is to call the map function to map the tokenize function to every sample in our dataset. Again, we remove the original columns since they are no longer needed.

The next step is to instantiate our pre-trained model. Now, this might seem a bit out of order compared to the previous notebooks we studied, but there is a reason for this, which you'll see shortly. For now, all you have to know is that just like with token classification, there is a special type of model we need for this task. Specifically, we need a model called AutoModelForSeq2SeqLM. If you've studied seq2seq in NLP with me in the past, this name should make complete sense and in fact it should give you a sense of comfort because it's all stuff you've seen before.

In particular, we know that the type of model we need for translation is seq2seq. seq2seq models are made up of two components the encoder and the decoder. The decoder is a language model because it's built to generate a sentence one token at a time in an auto regressive manner. This is just like, say, generating poetry, except that instead of starting with a completely random token, we conditioned on the hidden state output from the encoder, which is a vector representation of the input sentence.

In any case, we take the usual steps of calling from_pretrained and passing in our checkpoints. Once we have our model, we can then create our data collator. Again, because this is a new task, we're going to have a new data collator, called appropriately DataCollatorForSeq2Seq. Now you might think we've done the steps out of order because we usually do all our data processing before we create the model. But the reason we created the model in the previous step was because we're actually going to pass it into the data collator constructor.

Basically, this is going to be used to populate the decoder input IDs. So let's review what the data collator needs to do. Although do note that this is optional since you can just copy the code without understanding what's happening. This is for those of you who want to have some idea of what the data collator does. Okay, so we know that both the inputs and targets will be variable length sequences of token IDs. Therefore, they will need to be padded as well as converted into torch tensors.

That much we have in common with the previous sections of the course. But there is a new step since the decoder is itself a neural network. Again, we can relate this back to RNNs. We know that the decoder is an RNN and it makes its computations based on two vectors the input vector and the output of the encoder. We can imagine the input coming from the bottom and the encoder output coming in from the side. Transformers are structured in the exact same way.

The decoder is just an auto regressive model that takes as input the previous words in a sentence and tries to predict the next word in a translation system. The decoder is also conditioned on the input sentence we are trying to translate, which gives us the encoder hidden state. As such, the decoder input IDs are really just a shifted version of the targets. Conceptually, the first input will just be a generic start token from which we try to predict the first output.

Once we have the first output, we then want to generate the second output. The second output takes as input. The true first token of the translated sentence. So we're always trying to output the next token given the previous tokens in the sentence. Just like a language model, except that it's also conditioned on the input sentence we want to translate. In fact, let's zoom into this image and explain this in a more step by step fashion. Note that this image is for a chatbot, but the concept is exactly the same.

As you recall, this is just a manifestation of my rule. All data is the same. The exact same model that can be used for translation can also be used for chatbot. In any case, the first step is to pass the input sentence into our encoder. The encoder then processes the whole input sentence and produces one final hidden state. This hidden state then becomes the initial hidden state of the decoder. As you can see, coming in from the left. But the decoder is also an RNN, meaning.

Not only does it have a previous hidden state, but it also must have some input. Well, the input during training is simply a shifted version of the targets. Recall that this is exactly how a language model works. At the beginning we simply start with a generic go tag. From this and the initial hidden state, it must learn to generate the first word, which is "it". Note that "it" is also the input into the model at the next time step. From this the model must learn to produce

the word "is" and as you can see, that is also the input at the next time step. And this pattern keeps repeating such that the decoder input IDs are simply a shifted version of the targets. And this is so that the decoder learns to predict the next word in a sentence, given the previous words.

7. Model Inputs (Code)
======================

So in this lecture, we will be continuing our notebook. We'll begin by drawing a histogram of the sequence lengths of the inputs and targets. The reason we want to do this is because we want to specify a maximum sequence length for truncation. To do this, we'll first grab the translation column of the train set. Then we'll use a list comprehension to collect the lengths of each English sentence. So as you can see, most of the samples in the train set fall in the shorter end.

We would be justified in setting the maximum input length to be somewhere between 100 and 200. The next step is to draw a similar histogram, but for the targets instead of the inputs. So these are the French language sentences. So as you can see, the sequence lengths are pretty similar to the English sentences. So again, we would be justified in setting the maximum target length to be somewhere between 100 and 200. The next step is to write our tokenize function.

We'll begin by setting the maximum sequence length for both the inputs and targets to be 128. Then we'll define our tokenize function, which takes a batch of data as input. Inside the function, we begin by splitting up the samples into input and target lists. The next step is to tokenize the inputs. This is similar to what we saw before, except that we also specify the max length. The next step is to tokenize the targets. Again, we have the same code, except we use as_target_tokenizer.

Now, as you know, the Hugging Face API expects the targets to be in a field called labels. So that is where we will place the token IDs from the targets. Note that the attention mask is not needed, although the tokenizer does produce them. Finally, we return the Tokenized Inputs object, which contains both the input fields and the targets or labels field. The next step, as usual, is to map our tokenize function to the DatasetDict, as well as remove the original columns which we no longer need.

The next step is to load up our pre-trained model. This time the correct class to use is called AutoModelForSeq2SeqLM. After learning a bit about seq2seq, the reason for this name should be clear. seq2seq is the type of model required for translation and LM means language model, which describes how the decoder works. The next step is to create our data collator. As with token classification, we need to explicitly define a data collator and in this case it also accepts a model as input.

This model is used for the purpose of generating the decoder input IDs. The next step is to test out our data collator. We'll begin by reviewing the necessary input format. As you recall, the data collator expects as input a list of samples where each sample is a dict. This is opposed to what we would get if we index the dataset with a range of indices which would give us a dict of lists. So as you can see, each sample consists of three fields which are input, IDs, attention, mask and labels as expected.

The next step is to test our data collator on the above inputs. We'll also print out the keys of the result to see if we get back anything new. Recall that in the past we simply use the data collator to pad the sequences and to convert them into torch tensors. Okay. So as you can see, the data collator seems to have done one extra step, which was to create another field called decoder input IDs. As you recall, these are needed for the training process. The next step is to print out the labels of the data collated batch.

So as you can see, they are now torch tensors. Furthermore, we continue to use -100 for the targets that should be ignored. So that hasn't changed. The next step is to look at this new field, which is called decoder input IDs. So as you can see, we get back another torch tensor. Now we know that the first sentence was the shorter one, as per the above results. We can see that the decoder input IDs have been padded with the ID 59513. But interestingly, this ID also appears at the beginning of both sentences.

So it seems to have a dual function of being both a start token and a pad token for the decoder inputs. Remember that the decoder input IDs are just a shifted version of the targets. So in order to get a better idea of what this special token is, we're going to convert the IDs back into tokens for the decoder input IDs. So as you can see, the mystery token is in fact a pad. Curiously, this is also used as the start of sentence tag. The next step is to convert the targets back into tokens as well, just to confirm that the decoder inputs really are just a shifted version of the targets.

So as you can see, the decoder inputs really are just a shifted version of the targets. Interestingly, for the targets, we use the unknown tag for any target beyond the end of sentence tag. In fact, this is just because the -100 does not appear as one of the token IDs in the vocabulary. And just for completion sake, let's just print out the sample itself to see the real input and target sentences. So as you can see, it's just KDE. As some trivia, note that KDE is the group behind the Kubuntu Linux distribution.

8. Translation Metrics (BLEU Score & BERT Score) (Code Preparation)
===================================================================

So in this lecture, we are going to discuss the metrics that will be using in our notebook. Just as a brief review, let's remind ourselves why we need different metrics for the translation task. The main point is that little differences here and there are unlikely to change the meaning. For example, the cat chased the mouse and the cat ran after the mouse. Furthermore, different sentences can mean the same thing, and as such there can be more than one valid translation of an input.

As such, the BLEU score looks at n-grams, which are subsequences of tokens. The BLEU score is then a weighted average of n-grams for different sizes of n. It also chooses the best score over each reference. The final BLEU score is a score between zero and 100, but is sometimes expressed to be between a zero and one. In the beginners corner of this course. We already looked at the BLEU score. But that was through NLTK. In this section, we'll continue to use the BLEU score, but we use a new library that claims to compute the BLEU score more consistently than other packages.

We can install this library by using the command pip install sacrebleu. We can then use the metric in the usual way. We import the function load metric and then we call this function passing in the string sacrebleu. When we call metric dot compute, the predictions must be a list of strings and the targets must be a list of lists of strings. As you recall, this is because there can be more than one target per translated sentence. In the following lecture, we'll also be using another metric, which I think is more interesting and makes use of our knowledge of deep learning.

In particular, we'll be looking at a metric called the BERTScore. The BERTScore also handles this issue of slightly different sentences, still having the same meaning. It does this by internally using a pre-trained BERT model and computing contextual embeddings for both the predicted translation and the targets. Since these embeddings are just vectors, they can be compared by using cosine similarity. If we had a sentence like I ran to the store and compared it with I rushed to the store.

We would consider these to mean the same idea. The BLEU score marks the word ran as completely incorrect, but the BERT model knows that these two words essentially mean the same thing in this context. And this is what deep learning really is all about. It's about mapping inputs to a useful vector space so that we can infer relationships between those inputs. To use the BERTScore, we proceed as usual. We first need to install the BERTScore package by using pip install bert-score.

Then we can load in the corresponding metric by calling load metric passing in the string bert_score. Note that when you install the library it has a dash, but when you use it in code it has an underscore. Finally, we can use the metric by calling metric compute passing in a list of predictions and a list of lists of references, again using the same format as the BLEU score. Note that we do need one additional argument, which is the language. Again, this is specified by the two character language code for the BERTScore.

Note that it returns precision, recall and F1 individually for each prediction you give it. As such, if you want the overall score for all samples, you can take the mean.

9. Translation Metrics (BLEU Score & BERT Score) (Code)
=======================================================

So in this lecture, we will continue looking at our notebook. Moving on to metrics. We'll begin by installing two libraries which implement the metrics we need the BLEU score and the BERTScore. The next step is to import the load_metric function and then to load up our two metrics. The next step is to test our metrics on a few examples to demonstrate how they behave. In this course, we're using the BLEU metric because it's pretty standard, but we're also using the BERTScore metric since it overcomes drawbacks of the BLEU metric.

We'll begin with a simple sentence, which is I love cats. Note that the prediction is exactly the same as the target. So as you can see, the score turns out to be zero because the BLEU score doesn't really work with short sentences. Now let's try a longer sentence. Note that Marian is the framework which was used to train the model from this notebook. Again, the prediction is the same as the target. So as you can see, the score is 100. So the maximum BLEU score is 100 instead of just one.

Now we know that the BLEU score metric compares n-grams. Either they match or they don't. This means that it's not capable of detecting similar words or words with the same meaning. This is a real downside of this metric because for translation, what we really care about is that the meaning is correct, not that a specific set of words were used. This is also why the BLEU Score accepts multiple references to begin with. Although, of course, it would be very difficult to cover all possibilities.

For the next example, we're going to compare to similar but different sentences. I love cats and I like cats. We know that these sentences are very similar in the meaning, but only two out of three words are correct. Note that we must also specify the language code which is en for English. So notice how the first time you call metric.compute. This will actually download some version of BERT. As you recall, this is because internally the metric converts the words into contextual embeddings and then computes the cosine similarity between them.

Thus, the model is required to make these computations. So as you can see, we get an F1 of nearly 99%, which is a good sign that this metric understands meaning and doesn't just try to match words. Now as you recall, we cannot use metric.compute directly. We need to define our own compute metrics function. We'll begin by importing numpy as we normally do. The next step is to define a function which takes as input a tuple of predictions and labels.

Note that the API is inconsistent here and we actually get token IDs and not just logits. As you recall in the previous sections we got logits and labels, not predictions and labels. And this is because getting the predictions from the logits is not as easy as simply taking the argmax as we did earlier in the course. Instead, machine translation models use a special algorithm called beam search in order to find the most likely sequence of words. As such, it would be much simpler to just give us the token IDs instead of just giving us the logits and requiring us to call the beam search function ourselves.

Now we saw already that the metrics above take strings as input instead of tokens or token IDs. As such, the next step is to decode the predictions back into sentences. Note that this also makes use of the tokenizer, since it has all the information needed to convert strings into token IDs and vice versa. Interestingly, it somehow already knows that this is for the target language and not the input language. Now, for some reason, the tokenizer doesn't recognize the -100 values, so in the next step we're going to manually replace all the minus one hundreds in the labels with the pad token ID we saw before.

The next step is to then decode the labels into sentences as well. The next step is to get rid of extraneous whitespace and also to put the targets into their own lists as required by the metrics. The next step is to call metric compute for both the BLEU metric and the BERTScore metric. Finally, we return a dictionary containing both the BLEU score and the BERTScore. Recall that the BERTScore is returned per sample. So we take the mean.

10. Train & Evaluate (Code Preparation)
=======================================

So in this lecture, we'll be looking at how to do the final steps of our notebook, which will be to train and evaluate our model. Furthermore, we'll look at how to save the fine tune model and load it back in as a pipeline object. As you've seen in previous lectures, this basically involves creating a training arguments object, a trainer object, and then calling trainer.train. This section will follow the same process, but with a few crucial modifications.

We'll begin by creating the training arguments object, which now comes from the Seq2SeqTrainingArguments class. As before, the first argument is simply the path to save the data we generate as we train. In this example, we're going to set evaluation strategy to no because as you see, this is quite a time consuming process. Instead, we'll evaluate the model once before training and once after training to observe that our metrics improved. Another argument worth looking at is predict_with_generate.

This makes it so that when we evaluate our model will generate the predictions token by token, the same way we would do it if we were to generate a real translation. This is opposed to using the true decoder input IDs, which will be more optimistic because those are only one step predictions. When you use your model in practice, the true target will of course not be available. If you study time series analysis, you can relate this to that topic as well.

Using the true decoder input IDs would be equivalent to doing one step forecasts. So the model only ever has to predict one step ahead, which always looks pretty good, but not using the decoder input IDs would be equivalent to doing a multistep forecast. So if you want to predict the forecast for ten days into the future, you'll first predict one day ahead and then use that prediction to predict two days ahead and then use both those predictions to predict three days ahead and so forth.

In other words, any errors you generate in the preceding steps will propagate to later predictions, making it worse in general compared to the one step forecast. Similarly, it's much easier for the translation model to predict just one next word than it is to predict the whole sentence. The final argument I want to draw your attention to is FP16, which will be set to true. Normally we use 32 bits to store floating point numbers, so we call FP16 half precision.

This means that we're using half the space to store each number, which will speed up computation and take up less space at the cost of having less precise weights. For this task. This will really be necessary because our model is very large and even with this setting, you might run out of GPU memory every now and then. Note that in order to use this setting, you must be using the GPU. Once we create the training arguments, the next step is to create the trainer.

As with the training arguments, we'll now have a special trainer called the Seq2SeqTrainer. Luckily, the arguments are pretty much the same, so there's nothing more to discuss. As usual, we call trainer.train, which will start the training process. So as mentioned during the training process, we will not evaluate the model because it takes a long time. So instead we will manually evaluate the model on the test set by calling trainer.evaluate passing in the maximum target length.

We'll do this once before training and once after training to see if our metrics have improved. Once we've trained our model, we can save it using the usual function, trainer.save_model passing in the path where we want to save the model. We can then load it back in as a pipeline, just as you saw in the beginners corner by specifying the task as translation. Finally, we can translate any arbitrary text by passing in a string or list of strings.

11. Train & Evaluate (Code)
===========================

So in this lecture, we will be looking at the next portion of our notebook where we train and evaluate our transformer. We'll begin by creating an object of type Seq2SeqTrainingArguments. Note that this is a special training arguments class which we haven't used before. Specially made for seq2seq. One seq2seq-specific argument we'll be using is predict_with_generate which has been set to true. As you recall, we need to set this to true so that during evaluation the model will be evaluated without using the true targets as input into the decoder.

Instead, the decoder will generate the translation by itself in an auto regressive manner, just like a language model. In other words, it will generate one token at a time, and each token it generates will be fed back into the input for the next time step. Now, because even making predictions with a large model like this is so computationally intensive, we're going to set evaluation strategy to no. Instead, we'll run evaluation manually before and after training to check whether or not our model has improved.

For the same reason, we also set FP16 to true, which will cause the model to use floating point numbers with 16 bits instead of 32 bits. The next step is to instantiate a Seq2SeqTrainer object. Note that the arguments into the constructor are the same as what we've seen before, so there's not much to discuss. However, do note that like the training arguments, we have a special trainer class specifically made for seq2seq. The next step is to evaluate our model before we begin the training process.

As you recall, our pre-trained model has already been trained to translate English into French. As such, its performance shouldn't be too bad. So as you can see, BERT is being downloaded once again. This time it's a multilingual version of BERT, since, as you recall, our BERTScore metric needs to compute a score based on French sentences. Earlier we were evaluating English sentences, which used a different version of BERT. So our model does pretty good. About 86% BERTScore and about 38.9 BLEU score.

The next step is to call trainer.train to launch the training process. Note that sometimes the GPU runs out of memory, which means that even with this very small sample size and half precision floating points, we still reach the limits of CoLab. One easy solution is to simply restart the runtime and run everything again. But in practice, you might want to get a more powerful GPU. So now that our model has been fine tuned, we're going to evaluate it one more time.

Okay. So now the BERTScore is about 88% and the BLEU score has gone up to 43.4. So our model has definitely improved. The next step is to save our model by calling trainer.save_model. The next step is to load in our saved model as a pipeline object. The next step is to test our translator on an English sentence. It says, I hope this course has helped you on your data science journey. So if you speak French, please let us know whether or not this translation is any good.

12. Translation Section Summary
===============================

So in this lecture, we will be summarizing everything we learned in this section. This section focused on the task of neural machine translation, which is a kind of sequence to sequence task. Although the previous section looked at a task where our outputs and targets were sequences. This is different because the target sequence doesn't have the same length as the inputs. In the case of translation, each prediction does not correspond to each input. Unlike a task like NER. Computationally, this kind of task is quite a bit more difficult and even requires a whole new kind of model called seq2seq.

This involves both an encoder block and a decoder block, whereas the previous models we looked at were only encoder blocks. This section follow the same usual steps of our fine tuning process in Hugging Face. We began by looking at the dataset and tokenizer. The tokenizer in this section was a bit different because not only did we have to tokenize the input, we also had to tokenize the target. We then looked at how to prepare the model inputs, including the data collator in this section.

The data collator introduces a new kind of input, specifically the decoder input in order to understand why this was needed. We had to discuss a little bit about how seq2seq works in this course. The goal is really only to use the least amount of theory necessary to get through the Hugging Face notebooks. The next step was to discuss metrics for evaluating our translator outputs. We already learned about the BLEU score in the beginners corner, but in this section we also looked at the BERTScore, which is, in my opinion, much more interesting.

Instead of trying to match exact tokens, it instead maps each token into a contextual embedding using a BERT model such that tokens could be compared using a vector similarity instead of just match or no match. Finally we looked at how to train, evaluate and save our model for later use. We noted that with models as large as these special steps need to be taken for the training process to be feasible. For instance, using 16 bit floating point numbers.

At this stage we are reaching and even surpassing the limits of the CoLab platform.

###

1. Question-Answering Section Introduction
==========================================

So in this lecture we will be introducing the next section of this course, which is all about a very interesting topic known as question answering. Specifically, we will look at how to fine tune a transformer for this task. As you recall, this is the task where we give the transformer a piece of text and ask it a question based on that text. Currently, the state of the art allows us to do extractive question answering, meaning that the correct answer is simply a substring of the given text.

As a little exercise, it's worth thinking about how a neural network would be able to do that. Consider whether this is a problem of classification or regression and what the outputs and loss function might look like. In order to prime your mind to learn the content of this section, you may find it beneficial to review the beginner's corner where we looked at the question answering pipeline, which applies a pre-trained model with just one line of code.

So let's discuss a brief outline for this section of the course. As with the other sections of the course on fine tuning, we will follow the same high level steps. As usual, we begin by tokenizing the inputs and processing the inputs so that they can be passed into the model. In this section, this will be our most laborious task. We'll then look at how to compute metrics which, like the previous steps, will require a large amount of work. This will look very different from the previous sections, since we'll need to do quite a bit of work to convert the model outputs into an actual string of text.

After these preliminary steps, we can finally move on to training the model and evaluating the model after training is complete. As usual, this portion will be brief. So as a general theme for this section, basically it involves a little more API hunting, which means figuring out the right functions to call in what they do, but also a lot more in terms of getting down into the weeds, much more so than the previous sections of the course. This is primarily due to two reasons.

Reason number one is that, as you recall, inputs for question answering come in the form of context and question pairs. You can imagine a context as something like a Wikipedia page on some topic because of this context can be very long and we'll need some way to handle this, along with any complications that arise from how we choose to do that. The number two issue is that it's going to take quite a bit of work to convert our model outputs into an actual answer represented as text.

At a high level, this is because the neural networks output numbers while what we want is text. In my opinion, issue number one may simply be due to the fact that this is still a new library and the developers haven't yet had a chance to encapsulate these steps into a more convenient API. In any case, what this does mean is that like some of the previous sections, you will need to put on your programming hat and write actual code. This is not basic code like you'd see in a typical Udemy course, but real code that will require you to think algorithmically.

Now, just as a heads up, there is one quirk with the Hugging Face API that will become apparent in this section and this is that they tend to call everything an ID, so you'll have sequence IDs, example IDs, token type IDs, token IDs, all kinds of IDs. This becomes very annoying to keep track of since sequence and example are such generic words. I've done my best to give variables less insane looking names. But do keep in mind that one of the challenging things in this section is keeping track of what each variable actually is.

To be honest with you, when I first encountered this code, I found it to be quite boring and overwhelming. But in fact the code is quite interesting, so I encourage you to stick with it. Put an honest amount of effort into understanding each step, and it will become an interesting problem to solve if you are the type of person that likes to code. Plus, it's a very cool application of NLP, so if you want to train your own question answering system on a custom data set, this is something you'll have to know how to do.

2. Exploring the Dataset (SQuAD)
================================

So in this lecture we will discuss the SQuAD data set, which is the dataset we'll be using for this task. SQuAD simply stands for Stanford Question Answering Dataset. Now, as you already know, the task of question answering is still constrained in a few ways. We can't yet give a neural network a big database of knowledge and just ask it any question we want. Instead, we do what is called extractive question answering. What this means is that we're going to give the network a pair of texts, namely the question and the context which contains the answer to the question.

In that way, the answer is always a substring of the context. Note also that because of this, the network never has to actually generate any text, so we don't require an encoder decoder setup. Now there are some details that will become important when you want to actually write the code. Firstly, let's look at how we will load in the data. As you can see, we just call the standard function load_dataset passing in the string squad. The data set comes with five columns which are ID title, context, question and answers.

Note that the title is pretty much irrelevant. Interestingly, ID is not irrelevant, which may seem strange since we've ignored it up until this point. We'll discuss this more when the time comes. So let's look at some examples. Here's a context. It says, Architecturally, the school has a Catholic character. Atop the main building's gold dome, is a golden statue of the Virgin Mary, and so on and so forth. The question is what is in front of the Notre Dame main building?

And the corresponding answer is a copper statue of Christ. Note that the answer seems to have a funny format. Firstly, the text is stored in a list. We'll see why that makes sense shortly. Furthermore, we see that in addition to the text, we also get the position of the start of the answer in terms of characters. As you recall, a string is simply an array of characters. So if you think of the context as an array of characters, this would be the index of the start of the answer.

So for this example, the corresponding title happens to be University of Notre Dame. As you can see, this is irrelevant for finding the answer to the question. What should strike you as interesting is that the answer column is plural. This implies that there can potentially be multiple answers to the same question. This also explains why the answer data is stored in lists. Now, how can this be? Well, consider the question where did Super Bowl 50 take place?

One possible answer is Santa Clara, California. This is a true fact. But another possible answer is Levi's Stadium. This is also a true fact. So how can one question have multiple answers? Well, here's the context where this came from. It says The game was played on February seven, 2016 at Levi's Stadium in the San Francisco Bay area at Santa Clara, California. Depending on how you interpret this question, both answers would be valid. So this is an example of where the same question can have multiple valid answers.

Now, oddly, this data set is built such that for some questions, the exact same answer can appear multiple times. I'm not sure why that is. Finally, note that this only happens for the validation set for the train set. Although the column is called Answers. There is only one answer per sample. Since our neural networks loss function is only built for one target per input, this is a good thing since it means we don't have to do any extra work to split up multiple answers into separate training samples.

4. Using the Tokenizer
======================

So in this lecture we will move on to the next step of our question answering notebook. Now, as you recall, typically after we load in the data, we apply the tokenizer to convert the text into a numerical format for the neural network. At a high level. That seems simple, but as you've seen many times already, the devil is in the details. Firstly, let's note that the checkpoint will be using for this notebook will be either BERT or DistilBERT. This might surprise you since question answering seems like a pretty complicated task.

So you think it might require an encoder decoder setup? But this is not the case. This will be easier to understand once we discuss the model outputs and perhaps some theory about how transformers actually work. For now, it suffices to know that BERT and BERT-like models are the correct choice for the task. This means that we'll be using the same tokenizer that we used earlier in the course. As you know, this tokenizer can already handle two input sentences such as you saw in the textual entailment task.

Okay. So just to review what happens when we use the tokenizer to tokenize two pieces of text and how do we do it? Well, you'll recall that this works by simply passing in the two pieces of text as two separate arguments by convention will pass in the question first and then the context. If we decode the outputs from the tokenizer, meaning turn the token IDs back into words, we will get a big, long string containing both the question and the context

concatenated together. In particular, this always starts with the special CLS token, followed by the first sentence followed by the SEP token followed by the second sentence. And finally, one more SEP token. Now, please note that I'm using the word sentence loosely. In fact, the context may contain more than one sentence. In fact, it likely will most, if not all of the time. Now, one challenging aspect of question answering is that the context part of the input can be really long.

This is unlike the textual entailment or next sentence prediction examples, since for those the two inputs are just two actual sentences. In this case, the context will contain many sentences. Now, as you recall, BERT can only handle a limited number of tokens, but it wouldn't be a good idea to truncate the context, since the part we leave out may contain the answer to the question. And it also wouldn't be a good idea to truncate the question. Since then, we wouldn't know the question.

So what is the solution to this? The solution is to split the context into multiple windows. As a result, one data sample will turn into multiple data samples, some of which will contain the answer and some of which will not. So by doing this, we introduce a case where no answer can be found. Now you might be concerned for those of you very keen students, you might notice that we have a problem when the answer crosses the boundary between these windows.

If half of the answer is at the end of one context window and the other half of the answer is at the beginning of the next context window, then no answer will be valid and the model won't really be learning the answers to the questions. We solve this problem by using overlapping windows instead. In the Hugging Face library, the amount of overlap is called the stride. So the full tokenizer call looks like this. As before, we pass in the context and the question.

The next argument is max_length where we specify the maximum length of the entire input. This includes the question and the context and the special tokens. The next step is to specify truncation equals only_second. This means, since the context is the second input text, only truncate this but do not truncate the question which is the first input text. The next argument is the stride, which defines how much overlap there is between the context windows when they are split up.

And finally we set return_overflowing_tokens to true. A better name for this would probably be overlapping tokens since this corresponds to the tokens that overlap when we set the stride to a positive number. Importantly, note that when we call the tokenizer like this, it expands the data. If we have one question in context pair, this might be converted into multiple input samples depending on how long the context is. One thing that will become important later on is how we know which sample from the tokenized output corresponds to which sample from the tokenized input.

Imagine for instance, that we enumerated the original input samples. So we have sample zero, sample one, sample two and so forth. At the output, we might have 0, 0, 0, 1, 1, 2, 3, 3, 3 and so forth. This means that Sample zero got expanded into three separate model inputs. Sample one got expanded into two model inputs and so forth. Luckily we do get access to this data when we call the tokenizer as described above. This will return a new key we haven't seen before called overflow_to_sample_mapping.

It contains exactly these integers, specifically the original input sample indices. Now there's one more important argument into the tokenizer that I've left out until now. This is the argument return_offsets_mapping. We will be setting this true now like the previous overflow_to_sample_mapping. This probably seems very random and unnecessary, but don't worry, I feel the same way. It will all come together when we look at our next task. But this is just to set up the preliminaries, so let's just assume that it's useful.

Basically, when we set this argument to true, we will get back an additional tokenizer output in addition to the usual input IDs, attention mask and so forth. This will be called the offset mapping. What this does is for each model input, it's going to give us a list of tuples. Each of these tuples corresponds to a token in the input sequence. Specifically, each tuple will contain the start and end character positions of that token. So as an example, suppose the question is where is Bob?

And the context is Bob is at home. The first tuple is zero zero, which corresponds to the special CLS token because technically this doesn't take up any space. The second tuple goes from 0 to 5 because the word where contains five letters. The next tuple goes from 6 to 8 because the word is contains two letters and so forth. Note that after the question is complete, we have another zero zero tuple which corresponds to the SEP token which does not take up any space.

After this we have the tuples for the context which importantly start counting from zero. Now again, it might be totally unclear why we would even need this information. To give you some intuition for why we might need this. Consider that when we finally want to represent the answer to our question, the answer will be given as start and end positions of each token. So, for example, tokens 2 to 4, which corresponds to the phrase at home, but in order to convert this back into a string to present to the user, we must know where these tokens begin and end.

That is what is the first character and what is the last. So hopefully that gives you some idea of why this information is needed.

Finally we set return_overflowing_tokens to true, meaning we want to return the tokens in the overlapping parts of the context windows. So in addition to the input IDs and attention mask, which you've already seen, we get another item called overflow_to_sample_mapping. So let's print this out just to see what it is. So it's just a list of zeroes, which is not that informative. What I'm going to do is tell you right now what this means and then do a more meaningful demonstration in the next step.

So what does this mean? Well, as you recall, when we split up the context, we get back multiple samples. However, we may want to know what is the original sample index it came from? That's what this tells us. Since we only passed in one original sample, this is going to be zero each time because they all came from the zeroth original sample. Again, we print out the overflow_to_sample_mapping results. So hopefully these results make more sense. As you can see, all of our input samples have been split into four parts. This tells us that the first four outputs correspond to the original sample at index zero. The next four outputs correspond to the original sample at index one, and the final four outputs correspond to the original sample at index two. Now to really confirm that this is the case, we can also decode the input IDs.

So as you can see, this confirms that each of the first three samples really was split into four parts. This is why we see the same three questions repeated four times. So for the next step, we're going to go back to looking at a single context question pair with the same arguments. As you recall, we just added a new argument called return_offsets_mapping. This will return something that will be very useful throughout this notebook. We'll follow the tokenizer call by checking the keys of the output once again.

So as you can see, we get one new output, which is called offset mapping. So let's take a look at what this offset mapping actually is. So as you can see, it appears to be a list of lists of tuples. Note that I've also made a note in the comments describing what you see. So check that out as well if you need. Basically these tuples show us the character positions of each token in the model input. As you recall, the model input can really be conceptualized as a string, and a string is really an array of characters.

Note that special tokens like CLS and SEP always have the tuples zero zero. Otherwise, consider the question for this input. Recall that it's what is in front of the Notre Dame main building. The first tuple other than CLS is zero four. This makes sense because the first token of the question starts at position zero and it's four characters long, as in the word what has four characters in it.

The next tuple is five seven. This makes sense because the next word which is is starts at the next position and is two characters long. The next tuple is 8, 10, which also makes sense. Note that the final token is just one character which corresponds to the question mark. Another important detail to notice is that when we look at the context, the indices start back at zero.
