=======================

So in this lecture, we are going to continue on to the next step of our notebook. The next step is a very important step, and it will help us understand why we needed the information we retrieved from the tokenization. Since it will be required in order to get this job done, this lecture will start off with more preliminary, seemingly random steps. So it'll be good to just talk about what we are trying to accomplish. As you recall, we just learn how to expand our inputs into multiple context windows with some maximum size.

This is to ensure the inputs actually fit into the transformer we want to use and that the answer can actually be found in at least one of these context windows. The problem is this, as you recall, for any given answer, it has some starting position in the context given the data set. But after we split the context up into multiple parts, this is no longer valid. As such, we need to recompute where the answer actually is after expanding the context. So that's what we are trying to do at a high level.

Remember that if we do end up splitting the context into multiple windows, we will get multiple training samples out of one context. Question and answer triple. Of course, if we change the context by doing this splitting, we will also change the position of the answer, which is essentially the neural network target. As such, this lecture is really all about recomputing the targets after we split the context. Recall that for some of these context windows, the answer will simply not exist or may only exist in parts.

Now again, I want to mention a one more time that this lecture will begin with some seemingly random preliminary steps. Just assume that they are useful as we go along and wait until the end to see how it all comes together. Now, before we get into the details of this lecture, I want to give you another high level picture of what we are actually trying to do. This will help you understand how we actually train the neural network to perform this task.

Basically, you can imagine a neural network with a bunch of output nodes, one for each time step. Suppose that we treat question answering as a classification task. So all we really want to do is pick the correct time step for the answer. So we'll have one class per time step. If we have T time steps, then we have T classes. Since we need to choose a whole range of values, it will be convenient to simply find the start and end. Therefore, in fact, we will have two sets of output nodes for each time step.

Our target class will simply select a time step. In other words, if the answer exists between token five and token ten, then our target will be five for the first set of nodes and ten for the second set of nodes. In other words, our goal in this lecture is to determine the start index and end index of the answer, where both of these will be some integer between one and T, where T is the sequence length. Note that in code we start counting from zero, but for simplicity we don't show this.

So to get back to the details. Well, first note that there is an important function. You can call on the tokenizer outputs called sequence IDs. This takes in an integer corresponding to the input sample. As usual, we count from zero, one, two and so forth, just like array and list indices. The output is a list which looks as follows. Firstly, there are nones wherever there is a special token in the tokenized input. There are zeros which correspond to the tokens from the first sentence, and there are ones which correspond to the tokens from the second sentence.

Now, you might be wondering, isn't this pretty much the same as the token type IDs that we saw earlier in the course? The answer is yes. However, recall that token type IDs are not present in all types of models such as DistilBERT. Therefore, we can't depend on it. Instead, this sequence ID function can always be called no matter which model we use. Another thing you should notice about this is that this is a great example of the weird naming scheme.

Unfortunately, the phrase sequence IDs is so generic and so ambiguous that it's impossible to understand what this function does by its name alone. I mentioned this in the intro and you are now seeing this firsthand. Too many things in this library are called ID and too many things have very generic and ambiguous names. However, it's important to recognize it and be aware of it so that you can work around it. Now using this list of sequence IDs, we know where the context is contained.

However, this is only conceptual. Suppose that in the code we actually need the positions of where the context starts and ends. That is to say, we need the index of the first one and the index of the last one. This is a nice coding exercise, so if you'd like to pause the video and try it yourself before moving on. I would recommend you do. Otherwise, let's continue. So in order to find the first one, there is actually a handy method in Python called index which belongs to the list object.

This takes in an argument which is the element you want to find and returns the index of the first occurrence of that element. So this will give us the start of the context. In order to find the final one, we can use the same method, but we need to do some math. Firstly, recall that we can reverse a list using this weird syntax, the double colon followed by a negative one. Now we can use the index method again, which will return the position of the final one.

But for the reverse list, in order to get the corresponding position in the reversed list, we need to subtract it from the length of the list. We also need to subtract another one at the end. So this operation will give us the index of the final one, which corresponds to the end position of the context. The next question to consider is this Given the context window as a string, how do we determine the position of the start and end characters corresponding to the answer?

Furthermore, how do we determine whether or not the context window even contains the answer to begin with? This is where the offset mapping from earlier comes into play. Firstly, let's suppose we have the full context. In this case, we know the position of the start character since that is given in the data set. We don't know the position of the end character, but we can compute it simply by adding the length of the answer to the start position. This will give us the start and end if the context is not split up.

Now suppose that we do not have the full context, but only context windows. Let's break this problem up into parts and consider how we might determine that the answer is not contained in the context window. Corresponding to each context window. We have two important pieces of information, specifically the offset mapping and the token positions of the context start and end. Recall that the offset mapping is a list of tuples, one for each token containing the character positions of those tokens.

From calling sequence IDs. We also have the start token and end token so we know where the context starts and ends. Note that these line up. If we index the offset mapping at the context start, we will get the tuple containing the start and end positions of the first token in the context. The first element of this tuple will give us the first character of the context. Now suppose we index the offset mapping at the context end. If we do this, we will get the tuple containing the start and end positions of the last token in the context.

The second element of this tuple will tell us the last character of the context. So in summary, we're getting the beginning character of the beginning word and the last character of the last word. Now all we need to do is check these against the answer start character and the answer end character. So if the context window start character position is larger than the answer start character position, then the answer is not fully contained in the context.

This means that the answer starts before the context. The other situation we have to consider is if the answer goes beyond the end of the context. In this case, the context window end character position will be less than the answer end character position. So if either of these two cases are true, then the context does not fully contain the answer. In this case, we tell the neural network that the start position and end position of the answer are both zero.

This is a special code we will use when the answer does not exist. The other case we need to handle is when the answer is contained within the context. In this case, we need to actually find the start and end positions of the answer. Note that these are shifted now because the context window can start from anywhere with respect to the full context. Now, one thing to keep in mind is that this gets pretty confusing because we are essentially dealing with two different types of units at the same time.

Specifically, we have two types of indices. We have indices that index tokens and we have indices that index characters for the neural network target. We need to find the index of the start and end tokens because the neural network works with tokens. It doesn't know about characters. However, the answer in the data set is given by the position in characters. As such, we need to convert from character index to token index. To do this, we will simply loop through every tuple in the offset mapping.

This is essentially a naive linear search through every tuple to find where the character positions match and then return the corresponding indices. Before we begin, we initialize a counter variable i to context_start. Note that we do not want to start from zero because we already know where the context begins. Furthermore, we don't want to accidentally get one of the tokens from the question. Then we enter a loop that loops through every tuple in the offset mapping, starting at the context.

Inside this loop, we begin by splitting the tuple into the start and end character of the current token. Then we check whether this start character is equal to the start character in the answer. If it is, then we know that this token is the start token for the answer. So we set start_idx to i. So we just went from start character to start token. Once we found this, all that remains is to find the end token as well. To do this. We simply have another if statement that checks whether the end character of the current token is equal to the end character of the answer.

If this is the case, then we know that this token is the end token for the answer. So we set end_idx to i. Once we found this, we can break out of the loop. Since we know that both start_idx and end_idx have been found. At the end of the loop, we increment I so that I always contains the current position in the list. Alternatively, you could use enumerate. So once we've written this code, it will be useful to check that it actually works. In order to do this.

What we can do is index the input IDs by start_idx up to end_idx plus one. This will give us the token IDs of the answer. In order to convert this back into English text, we simply call tokenizer decode. Now the next little baby step will be to put our above code into a loop. As you recall, at a high level, our eventual goal is to tokenize whole data sets. But in order to do that, we need to split the input samples into multiple samples each. if their context needs to be split into multiple windows. However, this will necessitate recomputing the targets for each of the expanded samples.

In this block of code, we'll perform the above code again, but this time for every context window instead of just one. We begin by initializing lists to store the start and end values for each sample. Recall that we'll have one pair of indices for each context window. The next step is to loop through each offset mapping. We'll use enumerate so that we can get the loop index as well. Inside the loop, we begin by getting the sequence IDs for the sample. Now, if you're like me, you've already forgotten what sequence IDs even means.

Recall that this is a list like the token type IDs, which contains zeros in the positions that correspond to the question and ones in the positions that correspond to the context. The next step is to search through the sequence IDs in order to get the context, start and end indices. The next step is to find start_idx and end_idx. Suppose we encapsulated everything we wrote before into its own function called find_answer_token_idx. After we obtain start_idx and end_idx, we just append these to their corresponding lists.

7. Aligning the Targets in Python
=================================

So in this lecture, we'll be continuing our notebook and putting together the pieces we discovered in the previous steps. The next sort of arbitrary step is to check out this function called sequence IDs. This takes in an argument which specifies which input we'd like to look at. As you recall, there are four since the context was split up into four parts. So as you can see, this gives us a list which is reminiscent of the token type IDs we saw earlier in the course.

Importantly, we cannot use the token type IDs because they are not present in all models such as DistilBERT. To describe what we are seeing. This is a list that has the same length as the tokenized input. There are nones which corresponds with the special tokens like CLS and SEP. Zeros denote the tokens which are part of the question and ones denote the tokens which are part of the context. We'll be making use of this information later on in the notebook.

So just as a quick reminder, let's print out an example answer and understand why this is an issue. As you can see, the answer is represented with two pieces of information. First, we have the start index, and second, we have the actual text. The problem is this when we train our neural network, we need to have a target for each input. This target should point out the position of the answer within the context. The problem is that this representation of the answer doesn't work because it's relative to the original full context, not the actual model inputs where the context has been split up.

Furthermore, there may be some instances where the answer is not present. And finally, note that this answer is presented in terms of character positions and not token positions as required by the neural network. As such, the goal of this lecture is to basically figure out how to take the information we've gathered and convert it into proper neural network targets. Now, in order to do the next step, it'll be useful to know the type of the sequence IDs that we got back above.

So as you can see, it is a python list. So now that we know that the sequence IDs are represented as a list, we can use this to do the next step. We'll first begin by setting the sequence IDs from the first sample to its own variable, simply called sequence IDs. Our goal in this block of code is to figure out the indices of where the context starts and where the context ends. As you recall, the sequence IDs are formatted such that there are ones for every token.

That is part of the context. Therefore, the start of the context is simply the index of the first one. And similarly, the end of the context is the index of the last one. The challenge is how to find these indices. Luckily, since we know that the sequence_ids variable is a list, we can make use of python list functions such as index. This will tell us the location of the first one, which is the start of the context. So we'll call this variable ctx_start.

In order to find the final one, we can still make use of the index function, but we need to do a little math. Intuitively, we just need to flip the sequence IDs, find the first one in the flipped version and subtract the results from the length of the list. In detail, we first use the double colon minus one syntax to reverse the sequence IDs. Then we call index passing in one to find the first occurrence of a one. But in the flipped sequence IDs, we then subtract this from the length of the list and also subtract one.

This will give us the index of the final context token. Okay. So as you can see, the result is 13 and 98. This makes sense since the final token at position 99 is just a SEP. So the next thing we're going to do is the most challenging part of this lecture. Currently, we know the character positions of the answer within the full context. But in order to get the neural network targets, we want to know the token positions of the answer within the current context window.

So there are really two transformations happening here. First, we are going from character positions to token positions. And second, we are going from full context to partial context of the current input. As you recall, we're currently just looking at the first input sample for simplicity, but later we'll put this in a loop so that we can do the same thing for every sample. So let's begin by grabbing the answer start character and the answer end character.

As you recall, we've currently stored the answer data in a variable called answer. For the start character, this is simply stored in the key answer_start. We also index the result at zero since the actual index is stored in a list. For the end character, we simply need to add the size of the string to the start. In order to get the size of the string, we use the len function. Again, the answer itself is stored in the text key and we index the result at zero since it is stored in a list.

The next step is to grab the corresponding offset mapping, which we will simply call offset. Note that we grab the offset mapping at index zero. Since as you recall, we are currently only looking at the first input sample. The next step is to initialize the two variables we eventually want to return, which are start_idx and end_idx. We'll set these to zero since this is the default value for the target when no answer exists. The next step is to check whether or not the answer is fully contained within the context.

If it's not, then we can set start_idx and end_idx to zero, so we just return zero zero. So how do we actually check whether this is the case? Basically, we need to check whether or not the answer goes out of bounds, the bounds being the start and end characters of the context. There are two cases that would mean we are out of bounds. Case number one is that the answer start comes before the context start. And case number two is that the answer end comes after the context end.

And remember, we don't have the whole context. We only have a context window since we truncated the context and split it into multiple samples. Luckily we know how to find the context start and end positions since they are located in the offset mapping. As you recall, the offset mapping stores tuples containing the start and end characters of each token. Luckily, we also know which token is the start of the context and which token is the end of the context.

Since we stored these in ctx_start and ctx_end, the start of the context is the first character of the first token. In order to get this, we index the offset mapping at ctx_start, which returns a tuple telling us the start and end of the first token. Indexing this tuple at position zero will return the start. We do a similar operation for ctx_end, except that we check the tuple at position one, since that returns the end of that token. And finally, note that if this expression returns true, then the answer is out of bounds.

So there is nothing to do since start_idx and end_idx are already set to the correct values which are zero and zero. The else block covers the more complicated case where we have to actually find the position of the answer. Remember that we know where the answer is already in terms of character positions, but we do not know where the answer is in terms of token positions. The real trick here is keeping track of which variables are in units of tokens and which variables are in units of characters.

So in order to find these token positions, we're basically going to do a linear search through the offset mappings. Since the offset mappings contain the character positions, we just need to find where they match with the answer character positions we obtained above. We begin by setting a counter variable called i to ctx_start. Note that we shouldn't start looping from zero, since as you recall, the input contains both the question and the context and the counter starts at zero for both.

So we don't want to accidentally choose a position in the question by mistake. The next step is to loop through each tuple in the offset mapping again starting from ctx_start. Inside the loop we separate the tuple into start and end. Remember that these are the start and end positions of the current token. Then we simply need to check whether either of these match the answer's start and end. We begin by first checking the start. If the start of this token is equal to the start of the answer, then we know that this token is the start token of the answer, and thus we set start_idx to be the current token index, which is i.

At this point, you may want to break out of the loop since we found what we were looking for, but remember that we still need to find the end token as well. So in the next if statement we check the end position. If the end of this token is equal to the end of the answer, then we know that this token is the end token of the answer, and thus we set end_idx to be the current token index, which is i. At this point we can break out of the loop since the end token must come after the start so we can assume that the start has already been found.

Finally we increment i by one so that i always points to the position of the current token. And after we exit the loop, we print out start_idx and end_idx. So as you can see, we get 53 and 57, which is not too informative. The next step is to actually check whether or not our calculations are correct. To do this, we'll get the input IDs for the sample at index zero. Then we'll index these input IDs at the start_idx and end_idx we just found. So again, it's just a bunch of numbers.

Luckily, these are numbers that we can convert into text by calling tokenizer.decode. As you can see, this ends up giving us the correct answer, which is a copper statue of Christ. The next step in this notebook is simply for convenience. We're going to take all the code we just wrote and put it into a function so that we can call it later. This function is called find_answer_token_idx, which hopefully makes sense. As input, it takes in ctx_start, ctx_end, start_char and end_char and the offset mapping.

So it assumes we've already computed or retrieved these values. We won't describe what's inside this function since it's the same as above, except that when the target is zero zero, we won't print anything since we don't want this to be printed out later. So the final step in this lecture is to do what we just did again, but for all input samples instead of just one. As you recall, the context was split up into multiple parts. So even with just one context and question pair, we have multiple samples to operate on.

We begin by creating lists which will be used to store all the start_idxs and end_idxs we find. The next step is to loop through every offset mapping. We also use enumerate so that we get the index as well. Inside this loop, we grabbed the sequence IDs. Recall that the sequence IDs are like the token type IDs where they contain zero for the positions of the first input string and one for the positions of the second input string. In any case, this code is just a repeat of the above.

Same as above. We find ctx_start and ctx_end using the same code. The next step is to call the function find_answer_token_idx. This will give us back start_idx and end_idx. We then append start_idx and end_idx to their respective lists. Once we're done, we print out these lists, which, as a reminder, represent the targets for our neural network. So hopefully these results make sense. Notice how the answer appears at 53 to 57 for the first window and at 17 to 21 for the second window.

This makes sense because recall that there is a stride when we build the context windows, so there will be some overlap. And it turns out that for this example, the answer is positioned such that it appears in both context windows. However, for the third and fourth context windows, the answer does not appear, so the target is zero zero in these cases. Now just as a fun thought exercise. Here's something to consider. It is possible for the answer to show up in three context windows.

You might think this is impossible because our window size is 100 and our stride is 50, meaning that there is a 50% overlap between windows. And if that is the case, then the third window should not overlap with the first at all. Think about why this logic does not hold and what the mistake is.

8. Applying the Tokenizer
=========================

So at this point, we have everything we need in order to create our tokenized function, which we will then apply to each sample in our data set using map to get the model inputs. As you recall, this is the next step if we follow the same pattern as the previous sections in the course. For this notebook, we will end up writing two separate functions to process the train and validation sets. The reason for this is when we evaluate our model, we're going to evaluate it based on the string version of the answers.

But the string versions of the answers are already given in the data set, so there is no further processing to do. We'll see exactly how that works later on. So firstly, we'll be setting max length to 384 and stride to 128. You might be wondering why we would set max length to 384 if BERT can handle more tokens than this. The answer is that this is what Google used in their experiments. So we will follow what they did. Inside the tokenize function. We'll begin by noting that some questions in the data set are poorly formatted with leading and trailing whitespace.

So the first thing we'll do is strip the whitespace from each question. The next step is to call our tokenizer on our batch. Note that we'll be adding one extra argument which sets padding to the string max_length. In other words, we'll be padding all input samples to have the same length as the maximum sequence length. This is okay in our case, since we'll be truncating every input sample and splitting up large contexts. So we don't necessarily need to pad per batch and hence we won't need the data collator since many samples will reach this maximum length.

The next step in our function will be to pop the offset mapping column and the overflow_to_sample_mapping column, since these won't be needed by the model. Note that a better name for overflow_to_sample_mapping is simply original sample indices, since that's what they are. Hopefully this will help the code be more readable. We also store the answer in a variable called answers, and we create empty lists to store the start indices and end indices for each expanded input sample.

Once we've done this, the rest of the function is essentially a copy of what we already saw. In any case, it'll be helpful to review it once again. We begin by looping through each sample's offset mapping. Remember that our goal now is to find the start token index and the end of token index for each sample. These are used as targets for our network. We need to compute these manually because the data set only provides the answers in terms of character positions and the actual answer text.

So inside the loop, we begin by grabbing the original sample index. This part is new and it's important to understand. As you recall, after passing the data through the tokenizer, we expanded the number of samples. So multiple output samples correspond to the same input sample. This is important because they all point to the same answer, but the answer is stored in our unexpanded original inputs, so we need some way to map back to those. Luckily, we can do that because we've stored the original sample indices by indexing orig_sample_idxs at position i, we get back the original sample index.

From here we can index the answers list at this sample index to get the correct answer. And from here we can grab the answer start character position and the answer end character position as we did before. We also grab the sequence IDs as we did before. Note that this uses the index i because it looks at the tokenized output. Again, recall that the sequence IDs are just a list of zeros and ones where zeros represent the question and one represents the context.

From this, we can find the start and end token positions of the context. After this, we call our function find_answer_token_idx to get the start token index and the end token index for the target. Once we have these, we append them to their corresponding lists. Once we finish populating these lists, we then assign them to new columns called start_positions and end_positions. Note that we must use these names since they will be used by the trainer later on.

Lastly, we return the tokenized inputs objects. Okay. So once we have our tokenized function for the train set, we can pass it through the map function as usual. We can also take this opportunity to remove the original columns since they are no longer needed. The next step is to build a tokenized function for the validation set as well. Note that this will be a bit simpler because there is no need to compute any targets. Instead, when we evaluate our model, the metrics will be computed on string answers, which we already have from the data set itself.

One important step, which is not obvious at this point, is to understand the role of the ID field. We haven't needed this in the course so far, but if you print out one of the samples, you will see that this ID field is present. It's basically just a unique string ID that identifies each sample. Intuitively it makes sense why we would need this, since if the tokenization expands the inputs into multiple samples, the model inputs will no longer align with the original answers.

So we need some way to match up the true answers with each of the predicted answers. The first few steps of the tokenize function for the validation set are the same as for the train set. We begin by stripping white space from the questions. The next step is to call the tokenizer, as we did before, by padding everything to the max length. We will also pop the overflow_to_sample_mapping, but we will not remove the offset mapping like we did before. This will actually be kept because we're going to make use of it when we compute our metrics.

The next step will be to create an empty list, which will be used to store the ID of each sample. Recall that these are the IDs that we looked at on the previous slide. Unfortunately, this is yet another instance where we use the term ID in this section, so hopefully you're keeping track of which ID is which. The IDs we are looking at now are unique strings that identify each sample. So in this case, ID is more appropriate. So the next step is to loop through the range of inputs.

We don't actually need the inputs themselves, just the index. Inside this loop, we first grab the original sample index. We then index the original sample and grab its ID. Now we know the original sample ID for the sample at position I in the tokenized output. We append this to our list of sample IDs. Additionally, we are also going to modify the offset mapping field since as you recall, I just said that we will be making use of this when we compute metrics.

We begin by grabbing the sequence IDs at position i. Again, just as a reminder, this is a list which contains zeros at the positions for the question and one at the positions for the answer. The next step is to grab the offset mapping at position i. We then loop through each offset mapping and replace any item that does not belong to the context to none. Now that probably sounds pretty complicated, so it helps to visualize what we are doing. In fact, you've probably forgotten what offset mapping even is, since I've done that myself many times.

Recall that offset mapping is a list containing the tuples of the start and end character positions of each token. Our goal right now is to modify this list to replace any tuple that does not pertain to a context token with none. Again, this might seem like a completely random operation, but it will be useful when we compute metrics later on. Finally, once this loop is complete, we assign the sample IDs to a new column called sample_id. Then we return the tokenized model inputs.

As with the train set, once we finish defining the tokenized function for the validation set, we will apply it to every sample in the validation set by using the map function. So to summarize this lecture, we just implemented the tokenized functions for both train and validation, which we will subsequently map to each sample in our data sets.

10. Question-Answering Metrics
==============================

So in this lecture we will be looking at how to compute metrics for the question answering task as usual. Since this is a standard data set for NLP benchmarks, it should be expected that there is also an associated metric for this task. As you're pretty used to by now, we will load in the metric by calling load metric passing in the string squad. So what does this metric do? As you recall, I mentioned earlier in this section that the metric will be using does not involve any low level quantities like the position of the answer within the context.

Instead, the metric uses the actual answers themselves in the form of strings. So if the answer is Albert Einstein, then our prediction should also be Albert Einstein in order to be considered correct. Of course, this begs the question how do we convert our model predictions back into strings? That will represent the answer. This remains to be seen. For now, we'll just focus on how to call metric compute and the results we will get back. In terms of the results we get back, metric compute will return two items, the exact match score and the F1 score.

You can think of exact match like accuracy, which will return the percentage of results where the prediction exactly matched the true answer. On the other hand, F1 is more granular and looks at each token in each answer. So if the answer was general relativity, but your prediction was special relativity, you would at least receive partial credit. Also recall that for this data set there can be multiple possible true answers to a question. Therefore, the metric also takes this into account.

The next question to consider is how do we actually call metric compute? What inputs does it expect? As usual, we have two arguments to pass in which are predictions and references. Each of these should be lists of dictionaries. The prediction dictionaries will have a different format than the reference dictionaries. In particular, the reference or target dictionaries will be similar to what we have in the data set. Each of the target dictionaries will have an ID denoting which sample it pertains to.

They will also have an answers item which stores another dictionary, which is the exact same dictionary that we get from the data set. As you recall, that dictionary has two items which are text and answer start. The text contains the actual string answers, while answer_start contains the initial character index of the answer. Recall that these are both lists since each question context pairing can have multiple possible answers. The predicted answers are a bit simpler.

In this case, we only need to provide the ID and a key called prediction_text which contains a single string as the value. Note that these keys must be named as shown here since metric compute expects them to be this way. Furthermore, note that all of the items shown here are required and you will get an error if they are missing, even though they are not part of the answer. So for example, if you leave out the ID, that won't work. If you leave out answer_start,

that also won't work.

12. From Logits to Answers
==========================

So in this lecture, we are going to move on to a very important task. The task is to convert our model outputs back into a string answer so that we can call metric compute. And obviously this is not just so we can call metric compute, but so we can actually read the answers as well. It wouldn't make sense to have a question answering system but then not be able to read the answers. This is our second major task for this section, the first being how to process the model inputs.

Now in order to test our code and see some actual results immediately. We're not going to wait until we've trained our model. Our approach will be as follows We're going to download a pre-trained question answering model and use that to get model predictions that are not just random. Well then use the predictions of this pre-trained model to learn how to convert the results back into answer text. Once we figured out how to do this for a small subset of our data set, we can then apply it to the full data set after our model has been fine tuned.

Okay. So this is a preview of the code we'll be using. We'll begin by grabbing just the first 100 samples of our validation sets. We'll then define a new checkpoint for this trained model. Since this is a different model, we'll need a different tokenizer. However, our functions above use the tokenizer variable as a global variable, and as such, we'll need to overwrite the tokenizer object temporarily and change it back later so we save our current tokenizer in a variable called old_tokenizer.

We then assign the variable tokenizer2 to the existing variable tokenizer. Remember, this is because the function we wrote will access a global variable with this name. We then map the tokenized validation function to the mini data set and we'll call this small_validation_processed. Lastly, we reassign old_tokenizer to tokenizer, so we're changing it back. The next step will be to get our model outputs, whatever they may be. First we import torch and of course, we'll need a model class specifically built for this task.

It's called AutoModelForQuestionAnswering. The next step is to remove columns that the model doesn't use as input, such as sample ID and offset mapping. The next step is to get the GPU device, since this all must now be done manually. Now, as you recall, the data set acts like a dictionary. So the next step is to loop through each of the columns of the tokenized data set that we still have left and move them to the GPU. This will return another dictionary, but this time with all the data on the GPU.

The next step is to download our trained model and to also move that to the GPU. Finally, we call our trained model passing in the inputs to get the model outputs. We use torch.no_grad since we won't be using the gradients. Now, if you print out the model output, you'll see that it's an object of type question answering model outputs. This object has two attributes which are start_logits and end logits, both of which point to PyTorch tensors. So this should give you some idea of how this model works.

Suppose that we have a batch of N samples, each with a padded sequence length of t. Then we would expect both of these logits to be of size N by T. So what's the meaning of this? For simplicity. Suppose we only have one sample that is one question, one context, and one answer. So effectively the start logits will be a vector of size T and the end logits will also be a vector of size t. If we took the softmax over the time dimension, we would get the probabilities for each time step.

What we do with these probabilities will be described very shortly, but it would be a good idea to start thinking about this now. Now in order to work with these logits directly in code, it's more convenient to have them as numpy arrays. As you recall, in pytorch, we can get NumPy arrays from tensors by first moving them back to the CPU and then calling the numpy method. At this point, it's worth remembering that in our processed data set, we've stored an attribute called sample ID.

This is a unique string identifier corresponding to each question context pair. It's also worth remembering that by processing the data set, we expand the samples by splitting up context windows and as such some samples and hence sample IDs may be repeated. So if you check the length of the processed data set, it should be longer than the set of all unique sample IDs. We will demonstrate this in the notebook. Now. Why do we care about repeated sample IDs?

Well, as with many of the steps in this notebook, we just need to do some preliminary work that will all come together at the end. For now, we're just going to create a dictionary that maps each sample ID to all the possible indices of where that sample resides in the process data set. So for example, if the first sample gets split into four windows, then its sample ID would point to a list containing zero, one, two and three, which are the first four positions in the list containing all the processed samples.

This code you see here simply loops through all the sample IDs and stores the corresponding indices as values. Since there may be multiple index values per sample ID, we store them in a list. So now let's get back to our logits. At this point, we're going to tackle the big question how do we convert these logits into an actual prediction for the answer as text? To answer this question, we're going to need to do a bit of math. Usually we save the math for the advanced theoretical sections, but in this case, it's necessary to compute the answer.

If this math is too much for you, please note that the actual computation is simple. So you can skip this if you like, but then you won't understand why your computation looks the way it does. Either way, up to you. The prerequisite for this is just a basic probability. So study up on that if you want to understand how this works. So suppose that we take the softmax of the start logits and end logits to get the corresponding probabilities. Our simplified notation for this will be p of s1, p of s2, and so forth up to p of s big T. For the end tokens,

the corresponding probabilities will be p of e1, p of e2, all the way up to p of e big T. Now we could add a given x to be more precise, but I'll assume you know that this is implicitly the case. This is just to keep the notation simpler. So p of s sub i is the probability that the answer start token is at position i and p of e sub j is the probability that the answer end token is at position j. Our goal, of course, is to find the most probable answer. That is, the values of I and J that lead to the maximum joint probability p of s sub i and e sub j.

In order to simplify this computation, we're going to make the assumption that the distributions of start and end tokens are independent. By doing this, we can factorize the joint distribution into two separate distributions, p of s sub i times p of e sub j. And obviously this is exactly what we need since this is exactly our neural network outputs. So a naive solution might look like this. Before we do anything, we initialize the best i and j to none and the best score to minus infinity.

And then we do a loop for all values of I from one up to big T, and then we do a nested for loop for all values of J from one up to big T. So we're searching through all combinations of I and J and note that since this is pseudocode, we indexed from one instead of zero, as we might do in real code. Inside the nested loop, we compute the joint probability by taking p of s sub i and multiplying it by p of e sub j. This is our score. If this is greater than our current best score, then we make this our new best score and we assign the current I and J values to be the best I and J we have found so far.

From here, we know the best start token and the best end token. So all we need to do is a few more additional steps to convert the answer back into text. However, before we do that, there are a few ways we can make this better and more correct. Firstly, note that looping through all big T squared values is a lot. Big T in our case is 384, and since this is a nested for loop where we loop through the range of Big T both times the number of iterations is 384 times 384.

This is 147,456. That's a lot of computations we need to do. And remember, we need to do this for every sample. Instead, we're going to use a possibly suboptimal heuristic and only search through the top 20 best start positions in the top 20 best end positions. As a result, we'll only need to iterate 20 times 20 or 400 times, which is about 0.3% of what we'd have to do if we did a full search. Now you might be wondering if we're assuming that the start and end probabilities are independent.

Why do we need to search at all? Why can't we just take the maximum p of s sub i and multiply it by the maximum p of e sub j. The answer is that this won't always lead to a valid answer. For instance, if I is bigger than J, then that means the start comes after the end, which doesn't make sense. Also recall that out of the big T input tokens, not all of them refer to the context. As you recall, we start with the CLS token, then the question, then the SEP token, and then the context, and then a final SEP.

In other words, there are a bunch of checks we need to do to make sure that the answer is actually valid. As such, it is not feasible to just take the maximum start and maximum end probabilities. To be more specific, there are three cases in which we will consider the answer to be invalid. The first case is when either i or j corresponds to a token outside the context. The second case is when I is greater than j, meaning that the start is past the end, which doesn't make sense.

The third case we haven't discussed yet, but this is when the answer is too long. In this case, we will assume that this is so unlikely that we will not consider these answers in the code. We'll set the maximum answer size to 30 tokens. So the final improvement we're going to make is that we are not going to work with probabilities at all. As you recall, in deep learning, we prefer to work with logits since they are more numerically stable. Furthermore, this often allows us to use addition instead of multiplication, which also tends to be more stable and more efficient.

So how do we compute the score from only the logits? Firstly, let's consider how we would write down p of s sub i times p of e sub j in terms of logits. We'll call the start logit l of s sub i and the end logit l of e sub j. If we take the softmax of the start and end logits separately, we would exponentiate the logits and divide by their sum. However, note that these sums are always the same no matter the value of i and j. This is because they are the sum over all values of i and all values of J.

We can just call them Z, sub s and Z sub E, which is the letter we would normally use for normalizing constants. As such, the argmax of p of s sub i times p of e sub j is the same as the argmax of e to the power logit s sub i times e to the power logit e sub j. This allows us to get rid of z sub s and z sub e completely. This is because z sub s and z sub e are both independent of I and J. In other words, they are constant with respect to I and J. Therefore, it doesn't matter which values of I and J we choose.

As such, we can simply remove them from the expression. The next step is to remember that taking the argmax of a function is the same as taking the max of the log of that function. This is because the log is monotonically increasing. What this means is that if A is a number bigger than B, it is also true that log A is also bigger than log B. So if we find the max of log of something, we also find the max of that something as such. If you're suspicious of this claim, I recommend picking various values of A and B and trying it yourself.

So what happens if we take the log of this function? Well, recall that the log of a product is the sum of logs. This means we can split up the product. Furthermore, recall that the log in the exponential are inverse functions, so the log of the exponential of any number is just that number. Therefore we will get the same answer if we use logit s sub i plus logit e sub j as our score. This makes the score much easier to compute as we can just add the neural network outputs directly.

So at this point we have everything we need to write some code that will take some neural network outputs and compute predicted answers from them. We'll begin by setting some variables. Specifically, we set n_largest to 20, which represents the maximum number of logits we want to consider. We set max answer length to 30, which represents the largest number of tokens that can constitute a valid answer, and we set predicted answers to an empty list. We will populate this list in the following loop.

So our loop will be through each sample in the original tokenized data set. In practice, this would be the validation set we loaded in, but for this example it will be the small subsample version of the validation set. Inside the loop we begin by grabbing the sample ID and the context from the ID and context fields for the current sample. Note that we use the context because this contains the answer as text. The next step is to initialize the best score, which will start as minus infinity and the best answer which will start as none.

Unlike the previous example, we will not bother to store the best data and we will instead skip straight to computing the answer text itself. The next step is to use our dictionary that we created earlier that tells us which expanded input samples correspond to this sample ID. In other words, we are using the sample_id2idxs mapping to tell us which of the tokenized outputs to look at. This will give us the indices into the processed samples, as you recall.

Now just at a high level. Let's consider why we are doing this. We are doing this because we need to loop through every context window. At least one of these will contain the predicted answer for which the corresponding score is highest. Some of these context windows will not contain the answer at all. So basically we're going to loop through each of these context windows to find the best answer. So inside the loop we find the start logits and end logits for this particular model input.

As you recall, the logits are of size N by T for N inputs and t time steps. This will give us a vector of size T for each of the logits. The next step is to grab the offset mapping for this particular model input. As you recall, we modified this in our tokenized function above. Specifically, it started out as a list of tuples where each tuple contain the character positions of the start and ends of each token. However, we modified it so that for any position that did not correspond to the context, we replace the value with none.

As such, this is an easy way to determine whether or not we are within the context. The next step is to call argsort on the start logit and end logit. Note that we negate these arrays before we call argsort, since by default it sorts in ascending order. Since we want the largest logits first we negate the array which will cause the sorting to be descending instead. The next step is to loop through the first n_largest of both the start and end indices.

This is basically our nested for loop from earlier in this lecture. Inside the loop, we check our three cases that result in invalid answers. The first case we check is that the answer is not contained within the context. To do this, we can index the modified offset mapping at start_idx and end_idx. Since the modified offset mapping contains none for any token not in the context, we just need to check if either of these is none. If that is the case, we say continue.

Since there is nothing more to do, as these indices do not correspond to a valid answer. The next scenario we check is if end_idx is less than start_idx. If this is the case, we continue. The third scenario we check is if the length of the answer is greater than the maximum allowed answer length. This is basically the difference between end_idx and start_idx. And if this is the case, again, we continue. Finally, if we reach this point and we haven't yet continued on to the next iteration, we can compute the score.

As you recall, the score is just the sum of the start and end logits. Then we check if this score is better than our best current score. If it is, we set a best score to the current score and we update the best answer. At this point, we actually convert the answer back into text. To do this, we use the offset mapping once again. Again, recall that the offset mapping contains tuples with the character positions of start and end tokens. As such, if we want the first character of the answer, we index the offset mapping at start_idx.

This will give us a tuple of the start and end locations of the first token. We index this tuple at position zero because that's where it starts. This will give us the first character position of the answer. Similarly to find the last character, we index the offset mapping at end_idx and we grab the second element of the tuple there. Finally, we can index the full context at the first character up to the last character, and this will give us the answer as text.

The final thing we do once we've determined the best answer for this sample ID is to save it as a dictionary to a list of predicted answers. As you recall, this is the expected format when we call metric compute. At this point we can compute the true answers as well, since they also require a special format. Specifically, we make sure that each answer is a dictionary which contains the sample ID and a key called Answers that stores the answers from the original data sets.

And finally, once we have a list of predicted answers and a list of true answers, we can call metric compute.

====================================

So in this lecture, we will be continuing our notebook. As a reminder, our goal right now is to use metric compute. However, we can't use this until we are able to get the predicted answers into text format, since as you saw, that is the required input. Now, in order to develop our solution, we won't use the whole data set and we won't use our untrained model since we want to just see that it works. Instead, we'll take a small subsample of our validation set and we'll use a model already

fine-tuned on the SQuAD data set. To begin, let's create a small validation set, which will just be the first 100 samples from our full validation data set. The next step is to define a trained checkpoint, which will be DistilBERT fine-tuned on SQuAD. As you recall, we'll also need to load in the corresponding tokenizer since these are checkpoint specific. We'll call this tokenizer2. Now, one issue with this is that we use the tokenizer variable as a global variable in some of the functions above.

Instead of rewriting them or having to add new arguments. We'll just temporarily replace the existing tokenizer variable as we process the data. To do this, we'll save the existing tokenizer in a new variable called old_tokenizer. Then we set tokenizer to be tokenizer2. Next we map the tokenized FN validation function to the small validation data set. Once this is done, we change our tokenizer back to the old version. The next step is to pass this data through our trained model.

Interestingly, we haven't had to do this most of the time since to train the model. We just use the trainer and once training is complete, we just use the pipeline. So this should be interesting just to see how we actually use the model to get the outputs. To begin, we import torch and we also import a new kind of model, AutoModelForQuestionAnswering. The next step is to remove irrelevant columns that are not used by the model. These are sample ID and offset mapping.

We'll call the result small_model_inputs. We also set the format to torch. The next step is to get our GPU device. As you recall, when we use PyTorch, we need to explicitly move tensors to the GPU. The next step is to move our data tensors to the GPU. Recall that the model inputs acts like a dictionary. So that's why, as you can see here, we convert them to an actual dictionary. Basically what this is doing is looping through each column of the inputs, grabbing the tensor at that column.

Calling to device on that tensor and mapping it to the same key, which here is called K. This new dictionary is called small_model_inputs_gpu. The next step is to download our model from the trained checkpoint. Note that we also move the model to the GPU. Finally, now that everything is on the GPU, we call our trained model passing in the GPU inputs to get the output. Note that we use torch.no_grad because we don't want to compute gradients. The next step is to print out the outputs just to see what we get.

As you can see, we get an object of type question answering model outputs. As usual, this basically acts like a named tuple and we can see that it has two attributes which are start logits and end logits. The next step is to grab these start logits and end logits as NumPy arrays. Before we get the NumPy arrays, we must also move them back to the CPU. The next step is to remind ourselves what the sample IDs look like. As you can see, there are alphanumeric strings.

The next step is to check the length of our validation dataset's sample IDs to remind ourselves that they are not unique. As you can see, we have 10,822 sample IDs. Now to show that they are not unique. We'll put the sample IDs into a set, which is a data structure in which all values must be unique. We'll then print the length of this set. As you can see, when we make the sample IDs unique, we only have 10,570 sample IDs. Since the length of the set is less than the length of the original list, this means that some items are duplicates.

However, note that this is for the full validation set and not necessarily for the subsampled validation set. Either way, we should write code to handle the case where the sample IDs are not unique. What we need right now is a data structure that points from sample ID to all possible indices in the processed data set. That is to say, when the context is split up into multiple samples, we want to have some way of mapping to all the samples corresponding to the original sample ID in order to do this.

We begin by creating an empty dictionary which will store the sample ID as the key and sample indices as the value. The next step is to loop through all the sample IDs. We use enumerate to get the index at the same time. Inside the loop, we check whether or not the ID exists in our mapping. If it does not, then we create a new entry for this ID which will point to a list containing the current index i. Otherwise the sample ID already exists. In this case, we print a little message saying here and we append i to the existing list.

So as you can see, the word here never got printed out, meaning that there were no duplicate sample IDs in this list. The next step is to confirm the shape of our logits. As you recall, we expect them both to have the shape N by T where n is the number of samples and t is the padded sequence length. So as you can see, they both have the shape 100 by 384, which is what we expect. Note that the number of samples is still 100. Since none of the contexts were long enough to be expanded as we saw.

The next step is just a reminder of how to sort the indices of an array. Recall that we don't want to just sort the array because we don't care about the values themselves. Instead, we care about where the values are. For example, if time step five is the highest logit, then we want five, not the logit. This is why we call argsort instead of just sort. Note also that we negate the array first. Recall that when sorting we sort by ascending order by default.

If we negate the values first, this will effectively sort them in descending order. In this way, the largest values will always be at the front. So these are the sort indices. So let's see what happens if we plug the sort indices back into the original array. So as you can see, when we index the original array by the argsort indices, it is in fact sorted in descending order. The next step is to remind ourselves of the work we did to modify the offset mapping for the validation set.

As a reminder, since offset mapping is such an ambiguous name, we originally stored a list of tuples containing the start and end character positions of each token. We modified this for the validation set. The way we modified it was to get rid of any tuples not pertaining to the context. We set those values to none. So as you can see, we get a list containing many Nones and somewhere in the middle we start to see tuples of integers in increasing order.

These are the character positions of each token in the context which will be used in the next step. The next step is where we finally write some code to convert our model outputs into actual text answers. We begin by setting n_largest to 20. Recall that this is the number of start logits and end logits that we want to search. We also set an max answer length to 30, which is the maximum answer size we want to allow. Finally, we set predicted answers to be an empty list, which is where we will store the answers.

The next step is to loop through the original sub sampled validation set. Recall that this is tokenized inside the loop. We grab the sample ID and the context. Next we initialize best_score and best_answer. We will update these as we find better answers in the below loop. The next step is to loop through the expanded input samples. These are the samples that have been tokenized in order to retrieve the correct samples. We use our dictionary, which points from sample ID to the index of the tokenized samples that came from this sample ID.

Inside the loop. We obtain the start logits and end logits for this particular index. These are both size T arrays. The next step is to grab the offset mapping for this index. Recall that this is the processed and modified offset mapping which contains none for any position, not in the context. The next step is to sort the logits. The next step is to loop through the n_largest start logits and the n_largest end logits. Inside the loop we check the three cases for which an answer does not exist.

The first case we check is that the answer is not contained within the context. For this, we can use the offset mapping. Recall that the offset mapping is none for any token, not in the context. So if we index the context at start_idx and it's none, then we are not in the context. If we index the context at end_idx and that is none, then we are also not in the context. In either case, we continue. The next case to check is, if end_idx is less than start_idx.

In this case, the answer has negative length, which doesn't make sense. So we continue again. The third case we check is if the answer is too long. That is longer than max answer length. If this is the case, we continue. Finally, we handle the case where we do have a legitimate answer. In this case. We start by computing the score for this answer, which is the start logit plus the end logit. If you need to review why this is the score, please check the previous lecture.

The next step is to check whether or not this score is better than our current score. If it is, we'll save this score and compute the answer. So we set best score to be the current score. Next we grab the positions of the first character of the start token and the last character of the end token. As you recall, we can use the offset mapping since this is the information it stores. Finally, we retrieve the answer as actual text by indexing the context from the range first to last.

Once the loop is complete, we save the best answer using the format we learned about above. As you recall, this is a dictionary that stores the sample ID and prediction_text. The next step is to just remind ourselves of the format of the true answers. As you can see, they are dictionaries containing the answer_start character indices in the corresponding text. So this is the exact format we need when we call metric compute. Aside from putting this into a parent dictionary containing the sample ID as well.

The next step is to format the true answers as described above. We want a list of dictionaries, each of which stores the ID and the answers formatted like how we just saw. Once this is complete, we can call metric compute. So as you can see, this is the score for the trained checkpoint. We get 83 for exact match in 88.25 for F1.

14. Computing Metrics
=====================

So now that we know how to actually convert our model predictions into actual answers such that we can call metric compute, we are now going to write our function for compute metrics. Now normally in this course, you've seen that we can pass compute metrics into the trainer object, at which point the function would be called at the end of each training epoch. In this section, that won't be the case. Instead, we'll be calling the compute metrics function manually after training is complete.

Furthermore, we won't be following the usual API for compute metrics where the input is just a tuple of logits and labels. This would allow us to compute a validation loss, but in this case we'd prefer to look at the actual answers themselves. If you'd like to compute a validation loss based on the logits. You're welcome to do that as well as an exercise. So we'll begin by importing tqdm, which allows us to display a progress bar as our function loops through the data set.

The next step is to define our compute metrics function, which will not follow the same structure as we saw earlier in the course. As input into this function, we will take in an array of start logits, an array of end logits, the processed data set and the original data set. Recall that the process data set is the one where we've applied the tokenizer and the context. Windows have been expanded into multiple samples. Note that essentially all of this function will be a review of the previous lectures, so we'll go over this quickly.

We begin by creating a mapping from sample ID to the row index of the processed data set. This will be a dictionary called sample_id2idxs. To populate this dictionary. We loop through the sample IDs from the processed data set. We use enumerate so that we get the index as well, which we call i. Inside the loop, we check whether or not the ID is already in the dictionary. If it is not, we create a new entry where the value is a list containing the current index i.

Otherwise if the list already exists, then we append i to the existing list. The next step is to compute the predicted answers. We start by defining an empty list. The next step is to loop through the original data set using tqdm. Inside this loop we begin by grabbing the current samples ID as well as the context. The next step is to initialize the best score and the best answer. The next step is to loop through all the indices from the processed input samples that correspond with the current sample ID.

Inside this loop we grab the start and end logits at position index. We also grab the offset mapping at position index. At this point, it should be noted that the order in which you index the process data set is very important. Although both ways will work, one way is very slow and will not complete in a feasible amount of time. This is just due to how the data set object is implemented. So the better way to index the offset mapping is to first grab the desired row by using index and then grabbing the offset mapping attributes, not in the reverse order.

The next step is to sort the logits in descending order, as you've seen. Once we've done that, we can do a nested loop over the n_largest indices for both the start and end logits inside these nested for loops, we check the three conditions where no answer exists to review. This is what they are. Number one, we want to skip answers that are not fully contained within the context window. We know this is the case. If we index the offset, mapping at either start or end and it returns none.

Number two, we skip answers where the end position is less than the start position, which is impossible. And number three, we skip answers that exceed the maximum length we set above. If none of these three conditions are triggered, we compute the score for the current answer. We then check whether this score is better than our current best score. If it is, we make this our new best score and we also save the answer in a variable called best answer. We do this by indexing the context at the first character up to the last character, which we retrieve from the offset mappings.

Once we've completed the nested loop, we add our predicted answer to our list of predicted answers. And once we've populated all the predicted answers, we collect the true answers in another list in the format that metric compute expects. Finally, we call metric compute and return the result.

15. Computing Metrics in Python
===============================

So this lecture will be pretty simple since all we're going to do is take what we did previously and put it into a function which we will call compute metrics. The important thing to remember is that this is not like the compute metrics functions that we wrote earlier in the course. We will not be passing this into the trainer and the arguments are different. We will only run this function manually after training is complete. We'll begin by importing tqdm, which will display a progress bar as the function runs.

The next step is to define the compute metrics function as input. It takes in the start logits, end logits, the processed data set and the original data set. As you recall, the process data set is the one where the context has been split up into multiple windows. Inside the function. We begin by creating a mapping from sample ID to all the corresponding indices in the processed data set. This way, given any particular sample ID, we can find all the processed samples that belong to it.

Since you've seen this code before, I won't explain it again. The next step is to create an empty list where we will store our predicted answers. The next step is to loop over the original data set. Note that we use tqdm at this point. Inside the loop. We grab the sample ID for the sample and the context. The next step is to initialize the best score to minus infinity and the best answer to none. The next step is to find all the processed samples that come from this sample ID.

At this point, we use our mapping to get all the corresponding indices in the process data set, and we loop through this list of indices. Inside the loop. We use the current index to find the start logit array and the end logit array. Recall that these are both size T vectors. The next step is to retrieve the offset mapping for this sample. One thing that becomes very important at this point is to index the processed data set in the right order. Because of the way the dataset object is implemented.

It will be very slow if you retrieve the offset mapping first and the index second. On the other hand, if you want it to go faster, then you should index by index first and the offset mapping second. The next step is to sort the logits in descending order. The next step is to loop through the n_largest logit indices for both start and end. Inside this loop, we check the three cases for invalid answers once again. To review the first case is where the answer does not fully lie within the context window.

The second case is where the end is less than the start, which wouldn't make sense. And the third case is where the answer is too long. If we reach the next part of the loop, then the next thing to do is compute the score for this pair of logits. The next step is to check whether the score is better than our current best score. If it is, we first update the best score to be the current score. The next step is to use the offset mapping to find the first character and the last character of the answer within the full context.

Finally, we update the best answer to store the answer given by these character positions. After we exit the loop, we save the best answer we found along with the corresponding sample ID as a side note. Notice that for any particular sample ID there will always be one best answer. This is because we loop through all context windows and pick the best one. Once we finish populating the predicted answers list, we then compile the true answers like we did above.

Finally, we call metrics compute to return the metrics for this task. The next step is to test our function to make sure that it works. Note that since we haven't yet trained our model, we'll just use the same values for our subsampled data set that we used above. This is good also so that we can ensure that we get the same result. So as you can see, we still get the same result. 83 for exact match and 88.25 for F1.

16. Train and Evaluate
======================

So at this point, we finally have all the pieces we need in order to conclude this notebook. All that's left to do is train and evaluate this model. As usual, we begin by loading up a pre-trained model, but we need to make sure that we use the correct type of model. In this case, we'll use the class AutoModelForQuestionAnswering. As usual, we get a warning that the model head has not been trained. The next step, as usual, is to create a training arguments object.

None of these inputs should be particularly surprising at this point. Note that we use FP16 in order to speed up training. The next step is to create a trainer object. Again, none of these inputs should be particularly surprising at this point. After we create the trainer objects we call trainer.train to begin the training process. Note that the training process will take quite some time to complete. If you use BERT, this will take about two and a half hours on the full training set.

If you use DistilBERT, it takes about half this time. After training. What we would like to do is call our compute metrics function on the validation sets. However, this requires retrieving the model outputs and specifically the logits. To do this we call trainer.predict passing in the processed validation set. This will give us a prediction output object. Interestingly, although it's not obvious, this object can be split into a triple. The first item in this triple has the data we need, specifically a tuple of start and end logits represented as numpy arrays.

At this point, we can call the compute metrics function passing in these logits along with the processed and original validation data sets. So once you've trained the model, you might want to actually use it in the real world. As usual, we can make this easy by using pipelines. We begin by calling trainer.save_model to save the model to some desired location. Once we've done this, we create a new pipeline object specifying the task as question answering.

As you learned earlier in this course, we can then use this pipeline by passing in any context and question pair. The result will be a dictionary containing the answer along with the corresponding score.

17. Train and Evaluate in Python
================================

So in this lecture we will continue our notebook with the train and evaluation steps. We'll begin by calling from_pretrained and passing in our model checkpoints. Recall that this is a model with randomly initialized weights of the head, which we are going to fine tune. The next step is to create our training arguments object. As you can see, evaluation strategy has been set to no since we will call compute metrics manually, we'll save it every epoch and we set FP16 to true to speed up the training process.

The next step is to instantiate a trainer object. Since you've seen this before, there's not much to explain. Note that I've commented out a line of code which you can use if you just want to test this out quickly. This will randomly select 1000 samples out of the whole train set, so the training process will complete in a shorter amount of time. Also note that this takes about two and one half hours if you use BERT and about half that time if you use DistilBERT.

For this reason, we've used DistilBERT in this notebook. However, you're encouraged to try BERT by yourself. So let's call trainer.train. The next step is to evaluate our model. In order to do this, we need to get the predictions for the validation set. We can do this by calling trainer.predict. Now it's not clear what the output is, so let's check its type. As you can see, it's an object of type prediction output. The next step is to print the object itself just to see what it looks like.

So it seems to hold some arrays and some information about how long it took to compute. Now, it's not at all obvious that you can or should do this, but basically you can expand this trainer output object just like you would if it were a tuple. By doing this, we get three items, the first of which are the predictions we need. Let's now print out the predictions. So as you can see, we get a tuple of two arrays. These are the start logits and the end logits.

Let's now assign the start logits and end logits to their own variable names. The next step is to call the compute_metrics function passing in the logits we just got back. As you can see, the last two arguments are the processed data set and the original data set, both of which need to be passed in. So we get an exact match score of about 77% and an F1 score of about 85%. Note that if you use BERT, you can improve this result. The next step is to call trainer.save_model

to save the model to disk. The next step is to demonstrate how to use the fine tune model. As you recall, pipelines make this convenient.
