18. Question-Answering Section Summary
======================================

So in this lecture, we will be summarizing everything we learned in this section. This section at a high level was relatively straightforward. We have a new task question answering, and we wanted to learn how to fine tune a model for this task. It involves all the usual steps, the tokenize, the metrics, trainer pipeline and so forth. What made it more challenging was all the manual coding we had to do in order to glue all the pieces together. As you saw, it was definitely not as straightforward as the earlier fine tuning examples in this course.

So the first challenge we encountered was how to deal with long contacts. Although we could have extended the context window size, there would still be contexts that would have to be truncated. Furthermore, recall that transformers have quadratic time complexity. In other words, making the input length longer by some amount does not increase the processing time in proportion. Instead, it increases the time squared. As such smaller context, windows may be desirable.

The challenge with splitting up the context into multiple samples is that the answers become misaligned. Some context windows might not even contain the answer at all. Therefore, a lot of the effort we put into this section was to deal with this issue. The second challenge we encountered was how to compute metrics. We do have a function that will compute the metrics for us, but the challenge was actually converting the transformer outputs into a format that would be appropriate for the metric compute function.

We had to look at an algorithm to convert the output logits into an answer represented as text. This involved a bit of probability and a lot of coding. One of the challenging aspects of this section was keeping track of which variables look at character positions and which variables look at token positions. Also challenging is that many different things are called IDs with generic names like sequence ID. Now, one question to consider after going through this section might be How do I apply this to my own data sets?

Perhaps you'd like to build a question answering system for biology or a question answering system for history or a question answering system for legal documents. That would be nice since these kinds of documents tend to be convoluted and difficult to read. Well, at this point it would be helpful to remember my rule. All data is the same. By recalling this rule, you'll realize that nothing actually changes at this point in the course. We've had so much practice working with custom data sets, you will have no problem creating your own custom data set in the same format as what we looked at for this task.

Now, this always reminds me of a question I get sometimes, which is how do I actually get the data? Well, the reality is you have to create the data yourself or pay someone to collect it for you. The follow up question is, Well, why can't I automate it? Of course, this is sort of a circular question. Remember that what we just built is a question answering system. If we already had a computer program that could automate question answering perfectly such that the process could be done without a human, then we wouldn't be building such a system in the first place.

Of course, you may find data that already exists that you can make use of, but this would be the exception and not the rule. So as it stands, we still need human experts for this process.

1. Theory Section Introduction
==============================

So in this lecture we will be introducing the next section of this course, which is all about the theory behind Transformers. This section is where you will learn how Transformers actually work. To contrast this with the previous sections. Note that they were purely about how to use pre-existing libraries. Everything was done with just a few function calls. And thanks to this, we were able to accomplish a lot, even without knowing anything at all about how Transformers work.

Of course, there may come a time where you realize that you do in fact need to know how Transformers work. And if that's the case, then this section is for you. So let's look at a brief outline of this section and what you can expect to learn. Firstly, note that this section is purely theoretical. There will be no coding since it will take a significant amount of time just to understand the concepts. The next section will look at implementations. So you can go there if you want to see how to actually build a transformer from scratch.

However, do note that everything you need to build a transformer will be covered in this section. This section is code agnostic, so it doesn't matter if you use TensorFlow, PyTorch, or any other deep learning library. You should be able to take this theory and go implement transformers yourself. This is really the exercise for this section. If you can go directly from theory to code, then you can be sure that you understand transformers. Now, since this section is all conceptual, it's worth talking about how to make sure you are prepared.

Essentially, if you want to understand Transformers, it would be very helpful to have a good understanding of CNNs and RNNs. We'll be drawing on that knowledge multiple times throughout these lectures. You'll want to understand how convolutional filters work and specifically why it acts like a feature finder by using a simple dot product. You'll also want to understand how we can stack convolutional filters together so that we can find multiple features in an image.

These stacked filters are what give us the eventual convolutional layer that we used in CNNs. You'll want to understand some advanced CNN models, specifically the ResNet, which uses what we call skip connections. In addition, it would be helpful to understand other layers we use in CNNs other than convolution, such as pooling and batch norm. In terms of RNNs, it would be very useful to understand how RNNs can be used for NLP specifically for the same tasks we covered earlier in this course.

Like classifying text, classifying each token and sequence to sequence prediction. Furthermore, you'll want to understand how to track the shapes in such RNNs. Understand the shape of the inputs, the shape of the embeddings, and the shape of the hidden states. If you can do all of these things, then understanding transformers will be very simple. In terms of actual topics, we'll begin with a very basic form of self attention. This will lay the groundwork for the scaled dot-product attention, which will be discussed next.

After this, we'll add the attention mask, which you've seen earlier in this course. So by learning the theory, you will understand exactly what this does and how it's used. The next step will be to combine multiple attention heads together to form what we call multi head attention. However, even this is incomplete. It's merely a building block in a larger layer. In the next lecture, we'll discuss all the other building blocks we need and then combine them all to form the transformer block.

To give you an analogy, the transformer block is to transformers as the linear layer is to ANNs, meaning just as we build ANNs by stacking linear layers, we build transformers by stacking transformer blocks. From there, we'll go directly into how to build an encoder. As you recall, BERT is an example of an encoder. So at this point you'll understand how to build a BERT from scratch. The next step will be to look at how to build a decoder. As you recall, GPT is an example of that.

And the next step, which should come as no surprise, is how to combine the encoder and decoder to build a seq2seq transformer. As you recall, these types of transformers can be used for things like machine translation. After understanding the general architecture of Transformers, we will then look at specific instances of Transformers and the features that make them unique. Specifically, we'll look at BERT, GPT, GPT-2 and GPT-3, which have been the most popular and noteworthy transformers thus far.

2. Basic Self-Attention
=======================

So in this lecture we will begin discussing a basic form of self attention. The goal of this lecture is to gain an intuition for how attention works and then expand on this idea in the subsequent lectures. We'll begin this lecture by briefly reviewing how attention works in seq2seq. At this point, you may want to review the beginners corner in more detail. So the main thing we're trying to compute in RNNs with attention is the context vector.

As you recall, the context vector is the input to the decoder RNN in seq2seq. Looking at the context vector in more detail, we see that it's simply a weighted sum of the hidden states computed on the input sequence that is for each output time step t, the context vector for this time step, c of t, is the weighted sum of all the input hidden states across the input time steps t prime. This makes sense for the output time step t we want to know which input time step t prime to pay attention to, and because it's deep learning, we don't just pick a one time step, but we use a soft selection so that gradients can pass through.

As you recall, the weights for each of the input time steps are called the attention weights denoted by the symbol Alpha. Alpha is indexed by both t and t prime since it tells us how much input hidden state t prime matters to output time step t. Now briefly, how do we compute these attention weights? Well, as you recall, this is a weighted sum, so it makes sense that they should all be positive and sum to one. Luckily, we know a function that can meet this constraint and it's called the softmax.

So the attention weights are the softmax over all input time steps t prime over some score value, also indexed by t and t prime. Now how do we compute this score? The answer for this lecture is not too important, but if you're curious, it's just the output of a feedforward neural network that takes as input the hidden states of the encoder and concatenate them with the previous hidden state of the decoder s of t minus one. Now, if that sounds complicated, don't worry, as it's not really necessary to understand the self attention for Transformers.

The critical part to understand is we get the weights by taking a softmax. Okay. So with this background in place, we can now begin to understand a self attention. Basically, it's almost like RNN attention, but without the RNN. So suppose we have a sequence of feature vectors x1, x2 all the way up to x big T in this case, big T equals five. Now suppose we'd like to compute a new context vector, which is the weighted sum of these x's. In practice, we're going to compute a context vector for every time step, so we'll have A1, A2 all the way up to a big T since the computation for each time step is the same.

We can consider just one of these context vectors, a_i. As a side note, please be aware that the reason I've decided to use the index I instead of the index t as we did before with RNNs is because this tends to be the convention when describing self attention. Now, since this is for intuition, let's consider the meaning of these X's and these A's. Since we're doing NLP, you can think of the X's as embedding vectors, just like you would have for a CNN or RNN.

This is where we just map each word in our vocabulary to a vector. But these vectors are not context aware. For instance, the word bank always gets mapped to the same embedding vector, whether you mean river bank or whether you mean a financial institution. However, the A's, on the other hand, are context aware embeddings. These also can be thought of as embedding vectors, since they are, after all, still vectors. And furthermore we have one of them for each time step.

So for each word in the input sentence we have a context aware embedding vector a_i for i from one up to big T. As you can see, the way we compute a_i is similar to how we compute the context vector in attention for seq2seq RNNs. It's the weighted sum of x_j's where the weights are called alpha ij. Now the question is how do we get alpha ij? Well, in this simple form of self attention alpha is easy to compute relative to the RNN. In any case, let's first begin with a basic premise which you should understand well.

If you've studied convolutional neural networks or deep learning more broadly, the premise is that the dot product is like a measure of similarity or affinity. To give you some intuition for why this is the case, recall that the dot product is just an element wise product and summation of two vectors. However, you'll recall that if the two vectors both have a length of one, then this is equal to the cosine of the angle between the two vectors. As you recall, this is because the dot product is equivalent to the magnitude of x times, the magnitude of Y times, the cosine of the angle between x and Y.

But what do you get when you take the cosine of the angle between two vectors? Well, that's simply the cosine similarity. So the dot product is almost like the cosine similarity, except that we ignore the magnitudes of the vectors. Recall that cosine similarity is also closely related to the Pearson correlation coefficient. The equation for that is shown here, but please don't be worried by all the symbols. Basically what you want to be able to see is that the cosine similarity and the Pearson correlation are nearly the same, except that the Pearson correlation also subtracts the mean at every point.

Now, why is this important to know? Well, because it gives us yet another way of thinking of cosine similarity and in turn, another way of thinking of the dot product. To say two vectors are similar is almost like saying that they are correlated. In other words, these are all different ways of measuring a degree of sameness. Now. Why do we care about that? Well, another way to think of the dot product is in terms of convolutional neural networks. Basically, convolution is simply a sliding dot product where we slide some filter over an input image.

And at each point we take the dot product. Plotting all the resulting dot products gives us another image. This new image, which is in black and white, tells us where in the original image some feature was found. As an example, suppose my filter is an eye. If we slide this filter around an image of a face, we should find mostly small values close to zero after doing convolution, except where the eye is found. Where the AI is found, we should find large values since the filter will match the image in that particular location.

This creates a spike, or in terms of an image, a bright spot showing that this is where the eye can be found in the input image. That is to say, we find spots in the image where there is a match between the filter and the image itself. You can think of it like if the image matches the filter at this point, return one, otherwise return zero. In other words, dot products are nice because they are like feature finders or feature matchers. Using convolution.

We can train a neural network to find all kinds of features like eyes, noses, ears, glasses and so forth. After training a CNN, you can look at the filters to interpret what they have learned to find. Okay. So hopefully you're convinced that dot products are a way to measure similarity. Using this knowledge, we can now form our attention weights. You recall that we first started with a score. There's no constraint on the score, so it can be positive or negative and large or small.

The score between input i and j, as you may have guessed, is simply the dot product between x_i and x_j. You can think of this as how similar are word i and word j in terms of their embeddings. We call these scores e_ij. In order to convert these into attention weights, we take the softmax over the j index. This will ensure that the weights are all positive and sum to one. And so now we know how to fully calculate the simple form of self attention to obtain context aware embeddings from a sequence of input vectors.

We first compute the attention weights as previously described. Then the context vector simply becomes the weighted average of input embeddings weighted by these alphas.

3. Self-Attention & Scaled Dot-Product Attention
================================================

So in this lecture we will continue our discussion of self attention by adding a few new components to the simple form of self attention we just described. So let's consider why we might want to do this. Recall that in the previous lecture we computed the attention weights based on the input of vectors themselves. This means that for fixed inputs, these attention weights would always be fixed. In other words, there are no learnable parameters. In this lecture we will introduce some learnable parameters which will make the self attention mechanism more flexible and tunable for various tasks.

Now, one common way for people to introduce transformers is to immediately jump to this concept of queries, keys and values. Personally, I found this to be very confusing because the concept of queries, keys and values bears no relation to anything that came before in deep learning. It's not clear how we can use our existing knowledge of deep learning to derive this concept. In fact, the very first equation that shows up in the paper called Attention is All you need is this attention expression involving a softmax and matrices Q, K and V representing queries, keys and values.

In this lecture, I will show you where this concept comes from and how introducing new learnable parameters helps us to implement this idea. Our goal in this lecture will be to understand this attention matrix expression. We'll also see how this is easily built on our basic form of self attention that we looked at in the previous lecture. We'll begin by discussing where this concept of queries, keys and values come from. Please note that this is optional.

There is no need to understand how this works. In order to understand self attention. But it could help. In fact, you are already familiar with the concept of keys and values from dictionaries in Python. Dictionaries allow us to store data by using key value pairs. Each key points to a corresponding value. Of course, there are actual database technologies which use the same key value paradigm. Some popular examples are Redis, Memcached and Amazon's DynamoDB.

And of course, when you have a database, you often want to query the database to obtain data you might need for your application. So for example, the keys might be user IDs and the values might be your user preferences like video speed, language, whether captions should be on or off and so forth. And when we want to look up certain data in the database, we would query it with the user ID we are looking for. The database will return the data for which the query and key match.

This should sound familiar. The goal of introducing learnable parameters in self attention is to transform the input vectors. That is, all the x's into their respective roles as queries, keys and values. Okay. So what does self-attention look like when we introduce these parameters or weights? Well, as you may have expected, it's really nothing more than matrix multiplication. This is deep learning, after all. So we obtain the query vector. When we multiply X by WQ, we obtain the key vector.

When we multiply x by w k and we obtain the value vector when we multiply x by WV. And of course, we do this for all the X's from time step one up to Big T, so we'll have corresponding sequences of Qs, Ks and Vs. So what do we do with these Qs, Ks and Vs? Well, we do the exact same thing we saw in the basic form of self attention in order to make this less abstract. Let's use a running example. Suppose our input sentence is Bob cashed the check at the bank.

This is a useful example because as you know, the word check can have multiple meanings. It can mean, as in this example, a bill of exchange. Check can also be a verb. As in double check your homework. The word check has yet another meaning in the context of chess. Note that the word bank also has multiple meanings, but for the purpose of this lecture, let's just think of the word check. So check will correspond to our query vector q_i. Our model wants to know in what sense do we mean the word check?

In order to do that, we might want to pay attention to other words in the sentence, such as cashed and bank. So in order to compute these attention weights, we're going to dot this query vector q_i with all other key vectors k_j for j from one up to big T. This will give us big T attention scores. As you know, we then take the softmax, which will then give us big T attention weights. These attention weights are then used to form a weighted sum of the values.

Again, these are all vectors and we have one of them for each word in our sentence. So our attention vector for the word check will be the weighted sum of the value vectors. Now, in order to make this a bit less abstract, let's try to relate this back to our database analogy with graphics instead of equations. Firstly, as you can see here, we can imagine a vector space where all the key vectors live. These are denoted in blue. As you recall, there will be a key vector for every word in the input sentence, but only some of them are shown here to save space.

The purple vector represents our query, which is check. Importantly, note that the same word can be both the query and the key. Since it's possible for a word to pay attention to itself. So that's why Check can show up twice, once as a key and once as a query. Now you can see what's happening. What we're trying to do is find which key vector matches with the query. In vector space, we can think of a match as the vector that yields the largest dot product.

And in this case, that would be the word bank. As you can see, bank and check are very close together. Hence they are similar and would be considered the best match. So now we can think of what the attention weights might be. In this table we can see the dot products between the query and each key. Note that these are just made up numbers so they aren't designed to accurately reflect the graph on the left. Intuitively, the dot product between the query and the keys other than bank might be either very small or negative.

In other words, insignificant. The dot product between the query and bank might be large and positive. Now what happens when we take the softmax of these numbers? Well, the softmax will yield a number close to one. If an input is large relative to the others and a number close to zero otherwise. And again, these are made up numbers, but we'll say that the attention weight for bank is 0.94 and the attention weight for all the other words is 0.01.

As you can see, they add up to one. So what will the result be? Well, remember that after finding the attention weights, our attention output vector will be the weighted sum of value vectors weighted by these weights. In other words, we'll have 0.94 of the value vector for bank plus 0.01 of all the other value vectors in the sentence. Effectively, the attention output is almost just the value vector for bank, plus a bit of other stuff. Compare this to if we had taken the hard max instead of the softmax.

If that were the case, we would simply select whichever word gave us the highest dot product and use that value vector. Of course, in deep learning we don't do that. Now, although we technically answered this question on the previous slide, I want to state it more explicitly. Why do we want self attention? Admittedly, it seems kind of weird that we would want the input sentence to pay attention to itself. This is a bit different from how we introduced attention for RNNs, where each token in the output

had to decide which token from the input to pay attention to in order to generate a translation or a chatbot response? In this section, it appears that the input sequence merely pays attention to itself. And well, we've actually already seen why this is not weird. The example we saw was with word sense disambiguation. In other words, the word check with no context is ambiguous. We can disambiguate it by using the other words in the sentence. In particular, the words cashed and bank are clues that when we say check in this context, we mean the financial instrument and not, for instance, check your homework.

You can also imagine in your mind that the neural network can use this information to answer questions about the input where these answers appear as feature vectors. As an example, consider the sentence Alice drove to the store. Suppose the word store pays attention to the word drove. This might answer the question. How did Alice arrive at the store? Perhaps store pays attention to the word Alice. This might answer the question Who was at the store? Perhaps Alice pays attention to the word store.

This might answer the question. Where did Alice go? So you get the idea. This is just like how in convolutional neural networks we are looking to find certain features. Like, is there an eye in this image? Is there a nose? And if so, maybe this is a face. Now at this point, it's a good idea to introduce the concept of understanding shapes. If you've taken my courses before, then you know that this is a very important aspect of deep learning. It's important.

From linear regression up to ANNs, CNNs and RNNs, transformers are no different. While it's good to understand the intuition, if you ever want to actually build this stuff in code, you need to answer some basic questions. For instance, how big are the Q, K and V vectors? How big are WQ, WK and WV? Are there any constraints? Well, there must be, since we dot Q and K together implying that Q and K must have the same shape using the conventions of the Attention Is All You Need

paper, Q and K both have size d sub k. The value vector V has dimension D sub v. Importantly, both the input and output of a full attention layer have the same dimension, which they call d model. The reason for this will become clear when we discuss how to build the transformer block. But for now, just accept it as fact. Because of this, WQ and WK both have the shape d model by d_k and WV has the shape d model by d_v. In the Attention Is All You Need paper,

they set d_k and d_v both to 64 while D model is 512. In other words, while the input vectors are very large, they are compressed into relatively small dimension vectors when they are converted into queries, keys and values. So now that you know what the shapes are, let's do some math. So suppose that all the Xs have size 512. After mapping them to Qs, Ks and Vs, we get vectors of size 64. When we take the dot product between Q and K, we get a scalar and taking the softmax of several of these just gives us another scalar.

As such, our context aware embedding vectors will be the sum of Vs and hence will have the same size as the Vs, which is 64. Now you recall that I said the output of a full attention layer should still be d model. This will be addressed when we discuss multi-head attention. For now our output will have size d_v. Now there's one detail we haven't mentioned yet, which is that before we apply the softmax, we actually scale the product between the query and the key for some intuition on why we do this.

You want to look back at our study of linear regression and neural networks. You recall that when we initialize our weights, we divide by the square root of the dimensionality of w and x, which we normally call D. By doing this, we ensure that the output of the dot product has variance one if we assume that both the inputs and the weights also have variance one and are independent. In other words, this scales the variance of the output down, which keeps it from exploding.

Similarly, in this case, we divide Q dotted with K by the square root of D sub k, which is the dimensionality of both Q and K. And so this is why you may see the attention operation referred to as scaled dot product attention. We're doing a dot product between Q and K and then we're scaling by the square root of D sub k. Now. I promised you that by the end of this lecture we would understand the expression from the Attention Is All You Need paper. We are not quite there yet, but we are close.

The final step is to realize that we have a bunch of summations over element wise products. As I taught you in the prerequisites, whenever you see a summation over element wise products, you know that it's just matrix multiplication and hence can be vectorized. So let's see how to do that. Firstly, let's suppose our input x is a sequence of vectors stored as a matrix with the shape t by D model. Note that in this case each row of x is a feature vector for a specific time step.

Using this, we can get corresponding matrices for the queries, keys and values which we will call Big Q, Big K and Big V. We get these as usual, by multiplying by the corresponding W matrices, since WQ has the shape d model by d_k, Q will have the shape T by d_k. As you recall, when you do matrix multiplication, the inner dimension disappears. Similarly, K also has the shape T by d_k and V has the shape T by DV. The next step is to multiply Q by K transpose.

Now, why does this make sense? Well, since K has the shape T by d_k, K transpose will have the shape d_k by t since Q has the shape T by d_k and K transpose has the shape d_k by T. The result will be T by T. Mathematically, you should convince yourself that this results in multiplying every row of Q by every row of K. These rows are the different query and key vectors at different time steps. And this makes perfect sense, since as you recall, for every query vector we have t attention weights one for every input.

Furthermore, we have t query vectors for each of the t inputs making up t times t attention weights in total. So this perfectly matches what we did earlier. Note that after we take the softmax, the shape doesn't change. Dividing by the square root of d_k also does not change the shape. The final step is to multiply the attention weights by V, which has the shape T by d_v. So if we have a T by T multiplied by a T by d_v, again the inner dimension disappears and we get a T by d_v result.

This makes sense since we should end up with t attention vectors each with size dv. So using these shape calculations, you should be convinced that this matrix expression does in fact compute self attention like we previously described. Finally, note that in code we further vectorize the attention computation by handling multiple samples at once. So instead of just a T by D input, we have N by T by D. Now, one more thing before we conclude this lecture.

What is the difference between self attention and scaled dot product attention? In this lecture I've introduced both of these concepts at the same time, so it's important not to get them confused. To be clear, scaled dot product attention is the attention operation which can act on any queries, keys and values as described in this lecture. Self attention is where the queries, keys and values are all computed on the same input sequence. Hence the queries and keys both come from the same sequence and hence whichever key the query is paying attention to, it came from its own self sequence.

4. Attention Efficiency
=======================

Now, although our previous computation looked at a specific query word at location i, it should be clear that we are going to do this for all values of i from one up to big t, so every word in the input sentence will pay attention to some other word, possibly itself in the same input sentence. Because of this, it should be clear that we have a t squared computation for each word in the sentence. We have to compute attention weights for every word in the sentence.

That's a double for loop where both loops go from one up to Big T. As you know, t squared is quadratic in T, and if you plot the function t squared, hopefully you know that you will get a parabola. What you should recall is that parabolas grow fast. If our sequence length is t equals ten, then we have to compute 100 attention weights. If T equals 50, then we have to compute 2500 attention weights. If T equals 100, then we have to compute 10,000 attention weights.

In other words, the number of computations we need to do grows faster than the number of inputs we have. Eventually, the number of computations will get so large that we will need to limit the size of the inputs at some point. At the same time. Note that attention is still superior to RNNs for multiple reasons. Firstly, recognize that in our computation of the attention weights for q_i, we didn't have to consider the attention weights for any other query.

Q sub j where j is not equal to i. There should be a sense that we can parallelize this computation. For instance, if we have seven workers, they can compute all seven of the attention vectors at the same time. We don't have to wait for one to complete before we can compute the next. This is unlike RNNs, where for each hidden state h of T, we must have finished computing the previous hidden state h of t minus one since h of t depends on that value.

Another reason attention is superior to RNNs is that RNNs have trouble with long sequences, but not in the same way that attention does. Earnings have trouble remembering things too far in the past due to vanishing gradients. RNN units with gates such as the GRU and LSTM were created to fix this issue. But as we've demonstrated in my earlier courses, there is still a limit where these advanced units stop performing well. In fact, this was already demonstrated in the original RNN and attention paper as well.

It showed that as the number of tokens increased, RNNs without attention dropped in performance, while RNNs with attention maintained its performance. Here's another interesting fact to consider. Recall that RNNs are able to handle variable length sequences. This is possible since each h of t depends on h of t minus one. And there's no point at which we need to stop all the weights of an RNN unit do not depend on sequence length.

However, note that this is also true of the self attention weights. WQ and WK both have sized d model by DK and WV has D model by DV. The input into self attention has the shape T by D model and the output has the shape T by DV using only matrix multiplies. In other words, self attention is also capable of handling variable length sequences. Now, at this point, this is so advanced you probably forgot why this is significant. If that's the case, consider any model in scikit-learn.

These models require that all your inputs have the same dimension. Consider a regular feedforward ANN. This also requires that your inputs have the same dimension. Consider even a CNN without special modifications like global max pooling. This requires that all your images are the same size. So considering this, it's pretty neat that self attention can handle sequences of varying length. Although do note that because we want to use matrix multiplication, every input in the same batch must have the same length.

5. Attention Mask
=================

So in this lecture, we will be discussing one final addition to the self-attention mechanism, which is masking. You've already seen masking in this course since whenever we apply the tokenizer to a sentence, we always get back not just the token IDs but also the corresponding attention mask. Let's recall why we need the attention mask. There are two reasons. The second of which we'll understand in more detail later in the course. Reason number one is that, as you know, we need to pad our sequences so that each sequence in a single batch has the same sequence length based on the previous lectures.

You know that we must do this so that we can perform self attention using matrix multiplication, which is much faster because it is vectorized. When we introduce these pad tokens, we do not want our model to pay any attention to them as they are not relevant for understanding the sentence. Reason number two is related to the decoder. As you recall, the decoder is auto regressive, meaning it only looks at past tokens in order to predict the next token.

One way to accomplish this is to hide all the tokens beyond the last time step by using a mask. Intuitively, the function of the attention mask is very simple. Essentially, we want some way to set the attention weights for irrelevant tokens, such as padding to be zero. Now, how do we accomplish this? Well, at this point, we are ready to look at the original schematic for the self attention mechanism. This is from the paper. Attention is all you need. As you can see, we begin by taking a Q, K and V as inputs.

We then do a matrix multiply on Q and K. As you know, the next step is to scale the result. As you recall, we do this by dividing by the square root of d_k. After scaling, there is an optional step which is to apply the mask. After applying the mask, we apply the softmax. And after we apply the softmax, we do one final matrix multiply with V. Now, those of you paying close attention might notice something weird. What's weird about this is that the attention mask is applied before the softmax and not after.

So why is this weird? Well, we've seen in the code that in the attention mask, we use a one for locations where we do want to pay attention and we use a zero for locations where we do not want to pay attention. However, the softmax of zero is not zero and therefore it won't result in the corresponding attention weight being zero. As you recall, when we take the softmax, we first exponentiate all the values, but the exponent of zero is actually one.

The resolution to this is that in this formulation, the values in the attention mask are not actually zero for the locations where we do not want to pay attention. Instead, they are actually set to minus infinity, which is stated in the paper. Now just a minor note on convention. Notice how in this diagram no weights are included. Instead, queries, keys and values can be any matrices of inputs. Currently we are only looking at self attention, so it might not be clear why this is needed.

Since the queries, keys and values are all computed from the same x matrix. However, later in this section you'll see where this is not the case. And so this way of representing the attention operation becomes useful.

6. Multi-Head Attention
=======================

So in this lecture we will be continuing our discussion of the self attention mechanism. In particular, we're going to use the scaled dot product attention we previously discussed as a building block for a new block called Multi Head Attention. The best format for this lecture will be to start with how it works, followed by why it works. So how does multi head attention work currently? As it stands, our self attention mechanism only allows each token to pay attention to one thing using one set of attention weights.

But what if we would like our model to allow tokens to pay attention to multiple things? For instance, consider the sentence Bob cashed the check at the bank. Perhaps in one case, bank pays attention to the word cash so that we know it's a financial bank and not a river bank. In another instance, it might pay attention to Bob telling us who went to the bank. In this case, we create a new self attention layer with different weights. Specifically, suppose that we would like to compute this self-attention operation h times.

If we call the output of each individual self-attention a head, this means that we would like to have h heads. Then we would just need to have h different sets of weights. We'll call them W1Q, W1K and W1V, W2Q, W2K, W2V and so forth. In the Attention Is All You Need paper, they set the value of H to eight. Now of course after computing self-attention h times, we still need a way to combine the result back into a single block of sequence data which can then be passed into the next layer of the neural network.

The short answer is we can do that by concatenating all the heads and then multiplying by an output weight which we call w o. Of course the devil is in the details. As usual, it always helps to understand the shapes. We already know that the input to each self attention layer will be T by D model. The output will be T by DV as we previously showed. So what does it mean to concatenate these outputs? Well, we specifically concatenate them along the feature dimension rather than the time dimension.

So the result will be a bigger matrix of size t by h dv. Visually, you can imagine that if the output of each self attention is a small block after concatenation, these blocks will be lined up side by side. After doing this we multiply by w o which has the shape hd v by d model. In other words, we project the output back into its original dimensions. If we multiply a matrix of size T by hd v with another matrix of size DV by D model, the inner dimension will disappear and we will get back T by d model.

This also answers the question we previously posed, which was how can we make it so that the input and output both have the same size? The key is that the final step is to multiply by w o, which ensures that the output has the shape t by d model, which is the same as the input. Now just a minor note on convention: in the original paper, the diagram for a scaled dot product attention does not use any weights at all. Instead, the weights are included only in the multi head attention block, separate from the self attention operation.

Knowing this, we can understand the next diagram from the paper which shows a schematic for multi head attention. Firstly, you can see that there are h scaled dot product attention layers going in the depth direction. There are also as many linear layers which represent the weights. After going through each attention layers, we then concatenate the result and go through one final linear layer which represents w o. It's important to note again that just like attention itself, the operations we just discussed can be done in parallel.

Each self attention, it depends only on its own weights, and hence each head can be computed at the same time, leading to more efficient computation. This allows transformers to be faster, even though they may use more compute. Okay. So that's all there is to say about how multi-head attention works. But the important question now is why does it work? What is the point of repeating this self attention operation h times? Well, recall a running example. Bob cashed the check at the bank.

We've discussed that we can think of attention outputs as features that tell us something about the sentence. For example, when Bob pays attention to bank, the feature might be Where did Bob go? But we might also be interested in what Bob did. He cashed the check. We can only do this if we allow our layer to produce multiple features, each of which [unclear] to pay attention to different tokens in the sentence. One good analogy if you have past experience with deep learning is with CNNs.

We know that in CNNs we use convolutional layers to produce features from images. We already discussed how convolution is basically a feature finder. For example, if the filter is shaped like an eye, then we will find eyes. If the filter is shaped like an ear, it will find ears. But what if we would like to find eyes and ears? The answer is we need to pass our image through multiple convolution filters at once. We then concatenate the results which become image representations of where all these features were found.

At the end of the Attention Is All You Need paper. There are some nice visualizations that show what the attention weights have learned to do in practice. This lecture discussed some intuition, but the reality will always be more complex. In this case, the sentence is The law will never be perfect, but its application should be just. This is what we are missing, in my opinion. The figure shows the weights from two different attention heads and we can see that in both cases they allow the model to learn different aspects of the sentence.

In the bottom image, we can see that the attention is very localized, where the words pay attention only to its immediate neighbors. In the upper image, we can see that the attention is more spread out, allowing the words to pay attention to more context. The authors of the paper surmised that the attention weights seem to be learning about the structure of the sentence.

7. Transformer Block
====================

So in the previous lecture we learned how to use scaled dot-product attention as a building block for multi-head attention. In this lecture, we will take things to the next step. In particular, even the multi-head attention layer is just a building block of an even bigger building block. In this course, we'll refer to this block we're about to create as the transformer block. And it's the final block we need in order to build encoder models like BERT, decoder models like GPT, and a sequence to sequence models for each of these models, they really just take the transformer block and repeat it multiple times.

So once you understand this lecture, you'll understand the exact building block we use to build these more complex models. The first thing we're going to add is a feedforward ANN. As you recall, our multi head attention layer really only consists of linear transformations. But we know that neural networks are nonlinear, which is why they are able to learn such rich representations of our data. One way we can accomplish this is by passing the output of multi-head attention through a feedforward ANN. In the Attention Is All You Need paper, they use an ANN with one hidden layer and a ReLU activation in the middle.

The output layer has no activation, but note that this is not the case for all transformers. For instance, in the BERT paper they use the GELU activation, which stands for Gaussian Error Linear Unit. It basically looks kind of like the ReLU, except that it has a small dip on the left side and doesn't have a sharp elbow. The next detail we're going to discuss is the layer normalization block. This appears both after the multi-head attention and the feedforward ANN.

And so you can imagine this like four sequential steps. First, we have multi-head attention. Then we have layer normalization. Then we have the feedforward ANN and then we have another layer normalization. So what is layer normalization? Well, it's not my goal in this course to go into great detail about layer norm. But if you've ever heard of batch norm, then you should already have some intuition about what this does. Basically layer norm works the same as batch norm, except that instead of normalizing across the samples, it normalizes across the features.

The computations are still the same. We still begin by subtracting the mean and dividing by the standard deviation. But again, we do this across the features and not the samples. After this, we then apply gain and an adaptive bias, both of which are learnable parameters. Now, for those of you who have never heard of either batch norm nor layer norm, some basic intuition is this. We know that for neural networks, it often helps to normalize our data before passing it into a neural network.

If the values are too small or too large, the performance degrades. You can do some experiments to confirm this yourself. Thus, normalizing your data keeps it within a standard range of values. However, instead of just performing this operation at the input, we've turned it into a layer so that it can be repeatedly applied even between intermediate layers of the network. And if you don't care about theory, basically the practical view is that it helps neural networks train faster.

The third and final modification we are going to make is to add skip connections, also known as residual connections. As with the other topics we've discussed in this section, it would be really helpful if you have past experience with CNNs. Specifically, residual connections were first introduced with CNNs with a specific CNN called the ResNet, short for Residual Network. The intuition is this. Basically, researchers notice that if you keep adding more layers to a neural network, performance does not improve, but instead it degrades.

This seems very weird since theoretically, given some performance level at some number of layers, you can just add an arbitrary number of new layers, but just copy the input to the output. In other words, all of these new layers could just be identity functions. By doing that, the neural networks performance would remain the same. But instead they found that when they trained a large neural network with more layers, performance got worse. As such, they decided to explicitly add the identity function.

We call these skip connections because they're like a wire that connects the input directly to the output. Without making any changes, it skips the layer in between. Another way to think of it is like a residual connection. Since the neural network layer only has to learn what is left after subtracting the input from the output. And again, if you don't care about the theory, all you have to know is that this helps the neural network learn better. By the way, note that this also explains why the output shape of multi-head attention must be equal to the input shape.

If this were not the case then it would not be possible to add the input to the output. As you recall, if you want to add two matrices together, they both must have the same size. Furthermore, this also implies that the output of the feedforward ANN does not change the size. So in fact the input to the whole transformer block will be of shaped t by d model, as will the output. Okay. So now that we have our building blocks, we can look at the final transformer block.

Again, there are essentially four steps. We begin with multi-head attention. Note that this takes in three inputs, which are the queries, keys and values. But all of them are actually the same input, which is the input sequence, at least in the scenarios we've discussed so far. Then we have the add & norm block. This first adds the skip connection to the output of the multi head attention block and then applies layer norm. Next we pass this through a feed forward and at the same time this also has a skip connection so that after the ANN we have another add & norm block.

Personally, I think writing this out in pseudocode helps. On the first line we pass through a multi-head attention layer, add it to itself, and then apply layer norm. On the second line, we pass x through the ANN and add it to itself and again apply layer norm.

8. Positional Encodings
=======================

So in this lecture, we'll be discussing one final component of the Transformer network. This is separate from the previous lectures. Since the previous lectures were all about the transformer block, whereas this lecture is about a separate component. This lecture is all about the positional encoding, which solves one issue. You may have noticed the issue is this. Consider what would happen if we randomly rearranged all the words in our input sentence. Intuitively, this should confuse our model, just as it would confuse us.

When I speak these sentences to you, they make sense because the words are in a particular order. In other words, the ordering of the words coming out of my mouth right now matters. But note that this is not how self attention works. Self attention is what we call permutation invariant. It doesn't matter how we order the words in our input sequence because the amount of attention any token pays to another is purely a function of their embeddings and the weight matrices.

Although transformers are a sequence model, it appears that this crucial detail has somehow been lost. The answer to this problem is to simply add inputs that are position specific. That is to say, by looking at these new inputs, we can tell where in the sequence they came from. We call these positional encoding. Now, before we get into how positional encoding work, let's look at this from a higher level and talk about where they actually go. So far in this section, we've discussed the transformer block, but we haven't yet discussed what comes before the transformer block.

If you studied NLP with deep learning in the past, then you already know the answer. The input into our network is a sequence of token IDs and the first thing we do is map each of these token IDs to embeddings. In TensorFlow and pytorch, we would get these by using an embedding layer. Mathematically, all we are doing is creating a big table of embedding vectors where each row belongs to a different word or a different token. So if your input is word i, then we simply grab the ith row of the embedding table and that becomes our vector for word i.

These vectors are each of size D model, and so if we pass in a sequence of T tokens, they will first be converted into a sequence of T vectors. Each of size D model. In other words, a matrix of size T by D model, which is what we looked at earlier in the section. Of course, if we only do this and we pass this directly into the transformer block, the transformer will have no idea about the order of the tokens. The positional encodings simply add positional information to the existing embeddings.

So we're going to come up with a unique set of numbers to add at each position of the existing embeddings, such that this new set of numbers can uniquely identify which position they are located at. Now, how does this work in practice? In practice, we're going to create these positional encoding by using a set of sines and cosines at different frequencies. Notice that if you look at the original diagram for the transformer, it shows these positional encoding using a little circle with a sine wave inside it.

So that's what that represents. Let's now go into a little more detail about why this works. Firstly, let's begin with a single sine wave. Suppose our token is right at the beginning of the sentence at position zero. Since the sine of zero is zero, the value for the positional encoding would be zero. However, note that a single sine wave is not enough. If we go far enough, the sine wave simply repeats. So we can't tell the difference between position zero and whatever position is at two pi radians.

But let's consider what would happen if we include a cosine wave at the same frequency. Again, consider the word at the first position. The cosine of zero is one, so the positional encoding for this position would be a vector (0, 1): 0 from the sine and one from the cosine. Of course, this still doesn't lead to a unique encoding since at two pi radians cosine goes back to one. But now let's consider what if we just added more and more sines and cosines at different frequencies?

Even though sine and cosine repeats, they won't repeat at the same positions. So the combination of points from all of the sines and cosines will give us a unique vector for each position in the sequence. Now, just for completion sake, I'm going to show you the exact formulas they used for the positional encodings. Although this is not necessary for you to know unless you want to implement it yourself. Basically for even dimensions we use the sine and for odd dimensions we use the cosine. Inside the sine or cosine we take the position and divided by 10,000 raised to some power.

The power is a function of I divided by d model so that you can get different frequencies for different dimensions as shown in the previous slide. By the way, note that i goes from zero up to D model minus one, so that in total we have D model different sines and cosines. This must be the case since as you recall, we're going to add the positional encoding to the original embeddings. And in order to add a matrix to another matrix, they must have the same shape.

Now one question you might have is why not make the positional encoding learnable embeddings? The answer is that the authors tried this, but it didn't lead to any increase in performance. So one neat way to visualize how the positional encoding works, which I haven't seen anywhere else, is to use PCA to reduce the dimensionality of the embeddings. When we do this, we can see that in fact the positional encoding really do map each position to a unique location, which we can confirm by looking at the encoding in two dimensions.

Note that I've added a color gradient to each data point so that we can follow the positions from beginning to end. So it starts with red and then pink, and then purple, blue, green, yellow, and then back to red. In other words, the points are not scattered around randomly, but they follow a shape. This is not required, but it does show that positional encoding give us a sane representation of position in a sequence. In that way, we always know where we are in the sequence by looking at these points in a dimensional space.

And as you can see in this case, even a two dimensional space.

9. Encoder Architecture
=======================

So in this lecture, we will be looking at how to take everything you just learned about the different building blocks of a transformer and build an actual encoder. Now, don't be fooled by its name. Saying Encoder doesn't imply that we only have half of a model. As you recall, BERT is an example of an encoder only network, and it's one of the most popular transformers that exists. Encoder really just qualifies what tasks this type of neural network can be used for.

Other types of architectures are used for other types of tasks. I also want to give you an analogy which should be helpful. If you've taken my deep learning courses in the past, in particular, think of how we learned about CNNs. This uses a similar format. When we learned about CNN's, we first learned about convolution in this course. We first learned about self attention. Then we learned about how to build the convolution layer which involved multiple convolutions stacked together.

That's like multi-head attention. Then we learned about other layers that work well with convolution such as pooling and batch norm. In this course that's like the ANN, skip connections and layer norm. Finally, after learning about these components, we learned how to group them together and form a full neural network. So the encoder architecture is actually very simple. First, let's suppose our input is a sequence of T word IDs or token IDs. Then we put this through an embedding layer which you've seen before.

If you studied deep learning for NLP after the embedding layer, we then add positional encoding, which you just learned about after adding positional encodings. We then apply several transformer blocks. How many you add is obviously up to you, and as usual, you can consider that as a hyper parameter. As an example, the base version of BERT has 12 layers of transformer blocks and in the Attention Is All You Need paper. The encoder part of the model has six layers of transformer blocks.

From there you can add any model head you like. For instance, a binary layer for doing next sentence prediction. Now, as usual, it's always important to think about shapes. Luckily, thanks to the groundwork we've done already, this is going to be easy. So after passing our T token IDs into the embedding layer, we'll have a sequence of embeddings shaped like a matrix of size T by d model. Since the positional encodings have the same size after adding them, we will still have T by d model.

As you know, the transformer blocks don't change the shape of the data from input to output. So the input to every transformer block is T by d model and the output is the same effectively. What we end up with is t vectors each of size d model. Now when we're working with RNNs, we thought of these as hidden states, which we called H of T. There's nothing stopping you from doing that with Transformers. But this terminology is typically not used. Instead, we normally refer to these vectors as contextual embeddings.

The reason for this is notice how they are just like the first layer of embeddings in terms of their shape. Whether you're at the beginning of the network or at the end, you still have t embeddings of size d model. The difference is that at the beginning of the model, those embeddings are just from a lookup table. These embeddings do not take context into account. So the embedding for bank will be the same whether you mean a river bank or a financial institution.

However, this changes when you get to the end of the network, which does take context into account. Thanks to attention. Now when we look at the corresponding embedding for the word bank, it will have been transformed based on the surrounding context. Hence, we call them contextual embeddings. At this point, you have everything you need to build an encoder to perform the task we studied earlier in this course for completion sake, we'll look at how to do this conceptually.

So suppose we want to do text classification which involves assigning a single label to an entire text document. With RNNs, we normally take the final hidden state, push that through a final dense layer, and then apply the softmax. With transformers such as BERT. We actually take the first output embedding, push that through a final dense layer and so forth. So why is this the case? Well, remember that with Transformers Order doesn't matter. Everything can pay attention to everything else.

This idea actually shows up in the name of BERT, which stands for bidirectional encoder representations from Transformers, the key word being bidirectional. What this means is that every embedding can pay attention not just to tokens that came before, but also tokens that came after. As such, using the first output works just fine. This is opposed to a typical RNN which only pays attention to the past hidden state. Another task you can do as you recall.

Called token classification where we classify every token in the document. In this case, we take every contextual embedding and pass each of them through a final, dense layer with a softmax. When we do this, that it becomes just like an RNN we would use for the same task. So hopefully, you know what we would do from here to train the model, make predictions and so forth. So as an exercise, I would recommend taking what you just learned and implementing a transformer from scratch for both text and token classification.

Note that both TensorFlow and PyTorch contain a multi-head attention layers, but you could also build them yourself. Since they're just simple matrix multiplies.

10. Decoder Architecture
========================

So now that we've looked at how the encoder works, let's look at how the decoder works. The structure of this lecture will be a bit different from the encoder, since the key points are not really analogous to what we previously did. For this lecture, we'll begin with the main task of a decoder. Unlike the encoder, there is really only one task. The architecture is also pretty simple. In fact, it looks pretty much the same as the encoder for classifying text.

The main difference, which in my opinion is the most challenging part to understand, is how we get this model to perform the task we want. Specifically, we want a model that can predict the next token from the previous tokens in a sequence. You could always just do this naively, as we will see. But for Transformers, there is a very specific method that we will learn about. Note that this is more of an implementation detail, so feel free to skip it if you like.

But I think that it will be useful if you plan to move on to implementing Transformers yourself. I've learned that repetition is always useful, so since this is a challenging concept, I believe it's worth it to be introduced to it now and then to repeat it again when you actually implement a transformer. Okay. So let's begin with the task that the decoder performs. As you may have learned earlier in this course, the decoders job is to simply predict the next token in a sequence given the previous tokens.

So as an example, I might pass in the input. The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy and I would train the transformer to output dog. We would do this for all documents in our data sets. And in addition, note that each document turns into multiple effective samples. For instance, we want to predict quick given just the we want to predict brown given the quick, we want to predict fox, given the quick brown and so on and so forth. So all the tokens in a document have a chance to be the target given just the previous tokens.

As you recall, we call this a language model. It is a model that estimates the probability of token T, given token T, minus one, token t, minus two all the way down to token one. So it's worth noting at this point why this task is sufficiently generic. Consider some use cases for full sequence to sequence models like chatbots and language translation. Recall that the decoder is the second part of such a model. In both of these cases, we need to generate text for a chatbot.

We would condition on the encoder output after feeding it the prompts we want to respond to. For language translation, we would condition on the encoder output after feeding it the input sentence we want to translate, but in both cases after we read in this encoder output, what do we need to do is generate a new sequence which basically amounts to the task of generating text. It's simply that when we're talking about the decoder only, that means we're not conditioning on anything except past tokens.

And there's a further side note, which we'll discuss more when we talk about GPT. Even the decoder by itself can do some pretty amazing things. For instance, with just a decoder, we can still design a prompt such that the decoder will automatically know that it should perform the task of a chatbot or to translate text. And amazingly, we can do this even without explicitly training the decoder for those tasks. We can induce this behavior from the decoder by designing the right prompts which will be presented to the decoder as past tokens from which to continue generating texts.

This is sometimes called prompt engineering. In any case, that will be discussed later on in the section. Okay. So the next question is, what is the architecture of such a decoder? Interestingly, it looks a lot like the encoder. Specifically, it is a series of stacked transformer blocks. As input. We have our usual input sequence of token IDs which get converted into embedding vectors and combined with positional encoding. We then pass this through the series of transformer blocks at the output.

We have a final linear layer. Intuitively, we know that this final linear layer should be used for classification and it should have V output nodes where V is our token vocabulary size. In other words, this is just a standard sequence classifier which you have already seen. The main difference is that instead of using plain multi head attention, we use multi head attention with masking. This is also known as causal self attention. It is causal because it requires the model to only look at the past.

However, one crucial point is that this isn't technically part of the model itself. Instead, we induce this causality by inserting a specific mask ourselves, as mentioned at the start of this lecture. I think this is the most challenging part to understand. So one thing I want to mention before we start to discuss how masking is done is that we don't really need to do things this way. Let's think about this from a more first principles perspective. In order to build a causal language model, special masking is not technically needed.

We simply pass in as input a batch of partial sequences of token IDs and we make the target the next token ID in those sequences. As mentioned previously, one sentence say the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog actually results in multiple effective samples. We have "the" going to quick we have the quick going to brown and so forth. We can just pass in each of these inputs and targets into the model separately, no masking needed. So let's make sure we understand this point before we move on.

The special type of masking we're about to discuss is not really fundamental, but it is a neat trick. We don't need it to build a language model. We just have to make sure that we create our inputs and targets in the correct manner. So the trick is to understand how the attention weights can be modified to force the network to only pay attention to the past. Even if we pass in the whole sequence. Recall that our attention weights are a T by T square. Alpha ij is the amount that output i pays attention to input token j.

Consequently, we only want alpha ij to be greater than zero when i is greater than or equal to J. And we can accomplish this by setting the values of the mask appropriately. Note that equality is allowed because we also shift the sequence so the target is a shifted version of the input. What is important and useful to know is that when we implement this, notice how we would like only the lower triangular portion of the attention matrix to have non-zero values.

Now there's one really cool thing that I like about this trick. Let's think about what it allows us to do. To understand this. It is again helpful to return to RNNs. Consider how we might train an RNN to predict the next token in a sequence. One method is to use a simple text classification model where the input is a sequence and the output is the next token in that input sequence. However, as mentioned, this requires us to be intelligent about how we pass in the data.

For instance, consider the document. The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. This can be split into multiple samples. For the first sample, the input is the and the target is quick. For the next sample, the input is the quick and the target is brown. You've seen this before. But can we do this more efficiently? The answer is yes. Suppose that instead of just a text classification model with one output, we have an RNN with T outputs. That is for an input of length t.

The target is also of length T For a regular RNN, this is causal by default because the information always passes from beginning to end. There's nothing special we have to do. What makes this more efficient is that we no longer have to bother splitting up our document into multiple samples. The first target will be quick and it can only consider the input token "the". The second target will be brown and it can only consider the input tokens "the" and "quick".

This is because RNNs only know about the current input and the past inputs. But now let's consider why this doesn't work for a transformer. Well, the reason it doesn't work for a transformer is also one of the reasons why transformers are so powerful. It's because by default, every output node can pay attention to every input token. Therefore, by default, we allow the output at a specific time step to look at tokens from future time steps. And of course, the way to fix this is to mask the attention weights to prevent it from happening.

In other words, what we have accomplished with our special masking is this. The awkward and cumbersome method is to have a text classification transformer with just one output where we have to manually create input samples. But with masking we can have a token classification transformer with t outputs. Any input sequence only needs to be passed in once. We don't have to create separate input samples from this one pass. We can simultaneously learn all of the sub sequences.

So at this point, you technically know everything you need to know in order to build a decoder language model yourself. That's not to say it wouldn't be challenging, but it is possible. You now know more than I did when I first started studying the code for how to do it. If you want to give it a try, that would be great. But if not, that's okay too. It would be interesting if you did because you can make a note of where you got stuck and then compare your thinking now to what it will be after you look at the code in the next section of the course.

11. Encoder-Decoder Architecture
================================

So now that you understand both the encoder and the decoder in isolation, it's time to combine them into a full sequence to sequence transformer. Now, you technically already know most of the broad strokes. This is basically as far as most resources and books will go. You take the encoder, plug the output into the decoder, and simply put, you now have a transformer. But as with the decoder lecture, I want to go deeper than that, especially to discuss details that will actually help you implement this yourself.

So at this point you've probably seen this diagram many times, but it's worth going over. As promised, a transformer is made up of an encoder connected to a decoder. The encoder and decoder transformer blocks are as shown. Notice how the decoder transformer block is not the same as the encoder transformer block. Specifically, it seems to have an extra multi head attention layer. Why is this? Well, if we look closely, we can see that the first multi head attention is of the kind we discussed in the decoder lecture.

Specifically it is a causal self attention using a mask. However, the second multi head attention is just a regular attention. Importantly, we see that it takes an input from the encoder output. In particular, the encoder output becomes V and K for the decoder while Q comes from the previous attention. Now we know that in general the sequence length of the input is not equal to the sequence length of the output. This is one reason why we need sequence to sequence models in the first place.

So how can it be that we can mix a sequence output from the encoder with a sequence from the decoder? Let's convince ourself that it still works. As you recall, the values V and the keys k come from the encoder. The queries Q come from the decoder. Suppose that V has sized T input by DV and K has size T input by DQ. Note that they both have the same sequence length of T input. Q has the size t output by DQ. Now let's consider the first operation to compute the attention output.

We have Q times k transpose. This gives us a matrix of size t output by t input. This is because the inner dimensions disappear. In fact, we don't even need to go past this first step. Note that scaling by the square root of DQ doesn't change the shape, and applying softmax also does not change the shape. Therefore, the attention weights will have the shape t output by t input. As you recall for the attention weight alpha ij, this tells us how much output token I should pay attention to input token j.

In the case of translation, this makes perfect sense. When we are generating the i-th output word, we want to know which of the input words to pay attention to. How many input words are there? Well, there are t input input words for each output. We need to know how much to pay attention to each input. Hence an attention matrix of size t output by t input is exactly what we need. So another important fact about the transformer architecture, which is really not clear from the typical diagram shown before, is which encoder output is connected to which decoder input.

Firstly, notice that beside each of the transformer blocks there is a little index. This means that we are stacking the transformer block N times. In the Attention Is All You Need paper, they set N equal to six. But here's one point of confusion or ambiguity. It's not clear whether we take, say, the first encoder output and connect that to the first decoder input, then take the second encoder output and connect that to the second decoder input and so on and so forth.

Or is it the case that we simply go through all six encoder layers and then take the final encoder output and plug that into each decoder input? The answer turns out to be the latter. So here is an expanded diagram which shows that the data passes through all the encoder layers. First, only the final encoder output gets passed to the decoder, and it's this same output that gets passed into each decoder input. Another issue we haven't yet discussed is the topic of prediction or inference.

This is more of a topic for the decoder specifically, but since that lecture was pretty long, I've saved it for this one. Note that if you've already studied advanced RNNs with me in the past, then you already know how this works. So the issue is that during training time you've seen that we always pass in a shifted version of the true target into the decoder input. So if the sentence is the quick brown fox, the input becomes start tag the quick brown and fox, while the output becomes the quick brown fox end tag.

However, we have to recognize that this is not how the model will make predictions because when it's inference time we don't know the true target. Instead, it looks a lot like how we forecast a time series. We simply predict one token at a time and then use our previously predicted tokens as input into the next time step. So as an example, on time step one, the decoder input is just start tag and we have to predict "the". On time step two, the decoder input is start tag and the and we have to predict quick.

Now let's say on time step three, the decoder input is start tag the quick. But our model produces the word orange instead of brown. In that case, the next decoder input now becomes start tag the quick orange. We just use whatever the model predicted last. As a side note, there are other algorithms such as beam search which can improve the quality of the output, but that won't be discussed in this course. So that's pretty much it for the full sequence to sequence transformer.

Note that it was relatively short because we already spent a lot of effort building up the groundwork. By doing the groundwork first, the actual transformer itself becomes very simple. You can also imagine how we would train such a model. There would now be two inputs one for the encoder and one for the decoder. For instance, for translation, the encoder input would be the input sentence, while the decoder input would be the target sentence shifted to the right so that it starts with the start token.

The target would be the translated sentence not shifted, but offset by one relative to the input. So one single instance of a training sample actually involves a triple of items instead of just an input and output pair. To give you an example of this, consider the input. I am lazy, which we want to translate to French. The raw data is just the English sentence and the French sentence. So you might think the input is the English sentence and the target is the French sentence.

But when we actually train the model, we need an additional input in addition to this encoder input. We also have the French translation offset by a start token, which becomes the decoder input. The French translation then appears again in the target, but with the end token at the end. Again, this is pretty simple given what we know so far. Now, as a side note, this method is called teacher forcing. With teacher forcing, we pass in the true target into the decoder.

So the decoder only has to predict the next output token. Knowing the ground truth translation of the previous tokens. But recall that during inference time the model works differently because we do not have access to the true translation.
