In that case, the decoder works in true auto regressive fashion by predicting the next token using its own past predicted tokens.

12. BERT
========

So in this lecture, we will be looking at some of the specifics of BERT. Note that you already know many of these details having been through the previous lectures in the course. We know that BERT stands for bidirectional encoder representations from Transformers. We know that BERT is an instance of the encoder transformer and we know how it is. Pre-trained And then fine tuned for specific tasks. This lecture is designed to summarize the key points you've already learned and to provide a few more interesting details.

So let's begin by discussing the architecture of BERT. As you know, it's an encoder only transformer. We know what such a transformer looks like. We have the input tokens which are converted into embeddings. To this we add positional encoding. We then go through a series of transformer blocks which contain a multi-head attention and a few other bells and whistles. After these transformer blocks, we have prediction heads which are specified based on the task. To get into specifics.

There are two main model sizes which are compared in the BERT paper, BERT base and BERT large. BERT base was designed to have the same number of parameters as GPT, which came out prior to BERT. This is so that the comparison between them would be more fair for BERT base. We have 12 transformer blocks, a hidden vector size of 768 and 12 attention heads for BERT large. We have 24 transformer blocks, a hidden vector size of 1024 and 16 attention heads.

So as you know, one of the central ideas of the BERT paper is that they used unsupervised pre-training and then fine tuning to produce state of the art results. There were two pre-training tasks which are masked language modeling and next sentence prediction for masked language modeling. Here's how they generated the data. So basically for each token in a document, they give it a 15% chance of being selected for prediction. If a token is selected, then multiple things can happen.

Firstly, there is an 80% chance that the input for that token will be replaced with the mask token. Then there is a 10% chance that the token will be replaced with a random word from the corpus. Finally, there is a 10% chance that the token remains unchanged. So not only does the model have to learn how to replace missing tokens when they are masked, it also has to learn how to detect incorrect tokens. For next sentence prediction, there is also a little detail here that we didn't discuss.

The detail is that it actually includes masked tokens as well. Another detail is that although there are many possible combinations of sentences where the second sentence does not follow the first. The authors created a balanced data set, so the number of positive and negative samples were equal at a 50/50 split. So in terms of the data set, BERT was trained on a data set called BooksCorpus, which has 800 million words and English Wikipedia, which has 2.5 billion words.

This is not so relevant in isolation, but it's useful when you compare it with other models like GPT. The final topic of this lecture is to discuss some of the results from the BERT paper. Students of my courses are familiar with my motto Machine learning is experimentation and not philosophy. When you have questions like Is next sentence prediction a good task? Or is the fact that BERT is bidirectional relevant? The best way to answer these questions is not by theory, but by doing experiments and checking how they perform.

And of course, that's exactly what the authors have done. Looking at this table from the paper, we can see that when we remove the next sentence prediction pre-training task, the performance drops. Furthermore, when we remove bidirectionality, the performance also drops. Note that LTR stands for left to right, so they make the transformer read each input from beginning to end or left to right. Finally, they add a bidirectional LSTM and when they do this, performance drops even further.

So it appears that even the bidirectionality induced by the LSTM does not make up for the fact that it is missing from the transformer.

13. GPT
=======

So in this lecture we will be discussing the GPT series of models. As with the BERT lecture, we've already been exposed to many of the key points earlier in the course. However, unlike the BERT lecture, there are many details about the GPT series which are very different from what you have learned in the past, namely how we deal with downstream tasks after pre-training for example, like language translation. Now, before we begin this lecture, I want to remind you that this is just a summary.

There are many details we won't cover, but you can always look at extra_reading.txt and look up these details yourself. The GPT-3 paper is 75 pages long, so you can imagine that those details could make up a course all by itself. So let's start by reviewing the main points. The GPT models are decoder only models, which is evident from the acronym GPT. GPT stands for Generative Pre-trained Transformer. So again, we follow this paradigm of pre-training a transformer using an unsupervised task.

In this case, the task is to simply predict the next token, given the previous tokens in a document. What really makes GPT so interesting is that this task is so simple. We don't need a labeled data set. We can just pass in unlabeled text, which is readily found on the internet. The authors describe the lack of labeled data as a bottleneck and present GPT as a way to get around this bottleneck by having it learn useful things using only unsupervised training.

In this case, the simple task of predicting the next word in a sentence. And despite the simplicity of this task, you will see that the GPT models, especially GPT-2 and three, can do some amazing things. Now, as a side note, I want to mention that GPT version one actually came out before BERT. This is why BERT emphasizes that it is bidirectional, whereas GPT only reads documents from left to right. However, as you'll see, GPT-2 and GPT-3 are way more powerful and yet still only read documents from left to right and still only use the same simple pre-training task of predicting the next word in a document.

In terms of the architecture, the original GPT is just like the decoder we described earlier. As input, we have a sequence of token IDs. We convert these into embeddings and we add a positional encoding. It then goes through several transformer blocks. Note that these transformer blocks use causal self attention so each token can only pay attention to past tokens. In the case of GPT, we have 12 transformer blocks. Finally, we have a prediction head, which is a classifier used to model the next token probability.

So in the GPT paper they provide a diagram that summarizes the architecture of the model for different tasks during the fine tuning stage. It also describes how to structure the input, which you've seen some examples of earlier in this course. For example, with the plain text classification task, we have a start token, then the text we want to classify. Then the extract token, which is really just the end token. We then put this through the transformer and add a final linear layer For the entailment task,

we have the exact same architecture. The difference is that the input is structured differently. Specifically, we start with a premise followed by a hypothesis, but it's still just simple text classification where the input is a single string of text and the output is a class. For the text similarity task, we actually use the same transformer twice. This is perhaps to ensure that the model doesn't use any information about the ordering of the sentences.

So in the first pass, sentence one comes before sentence two, and in the second pass, sentence two comes before sentence one. Therefore, the output of this overall model is symmetric in the inputs. Once we have the output from the transformer, we then add them together and the result goes through a final linear layer. Finally we have the multiple choice task. This is similar to the last task in that we pass multiple different inputs through the same transformer body.

In this case, we format the text so that the question comes first and then one possible answer. So if we have N possible answers, we will have N inputs to pass into the transformer. After going through the transformer, we have one more linear layer, after which we take the softmax over the outputs for each answer. So nothing you haven't seen before. And if you're taking this course, then I'm sure you're comfortable with these concepts. Another interesting thing about GPT is how they did fine tuning.

During the fine tuning stage. They didn't just train the model to perform the specific task. Instead, they trained the model to perform the task while simultaneously also doing more language modeling. We call this the auxiliary language model. So you can imagine a model with two heads, one for language modeling and one for the task. The final loss was the loss for the task, plus the loss for the language model multiplied by some small constant to balance the losses.

Now, as usual, you might be wondering, does this actually work? And the answer is as usual. Machine learning is experimentation, not philosophy. It turns out that it only performs better sometimes. Other times, the transformer without an auxiliary language model performs best. However, in all cases, we see that a transformer without pre-training did not perform as well, and an LSTM with an auxiliary language model also did not perform as well. So here are some details about the original GPT. To pre-train the model,

the authors used the BooksCorpus data. As you recall, this was also used for BERT, but BERT was also trained on Wikipedia. The GPT-1 model had 110 million parameters.

14. GPT-2
=========

So now let's talk about GPT-2 on a surface level. GPT-2 makes some basic changes. It adds more data and more parameters. But you recall that when the GPT-2 first came out, it was big news. It was seen as so powerful that OpenAI was hesitant to release it to the public. For the first time ever, machine learning was able to generate long strings of very coherent text. Although this sounds almost commonplace today, it's important to keep in mind that this was simply not possible, even during the first few years of the existence of Transformers.

But that's only one of the amazing things about GPT-2. The other amazing fact about GPT-2 is that when the authors measured the performance of GPT-2 on downstream tasks, they did not use any fine tuning. In fact, they did not have to update the model at all. That is to say, they only trained the model to predict the next word in a sentence and magically it was able to perform different tasks like machine translation and text summarization. Now it didn't reach state of the art performance, but the relevant fact is that the model was even able to do this at all.

So how did the authors manage to get GPT-2 to perform tasks that it wasn't even trained to do? The answer lies in how we format the input, sometimes called the prompt. Remember that all GPT-2 does is essentially sentence completion. So given some prompts, the model tries to predict what comes next. One task that might seem familiar to you is text summarization. In order to encourage the model to perform this task. What we can do is after the text we want to summarize add a TL;DR and a colon.

When GPT-2 tries to continue this text, it will naturally produce a summary. So why does this work? Well, perhaps you have seen after browsing online forums such as Reddit, this is what people often do when writing their posts. If you're wondering, TL;DR stands for "too long; didn't read". And since GPT-2 is trained on a large portion of the internet, it has seen this and can replicate that behavior. The same goes for tasks like language translation. In order to perform this task, they prime the model with several example translations in the format.

English sentence equals French sentence. They end the prompt with a final English sentence that they actually want to translate, followed by an equal sign. GPT-2 is smart enough to figure out that it should continue by translating the final English sentence. However, what is pretty cool is that because the inputs are essentially just words, there was a lot of flexibility in terms of how you represent the prompt. For instance, we can prompt the model to do translation simply by saying translate from English to French, and then following that with the English sentence

we want to translate. The authors reason that GPT-2 learns to do this because there are several examples of English to French translations in the Pre-training text corpus. Some examples are shown in the table on this slide, which is taken from the paper. So let's go through some specifics about GPT-2. Firstly, GPT-2 has an order of magnitude more parameters than GPT-1: 1.5 billion versus 110 million. The reason for this will become clear shortly.

Another difference is that GPT-2 doubles the context size or sequence length going from 512 to 1024 tokens. Finally, in addition to a much larger model, they also use a much larger data set. In particular, the authors noted that although there's a large dataset called Common Crawl, which essentially covers the whole Internet, it was of poor quality. So they created their own dataset called WebText. This data set is based on links from Reddit posts that have achieved three or more karma, basically upvotes.

This results in 8 million documents and 40 gigabytes of data. Now one question we should ask about GPT-2 is why is it so large? Why did the authors decide to create a model with ten times more parameters? The answer is that it simply worked better. Lets recall my famous and annoying rule. Machine learning is experimentation, not philosophy. So what did the authors do? Well, they created multiple different models with different sizes. What they found was that over essentially all tasks, models with more parameters worked better than models with less.

In these charts, the horizontal axis represents the number of parameters, while the vertical axis measures performance. As you can see, performance generally increases as we increase the parameters. So what have we learned? We've learned that there is a whole new topic of study thanks to these new transformer models, which one might call prompt engineering. Essentially, it's a matter of figuring out how to talk to the AI to get it to do what you want it to do.

So to summarize the key points of GPT-2. Firstly, it is still a left to right causal language model like GPT-1. Compared to GPT-1, it uses much more data for Pre-training and has many more parameters, whereas GPT-1 has 110 million parameters. GPT-2 has 1.5 billion parameters, which is more than ten times larger. It also uses a larger context size or sequence length. GPT-1 uses a sequence length of 512 tokens, while GPT-2 has a sequence length of 1024 tokens.

And finally, and most importantly, GPT-2 does not use fine tuning. Instead, the authors show that it can perform specific tasks given a correctly formatted prompts.

15. GPT-3
=========

Okay. So now let's move on to GPT-3. Basically, it's the same story, more data and more parameters. But whereas GPT-2 was about ten times larger than GPT-1, GPT-3 is over 100 times bigger than GPT-2. It has 175 billion parameters. It also doubles the context size, again, going from 1024 to 2048. In terms of data. You'll recall that the authors of GPT-2 considered using Common Crawl, but found that performance suffered due to quality issues.

In this paper, the authors decided to use it anyway, but to filter it to improve its quality. In addition, they also used an expanded version of WebText called WebText2. They also add two book data sets and English Wikipedia. Now, this might not seem like much since listing out the number of parameters and the data sets that were used really do not portray the scale of this experiment. To give you some sense of this, the authors show a chart comparing the compute required to train several well known models.

So we have BERT Base on the left, followed by BERT Large and then the full GPT-3 on the right. Importantly, note that this is a log scale. So every time you move up a bar you are multiplying by ten. If you move up three bars, you are multiplying by 1000 or ten to the power of three. So to give you some sense of how large that is, consider a model that takes one day to train. Not so bad. Now, suppose it takes 1000 days to train. That is now nearly three years.

Of course, the authors use distributed training over many GPUs, which reduces the time. So as with GPT-2, the only training that happens is during the unsupervised training phase where the model learns to predict the next token in a sequence given the previous tokens. Again, there is no fine tuning step. However, the authors hypothesize that the model will indirectly learn to do various different tasks simply because they show up in the data set. In the paper they show a diagram where they call unsupervised pre training the outer loop.

Within this, the authors hypothesize that some in context learning is happening at the same time. For example, the model will see examples of arithmetic, and that explains why the model can do arithmetic. The model will see examples of spelling correction, and that's why the model can do spelling correction. The third example is with translation. So the authors call this the inner loop. So this explains how models like GPT-3 are able to perform tasks without any task specific data set or fine tuning.

So you'll recall that with GPT-2, the authors primarily used zero-shot transfer. That is, they prompted the model with a task but did not provide any examples of how to do that task. With GPT-3, they extend this by testing zero shot, one shot and few shot transfer. Basically, these names refer to the number of examples that are given in the prompts. So they are not training examples, they are prompt examples. So as an example, they use language translation for zero shot.

They simply say Translate English to French. Start a new line, say cheese and then use an arrow. And then the model completes the rest for one shot learning. You can see that they extend this by adding a new line before the final line, which is an example of what to do. In this example, we can see that it's a translation of sea otter. Finally for a few shot, they extend that again simply by adding more examples. So how well does this work? Well, it turns out that this works surprisingly well.

Keep in mind that GPT-2 worked well, but generally not as well as existing state of the art methods that were trained specifically for the given task. What was surprising about GPT-2 was that it was even able to do that to begin with. With GPT-3, there are even more surprises for a task called TriviaQA, which is a question answering task. We see the performance for a zero shot, one shot and few shot. On the horizontal axis, we have the number of parameters.

Observe that few-shot learning in the largest GPT-3 model actually beats the existing fine tuned state of the art. This means that GPT-3 being trained only to predict the next word in a sentence was able to perform better on a question answering task compared to a model trained specifically for that task.

16. Theory Section Summary
==========================

So in this lecture we will summarize what we learned in this section of the course. This section was all about the theory behind attention and transformers about how they work and what makes them work so well. To that end, we discovered two key characteristics that make Transformers work well compared to previous deep learning technologies. Firstly, we use the new operation called Self Attention, which allows the neural network to model long range dependencies.

This solves the problem of vanishing gradients that we see in RNNs. Secondly, we leverage massive amounts of data by using unsupervised pre-training. By doing this, we can use more data compared to what we would have if we only used a supervised data set. In this section, we learn how to build a transformer from the ground up using only basic operations like multiplication, a softmax and so forth. We learned about the basic scaled dot-product attention and how to combine these to get multi head attention.

We also learned about how to add masking to give us causal self attention which is used in the decoder. One important fact we learned about attention is that the operation is quadratic. We now understand why this is and what it means. We also learned about the positional encoding, which is necessary since attention has no concept of order. It is permutation invariant. Once we learned about these fundamental building blocks, we learned about different ways to combine them.

Specifically, we learned about the encoder, which is similar in functionality to a plain vanilla RNN that we might use for classifying text. We then learned about the decoder, which is similar in functionality to a language modeling and text generation RNN. And then we learned about the encoder decoder, which is similar in functionality to the sequence to sequence RNN. We then learned about specific models, namely BERT, GPT-1, GPT-2 and GPT-3.

BERT is an encoder model and we've already seen that it's useful for tasks like text classification, token classification and question answering. But importantly, we now understand why based on how the model is actually built. We then saw that all the GPTs are decoder models. We learned the surprising fact, which is that GPT models only learn to do a very simple task, which is to predict the next word in a sentence. Perhaps the most surprising fact is that GPT-2 and GPT-3 do not use any fine tuning at all.

It appears to simply know how to do specific tasks based on the prompt you give it. Now it's important to consider the implications of a model with these abilities. I think most people agree that this is not necessarily an example of reasoning. That is, GPT-2 and three did not learn how to add or answer questions, but rather it's more likely that these models are so large they learn a distributed representation of the training dataset. In other words, they've memorized the training corpus.

But this is not memory as in a direct copy in a database, but rather memory in terms of neural network weights. Finally, it's helpful to note that, as with CNN's, there are many different variations of transformers, so many, in fact, that it's impossible to study them all. However, the goal of this section is to provide you with the fundamentals such that exploring those variations will be easy. So at this point, if you want to go ahead and read the literature to see if there's a transformer that fits your specific needs, you can do that.

One example of this is the Longformer, which we did not use in this course. As you recall, attention has quadratic time complexity, so it has a limit in terms of the sequence length it can handle. The Longformer tries to solve this by introducing a variant of attention that is linear complexity. Consequently, it can handle longer sequences. Another example is the sparse transformer, which has complexity of N times the square root of N. So if you're interested in learning more about different kinds of transformers, you are now ready to read some papers.

And if you want to learn more about any of the models discussed in this section, check out the papers listed in extra_reading.txt.

###

1. Implementation Section Introduction
======================================

So in this lecture, we will be introduced to the next section of the course, which is all about how to implement transformers from scratch. Now, sometimes students get confused about what from scratch really means. I once came across a blog article that stated it was going to implement Decision Trees from scratch. But then in the actual code they used scikit-learn. Now, since this is an advanced section of the course, I will assume that you are an advanced student.

As such, I think you should have a pretty good idea of what we mean by from scratch. So maybe let's present this as a little quiz. Please take some time to consider. Other components involve layer norm and our feedforward ANN. And so the transformer block is also a module we need to build. Another component will need is the positional encoding. Luckily this can be directly reused in all the subsequent examples. And finally, we'll want to create an encoder module which will consist of transformer blocks, a positional encoding and a few other basic modules. So that seems like a pretty simple list. Multi-head attention, transformer block, positional encoding and finally the encoder itself.

Okay, so that's the transformer block. Once we have that, we can build the whole encoder. So what makes up an encoder? Well, we have some input sequence of categorical objects. We pass that through an embedding and we add the positional encoding. 3. How to Implement Multihead Attention From Scratch
====================================================

So in this lecture, we will be describing some of the key details behind the multi-head attention implementation in PyTorch. The reason we're doing this lecture is because it doesn't follow directly from the theory, as you might expect. I think the simplest way to do this will be to just show you what the code looks like and then to dissect it piece by piece. So I'm sure you're familiar with these imports. No need to explain these. So now we have the multi head attention class.

So we'll begin with the init function which takes in d_k, d_model and n_heads, which is the number of attention heads. Note that we will assume that d_k will be the same as d_v, so there's no need to pass in DV as well. Now just remember what we're trying to implement. We're trying to implement scaled dot-product attention, which is Q times K transpose, scale that, take the softmax and multiply by V. In this case, Q, K and V are just matrices multiplied by the same inputs.

And remember that we actually want to do this n_heads times. However, you'll recall that in Python and numpy and so forth, we always try to avoid for loops. So we don't want to do something like create a list of n_heads matrices for q, k and V do attention of that many times and then concatenate the results. Instead, it would be better to do all of the attentions all at once. To that end, we're going to create three linear layers, one for the key, one for the query and one for the value.

These represent our matrices from the theory lectures which are WQ, WK and WV. Now, you'll recall that linear layers don't just multiply by a matrix. They also add a bias vector. Technically, you can turn this off if you want to match the paper. Exactly. However, it's actually customary to just leave this in. Now, the shapes that get passed into these linear layers are important to make note of. So in theory, our input is of size d_model and the output is of size

d_k, and we want to do this n_heads times. So all of our attention operations happen on vectors of size d_k. But in the code you can see that the output is of size d_k times n_heads. This is because we're going to do all n_heads attentions all at once. The next step is to create the final linear layer, which, as you recall, is like a final projection after we concatenate all of the heads' attention outputs. Note again that we keep the bias vector. Also notice that the shapes are the opposite of what we have above.

The input is of size d_k times n_heads while the output is a size D model. This makes sense since before applying the final projection, each attention will output a vector of size DK and we have n_heads attention heads. The output should be of size d_model, since that's the size of every input and output in our transformer. So now let's have a look at the forward function. I would say that this is one of the most challenging parts of this section, but it's also one of the most crucial parts.

If you understand this, then you really understand attention and how to implement deep learning concepts in general. Firstly, note that as input into this function, we take in four values. Q, K, V and the mask. Note that these Q, k, and V values are not the ones that go into the attention equation, but the values we have before we multiply by the W matrices. If we're talking about the simple encoder with self attention. Q, k, and V would all be the same X input.

Also note that the mask is used since the input may have padding. So we begin by transforming Q, K and V, but now they represent the Q, K and V in our attention equation. As usual, it's important to track the shapes if you really want to understand what's going on. Firstly, the input is a batch of sequence data, so that will have the shape N by T by d_model. After going through these linear layers, they will be N by T by d_k times h. As you recall, we use the letter H to mean the number of attention heads and also remember that DQ is the same as DV, although I've written them separately.

The next step is to just store some of the shaped dimensions, namely the batch size and the sequence length. We'll call these n and T. Okay, so now this is the hard part. Our goal right now is to perform our self attention operation, which is the softmax of Q times K transpose scaled and then multiplied by V. What's confusing is how to do this when we don't actually have matrices. In the theory lecture we had matrices, but now we have sequences of size N by T by d_k.

In fact, we're just about to make things worse. Instead of three dimensional tensors, what we actually need are four dimensional tensors. You'll just have to trust me at this point and we'll see how it all works out at a later step. Note that Q, K and V are all reshaped using the same method, so it suffices to explain this once. The first thing we do is call the view function. This lets us reshape our data. Previously we had N by T by h times d_k. This reshaping splits up the different heads so we get N by T

by h by d_k. Now, in the next step, we're going to do a matrix multiply. But in order for this to work correctly, we need to change the order of the dimensions. Specifically, we need the h dimension to be second and the T dimension to be third. Consequently, we call transpose passing in one and two, which will switch the dimensions at index one and index two. And so the result is N by h by T by d_k. So the next step is where we compute the first matrix multiply that appears in attention, which is Q times K transpose.

You'll see that this is almost what we've written. Recall that the @ symbol is a new notation we use for the dot product, and the dot product is basically a more general version of matrix multiplication that also does broadcasting. So what's happening here? Firstly, you can see that we are transposing yet again. Specifically, we are transposing the last two dimensions of K at index minus one and index minus two, meaning we are switching these two dimensions.

We'll explain why that's needed soon. But first let's look at what the resulting shape will be. So remember that currently, Q, k, and V all have the same shape, N by h by T by d_k. If we switch the last two dimensions, we get N by h by d_k by T. So why do we want to do this? Well, basically just know that the dot product is like a matrix multiply and it also broadcasts. So how does the broadcasting work? Essentially, you can think of the last two dimensions as the matrices we're going to multiply and think of the first two dimensions component wise.

So the first two dimensions are N and H. So for all of the samples in all of the heads, we're going to do a matrix multiply between a matrix of size T by d_k and a matrix of size d_k by T. Now what would we get if we multiply two matrices of this size together? The result would be a T by T matrix. As you recall, the inner dimension always disappears and the inner dimensions always must match. In this case, the inner dimension is d k. So this is why we needed to do that transpose on K.

Now what is the result? The result is a new four dimensional tensor of size N by h by T by T. We then divide by the square root of d_k, which is just a scalar. The next line is a bit challenging, but not as challenging as the previous step. What we'd like to do next is apply the mask. Now, as you recall, the mask is a tensor of ones and zeros of size N by T. For each of the N samples,. We need to know which of the T tokens are actual tokens and which are just padding.

So it's two dimensional instead of four dimensional. So we can't apply the mask directly, say by multiplying the attention scores by the mask. But what we can do is add Nones to the middle dimensions, which will introduce superfluous dimensions of size one. So what we would end up with is going from N by T to N by one by one by T. This allows us to broadcast correctly. And so when we call the masked fill function, it applies the mask in the appropriate places.

Basically, wherever the first argument is true, apply the value of the second argument, which in this case is minus infinity. As you recall, we use minus infinity because the next step is to take the softmax. When we take the softmax, we exponentiate, and the exponent of minus infinity is zero, which will give us an attention weight of zero. As you can see, the next step is indeed to take the softmax of the attention scores, to get the attention weights.

So now that we have the attention weights, the next step is to multiply by the values V. Luckily, this can be done directly without any reshaping or transposing. Again, we apply the same rule as we learned above. The first two dimensions are component wise. So for each of the samples and for each of the attention heads do this multiply. And what do we want to multiply? We want to multiply a matrix of attention weights of size T by T by a sequence of value vectors of size T by d_k, and the result is T by d_k, or in full

it would be N by h by T by d_k. So now the next step is to do our final projection. But in order to do that, our data needs to be in the right shape. Basically, we're now going to do the reverse of what we did above. So we first transposed index one and index two. So the result is N by T by h by d_k. We want to have N and T be the first indices because all of our sequences have the shape N by T by something. The next step is to reshape this and combine the final two dimensions.

In other words, we get n by t by h times d k. Note that we also use contiguous. Don't worry too much about this as it's a lower level operation. Basically, it ensures that the data is laid out in memory correctly. And so the final step is to apply our projection layer, at which point we can return the results.

4. How to Implement the Transformer Block From Scratch
======================================================

So in this lecture, we'll continue our notebook with the transformer block. This will be pretty simple since we covered the basics in the intro. So now all we have to do is create the layers we need and then apply those layers in the right order. So first we have the init method. This takes in d_k, d_model and n_heads, all of which you've seen. We also have dropout, so this will take in the dropout probability. Note that this will be a recurring theme in this section.

Dropout will appear essentially everywhere, even though we didn't discuss it in the theory lectures. As you recall, dropout is used for regularization. In fact, there are even more places I could have added dropout. But I didn't want to make this too complicated. So inside the init method we begin by creating our two layer norms. The next step is to create a multi head attention layer, which we just wrote. Next we have the ANN. This has a hidden unit size of D model times four and a ReLU activation. In addition,

note that this also ends with a dropout. Finally, we create one last dropout. So the forward function is quite simple as well. This takes in some input sequence of size N by T by d_model and some mask of size N by T. Inside the function, we first apply multi-head attention passing in x as the query key and value along with the mask. We then add the residual connection and then apply the first layer norm. Next we pass the result through our ANN, then add the residual connection and apply the second layer norm.

Finally, we add dropout and then we return the result.

5. How to Implement Positional Encoding From Scratch
====================================================

So in this lecture, we will look at how to implement the positional encoding from scratch as before. This is a module, so we'll need the init method and the forward method. Note that this code has been adapted from one of the official PyTorch tutorials, except the dimensions have been transposed to be in the order that we're used to. That is N by T by D, where the batch size comes first and the time dimension comes second. In the PyTorch tutorials they tend to put the time dimension first.

So the init method takes in three arguments. d_model, max_len and dropout probability. Note that max_len is needed because that determines how many values we're going to precompute. The only actual layer we need is dropout, which we create first. The next step is just some computation to implement the positional encoding formulas. We'll explain it, since it's not exactly trivial. So we begin by creating a variable called position, which is just the list of integers from zero up to max_len.

Basically, this represents the pos variable in the formula. We also call unsqueeze(1). So this adds a superfluous dimension of size one at the end. So instead of a 1D array of size max_len we have a 2D array of size max_len by one. The next step is to create a variable called exp_term. This is just the integers from zero up to d_model, but counting up by two. Basically, this represents the 2i term in the exponent of the denominator. So, whereas the position variable counts across time, the exp_term variable counts across the feature dimension.

Next we have the div term which completes the denominator. Now, this is probably the most challenging part to understand. So in the equation we have 10,000 to the power of 2i divided by d_model. But in the code we have this very weird looking thing with the exp and the log and so forth. So why is this? Basically, this just makes things more numerically stable. 10,000 is a very big number, whereas E is not. It's about 2.7. And note that we also take the log of 10,000 and the log of a big number is usually a much more reasonable number.

Essentially, if you do the math, everything works out. So first we have exp of two times minus log of 10,000 divided by d_model. Now suppose that we bring the minus 2i divided by d_model outside. Then on the inside we have exp of log of 10,000, but exp and log are inverse functions. So we just end up with 10,000. And this is closer to the equation we have in the paper. So next we have pe, which we initialize to an array of zeroes of size one by max_len by d_model.

Basically, the first dimension of one makes this broadcastable because remember, we are going to add it to the embeddings of size N by T by d_model. Next, we assign the correct values to pe based on our formula. As you can see, it's sine of position times div term and cosine of position times div term. Note that we multiply instead of divide because we put a negative sign in the exponent. Okay. So one part that might be a little confusing about this is how we index pe, specifically the zero colon colon two and one colon colon two.

Basically, as you recall from your python studies, the syntax is start index, colon end index, colon step size and end index is optional. So for the sine portion, this is going to index zero two, four, six, eight and so forth. For the cosine portion, this will be one three, five, seven, nine and so forth. And finally, we call a register_buffer on pe. Basically, this is to be able to save and load the model correctly. So the module knows that this variable should be saved.

So next we have the forward function. This takes in an input of size N by T by D. Now, as you recall, PyTorch models can accept variable size sequences, so t might be different in each batch. As such, we can obtain the current sequence length by calling x.size(1) and we only need to index pe up to this point. Once we have that, we add it to x, call dropout and return the results.

6. How to Implement Transformer Encoder From Scratch
====================================================

So in this lecture, now that we have created all the building blocks we need, we can now build a full encoder. As usual, this amounts to filling in the init method. In the forward method. In the init method we take in the vocab size, maximum sequence length, d_k, d_model, number of attention heads, number of transformer blocks, number of classes at the output and dropout probability. Inside the init method. We create all the modules we need. We begin by creating our embedding which will convert our tokens into vectors.

As usual, this has the shape vocab size by d_model. The next step is to create our positional encoding, which we just built. The next step is to create our transformer blocks. We'll also encapsulate these transformer blocks in an nn.Sequential. Finally, we create one last layer norm and one last linear layer, which will both be used after the final transformer block where the final layer has n_classes outputs, because that's how many classes we have to choose from.

Okay, So next we have the forward method. This takes in some sequence input x and a corresponding mask. Inside the function. We first pass our sequence of tokens through an embedding. We then add the positional encoding. Next, we go through each of the transformer blocks, passing the mask through each one. Note that we couldn't have done the alternative, which is to pass X directly into the transformer blocks. This is because the mask needs to be passed through each block.

Next, we have an optional step, which depends on the type of task you are working on. In this case, we'll assume we're just doing text classification and so we only need to keep one of the final hidden vectors. As you know, by convention, we keep the first. So this goes from N by T by D to N by D. Finally, we apply a layer norm and one final linear layer, and then we return the result. Okay, so now let's test our encoder. We'll create an encoder with some dummy values.

Here, we've set the vocab size to 20,000, the max length to 1024, d_k to 16, d_model to 64, n_heads to four, n_layers to two, n_classes to five and dropout probability to 0.1. So that works. The next step is to test our encoder with some actual inputs to see if it runs without any errors. We'll begin by moving our model to the GPU if it exists. The next step is to create a random sequence of integers, which represents a sequence of tokens. So this will give us a sequence of tokens of size eight by 512 containing values from zero up to our vocab size.

We'll then convert this array to a torch tensor and move it to the GPU. The next step is to create a corresponding mask. Note that it has the same shape as the input, which is N by T. We set a few values to zero and then we convert it to a torch tensor and move it to the GPU. So the next step is to pass our data along with the mask through the encoder. So that works. Now let's check the shape of the output. So it's eight by five. So does this make sense?

Yes, this makes sense since we have a batch size of eight and five classes.

7. Train and Evaluate Encoder From Scratch
==========================================

Okay, So in this lecture, we will actually use our encoder on a real data set. Now, technically, we could just load in any data set and use any tokenizer, for instance, like what we did in the PyTorch course. In fact, you could even use TensorFlow's tokenizer if you wanted, which is actually much nicer than what comes with PyTorch. So we begin by installing transformers and data sets. Next, we import AutoTokenizer and DataCollatorWithPadding. So next we set our checkpoint to distilbert-base-cased, and we load the tokenizer from this checkpoint. Now, you might wonder, why did I choose DistilBERT and not just BERT. So one reason, as you recall, is BERT also uses segment embeddings, and it uses these to distinguish between the first sentence and the second sentence in the case where two sentences are passed in.

However, we didn't build any such embeddings, and as such, it's simpler to just use DistilBERT, which also does not use segment embeddings. So the next step is to import the load_dataset method. And the next step is to load in our data set. So for this example, we'll keep things simple and use SST-2, which you've already seen. As you recall, this is a data set for a sentiment analysis, and it's part of the GLUE benchmark. So the next step is to remind ourselves what this data set looks like.

So as you can see, we have a train set, a validation set and a test set. And each data set comes with three columns: sentence, label and idx. So the next step is to create our tokenized function, which applies the tokenizer to the sentence column of a batch of data. The next step is to apply our tokenized function to our data sets. We'll also create a data collator at this point. So the next step is to just print out the data collator, just to remind ourselves what's in it.

So as you can see, it includes information like vocab size, maximum sequence length, which side the padding goes on, which side we truncate on, and so forth. So the next step is to look at our tokenized data sets. So as you can see, some new columns have been added. There are now two new columns called Input IDs and Attention Mask. Input IDs are the token indices and Attention Mask tells us which tokens are real tokens and which are padding. So the next step is to remove obsolete columns from our data sets, including sentence and index.

We'll also rename label to labels to work with the PyTorch data loader, which we'll see very soon. So the next step is to print out our modified tokenized data sets. And as you can see, we now have three columns which are labels, input IDs and Attention mask. So the next step is to return to the PyTorch world and create data loader objects. The main difference between a train and validation is that for training we set shuffle equal to true. Also, note that we can pass in our data collator at this step.

So now let's look at what our data loader returns by printing out the shape of each item in the first batch. So we have a loop here and then we just break on the first iteration. So as you can see, the input IDs are shaped 32 by T and the attention mask has the same shape. The labels are just a 1D array of size 32, which makes sense since it's just sentiment analysis. So the next step is to check some of the values we'll need to pass into the encoder when we create one for this data sets.

We'll first check the number of distinct labels by casting the training labels to a set. So as you can see, we get back only zero and one. Which are the two classes? So the next step is to check the vocab size. As you can see, we have 28,996 tokens. The next step is to check the maximum sequence length. So basically for DistilBERT, it's always 512. So the next step is to actually build our encoder. Note that for some of these values, like DK, d_model and heads and so forth, I've just chosen these randomly, so feel free to play around with them yourself.

And again, we move the model to the GPU. So the next step is to create a loss and optimizer objects. Note that Hugging Face also includes an optimizer called AdamW, which is commonly used for Transformers. However, to keep things simple, we're just going to use regular Adam. The next step is to import datetime, which we'll use during training. So the next step is to define our training function as input. This takes in our model, criterion, optimizer, train loader, test loader and number of epochs.

Inside the function, we begin by initializing arrays to store our train losses and test losses. Next we enter a loop that runs epochs times. Inside the loop, we set the model to training mode and get the current time. We also initialize the train loss to zero and number of training samples to zero. Next we enter another loop that will loop through each batch in the train loader. Inside the loop, we begin by moving each tensor in the batch to the GPU. The next step is to zero the gradients.

The next step is to perform the forward pass and compute the loss. As you recall, the model takes in two inputs, which are the token IDs and corresponding mask. The next step is to call loss.backward() to compute gradients, and then optimizer.step() to do one step of gradient descent. Finally, we add the loss to our train loss. Now, importantly, note that the loss computed by PyTorch is the average cross entropy over the batch. However, in order to compute the average across the whole training set, we need to convert this back into a sum and then divide by the total number of training samples once the loop is complete.

So this is why we multiply by the batch size and also add the batch size to n_train. Once we've completed the loop, we then compute the average train loss. So the next step is to put the model into eval mode. We then initialize the test loss to zero and the number of test samples to zero. Then we loop through each batch in the valid loader. Inside this loop we go through all the steps again except for the validation set instead of the train set. In addition, we don't compute gradients, nor do we do any gradient descent, since we just want to evaluate the model on the validation set.

So once we finished evaluating the model, we then save the train loss and the test loss just in case you want to plot them later. We also print some information, including the epoch number, the train loss, the test loss, and the time it took for the epoch. And finally we return the train losses and test losses. So now let's run the train method. Okay. So as you can see, this completed relatively fast. The loss has decreased mostly in the train set and only slightly on the test set.

Now, since this is a classifier, what we are really interested in is how accurate the model is. We begin by putting the model into eval mode once again, although this is redundant since we did that on the second part of the training loop. The next step is to initialize n_correct and n_total to zero. As you recall, the accuracy is just n_correct divided by n_total. Next, we enter a loop that goes through each batch of the training set. Inside the loop, we move each item in the batch to the GPU.

The next step is to get our model outputs, which, as you recall, our logits. The next step is to get the prediction from the logits. Note that we can use the max function for this, which returns both the max and the argmax. The next step is to update n_correct and n_total. For n_correct, we just add the number of predictions that equal the labels and for n_total, We just add the number of samples in the batch. Once we're outside the loop, we compute the train accuracy by dividing n_correct by n_total.

The next step is to do the same thing for the test set. And lastly, we print our accuracies. So as you can see, we get about 93% on the train set and 80% on the test set. As an exercise, you might want to try computing other metrics like the F1 and AUC, since it could be that this data set is imbalanced.

8. How to Implement Causal Self-Attention From Scratch
======================================================

So in this lecture, we will be moving on to the decoder. As you'll see, most of this is surprisingly similar to the encoder with just a few minor changes. We'll begin with the same imports as the previous notebook. So the next step is to implement a new module which will call causal self attention. You'll notice that this is very similar to the multi head attention we previously implemented. The main difference is that we need a special mask so that each output can only pay attention to past tokens.

So firstly, notice that the first few parts of this are exactly the same as the encoder. We still have the same inputs, except that we also take in max len, which is needed to build our causal mask. However, DK is the same and heads is the same and all the layers are the same. Remember that the architecture of causal self attention is exactly the same. We're really just adding a mask before we apply the softmax. So this part is the same. The layers are the same. The final layer is the same.

And then we have something new. So you'll recall that we discussed in the theory lectures what this mask should look like. Basically, it's a mask where the lower left triangle is all ones and the diagonal is all ones, and then the upper right triangle is all zeros. It's also a size T by T, or in this case, max_len by max_len. Note that we can create such a matrix by using the PyTorch function tril, which means the lower triangular. There's also a function for upper triangular, which does the opposite.

So as with positional encoding, we call register_buffer so that this tensor is saved if we ever save the model. And notice that we also reshape the matrix to be of size one by one by max_len by max_len. Basically this is because, as you recall, during attention, our data is four dimensional and so the mask also needs to be four dimensional in order to broadcast correctly. So now we have the forward method, which is almost the same as before. Note that we also take in a mask, but this is a different mask than the one we just created.

The mask we just created was to make sure that the outputs only pay attention to past inputs. So that is our causal mask. On the other hand, the mask we take in as input is our padding mask, which is used to handle padding in the inputs. So most of this is the same as the encoder. We basically just have one new line where we apply the causal mask. And so just to go through this very quickly, so we have Q, K and V going through the Q, K and V linear layers. We get N and T, which are the first two parts of the shape. We reshape and transpose Q, K and V, and then we compute the attention weights.

So it's Q times K transpose over here, then we add the padding mask. So the padding mask is exactly the same as the encoder mask that we just looked at in the previous lectures. And so the new part is where we do the causal mask. So as you can see for the causal mask, we index the causal mask so that it only goes up to T, which is the sequence length of the current batch. And we still use the masked_fill function and we still do the softmax afterwards.

So everything other than this part where we do the causal mask is still the same. So since this lecture has been pretty short, we'll move on to the next few parts, which are also pretty simple. So the next module is the transformer block, which is nearly identical to the encoder. The main difference is that we also need to accept max_len as input since this needs to be passed on to the causal self attention module. In the forward method. We've also renamed the mask to pad_mask just to make things a bit more clear.

And next we have the positional encoding, which is also exactly the same.

9. How to Implement a Transformer Decoder (GPT) From Scratch
============================================================

So in this lecture, we will continue looking at how to implement the transformer decoder. At this point we have all our component modules and all that is left is to build the decoder module itself. So first we have the init method. This takes in the vocab size, maximum sequence length, d_k, d_model, number of heads, number of transformer layers and dropout probability. Note that the number of classes is not needed because the number of classes at the output is just the vocab size.

As you recall, the job of the decoder is to predict the next token in a sequence. So inside the init method, we create all of the modules we need. We begin with the embedding. Then we have the positional encoding. Then we have the transformer blocks. Then we have Layer Norm and then the final fully connected layer. Notice that this is pretty much the same as the encoder. And so next we have the forward method. This takes in some batch of input sequences called X and the corresponding pad mask.

Inside the function we pass X through all the usual layers. Note again that this is basically the same as the encoder, except that there is one crucial difference with the encoder. You'll recall that we used a many-to-one task. In other words, the input sequence was a sequence of many tokens while the output was a single class. As such, after going through the transformer blocks, we selected only the output at a single time step to be passed on to layer norm and then the final fully connected layer.

In this case we do not have a many-to-one task, but a many-to-many task and recall that we discussed this in the theory lectures. Although we could treat language modeling as a many-to-one task, this would require us to break up the input data such that a single sequence turns into multiple samples, but using a many-to-many task, we only have to pass in each sequence once and the model learns all of the sub sequences simultaneously. So let's run this.

And so the next step is to test our decoder with some dummy inputs just to make sure that it works without any errors as before. Note that these values are completely made up, so feel free to change them. The next step is to create our device object for the GPU and move the model to the GPU. So the next step is to create a random dummy input. As before. We'll create a sequence of integers with a batch size of eight and a sequence length of 512. Since the vocab size is 20,000, the token IDs can be anywhere from 0 to 20000.

Exclusive. We also move the resulting array to the GPU. The next step is to pass our input into the model. Note that we are not using padding for this first test. So as you can see, the result has the shape eight by 512 by 20,000, which makes sense. There are eight samples with a sequence length of 512 and each prediction can belong to one of 20,000 possible tokens. So the next step is to create a random padding mask of size N by T and to also move this to the GPU.

The next step is to test our model again with our mask. So as you can see, the model is able to produce an output without any errors. And as expected, it has the same shape.

=====================================================

Now, for this example, all we really need is some unlabeled text, but to make it as simple as possible, we'll just use the same data set that we used for the previous lecture, except that we'll ignore the labels.
