For this notebook, we only need the sentence. So the next step is to define our tokenized function, which will call our tokenizer on a batch of sentences and also truncate the result if needed.

The next step is to map the tokenize function to a raw data sets. We'll also create our data collator at this point. The next step is to print out our tokenized data sets. So as you can see, we now have a new column for the input IDs and the attention mask. Note that the attention mask is the pad mask. So the next step is to remove unnecessary columns, which in this case are sentence index and label. So the next step is to import the data loader class and to create a data loader object.

Now, technically, we could have kept the validation loader in this notebook, but for some reason I decided to remove it. In fact, it could still be useful because we still would like to see the loss go down for the out of sample data as well. So perhaps as an exercise you can try putting it back. But for this notebook, we'll create a train loader only. Notice again that the data loader takes in our data collator. So this will do stuff like truncation and converting the result into a torch tensor.

So the next step is just a copy of the previous notebook where we check how the train loader works. Inside the loop, we simply go through each item in the batch and print out its shape. We then break so that it only does this for the first step. Okay. So as expected, both the input IDs and mask are torch tensors of size N by T. Note that there are no labels. So the next step is to figure out what our pad token is, since this will be important later on in this notebook.

As you can see, this is stored as an attribute of the tokenizer, so we can just call tokenizer pad token ID. As you can see, the value is zero. So the next step is to create our actual decoder model with the correct size for the vocab and sequence length. Again, all of these hyper parameters are completely arbitrary, so feel free to choose your own. So the next step is to create our loss and optimizer objects. Since our task is still classification, we can still use the cross entropy loss.

However, there's a difference with this and the previous notebook. So unlike the encoder notebook, this adds a new argument called ignore index. So why do we have this? Well, as you recall, our transformer now is many-to-many using the data collator, every input will be padded to have the same sequence length as the longest sequence in the current batch. As such, although every input and hence every target will be of length T, not all of the T tokens will be actual tokens from the sequence.

Some of them will just be padding. But we don't want to include padding tokens in the loss. Otherwise, our model may be biased to produce padding tokens as outputs. In order to avoid that situation, we use the ignore index argument and we pass in the padding token ID so that padding does not contribute to the loss. The next step is to import datetime, which will be used to time the duration of each epoch during the training loop. So the next step is to implement our train method.

This takes as input the model, the loss function, the optimizer, the data loader and the number of epochs. Inside the function, we initialize the train losses to be an empty array. Next, we enter a loop for each epoch. Inside the loop, we set our model to train mode. We then capture the current time and we set our train loss to be an empty list. So in this notebook we'll compute the train loss per epoch a bit more simply, instead of adding all the losses together and dividing by the total number of samples, at the end, we'll simply take the average of the train loss per batch.

This is not as accurate since not all batches will have the same number of samples, but it keeps things simple. Next, we move through each batch in the train loader. Inside this loop, we move each item in the batch to the GPU. The next step is to call zero_grad to zero the parameter gradients. The next step is the most important step, which is to create the targets. Now, as you recall, all we currently have are the input IDs and the mask. However, this is unsupervised training.

This is a language model. The target is simply a shifted version of the input so that the transformer is learning to predict the next token in a sequence to compute the targets. We'll first make a copy of the input IDs. The next step is to shift the targets to the left. Note that this is okay and we're not losing the first token in each sentence. This is because we use the DistilBERT tokenizer. So the first token is always the special CLS token, and that's obviously not needed as a target.

Now, to do this shifting efficiently, what we can do is call torch.roll. So you can think of our tensor as like a circular buffer. So if everything gets shifted to the left, whatever is at the first position on the left, we'll get carried over to the end. Now, because we don't want to have CLS tokens at the end of each sequence in our targets, we will set the last time step in the targets to be the pad token, which we know will be ignored in the loss.

So the next step is to perform our forward pass to get the model outputs. Note that the model takes in the input IDs and the corresponding mask. Now, the loss is a bit complicated to compute because our targets are not a single label per sample, but a whole sequence of labels. So one simple way to do this, which I've commented out, is just to flatten the outputs in the targets. So while the output is initially N by T by V, where V is the vocab size, we can flatten the first two dimensions so that it becomes N times T by V for the targets which were a 2D array of size N by T, we make them into a 1D array of size N times

T. And so in that way the outputs are 2D logits, and the targets are just 1D labels. And so this is the same situation that we had before. But another way to do this, which is the one we're actually using, is to transpose the dimensions of the input so that the time dimension comes last. This is what PyTorch expects if your targets are more than two dimensions. So instead of n by t by V, it expects N by V by T. So the next few steps are just boilerplate code, which we've already seen.

We call loss.backward() to compute the gradients and then we call optimizer step to do one step of gradient descent. We then append our loss to the list of train losses. Once we get outside the loop, we then compute the train loss for the whole epoch to be the mean of the train losses list that we collected for each batch. Next, we save the loss for this epoch to our list of losses. Finally, we compute the time once again and the time difference between the start and end of this epoch.

And then we print the result. Once the outer loop is complete, we return the train losses, which can be plotted later if needed. So the next step is to call our train function. Please note that I've chosen the number of epochs arbitrarily, so feel free to play around. So the next step is to just play around with our trained model to see what it can do. At this point, we'll create the data loader for the validation set with a batch size of one. The next step is to put our model into eval mode and to get the output for the first sample in the validation set.

The next step is to check the shape of the output. As you can see, it's one by 12 by vocab size. The next step is to take the argmax over the vocab size, since that will tell us which tokens our model is trying to predict. As you can see, this returns a list of token IDs. The next step is to save these predictions to a new variable called prediction IDs. Now, since the prediction IDs are just integers, we would like to convert them into actual tokens so that they can be more easily read.

So as you can see, the result doesn't make a lot of sense. The next step is to check what the input for the sample actually was. So it says it's a charming and often affecting journey. Note that it's difficult to tell from this whether our model's predictions make sense. So to sort of check this, we can decode the first few input IDs concatenated with the prediction for only the next token. So as you can see, it says it's a good which is a totally reasonable guess.

So the next step is to look at how to actually use our model to generate text. We begin by creating an initial prompt, which is just it. In order to get our model to predict the next token, we need to think through what steps have to be done. The first step is to tokenize the prompt in order to convert the text into a sequence of integers that our model can read. So as you can see, we get our input IDs along with the corresponding mask. So the next step is to pass these input IDs and the mask into our model to get a corresponding output.

Now, importantly, note that we cut off the final time step from the input IDs. This is because with DistilBERT, every input sequence ends with the SEP token. But we don't care what our model predicts should come after the SEP token, as that is already the end of a sentence. Instead, we care about what our model predicts for the actual last token in our prompt. So as you can see, the result is one by four by vocab size. Note that the sequence length is four because the prompts got converted into five tokens.

So the next step is to look at how to get an actual prediction. So to get a prediction, we need to get the correct output, the correct output for the next token, and our sequence will be at the final time step. So we index the outputs at minus one in the time dimension. We'll call the result prediction IDs. So the next optional step is to decode the prediction ID. In practice, what we're going to do next is to put all of this into a loop so that we get a full sequence of text.

Only after we've gathered the full sequence will we bother to decode the tokens into anything we can read. So it's a because our first word was it's so it's it's a and then the model will generate something after that. So as promised, we're going to take all the code we just wrote and put it into a loop in order to generate a full sequence of text. This time we'll begin with the prompt. It's a as before. We'll begin by tokenizing the prompt. And also as before, we remove the final SEP token at the end and we also move the two tensors to the GPU.

The next step is to enter a loop that iterates a maximum of 20 steps. Note that I've chosen this number arbitrarily, so if you'd like to go longer than that, please feel free to do so. Inside the loop we pass in our input IDs along with the mask into the model to get the outputs. Now remember that the outputs have the shape N by T by V, but we only care about the final time step. So we index the outputs at the final time step and then we take the argmax to get the predicted token ID.

From there we concatenate the prediction with the current input IDs and make this the new sequence of input IDs. Actually, let's scroll up so we can see the whole thing. And remember that this is how we generate text. The model simply uses its past predictions as inputs during the next step. And although it's not really necessary, we can also keep creating the mask. Note that the default is no mask, which would have the same effect as a mask with all ones.

Finally, we check if the current prediction ID is equal to the SEP token ID, which signals the end of a sentence. If we find this, then we break out of the loop. So let's run this. Oh, and you could also put this into a function to generate text from an arbitrary prompt. So the next step is to decode the input IDs, since it now contains both the prompt and all the models generated words. So as you can see, the result is it's a good time, which kind of makes sense since our data set is a set of movie reviews.

And please note that the result will be different every time you run this. So one time I saw it's a good movie, which also makes sense.

11. Implement a Seq2Seq Transformer From Scratch for Language Translation (pt 1)
================================================================================

So in this lecture, we will move on to our next implementation, which is a full encoder decoder sequence to sequence transformer. At this point we've already built an encoder and we've already built a decoder. So now all we need to do is figure out how to put them together. So we begin with our usual imports. So the next step is to implement our multi-head attention class. Now you'll recognize that in the previous two notebooks we had slightly different versions of this class with the encoder.

There was no need for a causal mask, but for the decoder there was always a causal mask. In this version, since we need it to handle both cases, we'll need an option to use a causal mask. So that's this option right here. And note that the default value is false. Now, if we look at the init method, you'll see that all of the layers we create are the same. And at the end we only create a causal mask if causal has been set to true. So next we have the forward method.

Now, this will have a few major differences worth pointing out. So the inputs are the same. We still have Q, K, V and the padding mask and we still pass Q, k, and V through their respective linear layers. However, the next step is where things are different. As you recall, in the sequence to sequence transformer, it is possible to apply attention where we want to know which decoder output should pay attention to which encoder inputs. This corresponds to the part where the encoder is connected to the decoder.

So inside this attention layer, K and V come from the encoder while Q comes from the decoder. And because of this, they can have different sequence lengths, unlike the previous notebooks. So t output will come from Q while t input will come from K. The next step is to reshape Q, K and V. So what changes about this is that whereas before where we just had t, now we have either a t output or t input. The next step is to compute the attention scores. Note that this does not change.

In addition, note that the padding mask also does not change. So what does change a bit is how we apply the causal mask. In this case, when we index the causal mask, the third index goes up to t output, while the fourth index goes up to t input. Next, we compute the softmax, which is the same. Next, we multiply the attention weights by the values, which is the same. The next step is to reshape the attention outputs before going through the final fully connected layer.

In this case, the T dimension corresponds with T output. And finally, we do the final projection layer. So the next step is to implement the encoder block. This is what we call the transformer block in the encoder notebook, and it's pretty much the same as that notebook. Aside from the fact that when we create the multi head attention layer, we set causal to false. Okay, So next we have the decoder block, which is the transformer block from the decoder notebook.

Now, this is going to be very different from the previous notebook. So as you recall, when we discuss decoder only models, the decoder block only contains one causal self attention. But in the sequence to sequence transformer, the decoder block actually contains two attentions one for causal self attention and one for taking the input from the encoders outputs. Overall, we have three main blocks, two multi-head attentions and one ANN. As such, we'll need three layer norms.

Next, we have our two multi-head attentions, one with causal equal to true, and one with causal equal to false. The next step is to create our ANN layers, which is the same as before, and a dropout, which is the same as before. So the next step is to define the forward method. Now, note that this has many more inputs. It takes in the encoder output, the decoder input, the encoder mask and the decoder mask. So these are the padding masks. Note that this just follows the diagram from the Attention Is All You Need paper.

So the first step is to pass the decoder input through causal self attention. We then add the residual connection and layer norm as usual. The second step is to process the encoder output with another multi head attention. We then add the residual connection and layer norm as usual. The third step is to go through the ANN, and we then add the residual connection and layer norm as usual. Finally, we apply dropout and we return the results. And next we have positional encoding, which is exactly the same as before.

So the next step is to create our encoder. Note that this is slightly different from our first notebook. To make the differences more clear. Note that I tried not to delete anything. Instead, I simply commented out what used to be there. And we don't need any more. For example, we don't need any classes anymore because we no longer have any classifier head. So the next thing we don't need is inside the init method. And of course this is the classifier head which I just mentioned.

So the final thing that changes is inside the forward method. In our previous notebook we did a many-to-one task, but in this case we want all of the hidden vectors for every time step. So now there's no need to index the output at any particular time step. We do still perform the final layer norm, but we do not perform the final fully connected layer, which we also deleted from the init method. So the next step is to look at the decoder. Note that the inputs into the init method are the same as the encoder.

Okay, so inside the init method, we create all the layers you'd expect. So that's the embedding, positional encoding, decoder transformer blocks, layer norm and a final linear layer. So inside the forward method, we now take in many more inputs compared to the previous notebook. This takes in the encoder output, the decoder input, the encoder padding mask and the decoder padding mask. Inside the method, we first passed the decoder input through the embedding and positional encoding.

Next, we pass the result into the transformer blocks in addition to the decoder input. We also pass in the encoder output and the padding masks. Finally, we end with layer norm and a fully connected layer. It's good to keep in mind again that this is a many-to-many transformer since the output of our model is for generating text, as in the previous lecture. Okay, So next we have the full transformer class. This is pretty simple since we're going to use the encoder and decoder to do most of the work.

Inside the init method, we just take in an encoder and decoder we've already created and save them as instance variables. Inside the forward method, we take in all the model inputs and corresponding masks. Then we pass the data through the encoder, which gives us the encoder output. We then pass the encoder output into the decoder along with the decoder inputs and corresponding masks. This gives us the decoder output which we then return. So the next step is to test our model with some dummy inputs.

We'll begin by creating the encoder and decoder objects again with made up values for the inputs. We also create a transformer object passing in the encoder and decoder created above. The next step is to move both the encoder and decoder to the GPU. So the next step is to create some dummy data. This is useful because it forces us to think about what kinds of data need to be passed in. So we begin by creating an encoder input. We'll say it has a batch size of eight and a sequence length of 512.

The vocab size is 20,000, so we generate integers between zero and 20,000. Next, we create the decoder input. Note that this must have the same batch size, but it is allowed to have a different sequence length, which we should test to make sure that everything works as expected. So this time we set the sequence length to 256. Note that the vocab size can also be different. So we've set this to 10,000, so we generate integers between zero and 10,000. The next step is to create padding masks for both the encoder and decoder inputs.

In this case, the only requirement is that they have the same size as their corresponding inputs and contain only zeros and ones. So I've set the first half of the values to one and the second half to zero. Finally, we pass everything through the transformer and check the shape of the output. Okay. So as you can see, our model works as expected. We get a batch size of eight, a sequence length of 256 and a vocab size of 10,000, which matches what we set for the decoder.

12. Implement a Seq2Seq Transformer From Scratch for Language Translation (pt 2)
================================================================================

So in this lecture, we'll continue looking at our notebook where we will implement an encoder decoder transformer for translating language. At this point, we've already built our model and tested it on some dummy data. In this lecture we're going to download our actual data and train our model to produce useful translations. So we begin by downloading an English to Spanish dataset, which we originally used in my third NLP course. So hopefully this offers a nice point of comparison for those of you who took that course.

And if you didn't take that course, don't worry. The next step is to check out the first few lines of our file. So as you can see, each line contains two columns and it is a tab separated text file with English on the left and Spanish on the right. So the next step is to modify our data just a bit to work better with the Hugging Face data sets package. We'll begin by downloading our data using pd.read_csv. So as you can see, our data was loaded in correctly.

The next step is to check the shape of our data set. So as you can see, we have over 100,000 translation examples. Now in order to make this notebook run in a more reasonable amount of time, we're going to keep only the first 30,000 samples. It's also helpful that this data set is approximately sorted by sentence length. So by taking the first 30,000 samples, we are getting the shortest samples. And since transformers have quadratic time complexity, this will keep the computation down.

As an exercise, you might want to try selecting samples at random or even trying the whole data set, which will probably improve the model's performance. So the next step is to give our DataFrame column names which are used by the Hugging Face data sets package. We'll also write our dataframe out to a new CSV. The next step is to check the contents of our CSV just to ensure that they are what we expect. So as you can see, our CSV looks as expected. Importantly, we now have the column names en and es.

So the next step is to install Hugging Face transformers and other packages which will be used in the following code. The next step is to load in our data set using the load_dataset method. The next step is to print out our data set just to see what we got back. So as you can see, we get a data set of 30,000 samples with two columns called en and es as expected. So the next step is to do a train test split. So we have 21,000 training samples and 9000 test samples.

So the next step is to create our tokenizer. Now, theoretically, you could have just found tokenizers for Spanish and English and use them both separately. This would be a completely valid approach, and it's the first one I thought of when making this notebook. However, what I eventually decided to do was to just use the same tokenizer we used earlier in the course when we looked at translation fine tuning. So depending on which approach you take, there might be subtle differences in the upcoming code.

So the next step is to just test our tokenizer on the first training sample of our data set. So as you recall, with this tokenizer, when we want to tokenize the targets, we need to specify that this is the case. In order to do that, we use the text_target argument. We'll print out the tokens as well, just to remind ourselves that they do not look like BERT. So as you can see, the tokens do not look like how they do with BERT and BERT like models.

Importantly, note that unlike BERT, there is no start token. Recall that this is useful as the first input into the decoder. So we'll need to handle this particular issue later in this notebook. The next step is to check the original Spanish sentence. So as you can see, we get the same sentence as above, but without the special tokens and underscores at the beginning of every word. So the next step is to implement the preprocess function. As you recall, this will process both the inputs and the targets.

So we begin by setting the maximum sequence length for both the inputs and the targets. Note that I did not check these values, so you might want to do that yourself as an exercise. Inside the preprocess function, we first begin by tokenizing the English inputs which are located in the en column of the batch. We'll call the result model inputs. The next step is to tokenize the targets which come from the column es. We'll call the result labels. Importantly, we know that the tokenized target is located in the input IDs field of the labels variable, but what we need is to have everything in the same object so we save the target tokens to the model inputs variable using the field labels.

At this point, the English tokens are stored in a field called input IDs and the target tokens are stored in a field called labels. Finally, we return the model inputs. So the next step is to apply the preprocess function to our data sets. The next step is to look at our tokenized data sets. So as you can see, we have input IDs which correspond to the English inputs, the attention mask, which also corresponds to the English inputs and the labels. Now, you might find it odd that the labels don't have any mask, which I realized after writing this notebook.

I suppose you could have saved the mask from the tokenizer, but I never actually bothered to check. What we will do later is create our own mask on the fly. In fact, we still haven't created the decoder inputs which the mask should correspond with. So in other words, even if we did get the mask from the tokenizer, we couldn't use that because it's aligned with the targets and not the decoder inputs. So the next step is to create our data collator.

As you recall, there is a special kind of data collator for seq2seq. The next step is to test our data collator to remind ourselves how it works. We'll test it on the first five training samples. As you can see, we get a dictionary like object with the same keys as above. The next step is to look at the input IDs. So as you can see, the input IDs might be a little different than what you expect if you're used to working with BERT. Note that the padding value is not zero, which we can tell since most of the inputs end with 65,000.

It seems that zero is used for the end of sentence token, while 65,000 is used to pad. The next step is to look at the attention mask. So if we compare this to what we had above, we can see that it is in fact the case that 65,000 is the pad token. So the next step is to check the labels. So as you recall, one thing that's pretty weird about the tokenizer is that it pads targets differently than it pads the inputs. As you can see, the targets are padded with -100.

So the next step is to check which special IDs exist in our tokenizer. This will be useful in the coming steps. So as you can see, we have zero, one and 65,000. We know that 65,000 is padding and we know that zero is end of sentence, but we do not know what the one means. So the next step is to print out the special tokens. So as you can see, the ID one corresponds with the unknown token. This also confirms that zero is end of sentence and 65,000 is padding.

Now, one important thing to remember is that the tokenizer always adds an end of sentence token to everything you pass in to demonstrate this will tokenize the pad token. So as you can see, we get back two IDs corresponding to padding and end of sentence. So end of sentence is always added. The next step is to create a data loader object. As input we pass in the tokenized data set as well as the data collator we created above. So the next step is to check how our data loader works.

As before, we'll print out the shape of each item in the first batch and then break out of the loop. So as you can see, we get input IDs, attention mask and labels all of shape N by T. However, note that t for the input does not necessarily have to be the same as t for the output. So the next step is to check our tokenizer vocab size, which, as you recall, is one of the inputs into our model constructor. So the vocab size is 65,001. Now just for fun,

I wanted to see what some of these tokens were at the higher indices. So as you can see, it's a lot of weird Unicode. Please feel free to try other values for yourself. Now, one thing our tokenizer doesn't have, which is why I was doing some of this exploration is the start of sentence token. You'll recall that we need this when we form our decoder inputs, since this is how our model knows that it is at the start of a sentence, it's not possible for the decoder to have no decoder input.

I decided to add my own special token for CLS, which to my knowledge isn't used in this tokenizer elsewhere. As you can see, I've set it to be like an <s> tag, which hopefully makes sense since the end of sentence token is the corresponding end tag. Okay, so now let's check what happens if we tokenize our new token. So as you can see, this results in a new ID 65,001. Now, oddly, when you check the vocab size, you can see that it's not updated. So it still says 65,001 when it should really be one higher than our maximum token ID.

So the next step is to create our encoder decoder and transformer objects. As you can see, I've set the vocab size to be the tokenizer vocab size plus one for the reasons we just mentioned. The rest of the values are completely made up, so feel free to change them as you see fit. The next step is to move our model to the GPU. The next step is to create our loss and optimizer as before. We need the loss function to ignore padding tokens, which in this case is represented with -100.

So the next step is to implement our training loop. Now, since this lecture has been quite long already, we're going to take a break. In the next lecture, we'll continue on from this point.

13. Implement a Seq2Seq Transformer From Scratch for Language Translation (pt 3)
================================================================================

So in this lecture, we will continue looking at our notebook for a sequence to sequence transformer. So we're just at the point where we are about to implement the train method as input into this method, we take in the model, criterion, optimizer, train loader, validation loader and number of epochs. Inside the function. We create empty arrays to store the loss at each epoch for both train and test. Next we enter a loop for each epoch. Inside the loop, we set the model to train mode.

We capture the current time and we instantiate an empty list to store the loss for each batch. The next step is to loop through each batch in the data set. Inside this loop, we take each item in the batch and move it to the GPU. Next we zero the parameter gradients. So the next step is to create a model inputs and targets. We begin by grabbing each item from our batch, which includes the encoder input IDs, the attention mask, and the labels which represent the target.

At this point, we do not yet have the decoder inputs. So the next step is to create these decoder inputs. So we begin by first cloning the targets. As you recall, the decoder inputs should simply be a shifted version of the targets. Now, to contrast this with the last lecture, we actually had the decoder inputs and we wanted to create the targets. But in this lecture we have the targets and we're trying to create the decoder inputs. As such, we need to shift the targets to the right in order to get the decoder inputs.

To do this, we set shifts equal to one. This is the opposite of what we did before, where we set it to minus one. We also set dims equal to one, since that corresponds to the time dimension. Now recall that we've just used the roll function to shift, which will bring anything that was at the end of the targets to the front of the decoder inputs. But of course, the decoder input should start with the start tag. So in the next step we set all the decoder inputs at time index zero to be the start token ID, which is 65,001.

Now, you'll also recall that the model has an input for the decoder mask. This is because not every sequence in the decoder input will have the same length without padding, so the decoder mask is needed. However, before we create the decoder mask, we also have to modify the decoder input. As you recall, for some reason the tokenizer uses -100 as the padding token for the targets, but -100 is not a valid input into the transformer because these values need to go through an embedding matrix, and the embedding matrix can only be indexed from zero up to vocab size and hence -100 is not a valid input.

Therefore, what we need to do is change any values which are -100 into the tokenizer pad token ID. So the next step is to finally create the decoder mask. In order to create the decoder mask. We first create an array of ones with the same size as the decoder input. Then we set any value where we have padding to be zero. So it's zero where we have padding and ones everywhere else. The next step is to do our forward pass to get the outputs. We call our model with the encoder input, the decoder input encoder mask and decoder mask.

As before, when we compute the loss, the outputs have the shape N by T by vocab size, but we need vocab size to be in the second dimension and time to be last. So we transpose the output at index two and index one. The next step is to call loss.backward() and optimizer.step() as we normally do. We also append the loss for this batch to the train loss list. Then we compute the average train loss for the batch. So the next step is to do the same thing for the test set.

Since this code is essentially the same as above, there is no need to explain it again, except note that we put the model into eval mode before we begin. So once we have both our train loss and test loss, we save them to our arrays to store these losses. Finally, we print some information about the loss and duration of each epoch, and then we return the train losses and test losses. So the next step is to run our train method. Okay, So it takes about 20 seconds per epoch.

So the next step is to try our trained model as before. We'll go through the steps one by one first to show you how a translation might be generated. After that, we'll write a whole function that does all the work inside. So if we want to translate some arbitrary text, all we need to do is pass it into this function. So we'll begin by choosing an arbitrary sentence from our test set. Please feel free to change the index to translate some other sentence if you prefer.

We'll also print out the sentence we chose. So it says, Can I take a day off? The next step is to tokenize the input sentence. We'll call the result enc_input. Okay, so this is the encoder input. Now remember that our model has two inputs, one for the encoder and one for the decoder. So now we need to create the decoder input. Since we're now running the transformer in generative mode, the decoder input is just the sentence start tag again. We use the tokenizer to get the model inputs, but this time we use target mode.

So note again that when we tokenize any text, the end of sentence tag always gets added. So the next step is to move our encoder input and decoder input to the GPU. We then pass these to the transformer to get our output. Note that we index the decoder input and decoder mask only up to time step minus one instead of the whole array since the last value is the end of sentence tag. So the next step is to check the shape of our output. So as you can see, it's one by one by 65,002 as expected.

One sample, one time step and 65,002 is our vocab size. Now in practice, how we actually want to use the model is to use the encoder and decoder separately. To do this, we begin by obtaining the encoder output, which only looks at the encoder input along with its attention mask. In other words, just the English sentence. So note that the result has the shape of one by eight by 64. This means one sample, eight time steps and 64 hidden size. The next step is to pass the encoder output into the decoder, along with the actual decoder inputs.

So as you can see, the result is one by one by vocab size as before. Now just as a sanity check. It would be nice to confirm that what we just did by looking at the encoder and decoder in separate steps is equivalent to going through the whole transformer. So in order to check this, we'll check whether the two outputs are close in value. Note that we use the allclose function since floating point numbers are usually never exactly equal. So this confirms that the encoder and decoder separately produce the same output as the whole transformer model.

So the next step is to demonstrate how we will actually use the decoder to generate the rest of the translation. To do this, we first begin by setting some variables for the decoder input IDs and the decoder attention mask. As above, we leave out the last value, which is just the end of sentence tag that the tokenizer adds. The next step is to enter a loop that goes for a maximum of 32 steps. This means if we try to generate more than 32 tokens, we'll just stop.

Of course, you can make this longer if you choose. So inside the loop, we first pass our data through the decoder to get the decoder output. Remember that we've already obtained the encoder output. Now, you recall that I said that this process is done in an auto regressive fashion. So the whole idea is take this output, convert it into a prediction, append it to the decoder inputs, and then run the whole thing again to get the next output. In other words, generate one token at a time and then on the next round the token we just generated becomes part of the input.

So the next step is to get the prediction ID by taking the argmax over the decoder outputs at the final time step. So this is the same as the previous notebook. The next step is to append the prediction ID to the existing decoder input IDs. So the decoder input IDs have grown by one token, which is the latest predicted token. Next, we recreate the mask. Since we only have one sample and every decoder input is a relevant token, we can just create an array of ones that is the same shape as the decoder input IDs and also note that this is optional since it will work without a mask anyway.

Finally, at the end of the loop, we check if our prediction ID is equal to the end of sentence tag. If it is, we break out of the loop. since the model thinks we're done. So the next step is to check the translation. We can do this by decoding the decoder input IDs, which now contain every token generated by the model. So this is the translation. Note that it will be different every time you run this, but what I've noticed is that it's usually pretty close to being correct if it's not already fully correct.

So the next step is to check the ground truth translation. So as you can see, it's pretty close. Note that it's not necessary for the translation to be exactly the same as the prediction. Sometimes the prediction is different, but still a valid translation. One thing we can do actually is paste this into Google Translate and see what it gives us. So this gives us the correct original English sentence. So the next step is to take everything we just wrote and put it into a function called translate.

This demonstrates how you might do this in the real world where you just have a convenient function you can call whenever you want to translate text. As input into this function, we take in a single input sentence represented as a string. The first thing we do inside the function is to get the encoder outputs. To do this, we first tokenize the input sentence and move the result to the GPU. We then pass the tokenized inputs into the encoder along with its mask.

Note that the mask is just all ones since we only have one sentence and hence it is optional. So the next step is to set up the decoder inputs. Since we know what the first value should be, we can just create the inputs directly without tokenizing the start tag. As you recall, the start tag has the ID 65,001. So the next step is to enter the decoder loop where we will generate the output tokens one step at a time. As before, we'll go through 32 steps, but feel free to change this as needed.

So inside the loop, we begin by passing our current data through the decoder. This gives us the decoder output. Next, we take the argmax of the decoder output at the final time step. This gives us our prediction ID, the ID of the predicted token for this step. The next step is to combine the newest prediction with the existing decoder input IDs. Once we've done that, we can create our Decoder Attention mask. Again, it's all ones since we only have one sample.

Finally, we check whether the prediction ID is equal to the end of sentence tag, in which case we break out of the loop. So at the end of our function, we decode whatever is inside the decoder input IDs. Since we don't want the start tag to be included, we can simply start at time index one. We then end the function by printing out the translated text. So to test out our new function, we're going to translate a sentence. Okay, So to my knowledge, this is pretty close to the true answer.

In fact, we can just test it ourselves. So it seems to be correct.

14. Implementation Section Summary
==================================

So in this lecture, we will summarize what we learned in this section. This section was all about how to implement transformers from scratch. In this section we implemented multi-head attention, the positional encoding, and three different types of transformer models. Specifically, we learn how to implement an encoder, a decoder and an encoder decoder combined. We also learn how to train them on actual data sets and use them to make predictions which many resources are missing.

What is interesting about this section is that we were able to build these models with only basic parts. For example, the linear layer dropout, residual connections and layer norm. In other words, all stuff that existed in 2016. So technically you could have built transformers using 2016 technology in 2016 had you only invented the attention mechanism. So I think that's a crucial point to keep in mind in order to do really cool things in machine learning.

It's not just a matter of using a high level library and doing stuff people have already done. Instead, it requires us to think of new approaches and implement them ourselves using existing tools and not wait around for someone else to build the tools for us. You could have been the one to implement Transformers if you invented them first. So keep inventing, keep trying new things, and I'll see you in the next lecture.

2. How to install Numpy, Scipy, Matplotlib, Pandas, IPython, Theano, and TensorFlow
===================================================================================

Hey, guys. In this lecture, I'm going to show you how to set up your development environment so that you can follow along in these courses. First, let's talk about operating systems. The three big ones are Windows, Linux and Mac. Most, but not all of my courses are part of the deep learning series. Eventually, the deep learning models we're going to build will become so complex that we will need specialized libraries like Theano and TensorFlow to implement them.

Now, as of today, Theano and TensorFlow are not officially supported on Windows. So if you are interested in deep learning, you will discover pretty soon that there is not much you can do without a ton of work. Even working with non GPU enabled libraries like numpy and matplotlib can be a challenge, but these are at least possible on windows. If you really must use windows and have your python environment inside of windows and you don't want to try the virtual machine method, which I'm going to discuss next.

Then one good library I know of is called Anaconda. You can get Anaconda at Continuum I o slash downloads. I can't vouch for this 100% because I haven't used it myself, but I've known others who have found it at least usable. Now I'm telling you this from experience, I've worked with a ton of clients one on one, and Python development, especially when it involves the NumPy/SciPy stack is not easy when it's on windows. 1. How to Code by Yourself (part 1)
===================================

Everyone. And welcome back to this class. This lecture is about how to write code independently when you're doing machine learning. You always hear me say that the first thing you should try to do when you learn an algorithm is to go and write it up in code by yourself before looking at someone else's code. For some students who are new to machine learning, it's not quite clear how to do that. So this lecture is going to explain the thought process you should go through in hopes that it encourages more people to try to code by themselves.

I like to encapsulate this in the phrase When it's time to code, you must code. So remember this whenever you see some code that demonstrates the concepts we learned in this course, even if you don't fully understand what's going on, coding and copying examples from others helps you build muscle memory. Sometimes people act like they are going to learn tennis by reading a book about tennis. This is, of course, obviously not possible. You need to learn some things about tennis which are pure fact and reside in your brain as conscious facts.

But then you have to go out and practice those concepts on the tennis court. Once you've exhausted and mastered everything you know so far, then you can learn new techniques. So you have this continuous cycle of learning new techniques and then practicing them. Why is this? Because learning the technique as pure fact doesn't mean you truly understand it. Practicing the technique helps you think about the technique from a different perspective. Practicing again and again leads to new understanding, to learning in the subconscious, which is muscle memory.

You must go through this cycle. But some students naively think they are going to read an entire book about tennis and then be a tennis master the first time they try playing. This is very common, especially when all you really need to do is sit there and watch a video and no one can force you to type. A lot of people don't even think to try. So just remember this. Whenever you see code, when it's time to code, you must code. First we are going to talk a little bit about why you want to code by yourself.

A lot of the time, what an algorithm does or how it works isn't quite clear the first time you learn about it. Every individual will have their own unique background, so they might be familiar with the patterns and know exactly what to do. While some individuals might have questions that no one else has. Sometimes you gloss over details because you assume you know what's going on when really there are important things you're not considering. So this is how trying to code by yourself can help you.

Coding by yourself forces you to think about each and every detail. It forces you to think line by line. You have to be familiar with the data types and the shapes of all your variables, and they all have to fit together properly. Kind of like Lego blocks. As an example, you know that in order to do element wise matrix addition, both matrices have to have the exact same shape. So if you find that they are not the same shape, then one of your previous assumptions was incorrect.

So you should go back and correct it. LEGO blocks have to follow a specific set of rules in order to fit together properly. If you make incorrect assumptions about how Lego blocks fit together and you try to join them, it's not going to work without trying to build things by yourself. You will never discover these details. Let's now talk about how to code by yourself. Consider the supervised machine learning scenario. We know that we're going to have some inputs and some targets, and we want to try to make predictions from the inputs that are very close to the targets.

We typically call the inputs X and the targets Y. Sometimes we call the targets T, but for the purpose of this lecture, we'll call the targets Y. Now, this next point is a key point. It doesn't actually matter what X is and what Y is. It doesn't matter if you're looking at an eCommerce data set and the columns of X might be time on, say, time of day, how many pages the user looked at and so on. You could just as well be looking at a data set of x ray images where each column is the pixel intensity of an image.

This is the key point. We say all data is the same. When we do linear regression, we don't have different kinds of linear regression for e-commerce and x-ray images. Linear regression is the same algorithm no matter what your data set is. So we say all data is the same. Theoretically, I could give you a data set of X's and y's, and you could train a classification algorithm on it without me even telling you what the X's and Y's mean. You should get very comfortable with this idea.

Sometimes people say, well, this isn't practical because I want to do concrete examples. But that's because they're not thinking intelligently. In fact, this is the most practical thing we can do because it means everything we learn. We can apply it to any data set that exists. It means that I can train a model on an e-commerce dataset, but I can also train a model on an online advertising dataset without learning anything new at all. It's truly the greatest form of lazy programming.

Learn something once and apply it to every industry. One great consequence of all data is the same is that there is an unlimited amount of practice opportunity for you. You can download data sets from Kaggle, from Google, from Wikipedia, from Amazon or from anywhere else, and try the algorithms you learned. What this means is let's say you have a dataset you care about more than something we do in class. In class, we have to use datasets that everybody can understand.

For example, text and images. Everybody should know what text and images are. Text and images are just fundamental data types on the web. You probably don't even think about the fact that text and images are data because it's so trivial. In any case, we work with text in images a lot because everybody understands them. But let's say you're a biologist and you know about DNA. You find DNA and genomics really interesting. So you want to use the algorithms.

We learn on that. Well, that's great. And you should totally do it. Remember, all data is the same. So all you need to do is convert your data into the appropriate X's and Y's to plug into our machine learning algorithms. The downside is we most likely won't talk about DNA in class unless it's a very simple example, because most computer scientists are not biologists too, so they don't know what DNA is. They don't understand the specifics. So an example wouldn't make much sense to them.

This is opposed to text and images which make sense to everybody. Same goes with any other specialized field like finance, computer networking, cosmology, and so on. So now we can start out from the right place to talk about supervised learning. We have our X's and we have our Y's. What do we do with them in supervised learning? We know that there are two main things we want to do. We want to do training, and we want to do prediction. In scikit-learn, all the models have the same API.

It doesn't matter if you're doing logistic regression or decision trees or random forests. All supervised models in scikit-learn have the same two functions fit and predict. Fitting a model is just a synonym for training a model. If you don't believe me, you can go look at the scikit-learn documentation yourself. When you're learning about supervised machine learning, what you're really learning is what goes inside these two functions. What are the parameters and how are those parameters learned?

That's really what all supervised machine learning is filling in these two functions. Taking this perspective is going to give you a code structure, and it's going to make things much easier to visualize in your head. Let's now do a simple example to solidify this idea and this example, I'm going to give you an algorithm and you will implement it in code. There are two key points I want to make before I give you the algorithm. First, I'm not going to give you much intuition about how it works.

Second, I'm not going to derive the theory behind why it works. The reason I want to mention these two key points is that you should realize you don't need these two pieces of information in order to translate pseudocode into code. A lot of time these three concepts intuition, theory and implementation work to reinforce each other. The reason I'm focusing on implementation right now is because it's the one people most often miss or think they don't need at all, when really it's an extremely important part of the learning process.

Okay. So the pseudocode is as follows. In my predict function, as you already know, I'm going to take in some input data x. My predictions will be y hat equals x times w. You might already recognize this as linear regression. However, forget what you know about linear regression and pretend that this is just some formula I gave you. What I can tell you is that we are creating some kind of regression model. It should be clear from this equation that the parameters of the model are contained in this vector of weights.

W I would probably also tell you that w is a vector that's the same size as the number of feature vectors in x. Of course this must be true in order for the matrix multiply to be valid. This acts as kind of a sanity check so you can make sure everything is making sense. In my fit function. I want to perform this loop some number of times, since we know from the previous slide that the parameters of the model are contained in w, then it should be clear that inside the fit function what we want to do is update w not surprisingly, that's what's happening here.

Another good sanity check now because you're already familiar with linear regression, you probably already recognize this as gradient descent. Again, I want you to pretend that you don't know that, but what do you know? You know that iterative algorithms are common in machine learning and that gradient descent is among the most common. You could probably infer that this is some form of gradient descent, but you don't need to know what it is in order to write it in code.

What else do we know? You know what a for loop is and you know how to write one in Python. You know that X is an N by D matrix containing the input data and that Y is an N-size vector containing the targets. You know that Y hat is an N-size vector containing the predictions. You know that W is a D-size vector containing the model parameters. And you know how to add, subtract and multiply these objects. So we can write this algorithm without even knowing how it works.

Of course, a lot about how it works can just be inferred from this formula. Here is what you don't know. You don't know t the number of times the loop is supposed to run. You don't know eta, the learning rate. This is completely fine. You can't let this lack of knowledge stop you in your tracks. The truth of the matter is, there are going to be situations where you're not told the exact numbers to plug in. In many cases, the answer is going to be it depends on the problem.

The important thing is you have to get used to the idea of trial and error, and you have to make educated guesses based on what you already know. So what can you do when you know that the learning rate is supposed to be a small number? How small? It depends on the problem. Typically, it's a number less than one, like 0.1. If that doesn't work, you might try lowering it. Typically we lower it by factors of ten. So for example, the next learning rate we would try is ten to the minus two, ten to the minus three and so on.

Typically in machine learning, whether you're looking at a supervised or unsupervised algorithm, there is an associated cost or objective function that you're trying to minimize. Let's suppose I tell you it's squared error, which is typically what we would use for regression. Well, now you have a way to choose the number of iterations of the loop and the learning rate. How do we do this? So inside your fit function you would plot the cost as a function of iteration.

I always recommend doing this no matter what algorithm you're looking at. In general, you always want the cost to converge. The pattern you typically see is that there is a steep drop at the beginning and it slowly flattens out as the number of iterations increases. To choose the number of iterations you want to stop when the curve is sufficiently flat. You might recognize this as a diminishing returns scenario. The returns diminish because at the beginning we reduce the cost by a lot in just a few iterations.

At the end, we can do a lot of iterations, but we reduce the cost by almost nothing. An alternative is you could use a separate validation set and stop when the validation cost increases. But that is not the focus of this lecture. Unlike the training cost, the validation cost is not guaranteed to decrease at every round because that is not what we are minimizing with respect to. You can also use the same plot to help improve your learning rate if the cost explodes or becomes not a number.

Your learning rate is probably too high if your cost converges too slowly. You can try increasing your learning rate. So what does your final code look like? Well, we know we want to create a class with at least the predict and fit functions. You don't have to use a class, but I find that it encapsulates the code nicely and provides useful structure. Notice that once you have the algorithm in math, there isn't much work to convert it into numpy code. You know how to do matrix multiplication.

You know how to add? You know how to subtract. These are basic arithmetic operations that I'm sure you know already. If you don't know how to do these in NumPy, then I have a completely free NumPy course on the NumPy stack that you can take. Here is the second part of the code where we actually create an instance of the class and then use it. I've also included the squared error cost function just for completion sake. What's cool about this template is that it doesn't really change from one algorithm to the next.

The structure is basically always going to be this way, at least for supervised learning and unsupervised learning. So as we discussed before, it doesn't matter what algorithm you're implementing, whether it be linear regression, logistic regression, a neural network and so on, they always have the predict function and the fit function. And learning what goes inside these functions is equivalent to learning the algorithm. So in fact, one way to begin coding is to start with just this boilerplate code and then fill in the blanks.

2. How to Code by Yourself (part 2)
===================================

This is what is known in software engineering as test driven development or TDD in test driven development. We start by running tests because, number one, your code should always have tests. And number two, running the tests first forces you to think about how you expect your API to work, and it allows you to make sensible design decisions without being distracted by implementation details. So you see how this applies to every machine learning algorithm, not just linear regression.

In a lot of my older coding videos, I unfortunately decided to just write the code straight through. This helps some people but is not ideal for others. In addition, the coding videos will appear without warning. Generally, the lectures appear in the same pattern. First, we do theory and then we do the corresponding code. It pretty much always works this way. So after the theory lecture, you can be pretty sure we are going to implement that theory in code soon, unless it's so simple that it can be an exercise.

Now, to avoid treating everyone like they're in kindergarten, I don't have videos telling you that it's time to code. You're all proactive and independent adults, so when you learn some theory, you already know that it's time to try to put that into code by yourself. Sometimes you might get stuck and have no idea what to write in code or what the dataset is. In that case, it's fine to watch the next coding lecture, but don't watch it all the way through.

Watch just enough so that you know what the general idea is and what we're trying to accomplish, and then try to finish the rest yourself. Now, let's say you've watched just enough so that you know what we're trying to do. You know the theory, but you still can't complete the code. At this point, you may want to revisit the lecture theory to make sure you really understood each part. Failing that, you may want to try and get clarification on the discussion board.

Failing that, you may want to look at external resources as well. Sometimes you might be missing some important computer science background that I've assumed everyone already knows. That's perfectly fine because everyone has a different background. It's highly encouraged to do your own research, and this is also perfectly normal. It's not a bad thing. It's a good thing. Ask any university student if this is what they do, and they'll tell you that they do it all the time.

This builds a level of mental toughness and initiative taking that's important if you want to learn this stuff. There's a lot to learn and I myself am still learning new things every day. So let's say you've rewatched the theory lecture, you've looked at some external resources on the theory and you still don't understand how you would put it into code. Well, at this point, there are some great exercises you can do. Watch the coding lecture or look at the full code on GitHub.

Go through it and make sure you understand each line. The next step is without looking at the code again, try to implement it yourself. This by itself is a great learning tool. Many of the algorithms I've learned, I didn't understand them the first time. I learned them by looking at someone else's implementation and dissecting it. I looked at each part and I asked myself, Which parts did I get right and which parts did I get wrong? This helps you understand the mistakes you were making previously.

The cool thing about implementation is it works backwards too. We go from theory to code. But thinking in the reverse direction, the code actually helps us understand the theory better also. I always say that if you can't implement the code, then you don't understand the theory. So if you can learn the theory 50% of the way, try to implement the code, look at an existing implementation, and then finally get it working. Then you can be guaranteed that you now know more than just 50% of the theory.

The act of coding helps you understand more of the theory than if you had just learned the theory by itself. So implementation helps you understand the theory, but it also helps you understand the intuition as well. Intuition is a tricky thing. In fact, I would go so far as to say it's a trap in many or maybe even most cases. Intuition is built on experience. That means deriving the theory from first principles, or in other words, deriving the theory from facts you already know to be true and from implementation.

Students often want to hear intuition from people with lots of experience. But the problem is that this intuition is built on hindsight. Intuition is often both the first thing and the last thing you want to focus on. You want to have a little bit of intuition at the beginning, so you have some general idea of what you want to accomplish. Then after you learn the theory and implementation, you can reflect on the details and summarize them at a high level.

This is real intuition built on hindsight and experience. The big mistake beginners make is that they read some intuition written by someone else who actually has experience when they themselves do not. From an outsider's perspective, it's hard to tell the difference. Why is that? Well, if the beginner can regurgitate the same high level ideas as the expert, then it would sound like they are also an expert when in fact they are not. There are, unfortunately, too many courses out there that do this.

They want you to feel good that you learn something and they want to avoid you having to do anything too difficult. And because you can repeat the same high level intuitions, it actually sounds like you're an expert. So this is why I call it a trap. Knowing intuition not only makes you feel like you know something, it also makes you really confident about this fact. And then because your ego wants to maintain this confidence, you'll start making crazy statements like the theory and implementation are not important because you already understand the intuition.

But as you know, this is completely backwards. Let's summarize this lecture, since that was a lot of information to take in. Remember, this lecture is all about how and why you should code by yourself. First we talked about why this is because it ensures you are thinking about the details and filling in the blanks using your own pattern recognition skills. It ensures you don't just assume you know what's going on, but that you actually know what's going on.

There's a big difference, especially if you're the type of person that likes to assume you know everything. Next, we talked about how we talked about how all data is the same and it doesn't really matter what domain or industry your data is from, the same algorithms all apply. Next, we looked at the interfaces to the algorithms which are also conveniently all the same. So we have a kind of mix and match type of scenario where we can input any kind of data into any kind of model.

For example, you can use linear regression with financial data, or you can use a neural network on ecological data. All data is the same in all models have the same interface. Next, we talked about a strategy for how to actually apply this. You've probably already seen that we always alternate between theory and code. First we discuss the theory and then we implement that in code. Sometimes you might have to go back and revisit the theory, or sometimes you might have to look things up on the Internet.

That's perfectly normal. This is pretty much the day in the life of a university student. We're trying to build the same level of mental toughness and initiative taking in this course. So the strategy is stop after you learn the theory and try to code yourself. Sometimes you need a little more detail, so you might want to watch a little bit of the coding lectures to see what's going on and then try to implement the rest yourself. If you can't get it by yourself, then maybe try watching the whole thing or looking at the expected result and then trying to implement it from memory.

This by itself is a great learning tool because it allows you to ask yourself, What did I get right and what did I get wrong? So you can understand the mistakes you were making. Finally, it's important to understand the difference between intuition, theory and implementation and how they reinforce each other. It's important to not fall into the trap of only understanding intuition written by someone else, and then assuming that this equates to actually understanding everything.

In many cases, it's not about waiting for someone to tell you some nice phrases that trick you into thinking you understand the intuition, but rather you should seek intuition by working through the theory and the implementation. And lastly, don't forget when it's time to code, you must code.
